Cubulating infinity in hierarchically hyperbolic spaces
Matthew Gentry Durham

Abstract
. We prove that the hierarchical hull of any finite set of interior points, hierarchy rays, and boundary points in a hierarchically hyperbolic space (HHS) is quasi-median quasi-isometric to a CAT(0) cube complex of bounded dimension. Our construction extends and refines a theorem of Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto about modeling hulls of interior points and our previous work with Zalloum on modeling finite sets of rays via limits of these finite models. We further prove that the quasi-median quasi-isometry between the hull of a finite set of rays or boundary points and its cubical model extends to an isomorphism between their respective hierarchical and simplicial boundaries. In this sense, we prove that the hierarchical boundary of any proper HHS is locally modeled by the simplicial boundaries of CAT(0) cube complexes. This is a purely geometric statement, allowing one to important various topologies from the cubical setting. Our proof of the cubical model theorem is new, even for the interior points case. In particular, we provide a concrete description of the cubical model as a cubical subcomplex of a product of simplicial trees into which the hierarchical data is directly encoded. Moreover, the above boundary isomorphism is new for all non-cubical HHSes, including mapping class groups and Teichmüller spaces of finite-type surfaces. As an application of our techniques, we show that in most HHSes, including all hierarchically hyperbolic groups, the distance between any pair of points in the top-level hyperbolic space is coarsely the length of a maximal 0 0 -separated chain of hyperplanes separating them in an appropriate cubical model. For mapping class groups, this says that these cubical models cubically encode distance in the curve graph of the surface.



1.
Introduction

The hierarchy machinery of Masur-Minsky
[
MM99
,
MM00
]
, powered by techniques from hyperbolic geometry, revolutionized our understanding of the geometry of mapping class groups and Teichmüller spaces. In recent years, an analogous influx of ideas from CAT(0) cubical complexes has transformed the area, leading to a similar seismic shift.

At the center of this recent wave is the
cubical model machinery
of Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto
[
BHS21
]
. Roughly speaking, they proved that the coarse convex hulls of any finite set of interior points in any
hierarchically hyperbolic space

[
BHS17a
]
, such as the mapping class group, are quasi-median quasi-isometric to CAT(0) cube complexes (see also Bowditch
[
Bow19
]
). This is a higher-rank generalization of the fact that coarse hulls of finite sets of points in hyperbolic spaces are quasi-trees. These cubical models have proven to be quite useful. They were essential in the resolution of Farb’s quasi-flats conjecture
[
BHS21
]
, proofs of semihyperbolicity
[
DMS20
,
HHP20
]
and coarse injectivity
[
HHP20
]
of the mapping class group, and inspired Petyt’s proof that mapping class groups are quasi-isometric to cube complexes
[
Pet21
]
(though necessarily non-equivariantly
[
Bri10
]
), among other results
[
ABD21
,
DZ22
]
.

The main purpose of this article is to extend this cubical machinery to model the hulls of finitely-many hierarchy rays and boundary points in a natural compactification of any proper HHS called the
hierarchical boundary

[
DHS17
]
, which we introduced with Hagen and Sisto. Moreover, we provide a local description of this boundary via these cubical models. We summarize our main results as follows:

Theorem A
.

In any (proper) HHS, the hierarchical hull of any finite set of interior points, hierarchy rays, and boundary points admits a quasi-median quasi-isometry to a bounded dimensional CAT(0) cube complex. Moreover, this map extends to a simplicial isomorphism between the simplicial boundary of the cubical model and the hierarchical boundary of the hull.

In fact, our proof of the cubical model theorem is entirely new, even in the interior point case. Our construction is quite different from those contained in
[
BHS21
,
Bow19
]
, and provides a wealth of new hierarchical information, especially in the infinite case as compared with the limiting cubical models for hierarchy rays that we constructed with Zalloum
[
DZ22
]
(see Remark
1.1
). Moreover, the construction is quite explicit and hands-on, and this alternative perspective has some added benefits, which we will describe below.

This article lays a broad technical foundation for future work using cubical techniques to analyze the geometry of hierarchically hyperbolic spaces and their hierarchical boundaries. This includes further stabilizations of these cubical models along the lines of our work with Minsky and Sisto
[
DMS20
]
, as well as metric and topological properties of hierarchical boundaries, neither of which are discussed at any length in this article. Infinite diameter objects in HHSes can be extremely complicated, and this complexity accounts for the vast majority of the work in this paper. In a future expository paper, we will explain how to prove the stable cubical model theorem from
[
DMS20
]
in the relatively simple case of modeling the hull of a pair of internal points, which is enough to recover a proof of semihyperbolicity of the mapping class group along the lines of
[
DMS20
]
.

The rest of the introduction provides the context of our results, a detailed discussion of the construction itself, and also gives some applications of our refined techniques. We begin with a brief discussion of the relevant notions from the world of HHSes, then a comparative discussion between the cubical model construction from
[
BHS21
]
(Subsection
1.2
) and our own (Subsection
1.3
). With this foundation, we then discuss some applications, including our simplicial boundary results (Subsection
1.4
) and our ability to extract curve graph data from the cubical models (Subsection
1.5
). The rest of the introduction then discusses the new technical aspects of our results. We begin in Subsection
1.7
by working a detailed example motivating our new hierarchical accounting techniques (Subsection
1.8
), and a refined version of the distance-formula (Subsection
1.9
). We end with a detailed outline of how it all fits together (Subsection
1.10
).

1.1.
A brief HHS primer

Building off of work of Masur-Minsky
[
MM99
,
MM00
]
for mapping class groups, Brock
[
Bro03
]
, Rafi
[
Raf07
]
, and the author
[
Dur16
]
for Teichmüller spaces, and Hagen
[
Hag13b
]
and Kim-Koberda
[
KK14
]
for right-angled Artin groups, Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto
[
BHS17a
]
introduced the axiomatic notion of a
hierarchically hyperbolic space
(Definition
2.1
) to capture some of the coarse geometric features common to all of these objects. This abstract perspective has proven to be quite useful, and, at the time of writing, the class of HHSes has expanded considerably to include:

•

Hyperbolic spaces.

•

Mapping class groups
[
MM99
,
MM00
,
Aou13
,
BHS17a
]
and Teichmüller spaces, with both the Teichmüller
[
Raf07
,
Dur16
]
and Weil-Petersson metrics
[
Bro03
]
.

•

Many
[
HS20
]
, but not all
[
She22
]
, CAT(0) cubical groups, including all right-angled Artin and Coxeter groups
[
BHS17a
]
.

•

Fundamental groups of closed 3-manifolds without Nil or Sol summands
[
BHS19
,
HRSS22
]
.

•

Quotients of mapping class groups by large powers of Dehn twists
[
BHMS20
]
and pseudo-Anosovs
[
BHS17b
]
.

•

Surface group extensions of lattice Veech groups
[
DDLS21b
,
DDLS21a
]
and multicurve stabilizers
[
Rus21
]
of mapping class groups.

•

The genus-2 handlebody group
[
Che22
]
.

•

Artin groups of extra-large type
[
HMS21
]
.

•

The class of HHSes is closed under products and appropriate combinations
[
BHS19
]
, including graph products
[
BR20
]
and graph braid groups
[
Ber21
]
.

The general philosophy is that every HHS
\mathcal{X}
is built from an indexed family of uniform
\delta
-hyperbolic spaces
\{\mathcal{C}(U)\}_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}
, in the follow sense. The ambient space
\mathcal{X}
has a uniform coarse Lipschitz projection
\pi_{U}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
to each
\mathcal{C}(U)
, which function like a closest point projections to quasiconvex subsets in a number of ways. These combine into a global map to the full product of the associated hyperbolic spaces

\Pi:\mathcal{X}\to\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(U).

The elements of the index set
\mathfrak{S}
, called
domains
, satisfy certain mutually exclusive
relations
—
orthogonality

\perp
,
nesting

\sqsubset
, and
transversality

\pitchfork
. These relations generalize the various ways (isotopy classes of) subsurfaces can interact—namely when
U,V
are essential subsurfaces, then
U\perp V
stands for disjointness,
U\sqsubset V
for
U
being a subsurface of
V
, and
U\pitchfork V
when neither of those holds. The nesting and transverse relations determine certain
consistency inequalities
(Definition
2.2
) which control the tuples appearing in the image of
\Pi
, in the sense that every image tuple satisfies these inequalities, while every tuple satisfying the inequalities can be coarsely
realized
as the image of a point (Theorem
2.4
)
[
BKMM12
,
BHS17a
]
. On the other hand, orthogonal domains have mutually unconstrained coordinates and determine
product regions
(Subsection
2.6
), which are infinite-diameter quasi-isometrically embedded products coarsely generalizing the product structure of the thin parts of Teichmüller space discovered by Minsky
[
Min96
]
.

Importantly, there is a Masur-Minsky-style
[
MM00
,
BHS19
]

distance formula
(Theorem
2.9
),

(1)

d_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b)\asymp\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}[[d_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(\pi_{U}(a),\pi_{U}(b))]]_{K}

which says that the distance between any pair of points
a,b\in\mathcal{X}
is coarsely equal to the
\ell^{1}
-distance in the above product, as long as we ignore terms smaller than some uniform threshold
K=K(\mathcal{X})>0
. Any pair of points is connected by a
hierarchy path

[
MM00
,
Dur16
,
BHS17a
]
, a nice uniform quasi-geodesic which projects to an unparameterized quasi-geodesic in each hyperbolic space (Definition
3.5
). Notably, we will recover new proofs of the distance formula and the existence of hierarchy paths in this paper via our refined cubical model techniques (Corollary
C
), as Bowditch
[
Bow19
]
does in his alternate construction of cubical models; see Subsection
1.6
for a comparison of his related but very different median-based approach.

The
hierarchical
or
HHS boundary

\partial\mathcal{X}
of
\mathcal{X}

[
DHS17
,
DHS20a
]
is built out of the Gromov boundaries of the various hyperbolic spaces in the hierarchy. The boundary has an underlying simplicial structure, where each
0
-simplex is a point in
\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
for some
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, with higher dimensional simplices corresponding to collections of
0
-simplices supported on pairwise orthogonal domains. Besides Gromov boundaries themselves, one motivation for this construction was Thurston’s compactification of the Teichmüller space of a finite-type surface by projective classes of measured laminations on the surface. The HHS boundary of Teichmüller space with the Teichmüller metric is morally a combinatorial version of this, where one forgets the measure classes on the minimal components and only remembers the topological (or combinatorial) information of the laminations and the pairwise disjoint subsurfaces on which these minimal components are supported. Like Thurston’s boundary for Teichmüller space, the HHS boundary compactifies any proper HHS
[
DHS17
, Theorem 3.4]
, however we focus on the simplicial structure in this article and will not deal with a choice of topology. Moreover, automorphisms of
\mathcal{X}
act by homeomorphisms on the HHS boundary of
\mathcal{X}
(see Definition
2.18
), a fact which proved quite useful in
[
DHS17
]
for proving algebraic results like the Tits alternative for hierarchically hyperbolic groups.

Notably, when an HHS
\mathcal{X}
is a CAT(0) cube complex, then there is a simplicial isomorphism from the simplicial boundary
[
Hag13a
]
of the cube complex to its (untopologized) hierarchical boundary
[
DHS17
, Theorem 10.1]
. Theorem
D
below gives a local version of this theorem for any proper HHS.

Finally, one can take a
hierarchical hull
of any subset
A\subset\mathcal{X}
in an HHS
\mathcal{X}
(Definition
6.4
). Roughly, the hull of a set
A
is a coarse object consisting of points in
\mathcal{X}
which project close to the hyperbolic hull,
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(\pi_{U}(A))
, for all
U\in\mathfrak{S}
. Hierarchical hulls satisfy a notion of quasi-convexity called
hierarchical quasi-convexity

[
BHS19
]
(Definition
2.11
), which directly generalizes the notion of quasi-convexity from hyperbolic spaces and coincides with median convexity in the HHS setting
[
RST18
]
. The hulls of finite sets of interior points
[
BKMM12
,
EMR17
,
BHS19
,
BHS21
]
, hierarchy rays
[
DZ22
]
, and boundary points (Definition
6.4
) are particularly nice, and in many ways encode all of the hierarchical geometry between the objects in question. These hulls are the main objects we study in this paper.

1.2.
The original cubical model construction

The main construction in this paper is a new proof of Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto’s cubical model theorem
[
BHS21
, Theorem 2.1]
which also works for points at infinity. See also Bowditch
[
Bow19
]
for an alternative proof of the interior point case in the slightly more general setting of coarse median spaces satisfying certain hierarchical axioms (see also Subsection
1.6
). We will now briefly discuss their construction, before moving on to explain our version.

For the purpose of this discussion, let
F\subset\mathcal{X}
be a finite subset of interior points in an HHS. One of the basic properties of an HHS is that there exists a constant
K=K(\mathcal{X})>0
so that the set
\mathcal{U}
of
relevant domains

U\in\mathfrak{S}
where
F
has a large diameter projection
\mathcal{U}=\{U\in\mathfrak{S}|\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(F)>K\}
is finite (Corollary
2.8
). This allows us to work only with the domains in
\mathcal{U}
. Projecting
F
to
\mathcal{C}(U)
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we can take the hyperbolic hull
H_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(\pi_{U}(F))\subset\mathcal{C}(U)
. The
hierarchical hull

H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(F)
of
F
can defined to be the set of points in
\mathcal{X}
which project sufficiently close to
H_{U}
in each
U\in\mathcal{U}
(see Definition
6.4
).

Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto’s approach to cubulating
H
proceeds as follows: To each hyperbolic hull
H_{U}
, we can associate its Gromov modeling tree
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
. If
\#\mathcal{U}=1
(which can be arranged when
\mathcal{X}
is hyperbolic), then this tree would suffice as a cubical model, but in general one needs a higher-dimensional model because of the product regions in
\mathcal{X}
. At this point, one might hope to use the fact that
H
admits a geometrically meaningful map
H\to\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}
to a finite product of trees, which is morally a CAT(0) cube complex, though not exactly as the
T_{U}
need not be simplicial trees. However, the consistency inequalities (Definition
2.2
) constrain the image—this map is usually abritrarily far from being coarsely surjective. The idea is to induce a wall-space structure on
H
from a carefully defined collection of ambient “walls” in the product which are defined in terms of the trees.

The challenge is that domains in
\mathcal{U}
are usually related in a complicated fashion, and any given
U\in\mathcal{U}
usually contains many domains which
nest
(
\sqsubset
) into
U
(recall that nesting corresponds to subsurface inclusion in the surface case). Each domain
V\sqsubset U
with
V\in\mathcal{U}
not only has its own hyperbolic hull
H_{U}
and modeling tree
T_{U}
, but also is encoded in
\mathcal{C}(U)
by its
\rho
-set

\rho^{V}_{U}
(e.g., its boundary curves in the surface case) which are located near
H_{U}
(and hence
\phi_{U}(T_{U})
) in
\mathcal{C}(U)
. From the beginning
[
MM00
]
, these
\rho
-sets have been understood to encode important organizational information about how the various pieces of the hierarchy fit together.

Figure 1
.
A cartoon of the tree-walls in the Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto construction of cubical model for the hierarchical hull of
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\})
: The tree
\phi_{U}(T_{U})\subset\mathcal{C}(U)
models the hyperbolic hull
H_{U}=(\pi_{U}(\{a,b,c,d,e,f\}))
. One chooses the tree-walls (in blue) to coarsely encode lengths along the subtrees of
\phi_{U}(T_{U})
which are far from the
\rho
-points (gold) and leaves (purple). One then pulls back these tree-walls to obtain a wall-space on
H
, with the cubical model for
H
being the Sageev dual cube complex to this wallspace. Below is the setup for a domain
V\in\mathcal{U}
with
V\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}U
, i.e. no smaller domain in
\mathcal{U}
nests into
V
. In this case, since
\rho^{V}_{U}
is far from any branched point or point of
F
, the Bounded Geodesic Image Property
7
forces this tree
\phi_{V}(T_{V})
to be coarsely an interval, and the lack of any nesting domains means that the number of tree-walls is coarsely the length of the interval.

One of the key observations in
[
BHS21
]
is that, from the perspective of
U
, the information coming from
\rho
-sets in
U
is redundant in a strong way. In particular, the
\rho
-sets coming from nested domains
V\sqsubset U
can coarsely cover arbitrarily large portions of the tree
\phi_{U}(T_{U})
(including possibly all of it). Each
\rho^{V}_{U}
for
V\sqsubset U
also represents a large-diameter projection of
F
to
\mathcal{C}(V)
, making the small-diameter
\rho
-sets in
\mathcal{C}(U)
redundant. The construction in
[
BHS21
]
involves carefully picking a family of tree-walls in
\phi_{U}(T_{U})
in the complement of these
\rho
-sets, and then pulling them back to walls on the hull
H\subset\mathcal{X}
using projections coming from the hierarchical setup. See Figure
1
for a heuristic picture.

These tree-walls allow them to build a wall-space structure on the hull
H
as follows: Each tree-wall in
\phi_{U}(T_{U})
determines two half-trees of
\phi_{U}(T_{U})
, which pull back to a wall and two half-spaces in
H
under (the inverses of) the domain projection
\pi_{U}:H\to\mathcal{C}(U)
and closest-point projection
p_{U}:\mathcal{C}(U)\to\phi_{U}(T_{U})
. They then directly prove that the dual cube complex
\mathcal{W}
(provided by Sageev
[
Sag95
]
) to this wall-space admits a quasi-median quasi-isometry
\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{W}\to H
. The map
\mathfrak{p}
is constructed coordinate-wise for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
by taking a consistent tuple of half-spaces (in the wall-space sense), projecting them to
\mathcal{C}(U)
, and then intersecting over all of half-space projections. They then show that this tuple is consistent (in the hierarchical sense), and that it defines a quasi-isometry, both of which are nontrivial tasks.

One significantly complicating feature of this construction is the necessity of translating cubical techniques in the model to hierarchical facts in the hull via the map described above, which, for instance, notably elongated arguments in our paper with Minsky and Sisto (see especially
[
DMS20
, Section 5]
). One of the main technical advantages of our alternate approach is an intermediate object which encodes a wealth of hierarchical information in an explicitly cubical form, thus acting as an interface between these two perspectives. We explain how to build this bridge next.

1.3.
Cubulating hulls via products of trees

Figure 2
.
A cartoon of cluster formation and the collapsed trees in a hierarchical family of trees coming the setup in the example in Figure
1
: Instead of working in the hyperbolic space
\mathcal{C}(U)
, we work directly in the (abstract) modeling trees,
T_{U}
. Here, we identify
shadow
subtrees (gold) of
T_{U}
using the
\rho
-set data in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, group nearby shadows and leaves into
cluster
subtrees (green), and then collapse down these cluster subtrees to obtain the collapsed tree
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
. This process results in special
marked points
(green) on the collapsed trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
, which can encode both leaf data and
\rho
-set data (denoted by
\hat{\delta}
). These cluster subtrees can have arbitrarily large diameter and collapsing them can lead to the identification of multiple leaves. For the domain
V\sqsubset U
which is
\sqsubset
-minimal in
\mathcal{U}
, the corresponding collapsed tree
\widehat{T}_{V}
is an interval of coarsely the same length as the diameter of
T_{V}
. In fact, almost every domain in
\mathcal{U}
is one of these two-sided
bipartite
domains (Definition
9.2
), a fact which plays a key role in our arguments.

The starting point of our construction of the cubical model for the hull
H
is a different conclusion from their observation that
\rho
-sets encode redundant information. Instead of ignoring how the
\rho
-set information is encoded in the images of the trees
\phi_{U}(T_{U})\subset\mathcal{C}(U)
, we pull them back to the (abstract) tree
T_{U}
, group them into
cluster
subtrees, and then collapse these subtrees
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
to obtain a family of trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
which only encode the desired geometric information from each domain
U\in\mathcal{U}
while still encoding the organizational information provided by the
\rho
-sets. See Figure
2
for a heuristic picture.

We show that this family of trees
\{\widehat{T}_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
with its accompanying collapsed
\rho
-set data admits the structure of what we call a
hierarachical family of trees
(Definition
12.1
). We think of such a structure as an “exact” HHS structure, in the sense that it possesses many of the features of an HHS, while all of the coarseness has been eliminated. In particular, every hierarchical family of trees admits a family of
consistency equations
, for which the
0
-consistent set
\mathcal{Q}\subset\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}
(Definition
7.9
) behaves like an HHS in many important ways. Notably, the standard HHS structure on any right-angled Artin group
[
BHS17a
]
admits such an “exact” structure, where all of the associated coarseness constants are
0
. In this way, a hierarchical family of trees is as hierarchically nice as possible. See Subsection
1.7
for an explicit example in a RAAG, and item (
12
) of Remark
1.1
below for a discussion of how this setup relates to Casals-Ruiz–Hagen–Kazachkov’s notion of an
\mathbb{R}
-cubing
[
CRHK21
]
.

One could stop here and observe that
\mathcal{Q}
is a median subalgebra of a product of finite-valent trees, and hence quasi-median quasi-isometric to a CAT(0) cube complex (see
[
Bow19
, Section 2]
,
[
HP23b
, Proposition 2.12]
, or
[
CRHK21
, Theorem 4.16]
). However, we give an explicit direct proof. We prove that, up to quasi-median quasi-isometry of the space
\mathcal{Q}
, each collapsed tree
\widehat{T}_{U}
can be replaced by a simplicial tree in an appropriate sense via our Tree Trimming
10.2
techniques, which function like a fine-tuned version of the distance formula; see Subsection
1.9
below for a discussion. As a result, the
0
-consistent set
\mathcal{Q}
is contained in product of simplicial trees, i.e., a genuine CAT(0) cube complex
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}
. This allows us to work with the induced wall structure on
\mathcal{Q}
, and we prove that
\mathcal{Q}
with its induced
\ell^{1}
-metric is
isometric
to the cubical dual to this wall-space. This space
\mathcal{Q}
is the aforementioned bridge, simultaneously hierarchical and cubical.

While the above discussion ignored the possibility for allowing points at infinity and hierarchy rays, our construction works in that context, and we will discuss this aspect in the next subsection. However, for the purposes of summarizing the above discussion, we state the full version of our modeling theorem (Theorem
13.6
):

Theorem B
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a proper HHS,
F\subset\mathcal{X}
a nonempty finite set of interior points,
\Lambda
a (possibly empty) set of hierarchy rays and points in
\partial\mathcal{X}
, and
H
the hierarchical hull of
F\cup\Lambda
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be the set of relevant domains for
F\cup\Lambda
, and
\{\widehat{T}_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
the corresponding hierarchical family of simplicial trees. Then the following hold:

(1)

The
0
-consistent canonical subset
\mathcal{Q}\subset\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\left(\widehat{T}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}_{U}\right)
is weakly convex and isometric to its cubical dual. In particular,
\mathcal{Q}
is a finite dimensional CAT(0) cube complex, whose dimension is bounded in terms
\mathcal{X}
.

(2)

There exists an
L=L(|F\cup\Lambda|,\mathcal{X})>0
and an
L
-quasi-inverse pair of maps

•

a
L
-quasi-median
(L,L)
-quasi-isometry
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
and

•

a
L
-quasi-median
(L,L)
-quasi-isometric embedding
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{X}
with
d^{Haus}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{\Omega}(\mathcal{Q}),H)<L
.

(3)

There exists a collection of
0
-cells
\widehat{F}\cup\widehat{\Lambda}
(possibly at infinity) of
\mathcal{Q}
which are in bijective correspondence under
\widehat{\Omega}
and
\widehat{\Psi}
with
F\cup\Lambda
. Moreover,
\mathcal{Q}
is the cubical convex hull of
\widehat{F}\cup\widehat{\Lambda}
.

(4)

(Equivariance) If
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{X})
is an HHS automorphism, then the hull of
g\cdot(F\cup\Lambda)
admits a cubical model
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
with
g
inducing a cubical isomorphism
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

Remark 1.1
.

Some comments are in order:

(1)

Properness in the statement above is only required when the set of points at infinity
\Lambda
is nonempty. The assumption that
F
is nonempty provides a basepoint in the interior of
\mathcal{X}
, which is necessary for many arguments.

(2)

In conclusion (1),
\mathcal{Q}
is
weakly convex
in the sense that any pair of points in
\mathcal{Q}
can be connected by a geodesic in
\mathcal{Q}
, in fact a combinatorial geodesic in its cubical structure which
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
sends to a hierarchy path; see Proposition
14.8
.

(3)

In the case where
\Lambda
is nonempty, the set of relevant domains
\mathcal{U}
is frequently (and indeed generically
[
ST19
,
DZ22
]
) infinite. This fact alone necessitates new techniques, even if one wanted to utilize existing cubical model constructions from
[
BHS21
,
Bow19
]
. See Subsection
1.8
for a discussion of these new “passing-up”-type techniques.

(4)

The isometry between
\mathcal{Q}
and its cubical dual involves showing that hierarchical consistency in
\mathcal{Q}
corresponds
exactly
to cubical consistency in its dual, in the sense of Sageev
[
Sag95
]
. A coarse version of this correspondence is at the heart of the original construction in
[
BHS21
]
, but our version makes it completely explicit. See Subsection
13.2
for a discussion.

(5)

The notion of “canonical” for a tuple in
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\left(\widehat{T}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}_{U}\right)
(Definition
7.9
) is necessary to pick out an “internal point” of the model space
\mathcal{Q}
. It is a hierarchical analogue of the cubical canonicality condition which determines interior
0
-cubes (or equivalently the descending chain condition on ultrafilters, Definition
12.7
), and we make this connection explicit in the proof of the isometry between
\mathcal{Q}
and its cubical dual; see Proposition
12.17
in particular.

(6)

The map
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}\subset\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}
is defined as simply as possible in a coordinate-wise fashion as a combination of the various projection and collapsing maps described above; see Subsection
7.7
for a precise definition.

(7)

On the other hand, the coordinate-wise definition of the map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{X}
is more involved, as possibly arbitrarily large pieces of nesting information have been collapsed in the construction of
\mathcal{Q}
. The process of uncollapsing exploits the fundamentally cubical nature of
\mathcal{Q}
, namely the two-sided
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal
bipartite
domains as discussed in Figure
2
, though this part of the argument does not depend on any cubical techniques, including the fact that
\mathcal{Q}
is isometric to its cubical dual, which is a separate argument. In this way, we see our construction as highlighting the fundamentally cubical nature of HHSes, at least at the local level. See Section
9
for more details, especially Subsection
9.2
for a detailed outline of this point of the argument.

(8)

Equivariance
: The construction in Theorem
B
is invariant under any
HHS automorphisms

g\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{X})
. HHS automorphisms are isometries of
\mathcal{X}
which preserves the projections and other relevant hierarchical data of
\mathcal{X}
. In particular, the mapping class group is a group of HHS automorphisms of itself; see Subsection
2.7
for more details,
[
BHS19
, Subsection 1G]
(or equivalently
[
PS20
, Section 2]
) for a precise definition, and
[
DHS17
,
DHS20a
]
for various results about HHS automorphisms. Equivariance of the cubical models is an immediate consequence of the fact that the atoms of the entire construction are fundamental parts of an HHS, which are automorphism-equivariant by definition.

(9)

The quasi-median property
:
Coarse median
spaces were introduced by Bowditch
[
Bow13
]
to generalize feature common to both hyperbolic spaces and cube complexes, with the main motivating example being the mapping class group
[
BM11
]
. CAT(0) cube complexes are in fact
median
spaces (see e.g.
[
CDH10
,
Che00
,
Rol16
]
and
[
Bow22
]
for an overview), with their median structure directly encoding their
\ell^{1}
(or combinatorial) geometry, while the coarse median structure on any HHS encodes a large amount of its hierarchical geometry (see e.g.
[
RST18
,
BHS21
]
). The fact that the the model map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
is
quasi-median
(Definition
15.6
) says, at least in principle, that the cubical geometry of
\mathcal{Q}
directly encodes the hierarchical geometry of
H
. We note that the quasi-median property of these model maps was already obtained in the interior point case in
[
BHS21
]
and for limiting versions modeling hierarchy rays in
[
DZ22
]
, and this property has proved useful in essentially every application of this technology
[
BHS21
,
HHP20
,
DMS20
,
DZ22
]
.

(10)

Refined infinite models
: In our work with Zalloum
[
DZ22
]
, we produce a cubical model for any finite set of hierarchy rays by taking an unrescaled ultralimit of finite cubical models. While we are able to extract a good amount of information out of the fact that these limiting model maps were quasi-median quasi-isometries, the cubical models in Theorem
B
contain a vast amount of additional hierarchical information that is lost in the limiting process. In particular, as with the original construction in
[
BHS21
]
, every hyperplane in
\mathcal{Q}
is labeled by some relevant domain in
\mathcal{U}
and two hyperplanes cross if and only if their labels are orthogonal. Moreover, we recover the fact that the flat subspaces of the cubical models are mapped to (quasi-)flat subspaces of standard product regions in the HHS
\mathcal{X}
(Lemma
14.11
). These facts alone are quite useful for applications; see Theorem
F
below and
[
CRHK21
, Section 16]
for an in-depth discussion.

(11)

Extended tuples,
0
-cubes at infinity, and Roller boundaries
: Another motivation for the definition of points in the HHS boundary
[
DHS17
]
was the notion of a
generalized marking
from Minsky’s work on the Ending Lamination Theorem
[
Min10
, Section 5]
, which were required to build model manifolds for hyperbolic
3
-manifolds with geometrically infinite ends. The information of a generalized marking on a surface
S
can be coarsely encoded in a pair consisting of a marking on
S
(in the sense of
[
MM00
]
) and a point in the HHS boundary, or an appropriately constructed hierarchy ray (Proposition
3.14
). Any such generalized marking determines a tuple in
\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}(\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U))
satisfying a notion of
extended consistency
(see Definition
3.16
and Proposition
3.18
). These extended tuples play an analogous role to the
0
-cubes at infinity of a cube complex. In this article, we lay the foundation for locally modeling the space of extended tuples by the
0
-cubes at infinity of appropriately chosen cubical models, and thus for the possibility of defining a Roller-type boundary for any proper HHS.

One upshot of this discussion is that the infinite cubical models constructed in Theorem
B
also can also be used to organize the infinite hierarchies that arise in the context of the Ending Lamination Theorem, though with much less specificity than required for that theorem. See Bowditch
[
Bow19
, Section 11]
for an in-depth discussion of this connection in the context of the interval between a pair of points in the pants graph of a compact surface, where the pants graph is quasi-isometric to the Teichmüller space of the surface with the Weil-Petersson metric
[
Bro03
]
.

(12)

Relation to
\mathbb{R}
-cubings
:
The
0
-consistent subset
\mathcal{Q}
of the (possibly infinite) product of trees
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}
satisfies most the axioms for being a
discrete
\mathbb{R}
-cubing
in the sense of Casals-Ruiz–Hagen–Kazachkov
[
CRHK21
, Definition 4.2]
; see especially Definition
12.1
of our notion of a hierarchical family of trees which collects a similar set of properties as axioms. The key difference is that
[
CRHK21
, Definition 4.2]
requires the ambient space to be path-connected, which, in our setting, is tantamount to assuming that
\mathcal{Q}
is a CAT(0) cube complex. Thus one consequence of Theorem
B
is that the finite dimensional CAT(0) cube complex
\mathcal{Q}
admits a special discrete
\mathbb{R}
-cubing structure that is more hierarchical in flavor than the structure one might produce directly from its cubical structure; see
[
CRHK21
, Example 4.25]
for a discussion of the latter. As a consequence of their impressive machinery, we automatically obtain a host of other structural consequences for
\mathcal{Q}
, which would be interesting to explore.

As we will see below, our cubical model construction is quite robust, and we develop a number of tools for producing many variations of cubical models which can be tailored to a number of potential applications. For the rest of the introduction, we refer to
\mathcal{Q}
, or any of its variants, as a
cubical model
for the hull
H
.

Before we discuss boundaries, we pause to observe the following consequence of Theorem
B
, namely a new proof of (the upper bound of) the Distance Formula
2.9
and the existence of hierarchy paths:

Corollary C
.

In any HHS in
\mathcal{X}
, the upper-bound of the Distance Formula
2.9
holds. Moreover, any pair of interior points in
\mathcal{X}
are connected by a hierarchy path.

Proof.

Let
a,b\in\mathcal{X}
be points in an HHS
\mathcal{X}
, and fix
K=K(\mathcal{X})>0
sufficiently large. Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U|d_{U}(a,b)>K\}
be the set of
K
-relevant domains. Let
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b)
and
\mathcal{Q}
be a cubical model with cubical model map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
identifying
\hat{a},\hat{b}\in\mathcal{Q}
with
a,b\in H
.

Theorem
8.1
provides a distance formula-free proof that

\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}[[d_{U}(a,b)]]_{K}\asymp\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})

where the left-hand sum is the distance-formula sum (see equation (
2
) or Theorem
2.9
) while the right-hand sum is exactly
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})
. On the other hand, Proposition
14.8
provides a direct proof that
\widehat{\Omega}
sends any combinatorial geodesic in
\mathcal{Q}
between
\hat{a},\hat{b}
to a uniform quasi-geodesic—in fact, a hierarchy path—between
a,b
in
\mathcal{X}
, and hence the right-hand side gives a lower-bound for
d_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b)
. Both conclusions follow.
∎

To our knowledge, the difficult part (the upper-bound) of every proof of the distance formula involves constructing hierarchy paths, and our proof is no exception. Note that the proof that combinatorial geodesics in cubical models are sent to hierarchy paths (Proposition
14.8
) does not involve appealing to the median structure, which is necessary since currently the only proof that median paths are hierarchy paths uses the distance formula
[
RST18
, Theorem 5.2]
.

1.4.
Cubulating infinity in proper HHSes

In addition to a more robust cubical model construction, the main contribution of this article—and the bulk of the work therein—involves extending the cubical model construction to allow for points in the HHS boundary and hierarchy rays. This leads to a local description of the HHS boundary of any proper HHS via the simplicial boundaries of appropriately chosen cubical models.

The
simplicial boundary
of a CAT(0) cube complex was introduced by Hagen
[
Hag13a
]
. It is a fundamentally combinatorial object designed to capture not only the behavior of combinatorial geodesic rays, but also other more pathological ways of running off to infinity, like an infinite staircase (see
[
Hag13a
, Figure 1]
or
[
FFH23
, Figure 1]
). It is a simplicial complex whose simplices are
unidirectional boundary sets
or
UBSes
(Definition
16.2
), which are infinite equivalence classes of hyperplanes which encode a combinatorial form of divergence. The simplicial boundary of a cube complex is called
fully visible
if every UBS admits a combinatorial ray representative. Hagen proved that every UBS in a sufficiently nice cube complex (like those considered in this paper) admits a decomposition into
minimal
UBSes (see Theorem
16.6
). Hence every UBS in a fully visible simplicial boundary admits a decomposition with minimal combinatorial ray representatives each of which “points” in a well-defined direction.

The following summarizes our boundary isomorphism result:

Theorem D
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a proper HHS,
F\subset\mathcal{X}
a nonempty finite set of interior points,
\Lambda
a (possibly empty) set of hierarchy rays and points in
\partial\mathcal{X}
, and
H
the hierarchical hull of
F\cup\Lambda
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be any cubical model for
H
. Then the following hold:

(1)

The map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
extends to a simplicial isomorphism
\partial\widehat{\Omega}:\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial H
from the simplicial boundary of
\mathcal{Q}
to the HHS boundary of
H
.

(2)

The simplicial boundary
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
of
\mathcal{Q}
is fully visible.

(3)

(Equivariance) If
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{X})
is an HHS automorphism, then the cubical isomorphism
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}_{g}
induced by
g
induces a simplicial isomorphism
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

The map
\partial\widehat{\Omega}:\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial_{\Delta}H
extends
\widehat{\Omega}
in the following sense: Any combinatorial ray
\gamma
in
\mathcal{Q}
is sent to a hierarchy ray
\widehat{\Omega}(\gamma)\subset H
whose limiting simplex in
\partial H
(Definition
3.8
) is the image under
\partial\widehat{\Omega}
of the simplex in
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
corresponding to
\gamma
. Since
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
is fully visible, this correspondence is as complete as possible; see Theorem
16.29
for more details.

One consequence of the above theorem is a local description of the hierarchical boundary of any proper HHS, as we will now explain. Note that the simplicial isomorphism
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial H
holds regardless of the number of boundary points and hierarchy rays in
\Lambda
. Given a finite collection of simplices in
\partial\mathcal{X}
, one can construct a finite collection of hierarchy rays whose limiting data encode exactly these simplices; see Proposition
3.14
. Theorem
D
then says that the simplicial boundary of any cubical model of the hierarchical hull of this finite set of rays is isomorphic to the hull’s hierarchical boundary, which is (as a simplicial complex) precisely the desired set of simplices. Hence we obtain the following as a corollary:

Corollary E
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a proper HHS and
\Sigma\subset\partial\mathcal{X}
be any finite union of simplices. Then
\Sigma
can be realized as the hierarchical boundary of the hierarchical hull
H
of a finite set of hierarchy rays
\Lambda
in
\mathcal{X}
, and if
\mathcal{Q}_{\Sigma}
is any cubical model for
H
, then the model map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{X}
extends to a simplicial embedding
\partial\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}_{\Sigma}\to\partial\mathcal{X}
whose image is exactly
\Sigma
.

We note that both Theorem
D
and Corollary
E
are entirely geometric statements, and thus do not reference any sort of topology on any of the relevant objects. As such, they provide a foundation for future investigations of the various possible cubical-like topologies (see e.g.,
[
FFH23
]
) one can put on the HHS boundary of any proper HHS.

Moreover, Corollary
E
provides a potential tool for studying homological and homotopical properties of the HHS boundary
\partial\mathcal{X}
of a proper HHS
\mathcal{X}
. In particular, suppose
\partial\mathcal{X}
is endowed with a topology
\tau
for which the identity map
id:\partial\mathcal{X}\to\partial\mathcal{X}
induces a continuous map
(\partial\mathcal{X},\tau_{\Delta})\to(\partial\mathcal{X},\tau)
, where
\tau_{\Delta}
is the topology on
\partial\mathcal{X}
realized as a CW complex. Then any continuous map
S^{n}\to\partial\mathcal{X}
from the
n
-sphere intersects only finitely-many simplices, and hence Corollary
E
would allow one to employ cubical techniques to study the map. We thank Mark Hagen for pointing this connection out to us.

1.5.
Cubically capturing the curve graph

In our view, the greatest utility of these cubical models
[
BHS21
,
DMS20
,
HHP20
,
CRHK21
,
DZ22
]
is that they encode a wealth of hierarchical information into their cubical structure, thereby allowing one to employ a mixture of cubical and hierarchical techniques. A significant part of this article is spent developing a number of tools for extracting more information, not only in the case of infinite models but even for finite models. While we will discuss some of these tools in detail below, we first highlight one feature of this more robust model machinery—namely that one can construct cubical models which coarsely encode curve graph distance.

In our work with Zalloum
[
DZ22
]
, we were interested in studying, among other things, the cubical geometry of the hierarchical hulls associated to hierarchy rays which display “hyperbolic” behavior. For instance, this includes hierarchy rays in the mapping class group or the Teichmüller space of a finite type surface
S
which project to (unparameterized) quasi-geodesic rays in the curve graph
\mathcal{C}(S)
of
S
, examples of which are Morse
[
Beh06a
,
ABD21
]
and sublinearly-Morse rays
[
DZ22
]
(the latter were first defined in
[
QRT20
]
). As part of our analysis with Zalloum, we established one half of a correspondence between this sort of global hyperbolic behavior of rays in an HHS and certain hyperbolic features of their cubical representatives. In this paper, we complete this correspondence in a strong way, which we now explain.

Following Genevois
[
Gen20
]
, we say that two hyperplanes in a CAT(0) cube complex
X
are
L-separated
if the number of hyperplanes crossing both is less than
L
(see Definition
17.4
). Genevois proved that one can define a new distance
d^{X}_{L}
on the
0
-skeleton
X^{(0)}
of
X
by using the length of any maximal
L
-separated chain between any
0
-cells
x,y\in X^{(0)}
. Importantly, he proved that
(X,d^{X}_{L})
is
\delta_{L}
-hyperbolic, with
\delta_{L}
depending on the chosen
L
. The philosophy here is that separated hyperplanes encode hyperbolic behavior in CAT(0) cube complexes.

To setup our next theorem, let
\mathcal{X}
be an HHS with
unbounded products
(Definition
17.1
), a natural weak assumption which includes all of the main examples, including every hierarchically hyperbolic group. Let
S
be the label for the
\sqsubset
-maximal hyperbolic space (e.g., the curve graph
\mathcal{C}(S)
when
S
is a finite type surface and
\mathcal{X}
is the mapping class group), as constructed in our paper with Abbott and Behrstock
[
ABD21
]
; see Subsection
17.1
for a discussion.

Figure 3
.
Fake
L
-separation: A simple example which shows that the upper bound
d^{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}(x,y)\prec d_{\mathcal{C}(S)}(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{S}(y))
does not hold for general cubical models
\mathcal{Q}
and separation constants
L>0
. The standard HHG structure on
G=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\cong\langle a,b|[a,b]=1\rangle
consists of two lines labeled by
a,b
and a point for the top-level hyperbolic space
\mathcal{C}(S)
(which can be arranged for any HHS which is coarsely a product). Since this is a cube complex, cubical convex hulls coincide with hierarchical hulls, and are thus their own cubical models. Given any
L>0
and
n>0
, the cubical hull for
(id_{G},a^{n}b^{L})
will be a rectangle (in faded red), where the collapsed trees are just intervals. The set of (blue) vertical hyperplanes is
L
-separated because each is crossed by each of the
L
-many (green) horizontal hyperplanes, while the projection of the hull to the top level hyperbolic space
\mathcal{C}(S)
is a point (because
\mathcal{C}(S)
is a point). Fixing
L
and letting
n
grow shows that the upper bound does not hold for general cubical models.
Fake separation is easy to arrange elsewhere. For instance, the cubical model of the hull of the identity and any sufficiently high power of a Dehn twist in the mapping class group of a finite type surface consists entirely of fake
0
-separated hyperplanes.

Theorem F
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an HHS with unbounded products. For any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, let
H
be the hierarchical hull of
x,y
. Then there exists a cubical model
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}_{x,y}\to H
identifying
\hat{x},\hat{y}
with
x,y
so that

d^{\mathcal{Q}_{x,y}}_{0}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\asymp d_{\mathcal{C}(S)}(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{S}(y)),

where the constants in
\asymp
and the construction of
\mathcal{Q}_{x,y}
depend only on the hierarchical structure of
\mathcal{X}
.

•

Moreover, if
\gamma
is a hierarchy ray, then

\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(S)}(\pi_{S}(\gamma))=\infty\ \iff\ \mathrm{diam}_{0}^{\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma(0),\gamma}}(\gamma)=\infty.

In other words, the distance in the top-level hyperbolic space is coarsely encoded in the cubical geometry of a family of cubical models. One consequence of Theorem
F
is that a hierarchy ray
\gamma
determines a point in the boundary of
\mathcal{C}(S)
if and only if
\gamma
determines a point in the boundary of the contact graph of
\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma(0),\gamma}
. The
contact graph
, introduced by Hagen
[
Hag13b
]
, is a hyperbolic graph which plays an analogous role to the curve graph for cube complexes. Hence hyperbolic directions in the HHS
\mathcal{X}
coincide the hyperbolic directions in a family of uniform cubical models.

We note that
[
DZ22
, Theorem C]
provides the bound
d^{\mathcal{Q}}_{L}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\succ d_{S}(\pi_{S}(x),\pi_{S}(y))
for any cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
, where
L
is some constant depending on
\mathcal{X}
. In Lemma
17.6
, we strengthen this to
0
-separation (called
strong separation
in
[
BC12
]
) with a simpler proof which uses the fact that crossing hyperplanes are labeled by orthogonal domains, as discussed above in item (
10
) of Remark
1.1
. Thus Theorem
F
completes the relationship in the strongest way possible. See Figure
3
for a simple example of how “fake” separation can prevent the upper bound in Theorem
F
from holding in general cubical models, and Subsection
1.10
for a discussion of how to alter the cubical models to prevent fake separation from occurring.

In future work, we will develop a variation on this cubical machinery (along the lines of
[
DMS20
]
) to, following Genevois, define a new metric on the mapping class group which will make it quasi-isometric to the curve graph. We note that Incerti-Medici–Zalloum
[
IMZ21
]
have already done this for cube complexes which admit an HHS structure; see also Petyt-Spriano-Zalloum
[
PSZ22
]
for a generalization of these ideas to CAT(0) spaces, as well as Zalloum’s expository article
[
Zal23b
]
.

1.6.
Related results

The use of local approximations, for instance in manifold theory, is at least as old as modern mathematics. In geometric group theory, paramount examples include Gromov hyperbolic spaces and CAT(0) spaces, which are defined by
3
-point comparisons to tripods and Euclidean space, respectively. As discussed above, Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto’s cubical model theorem
[
BHS21
, Theorem F]
directly generalizes Gromov’s tree modeling theorem
[
Gro87
]
for hyperbolic spaces.

In
[
Bow13
]
, Bowditch introduced the notion of a coarse median space, a class of objects including cube complexes and HHSes which has proven very useful. Their defining property is a coarse version of the median property which hinges on local comparisons with cube complexes (see Definition
15.4
), which is in some sense a weak version of the cubical model theorem (see Remark
15.5
). In
[
Bow19
]
, Bowditch recovers the full cubical model theorem for interior points in the more general setting of coarse median spaces which satisfy certain HHS-like axioms. Notably, he recovers (as we do) a proof of the existence of median quasi-geodesic paths connecting any pair of points and gives a new proof of the distance formula (Theorem
2.9
). His approach is similar in spirit to ours, since his cubical model also arises as a subset of a product of trees. However, his approach is fundamentally different from ours (and the approach in
[
BHS21
]
) because it utilizes the median property as part of its axiomatic foundation, whereas medians are essentially a by-product of the hierarchical framework. Moreover, his construction does not allow for modeling hulls of median rays (which coincide with hierarchy rays in the HHS setting), whereas the infinite models we construct and their applications constitute the majority of work in this paper. We believe that any such extension of Bowditch’s construction would require an analogous set of tools to the ones we develop in this article; see Subsection
1.8
for a discussion of some of these techniques.

In
[
DZ22
]
, we proved with Zalloum that the hull of any finite collection of hierarchy rays in an HHS can be approximated by a CAT(0) cube complex which is obtained by taking an unrescaled ultralimit of the finite cubical approximations from
[
BHS21
]
. Our purpose in that paper was to study certain hyperbolic-like directions in HHSes, such as sublinearly-Morse geodesic rays, and we discussed in Remark
1.1
the ways in which the current article refines and strengthens this result. Much of that discussion did not actually involve any hierarchical geometry, and so we carried some of that work out in the framework of
locally quasi-cubical spaces
, namely coarse median spaces where median hulls of finite sets of points are quasi-median quasi-isometric to CAT(0) cube complexes. This more general framework is currently being further developed by Petyt-Spriano-Zalloum
[
PSZ23
]
. See Zalloum’s expository article
[
Zal23c
]
for a broad discussion of cubical approximations in geometric group theory.

Theorem
D
and Corollary
E
are related to recent work of Hamenstädt
[
Ham21
]
and Petyt
[
Pet21
]
, both of which give a global cubical model for boundaries of the mapping class group
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
of a finite-type surface
S
.

In
[
Pet21
]
, Petyt proves that
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
(in fact, any
colorable
hierarchically hyperbolic group, more generally) admits a quasi-median quasi-isometry to a finite dimensional CAT(0) cube complex. He accomplishes this in two ways, with the more relevant one being through work of Bestvina-Bromberg-Fujiwara
[
BBF21
]
. They proved that
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
admits a quasi-isometric embedding into a finite product of quasi-trees, and Petyt proved that this map is quasi-median. This global approximation is quite useful for studying the interior of
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
, e.g. it provides a new proof that
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
is bicombable though it does not recover semihyperbolicity
[
Ham09
,
DMS20
,
HHP20
]
. Moreover, it allows one to cubically approximate the hierarchical hull of any finite set of interior points or hierarchy rays with uniform constants (independent of the size of the finite set). In addition, this global model allows one to identify any number of boundaries, e.g. the Roller boundary, of this cube complex as a boundary of
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
. The main drawback of this construction is a lack of equivariance. In particular, the quasi-isometry from the global cubical model to
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
is not
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
-equivariant (and could not possibly be
[
Bri10
]
), and thus any notion of a boundary for
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
constructed in this way would necessarily provide limited dynamical information. In addition, it is yet unclear how hierarchical information is encoded in the cubical geometry, since, for instance, hyperplanes in Petyt’s global model do not come with domain labels, as in Theorem
B
.

In
[
Ham21
]
, Hamenstädt constructs a uniformly finite CAT(0) cube complex
C
which admits a proper coarsely surjective Lipschitz map
F:C\to\mathcal{MCG}(S)
. Remarkably, Hamenstädt constructs a homeomorphic identification of the Roller boundary of
C
with the space of complete geodesic laminations on
S
. As with Petyt’s construction, this map
F
is necessarily not
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
-equivariant, and in fact is also not coarsely bi-Lipschitz. Notably, these results are established via train-track techniques, which are quite different from our hierarchical approach.

Finally, Casals-Ruiz–Hagen–Kazachkov
[
CRHK21
]
have an ongoing program to study the asymptotic cones of HHSes. In doing so, they introduce the notion of a
real cubing
, which is a higher-rank generalization of a real tree, and prove that every asymptotic cone of an HHS admits the structure of a real cubing. Remarkably, they use this robust theory to prove that the mapping class group has a unique asymptotic cone (up to bi-Lipschitz equivalence). Their program is similar to ours in spirit, in that they need to wrestle with infinite sets of hierarchical data and they use cubical techniques to do so. See item (
12
) of Remark
1.1
for a discussion about how our setup is related to theirs.

1.7.
A basic motivating example

We will spend the rest of the introduction highlighting some of our techniques and laying out some questions. To motivate this discussion, we begin with a basic example of a combinatorial (and thus hierarchy) ray in a familiar cubical HHS, the Cayley 2-complex
\mathcal{X}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}*\mathbb{Z}\cong\langle a,b|[a,b]=1\rangle*\langle c\rangle
. This space is known as the “tree of flats” because it is a family of 2-dimensional flats (copies of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) connected in a tree-like fashion. See Figure
4
for the setup.

The standard HHS structure on
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}*\mathbb{Z}
consists of the infinite-valent tree
T
obtained by coning off all copies of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, which is the top-level space in the hierarchy, along with lower level spaces which are copies of
\mathbb{Z}
corresponding to the coordinate axes of each flat. In particular, every hyperbolic space in the structure is a tree. This HHS structure is “exact” in the sense discussed above: all of the constants associated to the structure can be taken to be
0
, and
\mathbb{Z}^{2}*\mathbb{Z}
is isometric to the
0
-consistent subset of the product of the hyperbolic spaces in the hierarchy (which are all lines except for
T
). Moreover, all projections in the hierarchical setup can be taken to be either the collapsing map
\mathcal{X}\to T
or closest-point projections (in
\mathcal{X}
) to the flats and their axes (see
[
BHS17a
]
for the general case of cubical HHSes). As mentioned above, this is the sort of exactness that we capture in the hierarchical families of trees used to construct our version of the cubical models (Theorem
B
) in the interior point case. We will now explain in some detail how our construction plays out in this example.

The set of combinatorial (and thus hierarchy) rays in
\mathcal{X}
admits a fairly simple description. Since we are in the cubical setting, the HHS and simplicial boundaries coincide (by, e.g.,
[
DHS17
, Theorem 10.1]
). They consist of two main types of simplices: a Cantor set of isolated
0
-simplices coming from the Gromov boundary of
T
and a family of isolated
1
-dimensional diamonds corresponding to each copy
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, where the vertices of the diamond correspond to the cardinal directions in the flat they represent. In particular, the HHS (or simplicial) boundary of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}*\mathbb{Z}
is a disconnected union of these diamonds and the Cantor set. Importantly, every combinatorial geodesic ray in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}*\mathbb{Z}
either eventually lies in a single flat or projects to a geodesic ray in
T
which runs off to infinity.

It is instructive to consider the hierarchical hull of a single combinatorial ray of the latter type:

\gamma=abca^{2}b^{2}ca^{3}b^{3}c\cdots.

The most obvious hierarchical family of trees associated to this setup consists of trees which are just the intervals obtained by projecting
\gamma
to all of the various hyperbolic spaces in the structure, excluding projections of diameter
0
. In particular, these intervals have the following form:

(1)

The infinite geodesic ray
T_{\gamma}=\pi_{T}(\gamma)
in
T
, and

(2)

For each
n
, an interval
T_{n,a}
of length
n
in the
a
-direction of the flat labeled by
d_{n}=abca^{2}b^{2}c\dots ca^{n-1}b^{n-1}c
.

(3)

For each
n
, an interval
T_{n,b}
of length
n
in the
b
-direction of the flat labeled by
d_{n}
.

Thus we have the following product of intervals:

\mathcal{Y}:=T_{\gamma}\times\prod_{n}\left(T_{n,a}\times T_{n,b}\right),

which we note is the product of the projections of
\gamma
to factors of the full product of
T
and the various copies of
{\mathbb{R}}
which give the HHS structure on
\mathcal{X}
, with the factors that are a point being ignored. In particular, the
0
-consistent set

\mathcal{Q}
of
\mathcal{Y}
is exactly the restriction of the
0
-consistent set of the full product of hyperbolic spaces, which is itself precisely
\mathcal{X}
. Note that this makes
\mathcal{Q}
precisely the
\ell^{1}
-convex hull of
\gamma
in
\mathcal{X}
, when the latter is identified as a subset of the product of hyperbolic spaces.

Moreover, the above two sets of information, namely (1) the geodesic ray
T_{\gamma}
and (2) the set of labels
d_{n}
which encodes the relevant flats (and thus their axes), both completely encode the limit set of
\gamma
in the simplicial (hierarchical) boundary of
\mathcal{X}
—namely the
0
-simplex corresponding to
\overline{\gamma}=\pi_{T}(\gamma)\in\partial T
—since the
\rho
-sets for the domains labeled by
d_{n}
diverge to infinity along
\pi_{T}(\gamma)
. See Figure
4
.

As for the cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
itself, the set of domain relations

•

T_{n,a}\perp T_{n,b}
are orthogonal for all
n
,

•

T_{n,a},T_{n,b}\sqsubset T_{\gamma}
nest for all
n
with
\rho^{d_{n}}_{T_{\gamma}}
being the collapsed point for the corresponding flat;

•

All other domains are transverse
\pitchfork
;

tells us how to piece together
\mathcal{Q}
as follows. As discussed in Subsection
1.1
, orthogonality does not constrain coordinates in
\mathcal{Y}
. Thus for each
n
,
\mathcal{Q}
will contain an isometrically embedded copy of the square
T_{n,a}\times T_{n,b}
. The rest of the relation data then tells us to glue these squares into their corresponding
\rho
-points along
T_{\gamma}
, namely that
T_{n,a}\times T_{n,b}
replaces
\rho^{d_{n}}_{T_{\gamma}}
for each
n
. Again, see Figure
4
.

Returning to our boundary discussion, we claim that only one of the above sets of data encoded by the ray is robust under (necessary) variations of the model construction. While the choice of
T_{\gamma}
as a modeling tree for the projection of
\gamma
to
T
is natural, using it also as the
collapsed
tree in the hierarchical product of trees in our construction is very special. As heuristically indicated in Figures
1
and
2
, the collapsing procedure in each relevant tree
T_{U}
involves “clustering” the nearby
\rho
-sets for domains which nest into
V
. In the entirely coarse general HHS setting, one needs some flexibility in choosing this
cluster separation constant
. See Subsection
7.2
for more details.

Figure 4
.
A ray in
\mathcal{X}\cong\langle a,b\rangle*\langle c\rangle
whose limiting behavior is only robustly encoded in domain data: The ray
\gamma=abca^{2}b^{2}ca^{3}b^{3}c\cdots
passes through an infinite sequence of flats, moving progressively further in each successive flat. The limiting behavior of
\gamma
is encoded not only in the geodesic ray
\pi_{T}(\gamma)
to which it projects in the tree
T
obtained by collapsing down all of the flats to points, but also in the sequence of flats, which is encoded as a sequence of vertices
\rho^{d_{n}}_{T}
of
T
. When building a cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
for
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(\gamma)
, one could take
\pi_{T}(\gamma)=T_{\gamma}
,
\pi_{d_{n}\cdot a}(\gamma)=T_{n,a}
,
\pi_{d_{n}\cdot b}(\gamma)=T_{n,b}
for the family of collapsed trees. However, trimming
T_{\gamma}\to\widehat{T}
the
c
-intervals from
T_{\gamma}
collapses it to a point
\widehat{T}
and gives another hierarchical family of trees, with a cubical model
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
for
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(\gamma)
which is
0
-quasi-median
(2,0)
-quasi-isometric under the induced collapsing map
\Sigma:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
. At the level of simplicial boundaries, the hyperplanes in
\mathcal{Q}
induced by the green, blue, and pink hyperplanes in
\mathcal{X}
represent the class corresponding to
\gamma
in
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
, while the green and blue hyperplanes represent
\Sigma(\gamma)
in
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, with
\Sigma
inducing the isomorphism
\partial\Sigma:\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
(between singletons). In this sense,
\gamma
is only robustly encoded in the domain labels for the green and blue hyperplanes.

In this special “exact” setting, we were able to collapse nothing, implicitly setting the cluster separation constant to be
0
, but any other choice of cluster separation constant larger than
0
would result in a cubical model that is quasi-median quasi-isometric to this special “exact” model, with constants depending on that choice of larger threshold. In general, the cluster threshold is always bigger than
1
.

In this case, choosing it to be exactly
1
results in collapsing the infinite ray
T_{\gamma}
to a point. While this may appear violent, one can show (using our Tree Trimming
10.2
techniques) that the corresponding map between hierarchical families of trees

T_{\gamma}\times\prod_{n}\left(T_{n,a}\times T_{n,b}\right)\to\prod_{n}\left(T_{n,a}\times T_{n,b}\right),

induces a
0
-quasi-median
(2,0)
-quasi-isometry
\Sigma:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
between their
0
-consistent subsets.

Moreover, this map
\Sigma:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
preserves the infinite family of (finite diameter) squares
T_{n,a}\times T_{n,b}
and hence the labeling data of the hyperplanes they determine in the model. These hyperplanes, on the other hand, encode
\gamma
as the unique
0
-simplex in the simplicial boundary of the model
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
. In fact, any choice of larger threshold would collapsed finitely-many of these squares, but any infinite subfamily is enough to determine
\pi_{T}(\gamma)\in\partial T
. Thus the hyperplane domain label data robustly encodes the limiting behavior, since every hyperplane labeled by
T
(and indeed even finitely-many hyperplanes with lower-level labels) may be lost in the process of forming a cubical model.

It is important to note that the purely coarse version of the cubical model first constructed by Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto
[
BHS21
]
has this exact same issue: there are no tree-hyperplanes (which they call
separators
) along
T_{\gamma}
in their setting. We expect that the same issue would arise in any potential extension of Bowditch’s construction
[
Bow19
]
which allows for modeling median rays. Thus, these sorts of issues are unavoidable.

By this point, we hope that this example has motivated the need for (and utility of) the ability to modify the trees used in constructing cubical models via small surgeries. The formal framework for this is what we have referred to as
tree trimming
, and we will discuss this below in Subsection
1.9
.

However, this example also highlights another necessary feature of our analysis: the ability to use domain label data to interpret the various ways in which a family of hyperplanes can diverge to infinity in a cubical model. This latter point is more subtle, and falls under the umbrella of what are often referred to as
passing-up
style arguments, which play a key role at several points in this paper, including for tree-trimming. We discuss these next.

1.8.
Hierarchical accounting techniques

Passing-up style arguments have been foundational to the HHS setting since the work of Masur-Minsky
[
MM00
]
on the hierarchical geometry of the mapping class group
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
of a finite-type surface
S
. Notably, this hierarchy machinery also played a central role in the construction of the model manifolds used in Brock-Canary-Minsky’s
[
Min10
,
BCM12
]
proof of the Ending Lamination Theorem, where the notion of “generalized marking” we discussed in Remark
1.1
arises. To contextualize our results, which fully realize some of the related structural results from
[
MM00
]
to the HHS setting, we will discuss that setting first.

Following
[
MM00
]
, identify
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
with its
marking graph
, a combinatorial model for
\mathcal{MCG}(S)
whose vertices are special filling collections of curves called
markings
, with two markings being connected by an edge when they differ by an
elementary move
on a minimal-complexity subsurface.

A
hierarchy

\mathcal{H}
between a pair of markings
\mu,\nu
is, roughly, a collection of geodesics between
\pi_{U}(\mu),\pi_{U}(\nu)\in\mathcal{C}(U)
in the curve graphs
\mathcal{C}(U)
of a collection
\mathcal{U}
of subsurfaces on which the markings
\mu,\nu
interact in a sufficiently complicated fashion. These hierarchies can be
resolved
to build hierarchy paths between any pair of markings
[
MM00
, Theorem 6.10]
, and the structure of
\mathcal{H}
is essential for proving the original distance formula
[
MM00
, Theorem 6.5]
. Our choice of notation
\mathcal{U}
here is intentional: all but boundedly-many of these subsurfaces
U\in\mathcal{U}
have the property that
\mu,\nu
project far apart in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, namely
d_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(\mu,\nu)\gg K
for some fixed
K=K(S)>0
depending on
S
. Hence most of the subsurfaces in
\mathcal{U}
are “relevant domains”, in the sense of Subsection
1.2
.

Importantly, a key property of any hierarchy
\mathcal{H}
between
\mu,\nu
is the following: Not only will every relevant subsurface
U\in\mathcal{U}
support a geodesic in
\mathcal{H}
, but the boundary curves
\partial U
of
U
will appear in the
1
-neighborhood of some vertex along a geodesic in
\mathcal{H}
which is supported on a relevant subsurface
V\in\mathcal{U}
of which
U\subset V
is a subsurface (see
[
MM00
, Theorem 3.1 and Theorem 4.7]
). This fact explains Masur-Minsky’s terminology for such domains, namely
large links
(see
[
MM00
, Lemma 6.2]
), and motivated the Large Links Axiom
6
for HHSes
[
BHS19
]
.

The
passing-up
principle, as it is now referred to, is a straight-forward consequence of the above: Given a large collection of relevant subsurfaces
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}
for some pair of markings
\mu,\nu
, there exists some relevant subsurface
W\in\mathcal{U}
so that the boundary curves of some further subcollection
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
spreads out along any geodesic between
\pi_{W}(\mu),\pi_{W}(\nu)
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
. Moreover, by making
\#\mathcal{V}
larger, one can force the boundary curves from subsurfaces in
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
to spread further out in
\mathcal{C}(W)
. Put another way, the boundary curves of large links cannot cluster nearby in every subsurface curve graph.

A weak version of this passing-up principle has appeared in a number of guises in the HHS setting: see Lemma
2.7
for the original HHS version (from
[
BHS17a
, Lemma 2.5]
and
[
DHS17
, Lemma 3.2]
), as well as a slight generalization due to Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto
[
BHS21
, Lemma 1.6]
, and Lemma
4.5
for a useful consequence due to Petyt-Spriano
[
PS20
, Lemma 5.4]
.

In this article, we prove a strong version of the passing-up principle, as well as a number of related statements, which realize more fully the organizational power of hierarchies from the surface setting. Moreover, we fully extend these techniques to allow for hierarchy rays and boundary points, thereby generalizing the organizational principles of Minsky’s infinite hierarchies. In particular, some version of the following theorem would be necessary to prove any version of the boundary isomorphism Theorem
D
with respect to any cubical model construction. Since the full statement is somewhat technical, we will only give the following informal version:

Proposition G

(Strong passing-up)
.

Let
a,b
be interior points, boundary points, or hierarchy rays in an HHS
\mathcal{X}
. Then for any sufficiently large collection
\mathcal{V}
of relevant domains for
a,b
, there exists a relevant domain
W
and large subcollection
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
, so that
V\sqsubset W
for all
V
and

\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(W)}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V}_{W}\right)

can be made as large as necessary by increasing the size of
\#\mathcal{V}
. Moreover, the
\rho
-sets
\rho^{V}_{W}
distribute proportionally evenly along any geodesic between
\pi_{W}(a)
and
\pi_{W}(b)
.

In the above, “proportionally evenly” means, roughly, that the
\rho
-sets
\rho^{V}_{W}
for
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
must not only globally spread out in
\mathcal{C}(W)
, but must also locally spread out. In particular, keeping the diameter of
d_{\mathcal{C}(W)}(\pi_{W}(a),\pi_{W}(b))
fixed and increasing
\#\mathcal{V}
forces a progressively larger proportion of any geodesic
[\pi_{W}(a),\pi_{W}(b)]
to be coarsely covered by these
\rho
-sets. See Proposition
4.7
for the full statement and discussion. We note that Zalloum
[
Zal23a
]
has already used Proposition
G
in his proof of effective rank rigidity for hierarchically hyperbolic groups.

Just as the passing-up principle and related ideas are essential for proving the distance formula in all of the relevant contexts
[
MM00
,
Dur16
,
BHS19
]
, this stronger version plays an essential role in several distinct ways in this article. The following are the highlights:

•

As discussed above in Subsection
1.7
, even though the Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto’s original cubical model construction did not require Strong Passing-Up
4.7
, some form of it is unavoidable to perform an analysis of the simplicial boundaries of these models. In particular, in proving any variant of the simplicial isomorphism in Theorem
D
, one is handed an infinite family of hyperplanes in a cubical model, whose only related hierarchical data is the family of hyperplane labels which come from the relevant domains for some finite set of interior points and hierarchy rays. In lieu of one domain labeling infinitely-many of these hyperplanes, extracting a domain of support for an infinite subfamily family requires knowing that the
\rho
-sets for those labels spread out in some domain. This argument uses Proposition
G
in a strong way. See Lemma
16.18
in particular.

•

Strong Passing-Up
4.7
is essential for proving that all but boundedly-many domains appearing in the set of relevant domains for a finite collection of points and rays in an HHS are
bipartite
domains, as discussed in Figure
2
. As discussed in Remark
1.1
, these bipartite domains are the most cubical part of the quasi-isometry between the hierarchical hull and its model, an argument which is otherwise fairly cubical-free. See Subsection
9.1
for details and Proposition
9.4
specifically.

•

The Bounding Large Containers Proposition
4.14
is a distance formula-related statement coming out this analysis. In Section
8
, it is essential for proving the distance comparison between hierarchical hulls and their cubical models, as well as proving the Tree Trimming Theorem
10.2
that we will discuss next.

•

Another application of Proposition
4.14
is an elementary direct proof that changing thresholds in the distance formula works, which, to our knowledge, does not exist in the literature. See Corollary
4.16
for details.

•

Notably, Strong Passing-Up
4.7
for a pair of interior points is necessary to prove standard Passing-Up
4.4
for hierarchy rays, as well as the basic but important fact that the set of relevant domains for a ray (or pair of rays) is countable (Lemma
4.13
).

•

It is standard that the Large Links Axiom
6
implies the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
. We give a proof of the converse in Proposition
4.15
, and hence that the Large Links Axiom is equivalent to Passing-Up, so that the latter could replace the former in the axiomatic setup of HHSes. We note that this fact was independently discovered by Hagen and Petyt
[
HP23a
]
.

1.9.
Tree-trimming: Refining the distance formula

In our discussion of the cubical model Theorem
B
and the variation which captures curve graph distance (Theorem
F
), we saw how modifying the trees involved in the construction of the cubical models can be quite useful, as it allows one to directly modify the cubical geometry of the models in a fine-tuned fashion. We will now discuss the machinery that makes this work, namely the Tree Trimming Theorem
H
below. Since it can be reasonably understood a refinement of the distance formula, we will begin our discussion there.

Given a pair of points
a,b\in\mathcal{X}
in an HHS, the distance formula (Theorem
2.9
) says that the distance between them in
\mathcal{X}
is coarsely the
\ell^{1}
-sum of distances in the hyperbolic spaces to which they project far apart:

(2)

d_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b)\asymp_{K}\sum_{U}[[d_{U}(a,b)]]_{K}.

In the above coarse inequality, we need to take
K
sufficiently large (depending only on
\mathcal{X}
), and then the additive and multiplicative constants of
\asymp_{K}
depend on
K
and
\mathcal{X}
. In particular, taking
K^{\prime}>K
provides a new distance estimate, in which any term
d_{V}(a,b)<K^{\prime}
disappears. While it is hard to overstate how useful this distance formula has proven in the literature, we have developed a refined version which has some added benefits.

One of the upshots of Theorem
B
is the introduction of a cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
where the distance is actually an
\ell^{1}
-sum:

(3)

d_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b)\asymp_{K}d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a},\hat{b}),

where
\hat{a},\hat{b}
are identified with
a,b
under the model map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b)
, and the
\widehat{T}_{U}
are the collapsed trees (actually intervals, in this case) associated to
a,b
and each
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

In our construction, we also need to choose some sufficiently large
K
(again, depending only on
\mathcal{X}
) to collect the
K
-relevant domains
\mathcal{U}
, which are precisely the domains contributing to the right side of equation (
2
). While
\mathcal{Q}
-distance again only gives a coarse estimate, an obvious benefit of this setup is that equation (
3
) allows one to (coarsely) account for how much each domain in
\mathcal{U}
contributes in this estimate. In other words, distance in the model
\mathcal{Q}
is a concrete realization of the distance formula.

However, this exact model has another benefit: we can perform small surgeries on each of the intervals
\widehat{T}_{U}
which only changes the coarse estimate to the distance in
\mathcal{X}
in a controlled fashion. Put another way, by extracting the precise contributions of the relevant domains—which is achieved by excising the redundant information coming from nesting, as discussed in Subsection
1.3
—we can further make
partial
alterations to the contributions of each relevant domain, while preserving the combinatorial information of the
\rho
-sets encoded in the trees. Compare this with the distance formula (
2
), in which the only dimension of flexibility results in the complete deletion of the data associated to a relevant domain.

The general version of this idea is the statement of our Tree Trimming Theorem
10.2
, which plays a crucial role in a number of places in this article. To state an informal version, observe that in the setup of our cubical model Theorem
B
, each of the collapsed trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
for
U\in\mathcal{U}
admits a decomposition
\widehat{T}_{U}=\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}\sqcup\widehat{T}^{c}_{U}
along the marked points
\widehat{T}^{c}_{U}
which encode the leaves and cluster (nesting) data (see Figure
2
). The subtrees
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
in the complements of these marked points encode the information for which
U
is responsible in equation (
3
). The idea is that we can delete (or insert) small subtrees into these complementary subtrees, and produce a new hierarchical family of trees whose cubical realization is
0
-median quasi-isometric to the original. See Figure
16
below for a heuristic picture.

The following is an informal version of Theorem
10.2
:

Theorem H

(Tree trimming)
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a cubical model arising from a hierarchical family of trees
\{\widehat{T}_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
associated to a finite set
F\cup\Lambda
of interior points, boundary points, and hierarchy rays in an HHS
\mathcal{X}
. Then there exists a
B_{tt}
depending only on the constants in the construction of
\mathcal{Q}
, so that if
B<B_{tt}
and
\Delta_{U}:\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
collapses at most
B
-many subtrees of diameter at most
B
in each complementary component of
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then

(1)

Then the collapsing maps
\Delta_{U}:\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
combine to produce a new hierarchical family of tree
\{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
over the same index set, and

(2)

The induced map
\Delta:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
on
0
-consistent sets is a
0
-median
(L,L)
-quasi-isometry, where
L=L(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|,B)>0
.

In particular,
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a new cubical model for
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

Notice that Theorem
H
allows one to delete arbitrarily many—and perhaps infinitely-many—small pieces from the various trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
, as discussed in the example in Subsection
1.7
, while only distorting the metric on the resulting model a bounded amount. Since the hyperplanes in our cubical models come directly from the collapsed trees, this ability to modify the trees at a small scale provides us great flexibility in controlling the cubical geometry of the models. For instance, Theorem
F
is a direct application of this philosophy. We will discuss some of the details of these applications in the next subsection.

1.10.
Outline of the paper and some proofs

Having given the broad strokes of the paper with some emphasis on various parts of the argument, we will now go into a bit more detail of how all of the pieces fit together.

In Section
2
, we give some background on HHSes, cube complexes, and (coarse) median spaces. In Section
3
