, we state the main definitions of the objects we are modeling in this paper: hierarchy rays, boundary points, and their hierarchical hulls. Some of this analysis is new, like the construction of hierarchy rays whose projections encode those of a basepoint and boundary point pair (Proposition
3.14
), and Definition
3.16
and Proposition
3.18
, which extends the hierarchical notion of consistency to rays and boundary points.

In Section
4
, we develop the hierarchical accounting techniques we discussed in Subsection
1.8
above. As discussed there, this is where we prove the Strong Passing-up Proposition
4.7
, which is useful for understanding how domain data spreads out in the various hyperbolic spaces, as well as the Bounding Containers Proposition
4.14
, which is important for distance formula-type arguments. In other words, the material here is foundational for the rest of the paper.

At this point, our hierarchical foundation is set, and in Section
5
, we set some notation, constants, and basic assumptions that we will carry throughout the rest of the paper.

In Section
6
, we begin the construction of the cubical models in earnest. Given a finite set of interior points, boundary points, and hierarchy rays
F\cup\Lambda
, the starting point is encoding the information of the projection of
F\cup\Lambda
to each relevant domain
\mathcal{C}(U)
for
U\in\mathcal{U}
and its accompanying
\rho
-data into a Gromov modeling tree
T_{U}
adorned with analogous
\rho
-data; see Definition
6.12
and Lemma
6.13
, as well as Definition
6.15
for the related notion of consistency. This reduction is quite useful, as the product of trees
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}
is a natural landing spot both for the coarse information coming from the hierarchical hull
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(F\cup\Lambda)
, as well as the exact information coming from the eventual cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
.

In Section
7
, we explain the procedure for building the collapsed trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
from the modeling trees
T_{U}
, by collapsing down nesting data. Part of this analysis involves translating the coarse hierarchical information encoded into the
T_{U}
into exact hierarchical information in the
\widehat{T}_{U}
, see Definition
7.7
and Lemma
7.14
. Definition
7.9
gives the precise definition of
0
-consistency, thus of the model
\mathcal{Q}
, and the map
\widehat{\Phi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
. This also involves developing a notion of canonicality, which is necessary for differentiating between interior points and points at infinity.

In Section
8
, we prove our distance comparison estimates between
H
and
\mathcal{Q}
(Theorem
8.1
). In particular, we prove that the distance formula sum in
H
is coarsely the
\ell^{1}
-distance in
\mathcal{Q}
. This statement is useful for proving the upper-bound of the Distance Formula
2.9
, as discussed in Subsection
1.8
above. More importantly, assuming the distance formula, this proves that
\widehat{\Phi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is a uniform quasi-isometric embedding.

In Section
9
, we build our map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
, the main part of which is defining a coordinate-wise map
\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}
. This involves a detailed analysis because of all of the information that has been collapsed
T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
. More specifically, given a canonical
0
-consistent tuple
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
, almost every coordinate
\hat{x}_{U}
coincides with a
marked point
, which corresponds to a possibly arbitrarily-large (if not infinite-diameter) cluster
C\subset T_{U}
. Deciding where to send
\hat{x}_{U}
in
C
involves considering essentially all of the domains nesting into
U
whose nesting data is contained in
C
. See Subsection
9.2
for a detailed description of the
cluster honing
process. As mentioned above, this part of the argument has a distinctly cubical flavor, as it depends on the ubiquity of the two-sided
bipartite domains
, and proving their ubiquity involves Strong Passing-up
4.7
.

In Section
10
, we develop our tree-trimming techniques, proving the Tree Trimming Theorem
10.2
and the fact that each of the collapsed trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
can be replaced by a simplicial tree, where the marked points are vertices (Corollary
10.9
). As discussed in Subsection
1.8
, Theorem
10.2
is a distance-formula type result, and the quasi-isometry statement depends directly on the Bounding Containers Proposition
4.14
, as well as an “exact” version of the cluster honing process from the above definition of
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
.

In Section
11
, we prove that
\widehat{\Phi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is coarsely surjective, the argument for which uses Theorem
10.2
in a crucial way. This involves proving Theorem
11.1
, which states that the composition
\widehat{\Psi}\circ\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}
is coarsely constant, where
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
and
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
are the model maps. This is the point in the article where the coarse nature of the hull meet most plainly with the exact nature of the model. In the course of the proof, one needs to compare the coordinate tuples for a point
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
and its image
\hat{y}=\widehat{\Psi}\circ\widehat{\Omega}(\hat{x})\in\mathcal{Q}
. Using coarse techniques, one can show that all but boundedly-many of these coordinates
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
are within bounded distance of each other (see Proposition
11.4
). However, one must show that all but boundedly-many coordinates are exactly the same, since
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\sum_{U}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
. Tree Trimming
10.2
solves this problem by allowing us to trim away the (a priori infinitely-many) intervals connecting the distinct
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
which are at bounded distance. Since the resulting map on cubical models is a uniform quasi-isometry with the images of
\hat{x},\hat{y}
at bounded distance, it follows that
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
was bounded after all; see Subsection
11.2
.

In Section
12
, we analyze the induced wall-space structure on
\mathcal{Q}
. In the interior point case, this involves moving to the exact setting of cube complexes built from hierarchical family of trees (Definition
12.1
), an axiomatization of the setup we extract from hierarchical hulls. The main purpose of this section is to develop a description of the walls in
\mathcal{Q}
, as well as how their half-spaces nest and intersect (Subsection
12.3
), as well as characterizing infinite descending chains (Proposition
12.17
). This description is crucial for our proof in Section
13
, in which we prove that
\mathcal{Q}
is isometric to its cubical dual, while also showing that non-canonical tuples in
\mathcal{Q}
(namely, those encoding points at infinity), are in bijection with the
0
-cubes at infinite in the cubical dual of
\mathcal{Q}
. It is also important for our analysis of the simplicial boundary of
\mathcal{Q}
in Section
16
.

In Section
15
, we complete the proof of Theorem
B
by analyzing and synchronizing the (coarse) median structures of the various objects involved, in particular by showing that the maps
\widehat{\Omega}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
and between
\mathcal{Q}
and its cubical dual are median-preserving. We have isolated the content of this section mainly because the straightforward nature of this discussion helps make transparent how the various parts of the construction play together.

In Section
14
, we pause to observe how to use the cubical geometry of the model of a pair of points to prove the existence of hierarchy paths (Proposition
14.8
) and the upper-bound in the distance formula (Corollary
14.3
). For this, we give a direct proof that any combinatorial geodesic (ray) in any cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
is mapped to a hierarchy path (or ray) back in
\mathcal{X}
. We then observe that the length of such image of such a
cubical path
(Definition
14.1
) between a pair of point
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
is coarsely the distance formula sum by Theorem
8.1
.

In Section
16
, we prove our boundary isomorphism statement, Theorem
16.1
, which we discussed in Subsection
1.4
. This involves laying out some background material, including Hagen’s simplicial boundary for cube complexes
[
Hag13a
]
and the simplicial structure on the HHS boundary. The key step for the isomorphism is Lemma
16.19
, which uniquely identifies a
0
-simplex in the HHS boundary of the hull
H
to any
0
-simplex (i.e., a minimal UBS) in the simplicial boundary of a model
\mathcal{Q}
, which combines with Hagen’s decomposition Theorem
16.6
to provide a hierarchical representative in the general case, and thus a simplicial embedding
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial H
. Surjectivity requires extra work, and proceeds by showing that
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
is visible, i.e. every point admits a combinatorial ray representative, which, moreover, can be chosen to have whatever limiting hierarchical data is required (Lemma
16.27
).

Finally, in Section
17
, we explain how to modify the cubical model construction to encode curve graph distance in the hyperplanes of the model, as in Theorem
F
. To modify the cubical construction, we take our inspiration from the surface world. Two simple closed curves
\alpha,\beta

fill
a compact surface
S
if the complement of their union is a union of disks, and it is not hard to show that if
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
are a pairwise filling collection of curves on
S
, then
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(S)}(\alpha_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\alpha_{n})\asymp n
. We introduce an analogous notion of filling for any pair of domains in an HHS (Definition
17.7
), and observe that the
\rho
-sets of a filling collection of domains must spread out in the top-level hyperbolic space. As an application using our Tree Trimming
10.2
techniques, we explain how to construct a cubical model for any pair of points so that two hyperplanes are
0
-separated if and only if their domain labels are filling. Hence long
0
-separated chains produce large distances in the top-level hyperbolic space; see Subsection
17.7
.

2.
Background on hierarchically hyperbolic spaces

In this section, we give the basic definitions and properties of hierarchically hyperbolic spaces. Most of the material is standard here, with
[
BHS19
]
the main reference. See also Sisto’s expository article
[
Sis17
]
.

2.1.
Coarse (in)equality notation

Many of the calculations in this paper are estimates which only hold up to some multiplicative and additive constants, which can depend on various background objects or quantities. In at attempt to limit notation burden, we will write

•

A\prec B
when there exist constants
K\geq 1,C\geq 0
so that
A\leq K\cdot B+C
.

•

We define
A\succ B
similarly, and set
A\asymp B
to mean
A\prec B
and
A\succ B
.

•

When we say “the constants in
A\prec B
depend on” certain quantities or objects, we mean that the constants
K,C
can be written as functions of those quantities or quantities related to those objects.

•

In particular, when we say that a coarse (in)equality depends only on an HHS
\mathcal{X}
, this means that they can be writing as functions of the fundamental constants associated to the HHS, which we lay out in the next subsection. See also Section
5
for a simplification of the constants associated to a fixed HHS.

2.2.
HHS axioms and key lemmas

Hierarchically hyperbolic spaces were introduced in
[
BHS17a
,
BHS19
]
. Since we need several of them, we will include the list of axioms, and some of their important consequences. See
[
CRHK21
]
for a thorough introduction and
[
Sis17
]
for a more philosophical survey.

We begin with the basic definition from
[
BHS19
]
:

Definition 2.1
.

The
q
–quasigeodesic space
(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{X}})
is a
hierarchically hyperbolic space
if there exists
\delta\geq 0
, an index set
\mathfrak{S}
, and a set
\{\mathcal{C}(W):W\in\mathfrak{S}\}
of
\delta
–hyperbolic spaces
(\mathcal{C}(U),\mathbf{d}_{U})
, such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

(Projections.)
There is
a set
\{\pi_{W}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow 2^{\mathcal{C}(W)}\mid W\in\mathfrak{S}\}

of
projections
sending points in
\mathcal{X}
to sets of diameter
bounded by some
\xi\geq 0
in the various
\mathcal{C}(W)\in\mathfrak{S}
.
Moreover, there exists
K
so that for all
W\in\mathfrak{S}
, the coarse map
\pi_{W}
is
(K,K)
–coarsely
Lipschitz and
\pi_{W}(\mathcal{X})
is
K
–quasiconvex in
\mathcal{C}(W)
.

(2)

(Nesting.)

\mathfrak{S}
is
equipped with a partial order
\sqsubset
, and either
\mathfrak{S}=\emptyset
or
\mathfrak{S}
contains a unique
\sqsubset
–maximal
element; when
V\sqsubset W
, we say
V
is
nested
in
W
. (We
emphasize that
W\sqsubset W
for all
W\in\mathfrak{S}
.) For each

W\in\mathfrak{S}
, we denote by
\mathfrak{S}_{W}
the set of

V\in\mathfrak{S}
such that
V\sqsubset W
. Moreover, for all
V,W\in\mathfrak{S}

with
V\sqsubset W
there is a specified subset

\rho^{V}_{W}\subset\mathcal{C}(W)
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(W)}(\rho^{V}_{W})\leq\xi
.
There is also a
projection

\rho^{W}_{V}:\mathcal{C}(W)\rightarrow 2^{\mathcal{C}(V)}
. (The similarity in
notation is justified by viewing
\rho^{V}_{W}
as a coarsely constant map
\mathcal{C}(V)\rightarrow 2^{\mathcal{C}(W)}
.)

(3)

(Orthogonality.)

\mathfrak{S}
has a symmetric and
anti-reflexive relation called
orthogonality
: we write
V\perp W
when
V,W
are orthogonal. Also, whenever
V\sqsubset W
and
W\perp U
, we require that
V\perp U
. We require that for each

T\in\mathfrak{S}
and each
U\in\mathfrak{S}_{T}
for which

\{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{T}\mid V\perp U\}\neq\emptyset
, there exists
W\in\mathfrak{S}_{T}-\{T\}
, so that whenever
V\perp U
and
V\sqsubset T
, we
have
V\sqsubset W
. Finally, if
V\perp W
, then
V,W
are not

\sqsubset
–comparable.

(4)

(Transversality and consistency.)

If
V,W\in\mathfrak{S}
are not
orthogonal and neither is nested in the other, then we say
V,W
are

transverse
, denoted
V\pitchfork W
. There exists

\kappa_{0}\geq 0
such that if
V\pitchfork W
, then there are
sets
\rho^{V}_{W}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(W)
and

\rho^{W}_{V}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(V)
each of diameter at most
\xi
and
satisfying:

(4)

\min\left\{\mathbf{d}_{W}(\pi_{W}(x),\rho^{V}_{W}),\mathbf{d}_{V}(\pi_{V}(x),\rho^{W}_{V})\right\}\leq\kappa_{0}

for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

For
V,W\in\mathfrak{S}
satisfying
V\sqsubset W
and for all

x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have:

(5)

\min\left\{\mathbf{d}_{W}(\pi_{W}(x),\rho^{V}_{W}),\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(V)}(\pi_{V}(x)\cup\rho^{W}_{V}(\pi_{W}(x)))\right\}\leq\kappa_{0}.

The preceding two inequalities are the
consistency inequalities
for points in
\mathcal{X}
.

Finally, if
U\sqsubset V
, then
\mathbf{d}_{W}(\rho^{U}_{W},\rho^{V}_{W})\leq\kappa_{0}
whenever
W\in\mathfrak{S}
satisfies either
V\sqsubset W
or
V\pitchfork W
and
W,U
not
\perp
.

(5)

(Finite complexity.)
There exists
n\geq 0
, the
complexity
of
\mathcal{X}
(with respect to
\mathfrak{S}
), so that any set of pairwise–
\sqsubset
–comparable elements has cardinality at most
n
.

(6)

(Large links.)
There
exist
\lambda\geq 1
and
E\geq\max\{\xi,\kappa_{0}\}
such that the following holds.
Let
W\in\mathfrak{S}
and let
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
. Let

N=\lambda\mathbf{d}_{{}_{W}}(\pi_{W}(x),\pi_{W}(x^{\prime}))+\lambda
. Then there exists
\{T_{i}\}_{i=1,\dots,\lfloor N\rfloor}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{W}-\{W\}
such that for all
T\in\mathfrak{S}_{W}-\{W\}
, either
T\in\mathfrak{S}_{T_{i}}
for some
i
, or
\mathbf{d}_{T}(\pi_{T}(x),\pi_{T}(x^{\prime}))<E
. Also,
\mathbf{d}_{W}(\pi_{W}(x),\rho^{T_{i}}_{W})\leq N
for each
i
.

(7)

(Bounded geodesic image.)

There exists
E>0
such that
for all
W\in\mathfrak{S}
,
all
V\in\mathfrak{S}_{W}-\{W\}
, and all geodesics
\gamma
of

\mathcal{C}(W)
, either
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(V)}(\rho^{W}_{V}(\gamma))\leq E
or

\gamma\cap\mathcal{N}_{E}(\rho^{V}_{W})\neq\emptyset
.

(8)

(Partial Realization.)
There exists a constant
\alpha
with the following property. Let
\{V_{j}\}
be a family of pairwise orthogonal elements of
\mathfrak{S}
, and let
p_{j}\in\pi_{V_{j}}(\mathcal{X})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(V_{j})
. Then there exists
x\in\mathcal{X}
so that:

•

\mathbf{d}_{V_{j}}(x,p_{j})\leq\alpha
for all
j
,

•

for each
j
and
each
V\in\mathfrak{S}
with
V_{j}\sqsubset V
, we have

\mathbf{d}_{V}(x,\rho^{V_{j}}_{V})\leq\alpha
, and

•

if
W\pitchfork V_{j}
for some
j
, then
\mathbf{d}_{W}(x,\rho^{V_{j}}_{W})\leq\alpha
.

(9)

(Uniqueness.)
For each
\kappa\geq 0
, there exists

\theta_{u}=\theta_{u}(\kappa)
such that if
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
and

\mathbf{d}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)\geq\theta_{u}
, then there exists
V\in\mathfrak{S}
such
that
\mathbf{d}_{V}(x,y)\geq\kappa
.

We refer to
\mathfrak{S}
, together with the nesting
and orthogonality relations, and the projections as a
hierarchically hyperbolic structure
for the space
\mathcal{X}
, and denote the structure by
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
.

We usually suppress the projection
map
\pi_{U}
for
U\in\mathfrak{S}
when writing distances in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, e.g., given
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
and

p\in\mathcal{C}(U)
we write

\mathbf{d}_{U}(x,y)
for
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(\pi_{U}(x)\cup\pi_{U}(y))
and
\mathbf{d}_{U}(x,p)
for

\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(\pi_{U}(x)\cup\{p\})
.
Given
A\subset\mathcal{X}
and
U\in\mathfrak{S}

we let
\pi_{U}(A)
denote
\displaystyle\bigcup_{a\in A}\pi_{U}(a)
.

2.3.
Basic HHS facts

We now collect some useful facts about HHSes from various places.

The following definition from
[
Beh06b
,
BKMM12
,
BHS19
]
, will be important for us:

Definition 2.2

(Consistent tuple)
.

Let
\kappa\geq 0
and let
(b_{U})\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}2^{\mathcal{C}(U)}
be a tuple such that for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
,
the
U
–coordinate
b_{U}
has diameter
\leq\kappa
. Then
(b_{U})
is
\kappa
–consistent
if for all

V,W\in\mathfrak{S}
, we have

\min\{\mathbf{d}_{V}(b_{V},\rho^{W}_{V}),\mathbf{d}_{W}(b_{W},\rho^{V}_{W})\}\leq\kappa

whenever
V\pitchfork W
and

\min\{\mathbf{d}_{W}(x,\rho^{V}_{W}),\mathrm{diam}_{V}(b_{V}\cup\rho^{W}_{V})\}\leq\kappa

whenever
V\sqsubset W
.

•

We refer to the above inequalities as the
consistency inequalities
.

The following is essentially immediate from Axiom
4
:

Lemma 2.3
.

There exists
\theta=\theta(\mathfrak{S})>0
so that if
x\in\mathcal{X}
, then the tuple
(\pi_{U}(x))\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(U)
is
\theta
-consistent.

The following Realization Theorem says that any tuple satisfying these inequalities can be coarsely realized as a point in
\mathcal{X}
. It is
[
BKMM12
, Theorem 4.3]
for mapping class groups,
[
EMR17
, Theorem 3.2]
for Teichmüller space with the Teichmüller metric, and
[
BHS19
, Theorem 3.1]
for the general HHS case:

Theorem 2.4

(Realization)
.

For each
\kappa\geq 1
there exists
\theta_{e},\theta_{u}\geq 0
such that the following holds. Let
\mathbf{b}=(b_{U})_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}2^{\mathcal{C}(U)}
be
\kappa
-consistent.

Then there exists
x\in X
so that
d_{U}(b_{U},\pi_{W}(x))\leq\theta_{e}
for all
U\in\mathfrak{S}
. Moreover,
x
is
coarsely unique
in the sense that

\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{X}}\{x\in\mathcal{X}|d_{W}(b_{W},\pi_{W}(x))\leq\theta_{e}\textrm{ for all }U\in\mathfrak{S}\}<\theta_{u}.

Remark 2.5
.

Notably, the proof of Theorem
2.4
in
[
BHS19
]
does not involve the hyperbolicity of the associated spaces
\{\mathcal{C}(U)\}_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}
. In this sense, realization is a purely hierarchical fact which plays a similar role to the existence of a coarse median in Bowditch’s analysis
[
Bow19
]
.

Perhaps the main technique in HHSes is taking an object in the ambient space
\mathcal{X}
and projecting it to all of the hyperbolic spaces, doing hyperbolic geometry, and then reassembling the pieces via hierarchical tools like the Realization Theorem
2.4
. Toward that end, and recognizing that HHSes are fundamentally coarse spaces, we will usually want to restrict ourselves to the
relevant
collection of hyperbolic spaces to which an object has a sufficiently large projection.

Definition 2.6

(Relevant domains)
.

Given a subset
A\subset\mathcal{X}
and
K>0
, we say that
U\in\mathfrak{S}
is
K
-
relevant
for
A
if
\mathrm{diam}_{U}(A)\geq K
. We denote the set of
K
-relevant domains for
A
by
\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(A)
.

The following Passing-Up lemma is a foundational fact about HHSes. Indeed, we will develop multiple more sophisticated version of it in Section
4
below, and, in Proposition
4.15
, prove that it is equivalent to the Large Links Axiom
6
.

Roughly speaking, the passing-up lemma says that if
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
have a large number of lower complexity relevant domains, then there must be some higher complexity relevant domain containing at least one of them, thereby allowing us to “pass up” the
\sqsubset
-lattice.

Lemma 2.7

(Passing-up)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an HHS with constant
E
. For every
D>0
there is an integer
P=P(D)>0
such that if
V\in\mathfrak{S}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
satisfy
d_{U_{i}}(x,y)>E
for a collection of domains
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{P}
with
U_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}_{V}
, then there exists
W\in\mathfrak{S}_{V}
with
U_{i}\sqsubsetneq W
for some
i
such that
d_{W}(x,y)>D.

We observe the following corollary, which is embedded in the proof of Theorem
2.4
in
[
BHS19
, Theorem 3.1]
, but not explicitly stated. We give a proof for completeness:

Corollary 2.8
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, the set
\mathrm{Rel}_{E}(x,y)
is finite.

Proof.

Let
C
be the coarse Lipschitz constant for all of the projections
\pi_{U}
for
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, and set
D>Cd_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)+C
. If
\#\mathrm{Rel}_{E}(x,y)>P(D)
, then the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
provides a domain
W\in\mathfrak{S}
with
d_{W}(x,y)>D
, which is impossible.
∎

Another fundamental fact about HHSes is the following distance formula, which originated in work of Masur-Minsky
[
MM00
]
, and found various iterations elsewhere
[
Bro03
,
Raf14
,
Dur16
,
KK14
]
before being generalized to all HHSes in
[
BHS19
]
:

Theorem 2.9

(Distance formula)
.

There exists
K_{0}=K_{0}(\mathfrak{S})>0
so that if
K>K_{0}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, then

d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)\asymp\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K}

where the coarse equality
\asymp
depends only on
\mathfrak{S}
and
K
.

As mentioned in the introduction, we will give a new proof of the more difficult lower bound in the distance formula in Corollary
14.3
below.

2.4.
Normalized HHSes

Sometimes, one is handed an HHS whose hyperbolic spaces contain more information than necessary. Following
[
DHS17
, Proposition 1.16]
, we assume that every HHS
\mathcal{X}
that we worked satisfies the following assumption:

Definition 2.10

(Normalized HHS)
.

An HHS
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
is
normalized
if there exists
C>0
so that for all
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, we have
\mathcal{C}(U)=\mathcal{N}_{C}(\pi_{U}(\mathcal{X})))
.

That is, we will always assume that our domain projections
\pi_{U}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
are uniformly coarsely surjective.

2.5.
Hierarchical quasi-convexity, gates, and induced HHS structures

The following notion of quasi-convexity generalizes the hyperbolic setting
[
BHS19
, Definition 5.1]
:

Definition 2.11
.

A subset
A\subset\mathcal{X}
is
k
-
hierarchically quasiconvex
for some
k:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
if

(1)

\pi_{U}(A)
is
k(0)
-quasiconvex for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
;

(2)

For all
\kappa\geq 0
and
\kappa
-consistent tuples
(b_{U})\subset\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(U)
with
b_{U}\subset\pi_{U}(A)
for all
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, if
x\in\mathcal{X}
satisfies
d_{U}(x,b_{U})<\theta_{e}(\kappa)
(where
\theta_{e}(\kappa)
is as in Theorem
2.4
), then
d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,A)<k(\kappa)
.

There is a natural notion of projection to a hierarchically quasi-convex subset, described in
[
BHS19
]
as follows:

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{X}
be
k
-hierarchically quasi-convex. For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, define a tuple
\mathfrak{g}(x)\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}2^{\mathcal{C}(U)}
by taking
\mathfrak{g}(x)_{U}
to be closest point projection to
\pi_{U}(A)
. Then
\mathfrak{g}(x)
is
\alpha
-consistent, where
\alpha=\alpha(k(0),\mathcal{X})
.

The map
\mathfrak{g}_{A}:\mathcal{X}\to A
is referred to as a
gate map
in much of the HHS literature because these hierarchically quasi-convex sets are median convex
[
BHS21
,
RST18
]
and coarsely coincide with the median gate associated to the coarse median structure on
\mathcal{X}
.

Another useful fact is that hierarchically quasi-convex subsets admit an induced HHS structure as follows (see
[
BHS19
, Proposition 5.6]
):

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
A\subset\mathcal{X}
be a
k
-hierarchically quasi-convex subset. Then considering
A
with the metric restricted from
\mathcal{X}
, the following give an HHS structure on
A
called the
induced HHS structure
:

•

Fixing a
K=K(A,k,\mathcal{X})>0
sufficiently large, the index set is
\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(A)=\{U\in\mathfrak{S}|\mathrm{diam}_{U}(A)>K\}
.

•

The sets of hyperbolic spaces is
\{\pi_{U}(A)|U\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(A)\}
.

•

All relations, projections, and relative projections are those coming from the ambient structure
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
, where the projections involve a further composition with the retraction
r_{U}:\mathcal{C}(U)\to\pi_{U}(A)
provided by quasi-convexity of
\pi_{U}(A)
in
\mathcal{C}(U)
.

2.6.
Product regions

Another important set of structures in an HHS are its product regions. The construction below, from
[
BHS19
]
, generalizes Minsky’s Product Regions Theorem
[
Min96
]
for Teichmüller space with the Teichmüller metric.

Roughly, each domain
U\in\mathfrak{S}
determines a product region in
\mathcal{X}
, which at the largest scale, consists of two components corresponding to those domains nesting into
U
and those orthogonal to
U
. Each of these components is in fact an HHS itself, where the underlying hierarchy is induced from the respective subcollections of domains in
\mathfrak{S}
. In more details:

Let
U\in\mathfrak{S}
. Let
\mathfrak{S}_{U}=\{V\in\mathfrak{S}|V\sqsubseteq U\}
and note that
U
is the
\sqsubset
-minimal domain in
\mathfrak{S}
so that
W\sqsubset U
for all
W\in\mathfrak{S}_{U}
. On the other hand, observe that the container part of the orthogonality Axiom
3
provides a domain
A\in\mathfrak{S}
so that
V\sqsubset A
for all
V\sqsubset U
, though we need not have
A\perp U
. Nonetheless, set
\mathfrak{S}^{\perp}_{U}=\{W\in\mathfrak{S}|W\perp U\}\cup\{A\}
.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{U}
denote the set of
\theta
-consistent partial tuples in
\prod_{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{U}}2^{\mathcal{C}(V)}
, and let
\mathbb{E}_{U}
denote the set of
\theta
-consistent partial tuples in
\prod_{W\in\mathfrak{S}^{\perp}_{U}}2^{\mathcal{C}(W)}
. Here, consistent partial tuples are exactly those satisfying the Consistency Inequalities
2.2
over a given restricted domain set. Moreover, both
\mathbb{F}_{U}
and
\mathbb{E}_{U}
are HHSes with their structures just the restrictions of the structure on
\mathcal{X}
to the relevant pieces (see
[
BHS19
, Construction 5.10]
).

We can now define a coarsely well-defined map
P_{U}:\mathbb{F}_{U}\times\mathbb{E}_{U}\to\prod_{V\in\mathfrak{S}}2^{\mathcal{C}(V)}
as follows. To any pair of tuples
(x,y)\in\mathbb{F}_{U}\times\mathbb{E}_{U}
and each
V\in\mathfrak{S}
, we define

\pi_{V}(P_{U}(x,y))=\begin{cases}x_{V}&V\sqsubseteq U\\
y_{V}&V\perp U\\
\rho^{U}_{V}&V\pitchfork U\\
\rho^{U}_{V}&U\sqsubset V.\end{cases}

The image of
P_{U}
is
\theta
-consistent
[
BHS19
, Construction 5.10]
, and hence defines a coarsely well-defined map
\mathbf{P}_{U}:\mathbb{F}_{U}\times\mathbb{E}_{U}\to\mathcal{X}
. We denote the image by
\mathbf{P}_{U}
. Note that
\mathbf{P}_{U}
contains many
parallel copies
of both
\mathbb{F}_{U}
and
\mathbb{E}_{U}
. We also have the following (see
[
BHS19
, Proposition 5.11 and Remark 5.14]
):

Lemma 2.14
.

For every
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, we have that
\mathbf{P}_{U}
and every parallel copy of
\mathbb{F}_{U}
and
\mathbb{E}_{U}
are uniformly hierarchically quasi-convex, with constant depending only on
\mathcal{X}
.

Hence admits a gate map
\mathfrak{g}_{P_{U}}:\mathcal{X}\to P_{U}
via Lemma
2.12
.

For our purposes, we need the following three lemmas. The first is immediate from the above definitions, but will prove useful in the proof of Proposition
3.14
below:

Lemma 2.15
.

For any
U\in\mathfrak{S}
and
x\in\mathbf{P}_{U}
, we have
d_{V}(\pi_{V}(x),\rho^{U}_{V})<\theta
for all
U\sqsubset V
or
U\perp V
.

The second is also immediate, and is useful in Proposition
3.14
below:

Lemma 2.16
.

If
U\perp V
, then
\mathbf{P}_{U}\cap\mathbf{P}_{V}\neq\emptyset
.

We finish with another useful lemma, which is obvious in the surface setting. We thank Mark Hagen for providing a proof in the general case. It allows us to gain extra control over the structure of the map
\rho^{V}_{U}:\mathcal{C}(V)\to\mathcal{C}(U)
when
U\sqsubset V
:

Lemma 2.17
.

Suppose that
V,W\sqsubset U
,
V\pitchfork W
, and
d_{U}(\delta^{V}_{U},\delta^{W}_{U})>10E
. If
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(W))>10^{10}E
, then
d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V}(\rho^{W}_{U}),\rho^{W}_{V})<3E
.

Proof.

Using surjectivity of
\pi_{W}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{C}(W)
, choose some
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
so that
d_{W}(x^{\prime},\rho^{V}_{W})>10^{10}E
. Now take the gate image
x=\mathfrak{g}_{P_{W}}(x^{\prime})
in
P_{W}
, so that we now have (1)
d_{U}(x,\rho^{W}_{U})<E
while still having (2)
d_{W}(x,\rho^{V}_{W})>10^{9}E
.

Item (1), the BGIA
7
, and our assumption that
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},\rho^{W}_{U})>10E
imply that
d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V}(x),\rho^{U}_{V}(\rho^{W}_{U}))<E
. On the other hand, consistency of
x
and item (2) imply that
d_{V}(x,\rho^{W}_{V})<E
.

Hence
d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V}(\rho^{W}_{U}),x)<2E
. But
d_{W}(x,\rho^{V}_{W})>10^{9}E
, and thus consistency of
x
(Lemma
2.3
) gives
d_{V}(x,\rho^{W}_{V})<E
. It follows from the triangle inequality that
d_{V}(\rho^{W}_{V},\rho^{U}_{V}(\rho^{W}_{U}))<3E
, as required.

∎

We note that in the above lemma, we need to assume that
\mathcal{C}(W)
has relatively large diameter. The only places we use this lemma involve where
W
is a
K
-relevant domain for some pair of points, where we can choose
K
as large as necessary, so this does not present an issue. Of course, most HHSes have the
bounded domain dichotomy
(Definition
17.1
), i.e. all domains are either coarse points or infinite diameter.

Finally, we observe that neither (1) the existence of a gate map to a hierarchically quasi-convex subset, nor (2) the fact that product regions are hierarchically quasi-convex depends on the Distance Formula
2.9
, as both are fundamentally consequences of Realization
2.4
; see
[
BHS19
, Section 5]
for details. Hence Lemma
2.17
does not depend on the distance formula either. This is important, because this lemma proves useful in a number of places throughout this article.

2.7.
HHS automorphisms

There is a natural notion of an automorphism of an HHS, which generalizes how mapping class groups act on their associated curve graph data. The definition originally appeared in
[
BHS19
]
and automorphisms were studied in depth in
[
DHS17
]
. The following definition, which is now standard (see e.g.,
[
PS20
, Section 2]
and
[
CRHK21
, Definition 10.9]
), is equivalent to the original via
[
DHS20b
, Section 2]
:

Definition 2.18
.

An
automorphism

g
of an HHS
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
is a quasi-isometry of
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
together with the following:

(1)

A bijection
\mathfrak{S}\to\mathfrak{S}
denoted by
U\mapsto gU
, which preserves nesting (
\sqsubset
) and orthogonality (
\perp
) (and hence also transversality).

(2)

For each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, an isometry
g:\mathcal{C}(U)\to\mathcal{C}(gU)
.

(3)

For each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we require
g\pi_{U}(x)=\pi_{gU}(gx)
.

(4)

For all
U,V\in\mathfrak{S}
, we require
g\rho^{U}_{V}=\rho^{gU}_{gV}
, whenever
\rho^{U}_{V}
is defined.

•

We let
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
denote the group of HHS automorphisms of
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
.

Remark 2.19
.

In what follows, we will see that our constructions only depend on these elementary parts, namely the ambient space
\mathcal{X}
, the hyperbolic spaces
\mathcal{C}(U)
, the projections
\pi_{U}
, and the relative projections
\rho^{U}_{V}
. As such, equivariance of our construction, as in Theorem
B
, will be automatic.

3.
Hierarchy rays and boundary points: Projections and consistency

The original cubulation construction from
[
BHS21
]
involved hulls of finite sets of interior points, but we are interested in extending this to hulls of finite sets of interior points, boundary points, and hierarchy rays, with the latter two first being studied in
[
DHS17
]
.

In this section, we will discuss boundary points, hierarchy rays and their associated domain projections. This leads us to an extended notion of consistency for these objects (Proposition
3.18
), which we use in various coarse arguments throughout the paper.

For the rest of the section, we fix a proper HHS
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S}
), where properness is useful so that we can apply Arzelà-Ascoli.

3.1.
Boundary points and their domain projections

In
[
DHS17
]
, we introduced, with Hagen and Sisto, a boundary construction for HHSes and used them to study HHS automorphisms and subgroup embeddings. For most of this paper, we will just need the notion of a boundary point and how to project it to the domains in
\mathfrak{S}
. However, in Section
16
, we will need a description of the simplicial structure of the boundary, and we defer that (equivalent) discussion until later:

Definition 3.1

(Boundary point, support set, orthogonal complement)
.

The HHS boundary of
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
as a set
\partial\mathcal{X}
consists of points
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
consisting of the following data:

(1)

A pairwise orthogonal set of domains
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\subset\mathfrak{S}
called the
support set
of
\lambda
;

(2)

For each
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, a boundary point
\lambda_{U}\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
.

(3)

A collection of constants
\{a_{U}|U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\}
with
\sum_{U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)}a_{U}=1
.

We denote the set of domains orthogonal to
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
by
\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)=\{V\in\mathfrak{S}|V\perp U\text{ for all }U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\}
.

Remark 3.2

(Ignoring simplex constants)
.

The constants
a_{U}
in item (3) of Definition
3.1
are superfluous for our purposes. They pick out a point in the boundary simplex generated by the rest of the data. Since domain projections do not distinguish different points in the interior of the same simplex, we will ignore these constants moving forward. See Subsection
16.3
below for a discussion of the simplicial structure on the HHS boundary.

We would like to associate to
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
a tuple of points
(\lambda_{U})\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
. For domains which are not in
\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
, we will see how to use
\lambda_{U}
for
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
to produce a projection. On the other hand, for domains in
\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
, we will need to fix a basepoint. This will not cause problems in what follows, since we will always assume that our set of finite points (whose hierarchical hull we are modeling) contains an interior point.

Boundary projections were defined in
[
DHS17
, Definition 2.6]
, but our approach actually coincides with the slightly more general and canonical approach from
[
ABR22
, Definition 3.12]
. For this, we need a brief discussion and some notation.

First, we note that the
\delta
-hyperbolic spaces
\mathcal{C}(U)
for
U\in\mathfrak{S}
are frequently not proper, and hence we cannot guarantee the existence of a geodesic ray representing a point in
\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
. Nonetheless, one can always find a
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic representative ray.

Let
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
be a boundary point. Let
N
denote the function provided by the Morse lemma for a
\delta
-hyperbolic space, where
\mathcal{C}(U)
is
\delta
-hyperbolic for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
.

If
V\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
and
U\sqsubset V
, then we let
\lambda^{U}_{V}
denote all points at least
E+N(1,20\delta)
away from
\rho^{U}_{V}
on all
(1,20\delta)
-quasigeodesic rays which represent
\lambda_{U}\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
and are based at points in
\rho^{U}_{V}
. We note that
\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda^{U}_{V}))<E
by the BGIA
7
.

Given
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
and a basepoint
{\mathfrak{o}}\in\mathcal{X}
, we can now define a tuple
(\lambda_{U})\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
:

Definition 3.3

(Based boundary projection)
.

Let
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
and fix a basepoint
{\mathfrak{o}}\in\mathcal{X}
. If
U\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
, then we set
\lambda_{U}=\pi_{U}({\mathfrak{o}})
. If
U\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
, then we define
\lambda_{U}\in\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
as follows:

(1)

If
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then
\pi_{U}(\lambda)
is the equivalence class of
\lambda_{U}
in
\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
.

(2)

If
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\cup\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
does not nest into any
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then set
\mathcal{V}=\{V\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)|V\pitchfork U\text{ or }V\sqsubsetneq U\}
and define
\displaystyle\pi_{U}(\lambda)=\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\rho^{V}_{U}.

(3)

If
U\sqsubset V
for some
V\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then
V
is the unique such domain in
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
. In this case, we let
\pi_{U}(\lambda)
be
\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda^{U}_{V})
.

•

Moreover, we set
\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(\lambda))=\pi_{U}(\lambda)
.

Remark 3.4
.

Unlike in
[
DHS17
]
, the benefit of this definition is that there are no choices made, and so the projections are canonical relative to the given HHS structure on
\mathcal{X}
.

3.2.
Hierarchy rays and their projections

One of the fundamental constructions in an HHS is that of a hierarchy path, the definition of which has a natural ray extension:

Definition 3.5

(Hierarchy path and ray)
.

Given
L>0
, a map
\mathbf{h}:[a,b]\to\mathcal{X}
is called a
L
-
hierarchy path
if
\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h})
is an unparameterized
(L,L)
-quasigeodesic for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
. If instead the domain of
\mathbf{h}
is
[0,\infty)
, then
\mathbf{h}
is called a
L
-
hierarchy ray
.

•

We denote the set of
L
-hierarchy rays in
\mathcal{X}
by
\mathcal{X}_{L}^{ray}
.

The existence of
L
-hierarchy paths in an HHS
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
for uniform
L=L(\mathfrak{S})>0
is known in several settings
[
MM00
,
Bro03
,
Dur16
,
BHS19
,
Bow19
]
, and we will deduce it from our model construction in Section
14
below. Moreover,
L
-hierarchy rays can be built from sequences of
L
-hierarchy paths when
\mathcal{X}
is proper via an Arzelà-Ascoli argument. We record their existence in the following lemma:

Lemma 3.6
.

There exists
L=L(\mathcal{X})>0
so that any pair of points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
are connected by an
L
-hierarchy path.

Notation 3.7

(Ray notation)
.

Throughout the paper, when working with a hierarchy ray
\mathbf{h}
, we will almost exclusively use
\mathbf{h}
as a label for a ray, not the image of a ray itself. So we adopt the notation
\overline{\mathbf{h}}=\mathbf{h}([0,\infty))\subset\mathcal{X}
to refer to the image of a hierarchy ray
\mathbf{h}
.

Since a
L
-hierarchy ray is itself an infinite-diameter
(L,L)
-quasigeodesic in
\mathcal{X}
, we will see that it must have some domains where it has an infinite projection. Since these play a similar to role to support sets of boundary points, we use the same terminology:

Definition 3.8

(Support set of a ray)
.

Given an
L
-hierarchy ray
\mathbf{h}
in
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
, the
support set
of
\mathbf{h}
is

\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})=\{U\in\mathfrak{S}|\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\pi_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{h}}))=\infty\}.

•

We denote the set of domains orthogonal to
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
by
\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\mathbf{h})
.

Lemma 3.9
.

For any hierarchy ray
\mathbf{h}
, the set
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
is nonempty and pairwise orthogonal. Hence there exists
\xi=\xi(\mathfrak{S})>0
so that
\#\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})<\xi
.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\neq\emptyset
is
[
DHS17
, Lemma 3.3]
. To see pairwise orthogonality, let
U,V\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
and suppose that
V\pitchfork U
or
V\sqsubset U
.

Using the fact that
\pi_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{h}}))
is an
(L,L)
-quasi-geodesic ray in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, then we may choose a time
t\in[0,\infty)
sufficiently large so that
\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h}([t,\infty))\cap\mathcal{N}_{E}(\rho^{V}_{U})=\emptyset
. Hence the transverse (when
U\pitchfork V
) or nested (when
V\sqsubset U
) consistency inequalities
4
plus the BGIA
7
imply that, for any
t^{\prime}>t
, we have
d_{V}(\pi_{V}(\mathbf{h}(t^{\prime})),\rho^{U}_{V})<2E
(when
U\pitchfork V
) or
d_{V}(\pi_{V}(\mathbf{h}(t^{\prime})),\rho^{U}_{V}(\mathbf{h}(t^{\prime})))<2E
(when
V\sqsubset U
). Either way, we get a contradiction of the assumption that
V\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
.
∎

For our purposes, the main difference between a hierarchy ray and a point in the HHS boundary is that the latter is possibly missing some data that needs to be supplemented by a basepoint. On the other hand, a hierarchy ray
\mathbf{h}
consists of points in
\mathcal{X}
, and hence has a natural projection to every domain. This, however, creates a new subtlety, because we want to record the whole projection of a
\mathbf{h}
to each domain in
\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\mathbf{h})
, which is possibly an arbitrarily long quasi-geodesic ray and not a coarse point. In Proposition
3.14
below, we will show that, when considering hierarchical hulls, one can replace boundary points with carefully constructed hierarchy rays, but we need two different but closely related notions of projections in the meantime.

Suppose then that
\mathbf{h}:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal{X}
is an
L
-hierarchy ray and
U\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\mathbf{h})
. Let
t_{U}\in[0,\infty)
be a time so that

d_{U}(\mathbf{h}(0),\mathbf{h}(t_{U}))+1\geq\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\pi_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{h}})).

Note that since
U\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\mathbf{h})
, we have
\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\pi_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{h}}))<\infty
, and hence such a time
t_{U}
exists, though is likely not unique.

We can now associate a tuple
(\mathbf{h}_{U})\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
to a hierarchy ray
\mathbf{h}
as follows:

Definition 3.10

(Ray projections)
.

Let
\mathbf{h}:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal{X}
be a hierarchy ray. For each
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
, we define
\mathbf{h}_{U}\in\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
as follows:

(1)

If
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
, then
\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h})=\mathbf{h}_{U}\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
is the equivalence class of
\pi_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{h}})
in
\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
.

(2)

If
U\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\mathbf{h})
does not nest into any
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
, then set
\mathcal{V}=\{V\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})|V\pitchfork U\text{ or }V\sqsubsetneq U\}
and define
\displaystyle\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h})=\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\rho^{V}_{U}.

(3)

If
U\sqsubset V
for some
V\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
, then
V
is the unique such domain in
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
. In this case, we let
\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h})
be
\rho^{V}_{U}(\mathbf{h}^{U}_{V})
.

•

Moreover, we set
\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(\mathbf{h}))=\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h})
.

•

On the other hand, if
U\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\mathbf{h})
, then we define
\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h})=\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h}(t_{U}))
, where
t_{U}\in[0,\infty)
is as above.

3.3.
Some useful statements about projections of hierarchy rays

We briefly pause to observe some useful facts about projecting hierarchy rays, which appear both in the proof that the hull of a finite collection of boundary points can replaced by the hull of a carefully constructed collection of hierarchy rays (Proposition
3.14
), and in our proof that tuples of ray (and hence boundary) projections satisfy an extended notion of consistency (Proposition
3.18
).

First, we need a basic fact from Gromov hyperbolic geometry, whose proof we include for completeness:

Lemma 3.11
.

For every
L>0,\delta>0
there exists
M_{0}=M(L,\delta)>0
so that the following holds: Let
X
be
\delta
-hyperbolic and
q:[0,a]\to X
an
(L,L)
-quasi-geodesic. Then for any time
t\in[0,a]
such that

\displaystyle d_{X}(q(0),q(t))+1\geq\mathrm{diam}_{X}(q([0,a]))

we have
|a-t|<M
.

Proof.

Let
t\in[0,a]
be as in the statement. If
[q(0),q(a)]
is a geodesic between
q(0),q(a)
, then the Morse property says that
d_{X}(q(t),[q(0),q(a)])<N=N(\delta,L)
. Hence there exists some
p\in[q(0),q(a)]
so that
d_{X}(q(t),p)<N
. Since
q(t)
is maximally far from
q(0)
, it follows that
d_{X}(p,q(a))<N+1
. But then
d_{X}(q(t),q(a))<2N+1
, and thus
a-t<(2N+1)L+L
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma
3.11
allows us to prove the following useful non-back-tracking statement for hierarchy rays:

Lemma 3.12
.

For any
L>0
there exists
M_{1}=M_{1}(L,\mathfrak{S})>0
so that the following holds. Suppose
\mathbf{h}:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal{X}
is an
L
-hierarchy ray and
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
. If
T_{U}\in[0,\infty)
is such that
d_{U}(\mathbf{h}(0),\mathbf{h}(T_{U}))+1\geq\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\pi_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{h}}))
, then for all
t\geq T_{U}
, we have

d_{U}(\mathbf{h}(t),\mathbf{h}(T_{U}))<M_{1}.

Proof.

Suppose first that
U\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\mathbf{h})
. Then we have
\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\mathbf{h})<\infty
, and hence
\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h})
is a segment. Since
\mathbf{h}
projects to an unparameterized
(L,L)
-quasi-geodesic, it can be reparameterized into a quasi-geodesic satisfying the assumptions of Lemma
3.11
. The conclusion in this case is now immediate.

On the other hand, if
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\cup\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\mathbf{h})
, then either (1)
U\pitchfork V
or
U\sqsubset V
for some
V\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
, or (2)
V\sqsubset U
for some
V\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})
. In each of these cases, the definition of
\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h})
requires taking points sufficiently far out the tail of
\mathbf{h}
, and so the conclusion follows from Definition
3.10
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.4.
Replacing boundary points with rays

In this subsection, we explain why we can reduce our considerations to hulls of rays instead of hulls of boundary points when our ambient HHS
\mathcal{X}
is proper. We will make this properness assumption going forward when dealing with boundary points, since these ray approximations will be necessary for certain arguments.

Lemma 3.13
.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
is a proper HHS and
L>0
. There exists
\theta^{\prime\prime}=\theta^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{S},L)>0
so that if
{\mathfrak{o}}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
, then there exists an
L
-hierarchy ray
\mathbf{h}_{\lambda}
with

(1)

\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}_{\lambda})=\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
,

(2)

For all
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, we have
[\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h}_{\lambda})]=[\lambda_{U}]\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
,

(3)

For all
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, we have
d_{U}(\pi_{U}(\mathbf{h}_{\lambda}),\pi_{U}(\lambda))<\theta^{\prime\prime}
,

(4)

\mathbf{h}_{\lambda}\subset\mathrm{hull}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}}({\mathfrak{o}},\lambda)
.

Proof.

For each
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, let
\gamma_{U}
be a
(1,20)
-quasi-geodesic ray in
\mathcal{C}(U)
from
{\mathfrak{o}}
to
\lambda_{U}
. For each
n
, let
a^{n}_{U}\in\gamma_{U}
be a point so that the sequence
(a^{n}_{U})
converges to
\lambda_{U}
in
\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
as
n\to\infty
. For each partial tuple
(a^{n}_{U})_{U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)}
, use the Partial Realization Axiom
8
to produce a sequence of points
p^{\prime}_{n}
with
d_{U}(p^{\prime}_{n},a^{n}_{U})<\alpha=\alpha(\mathfrak{S})
for each
n
.

Let
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}({\mathfrak{o}},\lambda)
and
\mathfrak{g}_{H}:\mathcal{X}\to H
be the gate map from Lemma
6.6
above. If we set
p_{n}=\mathfrak{g}_{H}(p^{\prime}_{n})
, then
d_{U}(p_{n},a^{n}_{U})
is uniformly bounded for each
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, while
d_{V}(p_{n},H_{V})
is uniformly bounded for each
V\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, where
H_{U}
is the hyperbolic hull of
\pi_{V}({\mathfrak{o}}),\pi_{V}(\lambda)
in
\mathcal{C}(V)
.

Let
h_{n}
be an
L
-hierarchy path between
{\mathfrak{o}}
and
p_{n}
. Since
\mathcal{X}
is proper, we can apply Arzela-Ascoli to the sequence
(h_{n})
to obtain a subsequence which converges to a hierarchy ray
h
. It follows from our choice of
a^{n}_{U}
above that
h
satisfies
\mathrm{supp}(h)=\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, and for each
U\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(h)
, we have
[h_{U}]=[\lambda_{U}]
by construction, proving (1) and (2).

For (3), observe that if
V\in\mathfrak{S}
with
V\sqsubset U
,
V\pitchfork U
, or
U\sqsubset V
for some
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}_{\lambda})
, then the definitions of
\pi_{V}(\lambda)
and
\pi_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{\lambda})
(Definition
3.3
and
3.10
) coarsely coincide. For
V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
, the desired bound follows Lemma
3.12
.

Finally for (4), observe that by items (2) and (3), quasi-convexity of
H_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{U}({\mathfrak{o}},\lambda_{U})
for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, and the fact that
h_{n}
projects to an
(L,L)
-quasi-geodesic in each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, it follows that
\pi_{U}(\overline{h})
is coarsely contained in
H_{U}
, with constants depending only on
\mathfrak{S}
and
L
. Hence there exists
\theta^{\prime\prime}=\theta^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{S},L)>0
so that
h\subset\mathrm{hull}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}}({\mathfrak{o}},\lambda)
, by Definition
6.4
of the hull. This completes the proof.

∎

With this lemma in hand, our replacement statement is essentially immediate.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
F\subset\mathcal{X}
be finite and nonempty. Let
\Lambda
be a finite sets of boundary points and
L
-hierarchy rays, with
\lambda(0)\in F
for any hierarchy ray
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Let
\widehat{\Lambda}
denote the set of hierarchy rays in
\Lambda
along with a replacement hierarchy ray
\mathbf{h}_{\lambda}
for each boundary point
\lambda
as constructed in Lemma
3.13
.

There exists
\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}(L,\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that

•

H_{\theta_{0}}=\mathrm{hull}_{\theta_{0}}(F\cup\Lambda)
and
\widehat{H}_{\theta_{0}}=\mathrm{hull}_{\theta_{0}}(F\cup\widehat{\Lambda})
are within bounded Hausdorff distance,

•

The gate maps
\mathfrak{g}_{H_{\theta_{0}}}:H_{\theta_{0}}\to\widehat{H}_{\theta_{0}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{\widehat{H}_{\theta_{0}}}:\widehat{H}_{\theta_{0}}\to H_{\theta_{0}}
are both uniform quasi-isometries depending only on
\mathfrak{S},L,|F\cup\Lambda|
,

with all constants in the above depending only on
\mathfrak{S},L,|F\cup\Lambda|
.

Proof.

The first conclusion is an immediate consequence of Lemma
3.13
and Lemma
3.12
. The second part of the statement is a straight-forward application of the definitions, the first conclusion of this proposition, Lemma
3.13
, and the Distance Formula
2.9
.
∎

Hence for the rest of the paper, we can restrict our consideration to only dealing with hierarchy rays.

Remark 3.15
.

To see that the above proposition has content, observe that if
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, then one can define a sequence of geodesic (hierarchy) rays
\gamma_{n}
which limit onto
+\infty
in the first component, while their projection of
\gamma_{n}
to the second component is
[0,n]
. The boundary point corresponding to
+\infty
in the first component does not see the second component. In the general setting, given
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
so that
\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
contains a domain
U
with
\mathrm{diam}\mathcal{C}(U)=\infty
and
U
not
\sqsubset
-maximal, then the union of all hierarchy rays which limit onto points in the smallest simplex in
\partial\mathcal{X}
containing
\lambda
will have infinite Hausdorff distance with
\mathrm{hull}_{\theta}(\lambda(0),\lambda)
for any
\theta
, as hierarchy rays are free to move in domains
V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
as they choose. Thus these special hierarchy ray representatives for boundary points are strictly necessary. See also work of Mousely
[
Mou19
]
for examples of more exotic Teichmüller rays and their limit sets in the HHS boundary of Teichmüller space, which takes advantage of the fact that Teichmüller geodesics are not hierarchy rays, but are very close to being so
[
Raf14
]
.

3.5.
Consistency of boundary and ray tuples

Our next goal is to show that the tuples we associated to boundary points (Definition
3.3
) and hierarchy rays (Definition
3.10
) satisfy uniform consistency conditions. We are treating them together because most of the discussion is similar, and having a statement for both may be useful in the future, despite our replacement Proposition
3.14
above.

The following definition simply generalizes standard consistency (Definition
2.2
) to allow for some coordinates to be at infinite.

Definition 3.16

(Extended consistency)
.

For
\theta>0
, we say that a tuple

(\lambda_{U})\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\left(\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)\right)

satisfies
extended
\theta
- consistency
if given any
U,V\in\mathfrak{S}
, we have the following:

•

If
U\pitchfork V
or
V\sqsubset U
, then
\min\{d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U}),d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})\}<\theta
.

•

If
U\sqsubset V
, then
\min\{d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V}),\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\lambda_{U}\cup\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V})\}<\theta
.

Remark 3.17
.

When
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then
\lambda_{U}\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
, and so necessarily
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})=\infty
when
V\pitchfork U
or
V\sqsubset U
, and
\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\lambda_{U}\cup\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V}))=\infty
when
U\sqsubset V
. Hence when
(\lambda_{U})
is
\theta
-consistent, then in all of these cases we have
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})<\theta
.

Observe that an immediate consequence of this definition is that if
(\lambda_{U})
is
\theta
-consistent, then
\{U\in\mathfrak{S}|\lambda_{U}\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)\}
is pairwise orthogonal.

We can now prove our consistency statement for tuples associated to boundary points and hierarchy rays.

Proposition 3.18
.

For every
L>0
, there exists
\theta_{L}=\theta_{L}(L,\mathfrak{S})>0
so that if
\lambda
is either an
L
-hierarchy ray or a point in
\partial\mathcal{X}
with a basepoint
{\mathfrak{o}}\in\mathcal{X}
, then the tuple of projections

(\lambda_{U})\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\left(\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)\right)

is
\theta_{L}
-consistent.

Proof.

Let
U,V\in\mathfrak{S}
. The proof is a case-wise analysis depending on how
U,V
relate to each other and to the domains in
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
.

First, note that
U\perp V
when
U,V\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, so we may exclude this. Similarly, when
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then
\lambda_{V}
is given by Definitions
3.3
and
3.10
, and we may that take
\theta_{L}=E
in these cases.

There are three main cases: (1)
U,V\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
; (2)
U\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
and
V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
; (3)
U,V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
.

(1)
U,V\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
: There are three main subcases: (1.a)
U\pitchfork V
; (1.b)
U\sqsubset V
, with the case of
V\sqsubset U
dealt with by the same argument as (1.b).

(1.a)
U\pitchfork V
and
U,V\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
: First suppose that both
\mathcal{W}_{U}=\{W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)|W\pitchfork U\text{ or }W\sqsubset U\}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{W}_{V}\neq\emptyset
. Then
\lambda_{U}=\bigcup_{W\in\mathcal{W}_{U}}\rho^{W}_{U}
and
\lambda_{V}=\bigcup_{Z\in\mathcal{W}_{V}}\rho^{Z}_{V}
.

We deal with further subcases:

•

There is some domain
Z\in\mathcal{W}_{U}\cap\mathcal{W}_{V}
.

–

If
Z\sqsubset U
, then
d_{V}(\rho^{Z}_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})<E
, and hence
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})<2E
. Similarly,
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<2E
when
Z\sqsubset V
. So we are done in this case.

–

Suppose instead that
Z\pitchfork U
and
Z\pitchfork V
. Let
x\in\mathbf{P}_{Z}
, the product region for
Z
in
\mathcal{X}
(see Subsection
2.6
). Then
d_{U}(x,\rho^{Z}_{U})<E
and
d_{V}(x,\rho^{Z}_{V})<E
by Lemma
2.15
. Since the tuple
(\pi_{U}(x))
is
\theta=\theta(\mathfrak{S})
-consistent, it follows that one of
d_{U}(\rho^{Z}_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<\theta+E
or
d_{V}(\rho^{Z}_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})<\theta+E
must hold, so we are done in this case.

•

Next, suppose that
\mathcal{W}_{U}\cap\mathcal{W}_{V}=\emptyset
. Now let
W_{U}\in\mathcal{W}_{U}
and
W_{V}\in\mathcal{W}_{V}
, and choose
x\in\mathbf{P}_{W_{U}}\cap\mathbf{P}_{W_{V}}
, which is possible by Lemma
2.16
. Then
d_{U}(x,\rho^{W_{U}}_{U})<E
and
d_{V}(x,\rho^{W_{V}}_{V})<E
, and one of
d_{U}(\rho^{W_{U}}_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<\theta+E
or
d_{V}(\rho^{W_{V}}_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})<\theta+E
must hold, and so we are done in this case.

The next subcase of (1.a) is when
\mathcal{W}_{U}\neq\emptyset
but
\mathcal{W}_{V}=\emptyset
. Then there exists some (unique)
Z\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
so that
V\sqsubset Z
, and
\lambda_{V}=\lambda^{Z}_{V}
, while
\lambda_{U}=\bigcup_{W\in\mathcal{W}_{U}}\rho^{W}_{U}
. Since
U\pitchfork V
, we cannot have that
Z\perp U
and hence
Z\in\mathcal{W}_{U}
. But then
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{Z}_{U})<E
and so
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<2E
since
V\sqsubset Z
. This proves this case.

The final subcase of (1.a) is when
\mathcal{W}_{U}=\mathcal{W}_{V}=\emptyset
. Then our assumptions that
U\pitchfork V
and
U,V\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
provide a domain
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
with
U,V\sqsubset W
. Let
\gamma_{U},\gamma_{V}\subset\mathcal{C}(W)
be
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic rays based at
\rho^{U}_{W},\rho^{V}_{W}
, respectively, which are both asymptotic to
\lambda_{W}\in\partial\mathcal{C}(W)
. Since they have the same limit, they are eventually
100\delta
-close. Using
E
-coarse surjectivity of
\pi_{W}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{C}(W)
(Definition
2.10
), we can choose
x\in\mathcal{X}
so that
\pi_{W}(x)
is as far away from
\rho^{U}_{W},\rho^{V}_{W}
as we want and within
E+100\delta
of both
\gamma_{U},\gamma_{V}
. The BGIA
7
then implies that
d_{U}(x,\lambda_{U})<E
and
d_{V}(x,\lambda_{V})<E
, while
\theta
-consistency of the tuple
(\pi_{Z}(x))
implies that one of
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<E+\theta
or
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})<E+\theta
. So we are done in this case.

This completes the proof of subcase (1.a).

(1.b)
U\sqsubset V
and
U,V\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)

For this subcase, we note that if
Z\in\mathcal{W}_{U}
, then
Z\in\mathcal{W}_{V}
since
U\sqsubset V
and therefore we cannot have either
Z\perp V
or
V\sqsubset Z
. Hence we have two cases, when
\mathcal{W}_{U}\neq\emptyset
and when
\mathcal{W}_{U}=\mathcal{W}_{V}=\emptyset
.

So first suppose that
Z\in\mathcal{W}_{U}\cap\mathcal{W}_{V}
. There are three subcases:

•

If
Z\sqsubset U
, then
Z\sqsubset U\sqsubset V
, in which case
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})<2E
because
d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V},\rho^{Z}_{V})<E
, and we are then done.

•

If
Z\pitchfork U
and
Z\sqsubset V
, then let
x\in\mathbf{P}_{Z}
. By definition of the projections, we have
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{Z}_{V})<E
and
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{Z}_{U})<E
, while
x\in\mathbf{P}_{Z}
implies that
d_{U}(x,\rho^{Z}_{U})<E
. Now if
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})>20E+\theta
, then
\theta
-consistency of the tuple
(\pi_{W}(x))
provides that
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(x_{V}),x_{U})<\theta
while Lemma
2.17
implies that
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\rho^{Z}_{V}),\rho^{Z}_{U})<3E
. An application of the BGIA
7
gives us that
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V}),\rho^{V}_{U}(\rho^{Z}_{V}))<E
, and hence
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V}),\lambda_{U})<\theta+E
, completing the proof of this subcase.

•

Suppose
Z\pitchfork U
,
Z\pitchfork V
, so that
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{Z}_{U})<E
and
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{Z}_{V})<E
. Suppose
d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V},\lambda_{V})>10E
and
x\in\mathbf{P}_{Z}
. Then
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{Z}_{V})<E
by definition of
\lambda_{V}
, while
d_{V}(\rho^{Z}_{V},x)<\theta
because
x\in\mathbf{P}_{Z}
(Lemma
2.15
). Now
\theta
-consistency says
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(x),x)<\theta
, while
d_{U}(x,\rho^{Z}_{U})<\theta
because
x\in\mathbf{P}_{Z}
. It follows then that
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<10\theta+10E
. as required.

The final subcase of (1.b) is when
\mathcal{W}_{U}=\mathcal{W}_{V}=\emptyset
. Since
U\sqsubset V
and
\mathcal{W}_{V}=\emptyset
, there must be a (unique) domain
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
so that
U\sqsubset V\sqsubset W
. Note that
d_{W}(\rho^{V}_{W},\rho^{U}_{W})<E
. Using
E
-surjectivity of
\pi_{W}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{C}(W)
, we can choose a point
x\in\mathcal{X}
so that
\pi_{W}(x)
is as far from
\rho^{V}_{W},\rho^{V}_{W}
as necessary and within
E+100\delta
of two
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic rays limiting to
\lambda_{W}
and based at
\rho^{U}_{W},\rho^{V}_{W}
, respectively (which eventually
100\delta
-fellow travel). By definition of
\lambda_{U},\lambda_{V}
and the BGIA
7
, we have
d_{U}(\rho^{W}_{U}(x),\lambda_{U})<2E
and
d_{V}(\rho^{W}_{V}(x),\lambda_{V})<2E
. On the other hand
\theta
-consistency and the fact that
d_{W}(x,\rho^{V}_{W})>10E
implies that
d_{V}(x,\rho^{W}_{V}(x))<\theta
and
d_{U}(x,\rho^{W}_{U}(x))<\theta
. So if
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})>10E+\theta
, then
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(x)),\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V}))<E
and
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(x)),\pi_{U}(x))<\theta
. Hence
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V}))<2E+\theta
, as required.

(2)
U\notin\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
and
V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
: There are three main subcases:

•

U\pitchfork V

–

If
\mathcal{W}_{U}\neq\emptyset
, then there is some
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
so that
W\pitchfork U
or
W\sqsubset U
, and
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{W}_{U})<E
. But since
V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
, we must have
W\perp V
. Hence
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},\rho^{W}_{U})<E
, and it follows that
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<2E
, and we are done.

–

On the other hand, we cannot have
\mathcal{W}_{U}=\emptyset
, for then there is some
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
with
U\sqsubset W
, which implies either
V\pitchfork W
or
V\sqsubset W
, both of which are impossible because
V\perp W
.

•

It is not possible for
U\sqsubset V
under the current assumptions, which imply that there is some
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
satisfying one of the following:

–

W\pitchfork U
and hence
W\sqsubset V
or
W\pitchfork V
, contradicting
V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
;

–

W\sqsubset U
and hence
W\sqsubset V
, another contradiction;

–

Or
U\sqsubset W
, in which case either
V\sqsubset W
or
W\sqsubset V
, both of which are contradictions.

•

Finally, if
V\sqsubset U
, then there must be some
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
satisfying
W\pitchfork U
or
W\sqsubset U
, since if
U\sqsubset W
then
V\sqsubset U\sqsubset W
, which contradicts
V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
. So when
W\pitchfork U
or
W\sqsubset U
, we have
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<2E
since
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{W}_{U})<E
and
d_{U}(\rho^{W}_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<E
because
V\perp W
.

This completes the proof of (2).

(3)
U,V\in\mathrm{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)
: This subcase has two main subcases, where
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
and when
\lambda\in\mathcal{X}^{ray}
.

In fact, when
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
, we have
\lambda_{U}=\pi_{U}({\mathfrak{o}})
and
\lambda_{V}=\pi_{V}({\mathfrak{o}})
for our fixed basepoint
{\mathfrak{o}}\in\mathcal{X}
. Hence consistency in this case follows from consistency of the projections of
{\mathfrak{o}}
.

It remains to consider when
\lambda\in\mathcal{X}^{ray}
. Let
t_{U},t_{V}\in[0,\infty)
be times so that
d_{U}(\lambda(0),\lambda(t_{U}))+1\geq\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\overline{\lambda})
and similarly for
t_{V}
, as in Definition
3.10
.

Suppose first that
U\pitchfork V
, and we may assume that
t_{U}\geq t_{V}
. Then Lemma
3.12
implies that
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\lambda(t_{U}))=d_{V}(\lambda(t_{U}),\lambda(t_{V}))<M_{1}=M_{1}(\mathfrak{S},L)
. So if
d_{U}(\lambda(t_{U}),\rho^{V}_{U})+E\geq d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})>\theta+E
, then
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})<M_{1}+\theta+E
, a required.

Finally, suppose that
U\sqsubset V
and
d_{V}(\lambda_{V},\rho^{U}_{V})>10E+10M_{1}+10\theta
. There will be two last subcases, depending on whether
t_{U}\geq t_{V}
or
t_{V}\geq t_{U}
.

If
t_{V}\geq t_{U}
, then
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V}),\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda(t_{V})))<E
and
d_{U}(\lambda(t_{V}),\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda(t_{V}))<\theta
, from which it follows that
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V}),\lambda(t_{V}))<\theta+E
. But then
d_{U}(\lambda_{U},\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda(V))<E+\theta+M_{1}
, as required.

On the other (and final) hand, if
t_{U}\geq t_{V}
, then
d_{V}(\lambda(t_{V}),\lambda_{V})<M_{1}
and
d_{V}(\lambda(t_{V}),\lambda(t_{U}))<M_{1}
. Thus
d_{V}(\lambda(t_{V}),\rho^{U}_{V})>E
, then the BGIA
7
implies that
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda_{V}),\lambda_{U})<2E
, as required.

This completes the proof of (3) and of the lemma.

∎

4.
Hierarchical accounting techniques

In this section, we establish some useful statements about the basic structure of HHSes. They all elaborate on the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
, which is a core property of HHSes that follows from finite complexity and the Large Link Axiom
6
. In fact, we use the passing-up lemma to prove a strong version of the large links axiom in Proposition
4.15
below, showing that the former can replace the latter as an axiom.

One of the main complications in this section is that it takes a bit of work to generalize Lemma
2.7
to allow for hierarchy rays instead of just internal points of
\mathcal{X}
. While this makes the narrative a bit circuitous, it is necessary for the rest of the paper.

Our first main goal is to prove a generalization of Lemma
2.7
to hierarchy rays, which we do in Lemma
4.4
below. This proof will rely on a stronger version of Lemma
2.7
, namely Strong Passing-up Proposition
4.3
, which we will also want to extend to allow for hierarchy rays. In order to minimize repetition of statements, we will begin by proving an extension of the Petyt-Spriano Lemma
4.5
assuming the extended version of Lemma
2.7
, namely Lemma
4.4
. Using the unextended versions of Lemmas
2.7
and Lemma
4.5
, we will prove the unextended version of the Strong Passing-up Proposition
4.3
. This will allow us to prove Extended Passing-up, Lemma
4.4
, completing the loop and the actual proof of the extended version of the Petyt-Spriano Lemma
4.5
, which then gives a proof of Extended Strong Passing-up Proposition
4.7
.

After this proof cycle is complete, we proceed with proving some other new facts. These include a strong version of the Large Links property
6
in Proposition
4.15
, and some other useful consequences, namely: the Covering Lemma
4.8
which we use frequently; the Bounding Containers Proposition
4.14
, which we need for various distance estimates; and finally an independent account of why changing thresholds in the distance formula works, Proposition
4.16
.

4.1.
No dense clusters, interior points

In addition to Lemma
2.7
, we need the following useful consequence due to Petyt-Spriano
[
PS20
, Lemma 5.4]
. Roughly, it says that whenever a large number of relevant domains for a pair
x,y
nests maximally into another relevant domain
W
, then their
\rho
-points cannot cluster into a small area along any geodesic
[x,y]\subset\mathcal{C}(W)
. In Lemma
4.5
below, we will extend this to allow for hierarchy rays and boundary points with the same conclusion, and essentially the same proof just with appropriately modified arguments.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
be an HHS and
D>50E
. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
and
W\in\mathfrak{S}
have
d_{W}(x,y)>D
. Suppose that

(1)

U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\sqsubsetneq W
satisfy
d_{U_{i}}(x,y)>3E
.

(2)

If
U_{i}\sqsubsetneq V\sqsubsetneq W
for some
i
, then
d_{V}(x,y)\leq D
.

Then if
n\geq P(D+2E)
, then

\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{U_{i}}_{W})>D-30E.

4.2.
Strong passing-up

Our first goal is to prove a strengthened version of the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
, which will allow us to generalize that lemma to allow for hierarchy rays.

Roughly, the stronger version says that not only does having a very large number of relevant domains between
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
guarantee the existence of a very large domain
W
between them, but we can both force arbitrarily many of the relevant domains to be contained in
W
while also forcing the
\rho
-sets of this colelction to spread out in
\mathcal{C}(W)
.

The second conclusion also requires some notation and a discussion. The idea is that not only can we force the
\rho
-sets to spread out, but we can force them to spread out in a relatively efficient manner. For this, we need a notion of subdivision of an efficient path between points in
\mathcal{C}(W)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(W)
.

Definition 4.2

(
\sigma
-subdivision)
.

Let
\gamma:I\to\mathcal{C}(W)
be a
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic path (i.e., segment, ray, or bi-infinite) in
\mathcal{C}(W)
between
\pi_{W}(x),\pi_{W}(y)
where
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
. For
\sigma>0
, we say that a subdivision
\{x_{i}\}
of
I
is a
\sigma
-
subdivision
of
\gamma
if the
x_{i}
decompose
I
into a collection of subintervals
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
so that for all but at most one
i
we have
|x_{i+1}-x_{i}|=\sigma
, with the (possibly nonexistent) extra subinterval for which
|x_{i+1}-x_{i}|<\sigma
.

We note that when one or both of
x
or
y
is a ray with
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, then a
\sigma
-subdivision does not determine an extra possibly shorter subinterval.

Suppose now that
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
,
K_{1}\geq 50E
,
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y)
and
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y)
so that
V\sqsubset W
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Let
\gamma:I\to\mathcal{C}(W)
be a
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic path between
x,y
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
.

Given a
\sigma
-subdivision of
\gamma
, let
\mathcal{W}_{i}
denote the set of domains
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
so that
p_{\gamma}(\rho^{V}_{W})\cap[x_{i},x_{i+1}]\neq\emptyset
, where
p_{\gamma}:\mathcal{C}(W)\to\gamma
is closest point projection. Note that any given
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
belongs in at most two
\mathcal{W}_{i}
when
\sigma\geq 10E+20\delta
. See Figure
5
.

Figure 5
.
A
\sigma
-subdivision: In the proof of Proposition
4.3
, subdividing
\gamma
allows us to iteratively apply Lemma
4.1
. The
\rho
-points (in orange) must lie
E
-close to
\gamma
, and have closest point projections to
\gamma
with diameter bounded by
2E
, as highlighted by
\rho^{V}_{W}
(in red). Each such projection therefore intersects at most two of the
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
, and hence the
\#\mathcal{W}_{i}
coarsely account for
\#\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

The second conclusion of the proposition says that, given any
n
, we can increase the number of domains in
\mathcal{V}
so that at least
n
of the
\mathcal{V}_{i}
are nonempty. This specific conclusion will be necessary in Subsection
9.1
.

The proof of this stronger proposition proceeds via an inductive argument using the Petyt-Spriano Lemma
4.1
as a hammer.

Proposition 4.3

(Strong passing-up)
.

For any
K_{2}\geq K_{1}\geq 50E
, there exists
P_{1}=P_{1}(K_{1},K_{2})>0
so that for any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, if
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y)
with
\#\mathcal{V}>P_{1}
, then there exists
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
so that
V\sqsubset W
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
and

\mathrm{diam}_{W}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V}_{W}\right)>K_{2}.

Moreover, for any
\sigma\geq 10E+20\delta
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
P_{2}(K_{1},K_{2},\sigma,n)>0
so that if
\#\mathcal{V}>P_{2}
, then we can arrange the following to hold:

•

If
\gamma:I\to\mathcal{C}(W)
is a
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic in
\mathcal{C}(W)
between
x,y
and
\{x_{i}\}
is a
\sigma
-subdivision of
\gamma
determining sets
\mathcal{W}_{i}
as above, then

\#\{1\leq i\leq k|\mathcal{W}_{i}\neq\emptyset\}\geq n.

Proof.

In the proof, we first set
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}+30E}(x,y)
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{0}
be the set of
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-maximal domains in
\mathcal{U}
. Let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
denote the set of
V\in\mathcal{V}
so that
V
does not nest into any domain in
\mathcal{U}_{0}
. Note that

•

\#\mathcal{U}_{0}<P(K_{2})
, otherwise the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
would produce a domain in
\mathcal{U}
containing some domain in
\mathcal{U}_{0}
, violating our
\sqsubset
-maximality assumption on
\mathcal{U}_{0}
;

•

Similarly,
\#\mathcal{V}^{\prime}<P(K_{2})
, otherwise Lemma
2.7
would produce some domain in
\mathcal{U}
containing some domain in
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, violating its definition.

Hence there is some
U_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
so that if
\mathcal{V}_{0}=\{V\in\mathcal{V}|V\sqsubset U_{0}\}
, then we can take
\#\mathcal{V}_{0}
to be as large as necessary.

Now either
U_{0}
satisfies both conclusions of the proposition, or not. If not, then let
\mathcal{U}_{1}
be the set of
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}_{0}}
-maximal domains in
\mathcal{U}_{0}
. As above, we have
\#\mathcal{U}_{1}<P(K_{2})
and all but at most
P(K_{2})
domains in
\mathcal{V}_{0}
must nest into some domain in
\mathcal{U}_{1}
. Hence there exists some
U_{1}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
so that if
\mathcal{V}_{1}=\{V\in\mathcal{V}_{0}|V\sqsubset U_{1}\}
, then we can take
\#\mathcal{V}_{1}
to be as large as necessary.

We can repeat this argument until arriving at the second level from the bottom of the
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-lattice, say at step
m
. This produces for us some
U_{m}\in\mathcal{U}_{m}
and
\mathcal{V}_{m}=\{V\in\mathcal{V}_{m-1}|V\sqsubset U_{m}\}
. But now all
V\in\mathcal{V}_{m}
satisfy
V\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}_{m}}U_{m}
, and hence we may apply Lemma
4.1
, which says that if
\#\mathcal{V}_{m}>P(K_{2}+30E+2E)
, then

\mathrm{diam}_{W}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{n}}\rho^{V}_{U_{m}}\right)>K_{2}+30E-30E=K_{2}.

To complete the proof of the first conclusion, we observe that at each step of the argument, we only ignored at most
P(K_{2})
-many domains from
\mathcal{V}_{i}
to guarantee they each nested into some domain in
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
, and then only needed to divide the number of domains in
\mathcal{V}_{i}
by at most
P(K_{2})
to find some very large subset all of which nested into
U_{i+1}
. Finally, the number of steps was bounded by the number of levels in the
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-lattice, which is bounded above by the number of levels in the
\sqsubset_{\mathfrak{S}}
-lattice, which is controlled solely by
\mathfrak{S}
.

The “moreover” part is essentially now a consequence of Lemma
4.1
as follows. Let
\mathcal{V}_{m}
and
U_{m}
be as above in this proof, and the
\mathcal{W}_{i}
be defined as the discussion before the proposition. Each
V\in\mathcal{V}_{m}
is contained in at most two
\mathcal{W}_{i}
, while we have
\mathcal{V}_{m}=\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{W}_{i}
. Since each
V\in\mathcal{V}_{m}
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-maximal in
U_{m}
, we can use Lemma
4.1
to bound
\mathcal{W}_{i}
by
2P(\max\{\sigma,52E\})
for each
i
. Hence given some
n
, we can increase
\#\mathcal{V}_{m}
as necessary to force at least
n
-many indices
i
so that
\mathcal{W}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, as required. This completes the proof.
∎

4.3.
Passing-up for hierarchy rays

We now prove the hierarchy ray version of the Passing-up Lemma
2.7
.

The proof involves approximating the rays by points along the hierarchy rays which have coarsely the same projections as the rays to the small domains in question, similar to the proof of Lemma
4.5
above. The idea is then to use some sort of passing-up type statement to produce a large domain
W
for the approximation points which contains at least one of the small domains for the rays. We need Proposition
4.3
, instead of Lemma
2.7
, to deal with the case where
W
is not in the support of either ray, since it allows us to force the geodesic between the projections of the rays to be long because it must pass close to a wide set of
\rho
-sets from our small domains.

Lemma 4.4
.

There exists
PU:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, depending only on
\mathcal{X}
and
L
such that the following holds: Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
, and let
D>50E
. If
V_{1},\dots,V_{N}\in\mathrm{Rel}_{5E}(x,y)
with
N>PU(D)
, then there exists
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{D}(x,y)
and
i
with
V_{i}\sqsubset W
. Moreover, if
V_{i}\sqsubset Z
for all
i
, then we can take
W\sqsubset Z
.

Proof.

Observe that if both
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, then this is just Lemma
2.7
. We deal with the case where both
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{ray}
, and indicate how to deal with the other cases afterwards.

Since the support sets of
x,y
are pairwise-orthogonal and hence have cardinality bounded by
\xi=\xi(\mathfrak{S})>0
, we may assume that the
V_{i}\notin\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
for all
i
by increasing
PU(D)
by
2\xi
.

By Lemma
3.12
, there exist times
t_{V_{1}},\dots,t_{V_{N}}
so that
d_{V_{i}}(x(t),x)<E
and
d_{V_{i}}(y(t),y)<E
. Choosing
t>\max\{t_{V_{1}},\dots,t_{V_{N}}\}
then gives

d_{V_{i}}(x(t),y(t))-2E>d_{V}(x,y)>3E.

We now want to apply a passing-up type statement. The effect of this will be to produce a larger
W
which contains at least one of the
V_{i}
, and for which we can make
d_{W}(x(t),y(t))
as large as necessary to conclude that
d_{W}(x,y)>D
. When
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, the original Passing-up Lemma
2.7
suffices. The difficulty will come when
W\notin\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, and this is where we need the main part of Proposition
4.3
.

The Strong Passing-up Proposition
4.3
implies that if
N>P_{1}(D+10E)
, then there exists
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{2D+10E}(x(t),y(t))
so that
V_{i}\sqsubset W
for all
i
and

\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{i}\rho^{V_{i}}_{W})<2D+10E.

There are two main cases: (1)
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, and (2)
W\notin\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
.

In case (1), if
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)-\mathrm{supp}(y)
, then
d_{W}(x,y)=\infty
and we are done, and similarly when
W\in\mathrm{supp}(y)-\mathrm{supp}(x)
. On the other hand, if
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cap\mathrm{supp}(y)
, then we are also done if
[x]\neq[y]\in\partial\mathcal{C}(W)
for then
d_{W}(x,y)=\infty
. But if
[x]=[y]
, then by definition of
\pi_{V_{i}}(x),\pi_{V_{i}}(y)
, we have
d_{V_{i}}(x,y)<E
, which is a contradiction of the assumption that
V_{i}\in\mathrm{Rel}_{5E}(x,y)
. This takes care of case (1).

For case (2), both
\pi_{W}(x),\pi_{W}(y)
are interior points of
\mathcal{C}(W)
. Since
V_{i}\in\mathrm{Rel}_{5E}(x,y)
for all
i
, the BGIA
7
implies that any geodesic
[x,y]_{W}
between
x,y
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
must pass within
E
of every
\rho^{V_{i}}_{W}
. But the diameter of these
\rho
-sets is
2D+10E
, and it follows then that the length of
[x,y]_{W}
is at least
D
, and hence
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{D}(x,y)
, as required. We note that we only needed the approximators
x(t),y(t)
to force the wide spread of these
\rho
-sets

Finally, if all domains
V_{i}\sqsubset Z
for some
Z\in\mathfrak{S}
, then we can choose
W\sqsubset Z
because Proposition
4.3
allows it. This completes the proof.
∎

4.4.
No dense clusters for rays

We are now ready to prove the extended version of the Petyt-Spriano Lemma
4.1
for rays:

Lemma 4.5

(No dense clusters)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
be an HHS and
D>50E
. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
and
W\in\mathfrak{S}
have
d_{W}(x,y)>D
. Suppose that

(1)

U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\sqsubsetneq W
satisfy
d_{U_{i}}(x,y)>5E
.

(2)

If
U_{i}\sqsubsetneq V\sqsubsetneq W
for some
i
, then
d_{V}(x,y)\leq D
.

Then if
n\geq P(D+2E)+2\xi
, then

\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{U_{i}}_{W})>D-30E.

Proof.

First, suppose the
U_{i}\sqsubset W
are as in the statement, but
\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{U_{i}}_{W})\leq D-30E
.

Next, observe by ignoring the at-most
2\xi
-many domains contained in
\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
when
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{ray}
, we may assume that
\pi_{U_{i}}(x),\pi_{U_{i}}(y)\in\mathcal{C}(U_{i})
for each
i
.

There are three cases: (1)
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cap\mathrm{supp}(y)
, (2)
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)-\mathrm{supp}(y)
(and same with
x,y
switching roles), and (3)
