W\notin\mathrm{supp}(x)\cap\mathrm{supp}(y)
. The arguments are similar and all use a localization trick from Petyt-Spriano’s proof, and in fact their proof works essentially verbatim in case (3). We will only present the mixed case (2), since it covers the other two.

Suppose then that
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)-\mathrm{supp}(y)
. Then
\pi_{W}(y)\in\mathcal{C}(W)
is an interior point, while
\pi_{W}(x)\in\partial\mathcal{C}(W)
determines a boundary point. By Lemma
3.12
, there exists times
\{t_{U_{1}},\dots,t_{U_{n}}\}
so that if
T>t_{U_{i}}
for all
i
and
t\geq T
, then
d_{U_{i}}(x(t),x)<E
for all
i
(since we are assuming that
E>M_{1}
from that lemma; see Section
5
). It follows from our assumptions and the first condition then that
d_{U_{i}}(x(t),y)>3E
when
t>T
. In addition, since
\pi_{W}(x)
is an unparameterized
(L,L)
-quasigeodesic, we may choose
t>T
that
d_{W}(x(t),\rho^{U_{i}}_{W})>10D
for all
i
.

For the Petyt-Spriano localization argument, we will choose two points
z^{-},z^{+}\in\mathcal{X}
as local representatives of
x(t),y
, respectively, as follows. If
d_{W}(y,\rho^{U_{i}}_{W})<10E
for some
i
, then set
z^{+}=y
. Otherwise, let
y^{\prime}
be a point on
[x(t),y]_{W}\subset\mathcal{C}(W)
with
d_{W}(y^{\prime},\rho^{U_{i}}_{W})\leq D-20E
and
d_{W}(y^{\prime},y)<d_{W}(\rho^{U_{i}}_{W},y)-5E
for all
i
. Since
\mathcal{X}
is normalized (Definition
2.10
),
\pi_{W}
is
E
-coarsely onto, so we can choose
z^{+}\in\mathcal{X}
with
d_{W}(z^{+},y^{\prime})\leq E
. Similarly define
z^{-}
by using
x(t)
instead of
y
.

By assumption,
y,x(t)
cannot simultaneously be
10E
-close to the union of the
\rho^{U_{i}}_{W}
, so
d_{W}(z^{-},z^{+})\leq D
by construction. On the other hand, the BGIA
7
implies that
d_{U_{i}}(x(t),z^{-})\leq E
and
d_{U_{i}}(y,z^{+})\leq E
, and hence
d_{U_{i}}(z^{-},z^{+})>E
for all
i
.

Thus if
n\geq P(D+2E)+2\xi
, then the Extended Passing-up Lemma
4.4
provides some
V\sqsubset W
with
d_{U}(z^{-},z^{+})>D+2E
and
U_{i}\sqsubset V
for some
i
. Since
d_{W}(z^{-},z^{+})\leq D
, we have
V\sqsubsetneq W
and thus
d_{W}(\rho^{V}_{W},\rho^{U_{i}}_{W})\leq E
for each
i
. The BGIA
7
then implies that
d_{U}(x,z^{-})\leq E
and
d_{U}(y,z^{+})\leq E
, and hence
d_{U}(x,y)>D
, which contradicts our assumptions. This completes the proof.

∎

Remark 4.6
.

Besides the fact that it works for rays, the above statement is different from the original in
[
PS20
]
in that it has a slightly larger smaller threshold,
5E
versus
3E
, and a slightly larger lower bound for
n
, namely
P(D+2E)+2\xi
. The latter is to allow us to ignore the at-most
2\xi
-many domains in the support of the rays.

4.5.
Strong passing-up for rays

Finally, we get our full version of Strong Passing-up, in which we use the same notation:

Proposition 4.7
.

For any
K_{2}\geq K_{1}\geq 50E
, there exists
P_{1}=P_{1}(K_{1},K_{2})>0
so that for any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
, if
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y)
with
\#\mathcal{V}>P_{1}
, then there exists
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
so that
V\sqsubset W
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
and

\mathrm{diam}_{W}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V}_{W}\right)>K_{2}.

Moreover, for any
\sigma\geq 10E
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
P_{2}(K_{1},K_{2},\sigma,n)>0
so that if
\#\mathcal{V}>P_{2}
, then we can arrange the following to hold:

•

If
\gamma
is any geodesic in
\mathcal{C}(W)
between
x,y
and
\{x_{i}\}
is a
\sigma
-subdivision of
\gamma
determining sets
\mathcal{V}_{i}
as before, then

\#\{i|\mathcal{V}_{i}\neq\emptyset\}\geq n.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
4.3
works essentially verbatim, replacing Lemma
2.7
with Lemma
4.4
.

∎

As a quick corollary, we observe the following useful Covering Lemma, which first appeared as
[
DMS20
, Lemma 2.15]
for interior points; see also
[
CRHK21
, Lemma 11.13]
for a different proof of the interior point case:

Lemma 4.8

(Covering)
.

For any HHS
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
, there exists
N=N(\mathfrak{S})>0
so that the following holds. For any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
and all
U\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(x,y)
, there are at most
N
domains
V\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(x,y)
so that
U\sqsubset V
.

Proof.

Let
U\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{V}=\{V\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(x,y)|U\sqsubset V\}
. If
\#\mathcal{V}>P_{1}(50E,50E)
, then the Strong Passing-Up Proposition
4.7
provides
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(x,y)
and a subcollection
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
so that
\mathrm{diam}_{W}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V^{\prime}}_{W}\right)>50E
. But this is impossible, since
U\sqsubset V
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, which forces that
d_{W}(\rho^{V}_{W},\rho^{V^{\prime}}_{W})<2E
, for any
V,V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Hence
\#\mathcal{V}<P_{1}(50E,50E)
, which depends only on
\mathfrak{S}
, as required.
∎

4.6.
Detecting rays from collections of domains

In Sections
12
and
16
, we will need to be able to convert infinite collections of domains in the relevant set of a finite number of interior points and hierarchy rays into the data associated to one of the rays. Since basic ideas required for such statements are contained in this section, we include them as the following lemma.

The first is an iterative applciation of Strong Passing-Up
4.7
for rays:

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
. For any
K_{1}\geq 50E
, if
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y)
has
\#\mathcal{V}=\infty
, then there exists an infinite subcollection
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
and a domain
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
so that
V\sqsubset W
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V}_{W})=\infty
.

Proof.

The proof is inductive. At each step, we either construct some domain in the support of
x
or
y
, in which case we are done, or otherwise we construct a properly nested chain of relevant domains for
x,y
, which must have bounded length by finite complexity, Axiom
5
.

For each
n>0
, apply Strong Passing-Up
4.7
to obtain a collection
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{n}\subset\mathcal{V}
and a domain
W^{1}_{n}\in\mathrm{Rel}_{n}(x,y)
so that
V\sqsubset W^{1}_{n}
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{1}_{n}
and

\mathrm{diam}_{W^{1}_{n}}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{1}_{n}}\rho^{V}_{W^{1}_{n}})>50En.

Consider the sequence
W^{1}_{1},W^{1}_{2},\dots\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(x,y)
of domains produced by this process. If there exists a domain
W
so that
W=W^{1}_{n}
for infinitely-many
n
, then we must have that
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, since
d_{W}(x,y)\succ n
for infinitely-many
n
, with the constants of
\succ
depending only on
\mathcal{X}
. Moreover, we can accomplish this infinite spread in
\mathcal{C}(W)
with domains in
\mathcal{V}
as follows. First, throw out the
n
for which
W^{1}_{n}\neq W
and similarly relabel the
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{n}
, then set
\mathcal{Z}_{1}=\bigcup_{n}\mathcal{V}^{1}_{n}
. Then it follows that
\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\rho^{Z}_{W})=\infty
, as required.

Suppose then that no such
W
exists after this first iteration. Hence we have an infinite distinct collection
\mathcal{V}^{1}=\{W^{1}_{1},W^{1}_{2},\dots\}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(x,y)
. Repeating this process, for each
n
, we obtain a subcollection
\mathcal{V}^{2}_{n}\subset\mathcal{V}^{1}
and a domain
W^{2}_{n}
so that
V\sqsubset W^{2}_{n}
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{2}_{n}
and
\mathrm{diam}_{W^{2}_{n}}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{2}_{n}}\rho^{V}_{W^{2}_{n}})>50En
.

This time, however, the domains in the
\mathcal{V}^{2}_{n}
need not be in
\mathcal{V}
. Nonetheless, we can find
\mathcal{V}
-representatives
for each
\mathcal{V}^{2}_{n}
as follows. Observe that for each
n
, there exists
V_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}_{n}
with
V_{2}\sqsubsetneq W^{2}_{n}
. However, by construction,
V_{2}=W^{1}_{k}
for some
k
, and hence there is some
V_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{1}_{k}\subset\mathcal{V}
so that
V_{1}\sqsubsetneq V_{2}\sqsubsetneq W^{2}_{n}
. This domain
V_{1}\in\mathcal{V}
represents
W^{2}_{n}
because
V_{1}\sqsubset W^{2}_{n}
implies that
d_{Z}(\rho^{V_{1}}_{Z},\rho^{W^{2}_{n}}_{Z})<E
for any domain
Z
into which both
V_{1}
and
W^{2}_{n}
nest.

Again, if there exists some
W
so that
W=W^{2}_{n}
for infinitely-many
n
, then necessarily
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, as before. Moreover, as observed above, we can realize this infinite diameter in
\mathcal{C}(W)
by a spread of domains in
\mathcal{V}
by taking their
\mathcal{V}
-representatives. More specifically, we throw out the
n
for which
W^{2}_{n}\neq n
, relabel, and set
\mathcal{Z}_{2}=\bigcup_{n}\mathcal{V}^{2}_{n}
. Then we can consider the collection
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{2}\subset\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{V}
-representatives for the domains in
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
, and we get that

\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{2}}\rho^{V}_{W})=\infty,

as required.

If no such
W
exists, then we can repeat this process. By finite complexity (Axiom
5
), this process can only be repeated boundedly-many times (controlled by
\mathcal{X}
), because at each step
k
and each
n
, we create a properly nested chain
V_{1}\sqsubsetneq V_{2}\sqsubsetneq\cdots\sqsubsetneq W^{k}_{n}
, where
V_{1}
is the
\mathcal{V}
-representative of
W^{k}_{n}
. Hence at some finite step
k
, we must have that there exists some
W\in\mathfrak{S}
so that we have
W=W^{k}_{n}
for infinitely-many
n
. Setting
\mathcal{Z}=\bigcup_{n}\mathcal{V}^{k}_{n}
, it follows then

\mathrm{diam}_{W}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\bigcup\mathcal{Z}}\rho^{V}_{W}\right)=\infty,

while these
\rho
-sets must also be
(E+20\delta)
-close to any
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic ray (or biinfinite path) between
x,y
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
by the BGIA
7
.

Finally, if we set
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
to be the
\mathcal{V}
-representatives for the domains in
\mathcal{V}^{k}=\{W^{k}_{1},W^{k}_{2},\dots\}
, then our observation above forces

\mathrm{diam}_{W}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\bigcup\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V}_{W}\right)=\infty,

as required. This completes the proof.
∎

4.7.
Bounding containers

Our next goal is to prove some statements which allow us to manage multiple thresholds in distance formula-like estimates. The first lemma says that the number of domains nesting maximally into
W
is coarsely bounded above by
d_{W}(x,y)
, thereby reversing the inequality. Its proof is essentially an iterative application of Lemma
4.5
.

Lemma 4.10

(Quantitative passing-up)
.

For any
K_{1}\geq 3E
and
K_{2}\geq K_{1}+47E
the following holds. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
, and set
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y)
. Suppose that
W\in\mathcal{W}
and let
\mathcal{V}=\{V\in\mathcal{U}|V\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}W\}
. Then
\#\mathcal{V}\prec d_{W}(x,y)
, with constants depending only on
\mathfrak{S}
and
K_{1}
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the cases where either
W\notin\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, since otherwise
d_{W}(x,y)=\infty
and the statement is vacuous.

Let
\gamma\subset\mathcal{C}(W)
be a geodesic between
\pi_{W}(x)
and
\pi_{W}(y)
, and let
p_{\gamma}:\mathcal{C}(W)\to\gamma
denote closest point projection. By the BGIA
7
, if
V\in\mathcal{U}
, then
d_{W}(\rho^{V}_{W},\gamma)<E
. Since also
\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\rho^{V}_{W})<E
, it follows that
\mathrm{diam}_{W}(p_{\gamma}(\rho^{V}_{W}))<2E
.

Let
x=x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}=y
be a subdivision of
\gamma
into subintervals
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
of length at most
10E
, so that
d_{W}(x_{i},x_{i+1})=10E
for all
i<n-1
. Note that
n\geq 5
by our assumption on
K_{2}
.

As in the proof of Proposition
4.3
, for each
i
, let
\mathcal{W}_{i}=\{V\in\mathcal{V}|p_{\gamma}(\rho^{V}_{U})\cap[x_{i},x_{i}+1]\neq\emptyset\}
. Since any given
V\in\mathcal{V}
belongs to at most
2
of the
\mathcal{W}_{i}
while also
\mathcal{V}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{W}_{i}
, it suffices to bound the cardinality of each
\mathcal{W}_{i}
since
10En=\lfloor d_{W}(x,y)\rfloor
.

For this, we observe that each
\mathcal{V}_{i}
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
4.5
, and hence must have cardinality at most
P(52E)
. This completes the proof.

∎

Remark 4.11
.

The need for the above proof, instead of using Lemma
2.7
directly, comes from the fact that the passing-up function
P
is likely to be exponential in the general setting. If it were linear, then we could directly apply it to obtain the conclusion of Lemma
4.10
.

Remark 4.12
.

We note that Lemma
4.10
is not unrelated to
[
RS09
, Lemma 5.1]
about certain anti-chains in relevant sets in the mapping class group setting. The main difference is that the roles of their double-thresholds are reversed: where we are trying to control medium-sized domains via large domains, they were controlling large domains via medium-sized domains. Nonetheless, we suspect that an general HHS version of their anti-chain statement can be derived from the ideas in this section, though we have not done so presently.

The proof of Lemma
4.10
has a useful corollary:

Lemma 4.13
.

Suppose that
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
. Then for any
K>50E
, we have
\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
is a countable set.

Proof.

Note that if
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, then
\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
is finite by Corollary
2.8
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
is infinite. By Lemma
4.10
, only finitely-many domains in
\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
can nest into any domain not in
\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
. On the other hand, if
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, then Lemma
4.5
implies that only boundedly-many
\rho
-sets for domains can cluster near any bounded-diameter segment of a
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic path between
\pi_{W}(x),\pi_{W}(y)
, as in the argument in Lemma
4.10
. Hence we can decompose
\gamma
into an infinite union of bounded length segments
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
, and realize the set of domains in
\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
which nest into
W
as the union of the sets
\mathcal{V}_{i}
of domains whose
\rho
-sets are close to
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
. Hence this set is countable, and this works for any
W\in\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, and hence all of
\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
is countable, completing the proof.
∎

We can now prove the following useful proposition, which will allow us to control medium-size domains in various distance estimates and double threshold arguments. First we set some notation.

Choose thresholds
K_{1}>50E
and
K_{2}>K_{1}+100E
. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{ray}
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{U}_{1}-\mathcal{U}_{2}
. The
\mathcal{V}
are “medium-size” domains which we will want to control.

The proposition says two things. First, it says that all but boundedly-many
V\in\mathcal{V}
nest into some
W\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
. Second, the lemma bounds the number of
V\in\mathcal{V}
which can nest into any given
W\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
terms of
d_{W}(x,y)
,
\mathfrak{S}
and
K_{2}
.

Proposition 4.14

(Bounding large containers)
.

Let
K_{1}>50E
,
K_{2}\geq K_{1}
, and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{ray}
with
\mathcal{U}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y),\mathcal{U}_{2}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(x,y),
and
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}_{1}-\mathcal{U}_{2}
. Then the following hold:

(1)

For all but
PU(K_{2})-1
domains
V\in\mathcal{V}
, there exists
W\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
with
V\sqsubset W
.

(2)

For any
W\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
, if
\mathcal{W}=\{V\in\mathcal{V}|V\sqsubset W\}
then we have
\#\mathcal{W}\prec d_{W}(x,y)
with constants depending only on
\mathfrak{S}
and
K_{2}
.

Proof.

For part (1), we use an iterative argument via Lemma
4.4
as follows: Let
n>PU(K_{2})
and enumerate
\mathcal{V}
, which is possible since
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}_{1}
is countable by
Lemma
4.13
. By applying Lemma
4.4
to
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
, we obtain
W_{1}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
so that, up to relabeling,
V_{1}\sqsubset W_{1}
. Now apply the lemma again to
V_{2},\dots,V_{n+1}
to obtain
W_{2}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
so that, up to relabeling,
V_{2}\sqsubset W_{2}
. Repeating this process finds containers in
\mathcal{U}_{2}
for all but
PU(K_{2})-1
domains (when
\mathcal{U}_{1}
is finite), and these only impact the additive error in the desired estimate from part (2) of the statement.

For part (2), now fix
W\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
. We may assume that
W\notin\mathrm{supp}(x)\cup\mathrm{supp}(y)
, for otherwise
d_{W}(x,y)=\infty
and the statement is vacuous. We will bound
\#\mathcal{W}
by iteratively applying Lemma
4.10
.

For ease of notation within this proof, let
A\sqsubset_{\mathcal{B}}C
denote the case where
A\neq C
and
D\notin\mathcal{B}
whenever
A\sqsubsetneq D\sqsubsetneq C
, noting that we are not requiring that
A\in\mathcal{B}
for this notation.

Proceeding iteratively, if
\mathcal{V}_{1}=\{V\in\mathcal{V}|V\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}W\}
, then
\#\mathcal{V}_{1}\prec d_{W}(x,y)
by Lemma
4.10
. Now if
V\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
, then
\#\{Z\in\mathcal{V}|Z\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}V\}\prec d_{V}(x,y)<K_{2}
, by definition of
\mathcal{V}
and again Lemma
4.10
. We can then proceed down the
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}
-lattice through
\mathcal{V}_{1}
, which has boundedly-many levels by finite complexity of
\mathfrak{S}
(Axiom
5
). This bounds
\#\{Z\in\mathcal{V}|Z\sqsubset V\}
for each
V\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
in terms of
\mathfrak{S}
and
K_{2}
, whereas we already bounded
\#\mathcal{V}_{1}
in terms of
d_{W}(x,y)
. Combining these gives the desired bound on
\#\mathcal{W}
.
∎

4.8.
Strong Large Links

We briefly observe that the standard Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
implies the statement of the Large Links Axiom
6
.

The proof of Lemma
4.5
from
[
PS20
]
only involves the BGIA
7
, the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
, and the assumption that the ambient HHS is normalized (namely that
\pi_{U}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
is coarsely surjective for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
). Hence the flow of logic from the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
to Lemma
4.10
via Lemma
4.5
does not depend on the Large Link Axiom
6
. The following corollary then allows us to, for instance, replace the Large Links Axiom
6
with the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
as an axiom in the definition of an HHS.

Note that the conclusion is actually stronger than the Large Links Axiom, in three ways. First, we get that the containers
T_{i}
are relevant domains themselves. Second, we get that the
T_{i}
are
\sqsubset
-maximal in
W
with respect
\mathrm{Rel}_{E^{\prime}}(x,y)
. Third, we can force close proximity of
\rho^{T_{i}}_{W}
to any geodesic between
\pi_{W}(x)
and
\pi_{W}(y)
, and not just rough proximity to
x
. We will make use of these additional features.

Proposition 4.15

(Strong large links)
.

There exist
\lambda\geq 1
and
E^{\prime}=E^{\prime}(\mathfrak{S})>50E
such that the following holds.
Let
W\in\mathfrak{S}
and let
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
and set
\mathcal{W}=\mathrm{Rel}_{E^{\prime}}(x,x^{\prime})
. Let

N=\lambda d_{W}(\pi_{W}(x),\pi_{W}(x^{\prime}))+\lambda
. Then there exists
\{T_{i}\}_{i=1,\dots,\lfloor N\rfloor}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{W}-\{W\}
such that for all
T\in\mathfrak{S}_{W}-\{W\}
, either
T\in\mathfrak{S}_{T_{i}}
for some
i
, or
d_{T}(\pi_{T}(x),\pi_{T}(x^{\prime}))<E^{\prime}
. Moreover, we have:

(1)

T_{i}\in\mathcal{W}
for each
i
.

(2)

T_{i}\sqsubset_{\mathcal{W}}W
for each
i
;

(3)

d_{W}([x,x^{\prime}]_{W},\rho^{T_{i}}_{W})\leq E^{\prime}
for each
i
and any geodesic
[x,x^{\prime}]_{W}
between
\pi_{W}(x),\pi_{W}(x^{\prime})
;

Proof.

Let
W\in\mathfrak{S}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{V}=\{V\in\mathcal{U}|V\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}W\}
. By the Quantitative Passing-Up Lemma
4.10
, there exists
\lambda=\lambda(50E,\mathfrak{S})>0
so that
\#\mathcal{V}<\lambda d_{W}(x,y)+\lambda
.

Hence if
T\in\mathfrak{S}_{W}-\{W\}
and
d_{T}(x,y)\geq 50E
, then
T\sqsubset V
for some
V\in\mathcal{V}
by definition of
\mathcal{V}
. The fact that
d_{W}(x,\rho^{V}_{W})\leq N
for each
V\in\mathcal{V}
follows from the BGIA
7
and by increasing
\lambda
a bounded amount, as necessary. This completes the proof.
∎

4.9.
Changing thresholds

We observe a final foundational application of Proposition
4.14
, which we believe has expositional value.

In
[
BHS19
]
and this paper, the proof of the Distance Formula
2.9
is intertwined with the proof of the existence of hierarchy paths. As a consequence, it is less than transparent that changing the relevance threshold should only affect the distance estimate up to coarse equality, because changing the relevance threshold requires one to build a new hierarchy path.

The following corollary explains why this sort of basic domain accounting works, regardless of whether the distance formula holds or hierarchy paths exist. The proof is a straight-forward application of the ideas in this section, which the reader can take as a warm-up for its more sophisticated analogues later in the paper.

Corollary 4.16
.

For any
K_{1}>50E
,
K_{2}\geq K_{1}+100E
, and any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{1}}\asymp\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{2}},

where the constants for the coarse equality
\asymp
depend only on
K_{2},\mathfrak{S}
.

Proof.

Since the right-hand sum (RHS) is smaller than the left-hand sum (LHS), it suffices to coarsely bound the LHS by the RHS. Note that each side is finite by Corollary
2.8
to the Passing-up Lemma
2.7
.

Denote the domains determining terms on the LHS but not the RHS by
\mathcal{V}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{1}}(x,y)-\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(x,y)
, i.e
d_{V}(x,y)<K_{2}
if
V\in\mathcal{V}
.

Then Proposition
4.14
says that up to ignoring all but
P(K_{2})
-many domains in
\mathcal{V}
, for each
V\in\mathcal{V}
there exists some domain
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(x,y)
determining a term on the RHS with
V\sqsubset W
. Moreover, it says that the number of domains
V\in\mathcal{V}
for which each such
W
accounts in this way satisfies
\#\mathcal{W}\leq A\cdot d_{W}(x,y)+A
, for some
A=A(K_{2},\mathfrak{S})>0
.

Hence, we have the desired conclusion, namely that

\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{1}}\leq A\cdot\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{2}}+A+P(K_{2}).

∎

5.
Coralling constants and standing assumptions

In this section, we briefly pause to set up some constants and standing assumptions which will be used throughout the rest of the paper.

5.1.
The constant
E

There are many constants which appear in the various basic statements associated to an HHS, and it is convenient to choose one which rules them all. We set this constant to be
E
, and we choose
E>L,M_{1},\theta_{L}
, where:

•

L=L(\mathcal{X})>0
is the hierarchy path constant from Lemma
3.6
.

•

M_{1}=M_{1}(\mathcal{X},L)>0
is the constant from Lemma
3.12
.

•

\theta_{L}=\theta_{L}(\mathcal{X},L)>0
is the constant so that all points
x\in\mathcal{X}
, boundary points in
\partial\mathcal{X}
, and
L
-hierarchy rays are
\theta_{L}
-consistent, as in Definition
3.16
(via Proposition
3.18
).

. Also, fixing
K
to be as large as necessary.

5.2.
The largeness threshold
K
and the set
\mathcal{U}

For the rest of the paper, we fix a constant
K=K(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
, whose size will be determined at various points along the way. Importantly, we will always be able to choose
K
to be larger than any of the other constants in the construction.

We set
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)=\{U\in\mathfrak{S}|\mathrm{diam}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)>K\}
. This will be the main set of domains of focus in the rest of the paper, and our task in this section is to reduce our analysis to studying the Gromov modelings trees of
F\cup\Lambda
in this set of domains.

6.
Hulls of rays: reducing to products of trees

In this section, we introduce the notion of a hierarchical hull for a finite collection of hierarchy rays, and make a first reduction of our analysis of these hulls to a coarse subset of the product of the associated Gromov modeling trees coming from each relevant domain.

This quasi-hierarchical setup replaces the hulls
H_{U}
of each
\pi_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
with Gromov modeling trees
T_{U}
, along with appropriate notions of projections and relative projections. The main technical statements are Lemma
6.13
, which encodes the important HHS features, like the BGIA
7
into this setup, and Lemma
6.16
, which says that the image of
H
and
\Lambda
into this product of the
T_{U}
satisfies a notion of consistency.

6.1.
Gromov trees associated to the hull of rays

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
be an HHS. Fix
F\subset\mathcal{X}
and
\Lambda\subset\mathcal{X}^{ray}
, so that if
\lambda\in\Lambda
is an
L
-hierarchy ray, then
\lambda(0)\in F
.

The first step toward understanding the hierarchical hull of
F\cup\Lambda
in
\mathcal{X}
is understanding the hyperbolic hull of
\pi_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
.

Gromov
[
Gro87
]
proved that the hyperbolic hull of a finite set of points in a hyperbolic space, i.e. the union of all geodesics between the points, is quasi-isometric to a tree. We need a generalization of this that allows for points at infinity.

Definition 6.1
.

If
X
is
\delta
-hyperbolic with
F\subset X
and
\Lambda\subset\partial X
both finite, then we let
\mathrm{hull}_{X}(F\cup\Lambda)
denote the union of the following:

•

all geodesics between pairs of points in
F
,

•

all
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic rays between points in
F
and points in
\Lambda
,

•

all bi-infinite
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesics between points in
\Lambda
.

The following is an exercise in
\delta
-hyperbolic geometry whose proof we sketch for the reader’s convenience:

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
X
be
\delta
-hyperbolic, with
F\subset X
and
\Lambda\subset\partial X
both finite. Then there exists a tree
T
and a
(1,C)
-quasi-isometric embedding
\phi:T\to X
which is within Hausdorff distance
C
of
\mathrm{hull}_{X}(F\cup\Lambda)
, where
C=C(\delta,|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
.

Proof.

The idea is to start with the hyperbolic hull of
F
and then to add each point in
\Lambda
, one at a time. In particular, each
\lambda\in\Lambda
closest point projects to a bounded diameter set
\pi_{H_{F}}(\lambda)
on
H_{F}=\mathrm{hull}_{X}(F)
, since
H_{F}
is uniformly quasiconvex. Moreover, any geodesic ray representing
\lambda
which is based at point in
F
must pass through a bounded neighborhood of
\pi_{H_{F}}(\lambda)
, with the bound depending on
|F|
and
\delta
. This allows us to choose a basepoint on the standard Gromov tree
T_{F}
for
H_{F}
and a representative ray for
\lambda
based on
T
near
\pi_{H_{F}}(\lambda)
, thereby extending
T_{F}
to a new tree
T
representing
\mathrm{hull}_{X}(F\cup\{\lambda\})
. One can repeat this process for each ray in
\Lambda
, with the bounds increasing at each step. The details are a straightforward exercise in
\delta
-hyperbolic geometry and we leave them to the reader.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

In what follows, we will only need that the map
\phi:T\to X
is a
(C,C)
-quasi-isometric embedding for
C=C(\delta,|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
, so that is what we will assume. In fact, this assumption is necessary for some applications, since the stable trees from
[
DMS20
]
are not almost isometrically embedded.

We refer to any tree
\phi:T\to X
satisfying the conclusions of Lemma
6.2
as a
Gromov modeling tree
for
\mathrm{hull}_{X}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

6.2.
Hierarchical hulls with their retracts and induced HHS structures

The notion of a
hierarchical hull
for a finite set of interior points in the mapping class group was introduced in
[
BKMM12
]
(as
\Sigma
-hulls), was extended to Teichmüller space in
[
EMR17
]
, and then fully realized in the HHS setting in
[
BHS19
]
. The definition we will give extends this to finite sets of interior points, hierarchy rays, and boundary points, but first we fix some notation.

Hierarchical hulls are built out of consistent tuples of points whose coordinates are contained in the hyperbolic hulls in each domain. More specifically, for each,
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, we set
H_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{U}(\pi_{U}(F\cup\Lambda))
.

Definition 6.4

(Hierarchical hull)
.

Given a constant
\theta>0
, we define the
\theta
-hull
of
F\cup\Lambda
to be all
x\in\mathcal{X}
so that
d_{U}(x,H_{U})<\theta
for all
U\in\mathfrak{S}
.

•

We denote the
\theta
-hull of
F\cup\Lambda
by
H_{\theta}=\mathrm{hull}_{\theta}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

As with the hyperbolic hull in a hyperbolic space, hierarchical hulls are hierarchically quasi-convex, in the sense of Definition
2.11
. The following is a definition chase, of which we provide a sketch:

Lemma 6.5
.

For any
\theta>0
, there exists
k:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
depending only on
\mathfrak{S},\theta
so that
H_{\theta}
is
k
-hierarchically quasiconvex.

Proof.

The first property is an easy consequence of the definitions and Lemma
3.12
. For the second, observe that if
\theta^{\prime}>\theta
, then
\mathrm{hull}_{\theta}(F\cup\Lambda)\subset\mathrm{hull}_{\theta^{\prime}}(F\cup\Lambda)
, and in fact they are in bounded Hausdorff distance of each other depending only on
\theta,\theta^{\prime},\mathfrak{S}
by the Distance Formula
2.9
. Hence using the Realization Theorem
2.4
, we can realize any tuple in the second condition, which then must be contained in
\mathrm{hull}_{\theta^{\prime}}(F\cup\Lambda)
for
\theta^{\prime}
depending only on
\theta,\mathfrak{S}
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Thus
H_{\theta}
admits a gate map
\mathfrak{g}_{H_{\theta}}:\mathcal{X}\to H_{\theta}
via Lemma
2.12
, and
[
BHS19
, Proposition 6.3]
provides says that this map is a coarse Lipschitz retract:

Lemma 6.6
.

For any
\theta>0
, the gate map
\mathfrak{g}_{H_{\theta}}:\mathcal{X}\to H_{\theta}
is a
(J,J)
-coarse Lipschitz retract, where
J=J(L,\theta,\mathfrak{S})>0
.

Remark 6.7
.

In our proof of the distance formula, we use the existence of such a retract to the hull of two interior points to construct the map from the cubical model of the hull back to the hull (Section
9
), which in turn is necessary to prove that combinatorial geodesics in the model are sent to hierarchy paths in the hull (Section
14
). However, the existence of such a retraction does not require the distance formula, see
[
BHS19
, Lemmas 4.6 and 4.15]
.

Finally, since each hull
H
is hierarchically quasi-convex, it has an induced HHS structure as in Lemma
2.13
, which we spell out in more detail here:

Lemma 6.8
.

For any sufficiently large
K=K(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
, the hierarchical hull
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(F\cup\Lambda)
admits an HHS structure with the following data:

•

The index set is
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

•

The sets of hyperbolic spaces is
\{\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)|U\in\mathcal{U}\}
.

•

All relations, projections, and relative projections are those coming from the ambient structure
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
, where the projections involve a further composition with the retraction
r_{U}:\mathcal{C}(U)\to\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
provided by quasiconvexity of
\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
in
\mathcal{C}(U)
.

This structure will come up in two places: when we discuss the coarse median structure of
H
in Section
15
, and when we analyze the HHS boundary of
H
in Subsection
16.4
.

Since the hull
H
is actually a coarsely-defined set made of consistent tuples over the index set
\mathfrak{S}
, it is natural to consider
H
with its induced structure as a set of consistent tuples over the index set
\mathcal{U}
. Each tuple of the latter type is only a partial tuple of the formal type, but we can complete such a partial tuple
(x_{U})_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
by setting
x_{V}=\pi_{V}(f)
for some fixed
f\in F
. This defines a map:

I_{H}:\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)\to\prod_{V\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(V).

The following lemma says that this map defines a quasi-isometric embedding
H\to\mathcal{X}
. The proof is a straight-forward exercise in the definitions, Realization
2.4
, and the Distance Formula
2.9
, which we leave to the reader:

Lemma 6.9
.

For any sufficiently large
K=K(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda)>0
and
\theta>0
, there exists
\theta_{K}=\theta_{K}(K,\theta,\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that if
(x_{U})_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
is a
\theta
-consistent tuple in the induced HHS structure on
H
, then
I_{H}((x_{U})_{U\in\mathcal{U}})
is a
\theta_{K}
-consistent tuple in
\mathcal{X}
. Moreover, this identification is a
(\theta_{K},\theta_{K})
-quasi-isometric embedding.

6.3.
Marked points on modeling trees

We briefly want to highlight a slightly complicating observation.

In what follows, the projections of the points of
F
and
\Lambda
will play an unsurprisingly pivotal role in our arguments. And while often one thinks of the points being modeled by a Gromov modeling tree
T
in a hyperbolic space
X
as being leaves of
T
—either abstractly or in
X
—this need not be the case.

For instance, if one chooses
F
to be three points which lie on a geodesic in
X
, then a perfectly fine modeling tree is the (sub-)geodesic between the outer-two most points, and so the middle point can be arbitrarily far from any leaf, both in the modeling tree
T
and its image in
X
.

This motivates the following terminology:

Definition 6.10

(Marked points on trees)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an HHS,
F\subset\mathcal{X}
a finite set of internal points, and
\Lambda
a finite set of
L
-hierarchy rays, with
F\neq\emptyset
. Let
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, and let
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
any
(C,C)
-approximating model tree for
\pi_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

•

Each element of
F\cup\Lambda
determines either an internal point on
T_{U}
or a boundary point of
\partial T_{U}
. When
x\in F\cup\Lambda
determines an internal point, we call it a
marked point
. Otherwise, we call it a
ray
for convenience.

Note that if
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then
\lambda_{U}\in T_{U}
is a marked point. In the end, the fact that marked points need not be leaves of our modeling trees
T_{U}
will not play a significant role, but it does complicate our arguments in a few places.

Remark 6.11

(Geodesics to and between rays in
T_{U}
)
.

Another point to note is that if
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, and
x\in T_{U}
is another point, then the “geodesic between”
x
and
\lambda
is actually the unique geodesic ray based at
x
in the equivalence class of
\lambda_{U}
in the Gromov boundary
\partial T_{U}
. Similarly, if
\lambda_{U},\lambda^{\prime}_{U}\in\Lambda
satisfy
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\cap\mathrm{supp}(\lambda^{\prime})
, then the “geodesic between”
\lambda_{U},\lambda^{\prime}_{U}
will refer to the unique bi-infinite geodesic ray in
T_{U}
whose ends are in the equivalences classes of
\lambda_{U},\lambda^{\prime}_{U}
in
\partial T_{U}
. Notably, in the above cases we have
d_{T_{U}}(x,\lambda_{U})=\infty
and
d_{T_{U}}(\lambda_{U},\lambda^{\prime}_{U})=\infty
. In what follows, we will not make a fine point of this.

6.4.
Tree projections and shadows

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, let
H_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(F\cup\Lambda)
. Let
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
be the
(C,C)
-quasi-isometric embedding with
d_{Haus}(\phi_{U}(T_{U}),H_{U})<C
for
C=C(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
provided by Lemma
6.2
, and let
\phi_{U}^{-1}
be its quasi-inverse. Let
p_{U}:\mathcal{C}(U)\to T_{U}
be coarse closest-point projection to
T_{U}
. Set
\beta_{U}=p_{U}\circ\pi_{U}:\mathcal{X}\to T_{U}
.

We begin by using the hierarchical projections between the
\mathcal{C}(U)
to define analogous projections between the trees
T_{U}
:

Definition 6.12

(Tree projections)
.

For
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
with
U,V
not orthogonal, we define
tree projections

\delta^{U}_{V}
and
\delta^{V}_{U}
as follows:

(1)

If
U\pitchfork V
, then we set
\delta^{U}_{V}=\mathrm{hull}_{T_{V}}(\phi_{V}^{-1}\circ p_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V}))\subset T_{V}
, and define
\delta^{V}_{U}
similarly.

(2)

If
U\sqsubset V
, then

•

We define
\delta^{U}_{V}=\mathrm{hull}_{T_{V}}(\phi_{V}^{-1}\circ p_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V}))\subset T_{V}
.

•

We define a map
\delta^{V}_{U}:T_{U}\to T_{V}
by
\delta^{V}_{U}(x)=\phi_{V}^{-1}\circ p_{V}\circ\rho^{U}_{V}\circ\phi_{U}(x)
for any
x\in T_{U}
.

•

When
U\sqsubset V
, we call
\delta^{U}_{V}\subset T_{V}
the
shadow
of
U
on
T_{V}
.

Figure 6
.
When
U\sqsubset V
, then
\rho^{U}_{V}
(in orange) is close to the image of
\phi_{V}:T_{V}\to\mathcal{C}(V)
, and hence
p_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V})
(orange) defines a bounded diameter subset of
\phi_{V}(T_{V})
, possibly with multiple components. Pulling this back gives bounded diameter subsets
\phi^{-1}_{V}(p_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V}))
of
T_{V}
(orange). Their convex hull of these is the
shadow

\delta^{U}_{V}
of
U
on
T_{V}
(the union of the pink and orange), which is then a bounded diameter subtree of
T_{V}
.

This following is a basic lemma which allows us to control the tree projections like the projections in an HHS:

Lemma 6.13
.

There exist
R=R(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
and
E^{\prime}=E^{\prime}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that the following hold:

(1)

If
U\pitchfork V\in\mathcal{U}
, then both
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset T_{U}
and
\delta^{U}_{V}\subset T_{V}
are sets of diameter at most
R
which are within
R
of some marked point in
F\cup\Lambda
of
T_{U},T_{V}
, respectively. Moreover,
d_{U}(\phi_{U}(\delta^{V}_{U}),\rho^{V}_{U})<R
, and similarly for
\delta^{U}_{V}
.

(2)

If
U\sqsubset V\in\mathcal{U}
, then
\delta^{U}_{V}\subset T_{V}
has diameter bounded by
R
. Moreover,
d_{V}(\phi_{V}(\delta^{U}_{V}),\rho^{U}_{V})<R
.

(3)

If
U,V\sqsubset W\in\mathcal{U}
with
U,V
not transverse, then
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{W}}(\delta^{U}_{W}\cup\delta^{V}_{W})<R
.

(4)

If
U\pitchfork V
and
W\sqsubset V
and
W,U
not orthogonal, then
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U}\cup\delta^{W}_{U})<R
.

(5)

(Bounded geodesic image) If
U\sqsubset V\in\mathcal{U}
, then any component
C\subset T_{V}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{U}_{V})
satisfies
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U}(C))<E^{\prime}
.

(6)

If
U,V,W\in\mathcal{U}
with
V,W\sqsubset U
,
V\pitchfork W
, and
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},\delta^{W}_{U})>10E^{\prime}
, then
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V}(\delta^{W}_{U}),\delta^{W}_{V})<E^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Item (1): By choosing
K=K(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
sufficiently large and the fact that
|F\cup\Lambda|>1
, for at least one
f\in F\cup\Lambda
, we have
d_{U}(f,\rho^{V}_{U})>E
. Hence
d_{V}(f,\rho^{U}_{V})<E
by the
\pitchfork
-consistency Axiom
4
or Proposition
3.18
when
f\in\Lambda
, and so
f\in T_{U}
and
\phi_{U}^{-1}\circ p_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U})
are within
R=R(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
, as required. A similar argument for
\phi_{V}^{-1}\circ p_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V})
completes the proof.

Item (2): This follows immediately from the observations in item (1), plus the fact that
\phi_{U}
and
\phi_{V}
are
(C,C)
-quasi-isometries with
C=C(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
.

Items (3) and (4) are straight-forward applications of items (1) and (2), plus the facts that the analogous bounds hold for their
\rho
-versions.

Item (5): The fact that
\phi_{U}
is a
(C,C)
-quasi-isometry and the Morse lemma imply that there exists an
E^{\prime}=E^{\prime}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that if
\gamma\subset T_{V}
is a geodesic segment which avoids the
E^{\prime}
-neighborhood of
\delta^{U}_{V}
, then
\phi_{U}(\gamma)
is a
(C,C)
-quasi-geodesic whose geodesic straightening avoids the
E
-neighborhood of
\rho^{U}_{V}
. The BGIA
7
then implies that
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\phi_{U}(\gamma))<E
, and hence
\delta^{V}_{U}(\gamma)=\phi^{-1}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\phi_{U}(\gamma))
has diameter bounded solely in terms of the quasi-constants of
\phi^{-1}_{U}
, and hence in terms of
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

Item (6): This is an easy consequence of Lemma
2.17
and the other items in this lemma. We leave the proof for the reader.

This completes the proof.

Figure 7
.
A cartoon of the BGI property for the tree projections, which constrains the map
\delta^{V}_{U}:T_{V}\to T_{U}
when
U\sqsubset V
. Choosing
x,y
(green) sufficiently far from
\delta^{V}_{U}
in
T_{V}
means that any straightening
\gamma^{\prime}
(pink) of
\phi_{V}(\gamma)
(green) in
\mathcal{C}(V)
will avoid the
E
-neighborhood of
\rho^{U}_{V}
(red).

∎

Remark 6.14
.

While the largeness constant
K
does appear in item (1) of the above lemma, it’s role is simply forcing the existence of the point
f\in F\cup\Lambda
. Any sufficiently large
K
will do, and so our ability to independently increase
K
in what follows is not affected.

6.5.
Extended tree consistency

One of our ultimate goals is to think of points in the hierarchical hull of finites sets of interior points and hierarchy rays as
0
-consistent tuples in some product of trees. Our first reduction toward that is replacing points in the hull (including at infinity) with points in the product of associated trees
T_{U}
. Toward that end, we need the following notion of (extended) consistency for points in
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}\cup\partial T_{U}
, which is a direct analogue of Definition
2.2
and Definition
3.16
:

Definition 6.15

(Extended tree consistency)
.

Given
\alpha\geq 0
, we say that a tuple
(x_{U})\in\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}\cup\partial T_{U}
is
\alpha
-consistent
if the following hold:

(1)

If
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
and
U\pitchfork V
, then we have

\min\{d_{U}(x_{U},\delta^{V}_{U}),d_{V}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})\}\leq\alpha.

(2)

If
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
and
U\sqsubset V
, then we have

\min\{d_{V}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V}),\mathrm{diam}_{U}(x_{U}\cup(\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}))\}\leq\alpha.

Given some
\alpha\geq 0
, we set

•

\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
to be the set of
\alpha
-consistent tuples in
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}
.

•

\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{\alpha}
to be the set of extended
\alpha
-consistent tuples in
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}\cup\partial T_{U}
.

•

Set
\mathcal{Z}^{\infty}=\overline{\mathcal{Z}}-\mathcal{Z}
, namely the set of tuples for which at least one component .

There is a map
\Psi:H\to\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}
given by the product of the compositions
\psi_{U}:=\phi^{-1}_{U}\circ p_{U}\circ\pi_{U}:H\to T_{U}
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, which associates a tuple of points in
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}
to any point in
H
. More generally, given an
L
-hierarchy ray
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we can associate a tuple
(\lambda_{U})\in\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}\cup\partial T_{U}
as follows:

•

If
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then
\lambda_{U}
corresponds to the geodesic ray in
\partial T_{U}
in the class of the quasi-geodesic ray
\psi_{U}(\lambda)
.

•

If
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then we take
\lambda_{U}=\psi_{U}(\lambda)
.

First, we observe that
\Psi(H)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
and
\Psi(\Lambda)\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\infty}_{\alpha}
for an appropriate choice of
\alpha
:

Lemma 6.16
.

There exists
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
such that for any
x\in H
, we have
\Psi(x)\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{0}}
, and any
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we have
\Psi(\lambda)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\infty}_{\alpha_{0}}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in H
. The corresponding tuple
(\pi_{U}(x_{U}))
is
E
-consistent by the Consistency Axioms
4
and our choice of
E
in Section
5
. Moreover, by definition of
H=H_{\theta}(F\cup\Lambda)
, we have
d_{U}(\pi_{U}(x_{U}),H_{U})<\theta=\theta(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)
, and hence
d_{U}(\pi_{U}(x_{U}),\phi_{U}(T_{U}))<\theta^{\prime}
for some
\theta^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
. A similar conclusion holds when replacing
x
with
\lambda\in\Lambda
by Proposition
3.18
, where the proximity of
\pi_{U}(\lambda)
for
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
is guaranteed by the fact that we have defined
H_{U}
to be the hull of
\pi_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
. At this point, a straight-forward argument using Lemma
6.13
gives the required consistency constant
\alpha_{0}
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

We also want to be able to move in the opposite direction.

The family of maps
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
for
U\in\mathcal{U}
combine into a component-wise map
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}\to\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{C}(U)
. We can extend this to a map
\Phi:\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}\to\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(U)
by choosing a point
f\in F\neq\emptyset
, setting
T_{V}=\{f\}
for each
V\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathcal{U}
, and defining
\phi_{V}:T_{V}\to\mathcal{C}(V)
by
\phi_{V}(f)=\pi_{V}(f)
. The idea here is that
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(V)}(F\cup\Lambda)<K
if
V\notin\mathcal{U}
.

The following proposition says that the tuples in
\Phi(\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha})
are once again consistent. It is a simple application of Lemma
6.13
and the definitions. In the next subsection, we will use this fact to extend
\Phi
to a map to
H
.

Proposition 6.17
.

For any
\alpha\geq 0
, there exists
\beta=\beta(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,K,\alpha)>0
so that if
x=(x_{U})\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
, then
\Phi(x)
is
\beta
-consistent.

Proof.

Observe that if
V\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathcal{U}
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
with either
U\pitchfork V
or
U\sqsubset V
, then
\delta^{U}_{V}\cap\mathcal{N}_{A}(f_{V})
for some
A=A(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,K)>0
and any
f\in F
. Hence, by choosing
f\in F
and setting
x_{V}=f_{V}
for each
V\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathcal{U}
, we need only concern ourselves with the domains in
\mathcal{U}
. Notably, for the domains in
\mathcal{U}
, we will get a consistency constant independent of
K
.

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, let
x^{\prime}_{U}=\phi_{U}(x_{U})
. There are two main cases to consider:

U\pitchfork V
: Without loss of generality,
\alpha
-consistency of
(x_{U})
says that
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\delta^{V}_{U})<\alpha
. Item (1) of Lemma
6.13
says that
d_{U}(\phi_{U}(\delta^{V}_{U}),\rho^{V}_{U})<R
, while
\phi_{U}
is a
(C,C)
-quasi-isometric embedding, and so
d_{U}(\phi_{U}(x_{U}),\rho^{V}_{U})<R+C\alpha+C
, which depends only on
\mathfrak{S}
,
|F\cup\Lambda|
, and
\alpha
, as required.

U\sqsubset V
: By
\alpha
-consistency, we have
\min\{d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{V}_{U}),\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(x_{U}\cup\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}))\}<\alpha
. The case where
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{V}_{U})<\alpha
follows similarly from Item (2) of Lemma
6.13
as argued above. The remaining case is when
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(x_{U}\cup\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}))<\alpha
and
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{V}_{U})\geq\alpha
.

For this, observe that
\phi_{U}\circ\delta^{V}_{U}=\phi_{U}\circ\phi^{-1}_{U}\circ\rho^{V}_{U}
, where
\phi_{U}
and
\phi^{-1}_{U}
are
C
-coarse inverses. Since
\phi_{U}
is a
(C,C)
-quasi-isometric embedding, it follows that
\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\phi_{U}(x_{U})\cup\phi_{U}(\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V})))<C\alpha+C
. Hence,
\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\phi_{U}()\cup\rho^{V}_{U}(\phi_{V}(x_{V})))<C\alpha+2C
. This completes the proof.

∎

7.
Collapsed trees and the model space
\mathcal{Q}

In this section, we will develop the basic structure of our cubical models. Our first goal is to explain how to modify the collection of trees
\{T_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
from the last section by collapsing certain subtrees arising from the shadows of nested large domains. The idea is that these shadows encode redundant information on the trees, and so collapsing them removes this redundancy. In particular, removing these redundancies removes the coarseness from relative projections and consistency, giving us an exact model for the hull.

The main technical statements in this section are Lemma
7.14
, which is a collapsed version of Lemma
6.13
and explains how the hierarchical structure is encoded in these collapsed trees, as well as Lemma
7.17
, which explains how the structural information provided by Strong Passing-up
4.7
is encoded in the collapsed data.

7.1.
Abstracting the tree reduction setup

For this section, we let
F\cup\Lambda
and
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
be as in Section
6
. In what follows, it will only be necessary that the rest of the setup satisfies the main outputs of the last section, namely Lemma
6.13
, Lemma
6.16
, and Proposition
6.17
. For this, we only need the following slightly more general setup which is useful for applications, see in particular Subsection
7.3
below:

Definition 7.1

(Reduced tree system)
.

A
reduced tree system
for
F\cup\Lambda
and
\mathcal{U}
is the following set of data:

(1)

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have a tree
T_{U}
and a
C
-quasi-median
(C,C)
-quasi-isometric embedding
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
so that
d^{Haus}_{U}(\phi_{U}(T_{U}),H_{U})<C
.

(2)

For each
a\in F\cup\Lambda
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
, there exists a marked point or ray
a_{U}\in T_{U}\cup\partial T_{U}
with
\phi_{U}(a_{U})=\pi_{U}(a)\in\mathcal{C}(U)\cup\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
.

(3)

There exists
R=R(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
and for any
V,U\in\mathcal{U}
with
V\sqsubset U
or
V\pitchfork U
, there exists
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset T_{U}
so that items (1)–(4) and (6) of Lemma
6.13
hold.

(4)

For any
V,U\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\sqsubset V
, the composition
\rho^{V}_{U}=\phi_{U}^{-1}\circ\rho^{V}_{U}\circ p_{V}\circ\phi_{V}:T_{V}\to T_{U}
satisfies item (5) of Lemma
6.13
.

See Definition
15.6
below for the definition of quasi-median, which we include here for future applications of this setup; see also Lemma
15.7
.

In particular, we have the following:

Proposition 7.2
.

Any family of Gromov modeling trees
\{T_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
for
F\cup\Lambda
can be converted into a reduced tree system which satisfies the conclusions of Lemma
6.16
and Proposition
6.17
.

7.2.
Clusters and the tree decomposition

In what follows, we let
F,\Lambda
be finite with
F\neq\emptyset
, and
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
be the set of relevant domains for
F\cup\Lambda
with
K
as in Subsection
5.2
. Fix a reduced tree system for this setup as defined in Definition
7.1
, for which we can freely assume the relevant statements from Section
6
by Proposition
7.2
. We will now proceed to explain how to cluster the
\delta
-set data as our first step for producing our collapsed trees.

Let
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
. In Lemma
6.13
, we saw that when
V\sqsubset U
or
V\pitchfork U
, that the shadow
\delta^{V}_{U}
of
\rho^{V}_{U}
on
T_{U}
has diameter bounded by
R=R(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda)>0
. Moreover, in Definition
6.12
, we constructed these shadows to be subtrees of
T_{U}
.

We now fix
r=r(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
, called the
cluster separation constant
, to be determined later (see Lemma
7.14
). In particular, our threshold constant
K
can be chosen independently of
r
.

Now let
T^{c}_{U}
to be the union of

(1)

the
2R
-neighborhoods in
T_{U}
of the shadows
\delta^{V}_{U}
of the
V\in\mathfrak{S}
for
V\sqsubset U
,

(2)

the
2R
-neighborhoods in
T_{U}
of the marked points of
T_{U}
(i.e., the points of
F
and
\Lambda
which are not rays, Definition
6.10
), and

(3)

The convex hull of any collection of sets from (1) and (2) which are within distance
r
of each other in
T_{U}
.

Remark 7.3
.

The reason for the choice of
2R
above becomes apparent in the proof of Proposition
9.1
below, in which we build a map from our model
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
(Definition
7.9
below), back into
\mathcal{Y}
.

We call the components of
T^{c}_{U}
from item (3)
clusters
1
1

1

We borrow the term “cluster” from
[
DMS20
]
, though with a slightly different usage.
. Our flexibility in choosing
r
says that we can force clusters to be as far apart as we need. This is important in Theorem
8.1
below, in which we compare the distance in our model to the Distance Formula
2.9
sum in the hull
H
.

Set
T^{e}_{U}=T_{U}-T^{c}_{U}
. We call the components of
T^{e}_{U}

edge components
. Notice that since each component of
T^{c}_{U}
is convex by construction, we have that
T^{c}_{U}
and
T^{e}_{U}
are both disjoint unions of subtrees of
T_{U}
.

Remark 7.4

(Cluster philosophy)
.

Roughly speaking,the components of
T^{c}_{U}
encode parts of
T_{U}
which are already encoded in other domains not orthogonal to
U
, while
T^{e}_{U}
encodes the parts of
T_{U}
which are unique to
U
. That is,
T^{c}_{U}
contains redundant information, and
T^{e}_{U}
contains information unique to
U
. This is the same principle for the subdivision points in the original cubulation construction from
[
BHS21
, Section 2.1]
, as discussed in Subsections
1.2
and
1.3
of the Introduction
1
.

7.3.
A reduced tree system for stable trees

The stable tree construction from
[
DMS20
, Theorem 3.1]
produces trees with exactly this sort of decomposition
T_{U}=T^{c}_{U}\cup T^{e}_{U}
, where the relative projection data for nested domains is encoded in the components of
T^{c}_{U}
. More specifically, there is a uniform
C
-quasi-median
(C,C)
-quasi-isometric embedding
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
satisfying

•

For each component
D\subset T^{c}_{U}
, there is an associated collection of domains
\mathcal{U}_{U}(D)\subset\mathcal{U}
so that

•

For each
V\in\mathcal{U}
with
V\sqsubset U
, there exists a unique component
D_{V}\subset T^{c}_{U}
so that
V\in\mathcal{U}_{U}(D_{V})
,

•

V\sqsubset U
for each
V\in\mathcal{U}_{U}(D)
, and

•

d^{Haus}_{U}(\phi_{U}(D),\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{U}_{U}(D)}\rho^{V}_{U})<C
.

As such, given
V\sqsubset U\in\mathcal{U}
, we can define
\delta^{V}_{U}=\phi^{-1}_{U}(p_{D_{V}}(\rho^{V}_{U}))
, and this family of
\delta
-sets, along with the
\delta
-sets and -maps for
V\pitchfork U
and
U\sqsubset V
will satisfy Definition
7.1
. Moreover,
[
DMS20
, Theorem 3.1]
also provides a cluster separation constant
r=r(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that the components of
T_{U}=T^{c}_{U}\cup T^{e}_{U}
satisfying the properties discussed in Subsection
7.2
above.

We have made these observations to set ourselves up for future work, in which we will want to be able to plug in constructions like these stable trees into the cubulation machine we develop in this article. We will elaborate on how these different machines work together this in a forthcoming expository paper
[
Dur23
]
.

7.4.
Collapsed trees

Fixing the setup as before, in particular at the level of generality of a reduced tree system for our finite sets
F,\Lambda
, we now define our main trees of interest:

Definition 7.5

(Collapsed trees and rays)
.

Let
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
be the quotient map obtained by collapsing all components of
T^{c}_{U}
to points. Since each shadow is convex, the quotient space
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a tree, which we call the
collapsed tree
associated to
U
. The points in
F\cup\Lambda
determine
marked cluster points
or rays in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. In particular, when
\lambda\in\Lambda
with
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, then either

•

There exists a unique cluster
C_{\lambda_{U}}\subset T^{c}_{U}
containing a geodesic representative
\gamma_{U}
of
\lambda_{U}\in\partial T_{U}
, in which we set
q_{U}(\lambda_{U})\in\widehat{T}^{c}_{U}
to be equal to the cluster point
q_{U}(C_{\lambda_{U}})
.

•

Otherwise, any geodesic representative of
\lambda_{U}
projects to an unparameterized geodesic ray in
\widehat{T}_{U}
, and we set
q_{U}(\lambda_{U})\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
to be the class of that ray.

The following is clear from this discussion:

Lemma 7.6
.

The quotient map
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
preserves the decomposition
\widehat{T}_{U}=\widehat{T}^{c}_{U}\sqcup\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
, into clusters whose images in
\widehat{T}_{U}
are points, and edge components, on each of which
q_{U}
restricts to an isometry.

In what follows, this decomposition—especially the fact that all relative projections are now collapsed to points—will simplify many of our hierarchical arguments. See Figure
8
for a schematic.

Figure 8
.
The shadows
\delta^{V}_{U}
(orange) combine together with leaves (purple) to form clusters (green). This can involve collapsing nearby leaves to a point (for
d
and
e
), far away leaves to a point (for
a
and
b
), or even a ray covered with shadows to a point (as with
\lambda
). Other leaves (like
f
and
g
) are essentially unaffected.

7.5.
Relative projections for collapsed trees

We finish our initial discussion by defining new relative projections for our collapsed trees.

Consider the product space
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}
, as well as the extended product
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
. These spaces inherit a “quasi-hierarchical” structure, in the sense that the index set
\mathcal{U}
inherits the relations
\sqsubset,\pitchfork,\perp
, and we have natural projections as follows:

Definition 7.7

(Collapsed tree projections)
.

The projections and relative projections between
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
and the collapsed trees
\widehat{T}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
for
U\in\mathcal{U}
are as follows:

•

The domain projections are just the natural component-wise projections
\pi_{\widehat{T}_{U}}:\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\to\widehat{T}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
.

•

When
U\pitchfork V
, the relative projection from
\widehat{T}_{U}
to
\widehat{T}_{V}
is
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}=q_{V}(\delta^{U}_{V})\in\widehat{T}_{V}
.

•

When
U\sqsubset V
, the relative projection from
\widehat{T}_{U}
to
\widehat{T}_{V}
is
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}=q_{V}(\delta^{U}_{V})
.

•

Finally, when
U\sqsubset V
, the relative projection from
\widehat{T}_{V}
to
\widehat{T}_{U}
is
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=q_{U}\circ\delta^{V}_{U}\circ q^{-1}_{V}
.

Remark 7.8
.

The above structure is not a genuine hierarchical structure because it does not satisfy the Large Links Axiom
6
or, equivalently, the Passing-Up Lemma
2.7
. In some sense, that is the whole point of this construction, namely collapsing redundant information contained up the
\sqsubset
-lattice.

7.6.
0
-consistency and the model space
\mathcal{Q}

Since we have a set of relative projections for
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
, we now can define
0
-consistency:

Definition 7.9

(
0
-consistency and
\mathcal{Q}
)
.

A tuple
(\hat{x}_{U})\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
is
0
-
consistent
if we have

(1)

If
U\pitchfork V\in\mathcal{U}
, then either
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
or
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}_{V}
.

(2)

If
U\sqsubset V\in\mathcal{U}
, then either
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}_{V}
or
\hat{x}_{U}\in\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{x}_{V})\subset\widehat{T}_{U}
.

•

We set
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
to be the set of
0
-consistent tuples in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
.

•

(Canonicality) We let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
denote the set of tuples
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
which are also
canonical
in the sense that

–

There does not exist
U\in\mathcal{U}
so that
\hat{x}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
represents a ray, and

–

\#\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{a}_{U}\}<\infty
for all
a\in F
.

Remark 7.10
.

Canonicality is a finiteness condition, and plays a similar role to the canonical condition on sets of hyperplane orientations in a cube complex, hence the name. It is only necessary in the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
when
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
, since otherwise each
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a finite tree,
\mathcal{U}
is finite, and therefore
\mathcal{Q}=\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. Its purpose is to pick out “interior” points in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
, and it is strictly necessary because one can build a variety of examples of an HHS
\mathcal{X}
and a hierarchy ray
h\subset\mathcal{X}
whose projection to
\mathcal{C}(S)
(where
S
is
\sqsubset
-maximal) has infinite diameter, while the quasi-geodesic ray
\pi_{S}(h)
is coarsely covered by
\mathcal{V}=\{\rho^{U}_{S}|U\in\mathcal{S}-\{S\}\}
, with each
U\in\mathcal{V}
being
\sqsubset
-minimal. Consequently,
\widehat{T}_{S}
would be a point, but tuples representing the basepoint of
h
and
h
itself would both be
0
-consistent and yet differ on infinitely-many domains. See Subsection
1.7
for an explicit example, as well as
[
DZ22
, Proposition 7.21]
. See also
[
LLR18
,
BLMR20
,
CMW19
]
for explicit examples in Teichmüller space.

As a consequence of the canonical condition in Definition
7.9
, we get that the
\ell^{1}
-sum on
\mathcal{Q}

d_{\mathcal{Q}}(x,y)=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})

defines a metric on
\mathcal{Q}
, where
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\mathcal{Q}
. In particular, the canonical condition guarantees that the sum has finitely-many non-zero terms, which, in turn, guarantees that the triangle inquality holds.

7.7.
The map
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}

Our next goal is to prove a consistency result for points in
H\cup\Lambda
, which will complete the reduction of the coarseness of consistency to nothing. First, we need some notation and a lemma.

Recall from Definition
6.15
, when
\alpha>0
, then
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{\alpha}
denotes the set of
\alpha
-consistent tuples in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}\cup\partial T_{U}
. Moreover, in Lemma
6.16
, we saw that there exists
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that for any
x\in H\cup\Lambda
, we had that
\Psi(x)\in\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{\alpha_{0}}
. We can extend this to a map to

\widehat{\Psi}:H\cup\Lambda\to\overline{\mathcal{Y}}

by applying the quotient maps
q_{U}
coordinate-wise. In particular, for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have a map
\psi_{U}:\mathcal{X}\to T_{U}
given by
\psi_{U}=\phi^{-1}_{U}\circ p_{U}\circ\pi_{U}
. We can extend this to a map
\hat{\psi}_{U}:\mathcal{X}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
to the collapsed trees by composing with the quotient maps, namely
\hat{\psi}_{U}:\mathcal{X}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
, where
\hat{\psi}_{U}=q_{U}\circ\psi_{U}
.

For a point
x\in H\cup\Lambda
, we will write
\psi_{U}(x)=x_{U}
and
\hat{\psi}_{U}(x)=\hat{x}_{U}
. Thus when
x,y\in H\cup\Lambda
, we will write
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})
and
d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
instead of
d_{T_{U}}(\psi_{U}(x),\psi_{U}(y))
and
d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{\psi}_{U}(x),\hat{\psi}_{U}(y))
, respectively.

Before our consistency proof, we need to record some basic features of the construction. It will sometimes be useful to highlight coordinates
\hat{x}_{U}
of
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
which live in edge components of
\widehat{T}_{U}
. For a given
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, we denote the corresponding set of domains by
E(\hat{x})=\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{x}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}\}
.

Lemma 7.11
.

The following hold:

(1)

If
U\pitchfork V\in\mathcal{U}
, then both
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
and
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}_{V}
coincide with marked cluster points.

(2)

If
U\sqsubset V\in\mathcal{U}
, then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
coincides with a cluster point.

(3)

For any
U\in\mathcal{U}
and any
x,y\in H
, we have
d_{U}(x,y)\asymp d_{T_{U}}(x_{u},y_{U})\geq d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
, with the constants in
\asymp
depending only on
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

(4)

If
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, then
\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{a}_{U}\text{ for some }a\in F\cup\Lambda\}
is pairwise non-transverse, and hence has cardinality bounded in terms of
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

Proof.

Item (1) follows from item (2) of Lemma
6.13
. Item (2) follows directly from the construction. Item (3) is an immediate consequence of the definition of the hull
H
and the construction in this subsection.

Finally, for item (4): Suppose
U,V
are transverse and
\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{a}_{U}
for any
a\in F\cup\Lambda
. Since
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
coincides with a marked cluster point by item (1), it follows from
0
-consistency of
\hat{x}
that
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, and hence
\hat{x}_{V}
coincides with a marked cluster point again by item (1). This completes the proof.

∎

With this in hand, we can prove our consistency result:

Proposition 7.12
.

If
R>\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}
, then for any
(x_{U})\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\alpha_{0}}
, we have
(q_{U}(x_{U}))\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Proof.

Let
(x_{U})\in\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{\alpha_{0}}
. We will show that
(q_{U}(x_{U}))\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
is
0
-consistent (Definition
7.9
).

First suppose that
U\pitchfork V\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
(x_{V})
is
\alpha_{0}
-consistent by Lemma
6.16
, we have, without loss of generality, that
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\delta^{V}_{U})<\alpha_{0}<R
. Hence
q_{U}(x_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
.

Next suppose that
U\sqsubset V\in\mathcal{U}
. If
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<\alpha_{0}<R
, then
q_{V}(x_{V})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, and we are done. Otherwise, we have
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(x_{U}\cup\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}))<\alpha_{0}
while also
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})>\alpha_{0}
. By taking
\alpha_{0}>E^{\prime}
, we have that
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V})\cup f_{U})<E^{\prime}
for some
f\in F\cup\Lambda
by the BGI property in item (5) of Lemma
6.13
. In particular, both
x_{U}
and
\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V})
are within
\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}<R
of
f_{U}
, and hence
q_{U}(x_{U})=q_{V}(\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}))
. Finally, observe that
q^{-1}_{V}(q_{V}(x_{V}))
is contained in the component of
T_{V}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{U}_{V})
containing
x_{V}
, and hence
q_{V}(\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}))=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(q_{V}(x_{V}))
. This completes the proof.

∎

Remark 7.13
.

It is not clear, a priori, that the map
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is coarsely-dense. This will follow from Theorem
11.1
, in which we build a map
\widehat{\Phi}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
and show that
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(x,\widehat{\Phi}\circ\widehat{\Psi}(x))
is bounded independent of
x\in\mathcal{Q}
. Unsurprisingly, this will depend crucially on a version of coarse surjectivity for
\Psi:H\to\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{0}}
, which we prove first below in Proposition
11.4
.

7.8.
Collapsed tree control

We finish this section with the analogue of the Tree Control Lemma
6.13
for our collapsed trees, which we will cite many times. In order to make citations easier in the proofs that follow, we have made it an omnibus lemma by including some of the conclusions of Lemma
7.11
and Proposition
7.12
.

Lemma 7.14

(Controlling the collapsed trees)
.

The following hold:

(1)

For any
x\in H\cup\Lambda
, the tuple
(\hat{x}_{U})
is
0
-consistent.

(2)

If
U\pitchfork V\in\mathcal{U}
, then both
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
and
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}_{V}
coincide with marked cluster points.

(3)

If
U\sqsubset V\in\mathcal{U}
, then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
coincides with a cluster point.

(4)

For any
U\in\mathcal{U}
and any
x,y\in H
, we have
d_{U}(x,y)\asymp d_{T_{U}}(x_{u},y_{U})\geq d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
, with the constants in
\asymp
depending only on
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

(5)

There exists a cluster separation constant
r=r(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that if
x,y\in H
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
\hat{\psi}_{U}(x),\hat{\psi}_{U}(y)
lie in distinct components of
\widehat{T}^{c}_{U}
, then
d_{U}(x,y)>50E
.

(6)

If
U\in\mathcal{U}
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal and
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
have
d_{U}(a,b)>K
, then
d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U})\succ K
, with the constant in
\succ
depending only on
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

(7)

(Bounded geodesic image) Suppose
V\sqsubset U
,
C\subset\widehat{T}_{U}-\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
is a component, then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(C)
is a point. In particular, if
f\in F\cup\Lambda
with
\hat{f}_{U}
contained in
C
, then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(C)=\hat{f}_{V}.

(8)

If
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
, then
E(\hat{x})=\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{x}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}\}
is pairwise orthogonal.

(9)

If
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
, then
\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{a}_{U}\text{ for some }a\in F\cup\Lambda\}
is pairwise non-transverse, and hence has cardinality bounded in terms of
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

(10)

If
U,V,W\in\mathcal{U}
with
V,W\sqsubset U
,
V\pitchfork W
, and
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U})
, then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}
.

Proof.

Item (1) is a consequence of Proposition
7.12
, in addition to Lemma
6.16
, which together show that
\Psi(H\cup\Lambda)\subset\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{\alpha_{0}}
and hence
\widehat{\Psi}(F\cup\Lambda)\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Items (2)–(4) are items (1)–(3) of Lemma
7.11
.

For item (5), if
\omega_{U}(x),\omega_{U}(y)
are in distinct clusters, then
d_{T_{U}}(x,y)>r
, and hence
d_{U}(x,y)\succ r
by item (4). Hence choosing
r
large enough gives the desired lower bound.

For item (6): When
U\in\mathcal{U}
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal with respect to
\mathcal{U}
, then
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
only collapses the
2R
-neighborhoods of leaves in
T_{U}
. Hence if
d_{U}(a,b)>K
so that
d_{T_{U}}(a_{U},b_{U})\succ K
, then
\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U}
are only separated by at most
|F\cup\Lambda|-2
clusters, each of which has diameter at most
2R|F\cup\Lambda|
. The claim follows.

For item (7): By item (1), if
\hat{f}_{U}
is a marked point or ray in
C
, then
0
-consistency
(\hat{f}_{V})
implies that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{f}_{U})=\hat{f}_{V}
because
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U}(f_{U}),f_{V})<\alpha<R
. On the other hand, since
C\cap\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\emptyset
, we have
d_{T_{U}}(q^{-1}_{U}(C),\delta^{V}_{U})>R>E^{\prime}
. Hence item (5) of Lemma
6.13
says that
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V}(C))<E^{\prime}
, while also
\delta^{U}_{V}(f_{U})\subset\delta^{V}_{U}(C)
. Hence
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V}(C),f_{V})<\alpha+E^{\prime}<R
, where we guarantee the last inequality by increasing
R
as necessary. It follows then that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(C)=\hat{f}_{V}
, as claimed.

For item (8): Supposing that
U,V
are not orthogonal. When

U\pitchfork V
or
V\sqsubset U
and
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, we are done by items (1) and (3). When
V\sqsubset U
and
\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, item (7) implies there is some
f\in F\cup\Lambda
so that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{x}_{U})=\hat{f}_{V}
, while
0
-consistency says that
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{x}_{U})
, giving
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{x}_{U})\in\widehat{T}^{c}_{U}
.

Finally, item (9) is item (4) of Lemma
7.11
, while item (10) follows from item (6) of Lemma
6.13
.
∎

7.9.
(Non-)canonicality of
F
and
\Lambda

We pause to observe the following:

Lemma 7.15
.

We have
\widehat{\Psi}(H)\subset\mathcal{Q}
and
\widehat{\Psi}(\Lambda)\subset\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

We first show that
(\hat{x}_{U})
is canonical in the sense of Definition
7.9
when
x\in H
. Let
a\in F
. Since
x\in H
, it follows that
\hat{x}_{U}\not\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
for any
U
. So it suffices to prove that
\#\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{a}_{U}\neq\hat{x}_{U}\}<\infty
. We may assume that
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
, since this is trivial otherwise.

To see this, assume for a contradiction that
\#\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{a}_{U}\neq\hat{x}_{U}\}=\infty
. By item (4) of Lemma
7.11
,
\hat{x}_{U}
coincides with a marked cluster point for all but boundedly-many
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Hence, there exists an infinite subset
\mathcal{V}\subset\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{a}_{U}\neq\hat{x}_{U}\}
so that
\hat{x}_{V},\hat{a}_{V}
are at distinct cluster points in
\widehat{T}_{V}
, and hence
d_{V}(x,a)>E
for each
V\in\mathcal{V}
. Thus the Passing-up Lemma
2.7
provides that for any
N>0
, if
V_{1},\dots,V_{N}\in\mathcal{V}
are distinct, then there exists
W\in\mathcal{U}
with
d_{W}(x,a)>N
. However, the Distance Formula
2.9
provides that
\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}[d_{U}(a,x)]_{K}\asymp d_{\mathcal{X}}(a,x)<\infty
is finite, which is a contradiction, since the previous fact says that the sum should be infinite. This completes the proof that
(\hat{x}_{U})\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Finally, we prove that
(\hat{\lambda}_{U})\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
when
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Since we are done if
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
for some
U
, suppose not. Let
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, and since
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\notin\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
, it follows that
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
coincides with a cluster containing the collapsed relative projections
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
for infinitely-many domains
V\sqsubset U
. Moreover, we have that the corresponding tree relative projections
\delta^{V}_{U}
form an
r
-dense subset of any ray in
T_{U}
corresponding to
\lambda_{U}
. Thus we can choose an infinite collection of domains
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}
so that for each
V\in\mathcal{V}
we have

•

V\sqsubset U
,

•

V
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal,

•

\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}_{U}
, and

•

\delta^{V}_{U}
is at least
100K
-away from any branched point or marked point of
T_{U}
.

By construction, we have that
d_{T_{V}}(a_{V},b_{V})<E^{\prime}
for each
V\in\mathcal{V}
and any
b\in F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda\}
. Since
V\in\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
, it follows that there exists some
b\in F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda\}
so that
V\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(b,\lambda)
, and so we must have
d_{V}(a,\lambda)>K-E
. The proof of item (6) of Lemma
7.14
then implies that
d{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{a}_{V},\hat{\lambda}_{V})\succ K
, meaning
\hat{a}_{V}\neq\hat{\lambda}_{V}
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}
. Since
\mathcal{V}
is infinite by construction, we are done. This completes the proof.

∎

Remark 7.16
.

We invoked the Distance Formula
2.9
in the proof of Lemma
7.15
. This is only necessary when
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
, and hence does not affect our proof of the lower bound of the distance formula in Corollary
14.3
.

7.10.
Encoding passing-up

In this final subsection, we observe how Strong Passing-Up
4.7
is actually encoded into the collection of domains
\mathcal{U}
and their collapsed trees. The following structural lemma is necessary for studying
\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
, in particular as a cubical boundary. Its proof is actually an application of Lemma
4.9
.

Roughly, it says that any infinite collection of domains in
\mathcal{U}
can be converted into a collection of domains whose
\hat{\delta}
-sets (i.e., collapsed relative projections) run out a ray end of one of the trees.

Lemma 7.17
.

For any infinite collection
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}
, there exists an infinite subcollection of domains
\{V_{1},V_{2},\dots\}\subset\mathcal{V}
, elements
a\in F
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
, so that the following hold:

(1)

V_{j}\pitchfork V_{i}
for
i\neq j
;

(2)

For
j>i
, we have
\hat{\delta}^{V_{j}}_{V_{i}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}
and
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{j}}=\hat{a}_{V_{j}}
;

(3)

For
k<i
, we have
\hat{\delta}^{V_{k}}_{V_{i}}=\hat{a}_{V_{i}}
and
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{k}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{k}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}
be infinite. Up to passing to an infinite subsequence, we may assume that there are
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
with
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(a,b)
, and note that at least one of
a\in\Lambda
or
b\in\Lambda
, and possibly both.

By Lemma
4.9
, there exists an infinite subcollection
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
and a support domain
W\in\mathrm{supp}(a)\cup\mathrm{supp}(b)
so that
\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V}_{W})=\infty
. By the BGIA
7
, we have that
d_{W}(\rho^{V}_{W},[a,b])<E
, where
[a,b]
is any
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic between
a,b
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
. Up to passing to a further infinite subcollection of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
if necessary, we may also assume that
d_{W}(\rho^{V}_{W},\rho^{V^{\prime}}_{W})>100K
for any
V\neq V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. In particular, we may assume that
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
is pairwise transverse.

Without loss of generality, assume that
b=\lambda\in\Lambda
. We now explain how to reduce to the case where
a\in F
. To see this, observe that we can again pass to a further infinite subcollection of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
so that if
f\in F
, then
\rho^{V}_{W}
is at least
K
-far away from
f
and within
100E\delta
of any
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic ray between
c
and
\lambda
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
. As such, the BGIA
7
implies that
d_{V}(a,c)<E
for any
c\in F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda\}
, which implies that
V\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K-E}(c,\lambda)
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

Thus we may assume that
a\in F
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is countable (Lemma
4.13
), enumerate
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\{V_{1},V_{2},\dots\}
, where we are taking
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
to be the subcollection as refined above. Moreover, up to reordering, we may assume that the
\rho^{V_{i}}_{W}
run monotonically (with
i
) out the end of any
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic ray from
a
to
\lambda
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
, with, as above,
d_{W}(\rho^{V_{i}}_{W},\rho^{V_{j}}_{W})>100K
for each
i\neq j
.

Item (1) of the lemma is immediate from the above. Items (2) and (2) of the lemma are now straight-forward to verify using
0
-consistency of
a
and
\lambda
(Proposition
7.12
), and the fact that the
\rho^{V_{i}}_{W}
escape out toward
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{C}(W)
monotonically. This completes the proof.
∎

We will also later need the following lemma, in which
\mathrm{supp}^{\sqsubset}(\lambda)
denotes the set of domains in
\mathcal{U}
which nest into some domain in
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
.

Lemma 7.18
.

For any
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
a\in F
, we have
\#\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{\lambda}_{U}\neq\hat{a}_{U}\}-(\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\cup\mathrm{supp}^{\sqsubset}(\lambda))<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be the set in the statement. Then for any
V\in\mathcal{V}
, the fact that
\hat{\lambda}_{V}\neq\hat{a}_{V}
implies that
V\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(a,\lambda)
since our cluster separation constant satisfies
r\gg 50E
. Thus if
\#\mathcal{V}=\infty
, Lemma
4.9
provides
W\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
and an infinite subcollection
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
so that
V\sqsubset W
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, which contradicts the definition of
\mathcal{V}
. This proves the lemma.
∎

7.11.
Equivariance for
\mathcal{Q}

In this subsection, we briefly observe that the construction of our models can be done equivariantly with respect to HHS automorphisms (Subsection
2.7
). The basic observation is that the construction depends only on the fundamental parts of an HHS, which any automorphism preserves.

Corollary 7.19

(Equivariant models)
.

If
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
is an HHS automorphism, then one can construct a cubical model
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
for
g\cdot(F\cup\Lambda)
so that
g
induces an isometry
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}_{g}
, and moreover a bijection
\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

By Definition
2.18
,
g
preserves projection sizes (up to relabeling), and hence
g\cdot\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(g\cdot(F\cup\Lambda))
. Moreover, given
U\in\mathcal{U}
, any Gromov modeling tree
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
for
\pi_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
can be turned into a modeling tree
T_{gU}
for
\pi_{gU}(g\cdot(F\cup\Lambda))
in the obvious way, by composing
T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)\to\mathcal{C}(gU)
, with the last map the isometry
\mathcal{C}(U)\to\mathcal{C}(gU)
induced by
g
. Similarly, since
g
preserves the relative projections, one can construct cluster and the collapsed tree
\widehat{T}_{gU}
from
\widehat{T}_{U}
in a similar fashion. Hence
g
induces an isometry between
\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{gU}
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
which preserves labels of marked points and rays, in that
g\cdot\hat{a}_{U}=\widehat{g\cdot a}_{gU}
for each
a\in F\cup\Lambda
, while also preserving cluster points, in that
g\cdot\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{g\cdot V}_{g\cdot U}
.

As such,
g
induces a bijection between
0
-consistent sets
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\to\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{g}
, which preserves canonicality. We leave the details to the reader. This completes the proof.
∎

This proves part of the equivariance statement of Theorem
B
from the introduction. We will show in Corollary
16.30
that the map
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}_{g}
is a cubical isomorphism and induces a simplicial isomorphism between their simplicial boundaries.

8.
Distance estimates for
\mathcal{Q}

In this section, we prove the following theorem, which allows us to estimate distances in
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(F\cup\Lambda)
by distances in
\mathcal{Q}
. Notably, the sum on the left is the sum from the Distance Formula (Theorem
2.9
), though our proof is completely independent of it and, in fact, will be used to give the upper bound in the distance formula in Corollary
14.3
in the Section
14
.

In the following, for numbers
A,K
the notation
[A]_{K}
mean
0
when
A<K
and
A
when
A\geq K
. For
x\in H
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we will write

•
