x=\pi_{U}(x)\in\mathcal{C}(U)
;

•

x_{U}=\psi_{U}(x)\in T_{U}
;

•

\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\psi}_{U}(x)\in\widehat{T}_{U}
.

Theorem 8.1
.

There exists
K_{1}=K_{1}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that for any
K_{2}\geq K_{1}
and any
x,y\in H
, we have that

\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{2}}\asymp\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})

where the constants in
\asymp
only depend on
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
and the choice of
K_{2}
.

Before beginning the proof, we make a couple of simplifying observations:

First, note that if
x,y\in H
and
V\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
but
V\notin\mathcal{U}
, then
d_{V}(x,y)<K+\epsilon
for some
\epsilon=\epsilon(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
. Hence by taking
K_{2}\geq K_{1}>K+\epsilon
, we may increase the threshold to remove any extraneous domains. That is:

\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{2}}=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{2}}.

We next observe that for any
x,y\in H
, we have
d_{U}(x,y)\asymp d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})
, with constants depending only on
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

We prove the two bounds in Theorem
8.1
separately. For the upper bound, we only need the basic details from the construction of the
\widehat{T}_{U}
and the passing-up type arguments from Section
4
. Note that this proposition is used in proving the existence of hierarchy paths and the upper bound of the distance formula, which were summarized in Corollary
C
.

Proposition 8.2

(Upper bound)
.

There exists
K_{1}=K_{1}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that for any
K_{2}>K_{1}
and any
x,y\in H
we have

(6)

\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{2}}\succ\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})

with the constants in
\succ
depending only on
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,
and
K_{2}
.

Proof.

First, item (4) of Lemma
7.14
says that
d_{U}(x,y)\succ d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, where the constants in
\succ
depend only on
\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|
.

Now note that if
U\in\mathcal{U}
satisfies
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{y}_{U}
, then the corresponding term disappears on the right-hand side. Hence it suffices to account for the domains in
\mathcal{V}=\{V\in\mathcal{U}|d_{V}(x,y)<K_{2}\textrm{ and }\hat{\psi}(x)\neq\psi(y)\}
, which appear on the left side but not the right.

For this, item (8) of Lemma
7.14
says that
\hat{x}_{U}
is not contained in some cluster point of
\widehat{T}_{U}
for only boundedly-many
U
, and the same for
\hat{y}_{U}
. This means that, up to ignoring boundedly-many domains, if
V\in\mathcal{V}
then
\hat{x}_{V}
and
\hat{y}_{V}
are at distinct cluster points, and so
d_{V}(x,y)>50E
by item (5) of Lemma
7.14
.

We are now done by Proposition
4.14
, which says that up to ignoring boundedly many such
V
, there is some
W\in\mathcal{U}
with
d_{W}(x,y)>K_{2}
and
V\sqsubset W
for each
V\in\mathcal{V}
. Moreover, for any given
W\in\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}
with
d_{W}(x,y)>K_{2}
, we have
\#\{V\in\mathcal{V}|V\sqsubset W\}\prec d_{W}(x,y)
. That is, each
V\in\mathcal{V}
is accounted for by some
W\in\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}
, and each such
W
accounts for boundedly-many
V\in\mathcal{V}
. Hence we can adjust the multiplicative constant to allow the terms in the distance estimate in (
6
) associated to the collection of such
W
to account for the missing
\mathcal{V}
terms, and the additive constants to account for the boundedly-many terms associated to the domains in
\mathcal{V}
which were left out of this process.

∎

It remains to prove the following:

Proposition 8.3

(Lower bound)
.

There exists
K_{1}=K_{1}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that for any
K_{2}>K_{1}
and any
x,y\in H
we have

(7)

\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}[d_{U}(x,y)]_{K_{2}}\prec\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})

with the constants in
\prec
depending only on
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,
and
K_{2}
.

For the lower bound, we have to work a bit harder. Observe that since
d_{U}(x,y)\prec d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})
for each
U
, it suffices to show that

\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}[d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})]_{K_{2}}\prec\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}).

The trouble comes from domains
U\in\mathcal{U}
with
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})>K_{2}
but there is a discrepancy
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})>d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
. Since
\widehat{T}_{U}
is obtained from
T_{U}
by collapsing clusters, some analysis of these collapsed clusters is needed. In particular, any difference in distances occurs because of distinct clusters of shadows
C_{1},\dots,C_{n}
occurring in order along the unique geodesic
[x_{U},y_{U}]_{T_{U}}
. To each such cluster
C_{i}
, we will associate a collection
\mathcal{V}_{i}
of
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal domains
V\in\mathcal{U}
with
\delta^{V}_{U}\in C_{i}
, and then use the Bounding Large Containers Proposition
4.14
(and a localization argument) to conclude that
\#\mathcal{V}_{i}\asymp\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(C_{i}\cap[x_{U},y_{U}]_{T_{U}})
for each
i
. The fact that the
V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
are
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal will say that, up to ignoring boundedly many such
V
, we have
d_{V}(x,y)\succ K
by item (6) of Lemma
7.14
. Hence each such minimal domain makes a large contribution to the distance sum for
\mathcal{Q}
, and since each can only nest into boundedly-many larger domains by Covering Lemma
4.8
, it follows that the collection of minimal domains can account for the distance in each
U
lost by collapsing clusters, up to increasing the multiplicative constant a bounded amount.

We note that the proof uses an auxiliary lemma, which we state and prove after the proof since it is notation-dependent.

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

Continuing with the above notation, for each
i
, let
C^{\prime}_{i}=C_{i}\cap[x_{U},y_{U}]{T_{U}}-\left(B_{\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}}(x_{U})\cup B_{\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}}(y_{U})\right)
.

Observe that for each
i
, if we set
\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{V\in\mathcal{U}|V\sqsubset U\text{ and }\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C_{i}\}
then
\{\delta^{V}_{U}|V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}\}
is
(r+2R)
-dense in
C_{i}
. Hence we can find a subcollection
\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}
so that

•

Each
V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal;

•

For each
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, we have
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C_{i}^{\prime}
;

•

\{\delta^{V}_{U}|V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}\}
is a
(2r+4R,r)
-net on
C_{i}^{\prime}
(see Definition
8.4
below).

Note that the increase in the net constants in the third item is required to guarantee the second item.

In particular, we have
\#\mathcal{V}_{i}\succ\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(C_{i}^{\prime})\geq\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(C_{i})-2E^{\prime}
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}
. Then, by construction,
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},x_{U})>\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}
and
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},y_{U})>\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}
. Moreover, up to ignoring at most
N_{1}=N_{1}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
domains
V\in\mathcal{V}
, Lemma
8.5
provides
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
where
C^{\prime}
separates
a_{U}
from
b_{U}
in
T_{U}
and
V\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(a,b)
. Hence the BGI property (5) of Lemma
6.13
says that
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V}(a_{V}),\delta^{U}_{V}(x_{V}))<E^{\prime}
and
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V}(b_{V}),\delta^{U}_{V}(y_{V}))<E^{\prime}
.

Lemma
6.16
says that the tuples
(a_{U}),(b_{U}),(x_{U}),(y_{U})
are all
\alpha_{0}
-consistent. Thus since
d_{T_{U}}(a_{U},\delta^{V}_{U})>E^{\prime}+\alpha_{0}
and similarly for
b_{U},x_{U},y_{U}
,
\alpha_{0}
-consistency forces that
d_{T_{V}}(a_{V},\delta^{U}_{V}(a_{U}))<\alpha_{0}
, and similarly for
b_{V},x_{V},y_{V}
. As such, we get that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},a_{V})<\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}
and
d_{T_{V}}(y_{V},b_{V})<\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}
, and so that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},y_{V})\geq d_{T_{V}}(a_{V},b_{V})-2\alpha_{0}-2E^{\prime}
, from which we can conclude that
\hat{a}_{V}=\hat{x}_{V}
and
\hat{b}_{V}=\hat{y}_{V}
by requiring
R>\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}
.

On the other hand, since each
V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal and
d_{V}(a,b)>K
, item (4) of Lemma
7.14
says that
d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{a}_{V},\hat{b}_{V})\succ K
. Hence we obtain that
d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{x}_{V},\hat{y}_{V})=d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{a}_{V},\hat{b}_{V})\succ K
.

Thus for each
U\in\mathcal{W}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(C_{i}\cap[x_{U},y_{U}]_{T_{U}})

\displaystyle\prec

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\#\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}K\cdot\#\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\prec

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}}d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{x}_{V},\hat{y}_{V})\right).

Finally, the Covering Lemma
4.8
says that any domain
V\in\mathcal{U}
can nest into only boundedly-many domains in
\mathcal{U}
, with bounds determined by
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
since Lemma
4.8
applies to each pair
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
. Hence, each
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal domain
V\in\mathcal{U}
must account for at most boundedly-many
U\in\mathcal{W}
in the above way. Thus we can account for the distance lost by collapsing clusters in each domain in
\mathcal{W}
with these large
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal domains by increasing the multiplicative constant for the estimate in (
6
) by a bounded amount. This completes the proof.

Figure 9
.
Proof of Proposition
8.3
: The subcluster
C^{\prime}
isolates the portion of
C
between the
R
-neighborhood of
x_{U}
,
\mathcal{N}_{R}(x_{U})
, and
y_{U}
. The point
z\in H
coarsely projects the same as
y
and the rest of the ray
b
to all domains with shadows in
C^{\prime}
.

∎

The following definition was used in the proof of Proposition
8.3
:

Definition 8.4

(Net)
.

Given constants
a,A\geq 0
, a
(a,A)
-
net
on a subspace
Y\subset X
of a metric space is a collection
Z\subset Y
of points so that:

(1)

Z
is
a
-coarsely dense in
Y
, and

(2)

For any
z,z^{\prime}\in Z
, we have
d_{X}(z,z^{\prime})\geq A
.

The following lemma was also used in the proof, and we use the same notation:

Lemma 8.5
.

There exists
N_{1}=N_{1}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that for each
i
and for all but
N_{1}
-many domains
V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}
, there exists
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
so that
d_{T_{V}}(a_{V},b_{V})\succ K
.

Proof.

By construction, the set of
\delta^{V}_{U}
for
V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\prime}
is a
(2r+4R,r)
-net in
C^{\prime}
(Definition
8.4
), and hence only boundedly-many
\delta^{V}_{U}
sets are within
E^{\prime}
of a branch point of
T_{U}
depending on the uniform bound on branching of
T_{U}
. Each remaining
\delta^{V}_{U}
lies along an edge of
T_{U}
, and hence separates
T_{U}=T_{x}\cup T_{z}
into two components, the component containing
x_{V}
and the component containing
z_{V}
. Let
l_{x}
be the marked points and rays of
T_{U}
in
T_{x}
, and similarly for
l_{z}
. Since each such
\delta^{V}_{U}
is
E^{\prime}
-away from any branch point, the BGI property in item (5) of Lemma
6.13
implies that the geodesics in
T_{U}
between any pair of marked points or rays in
p,q\in l_{x}
must avoid the
E^{\prime}
-neighborhood of
\delta^{V}_{U}
, and thus
d_{T_{V}}(p_{V},q_{V})<E^{\prime}
, and similarly for marked points and rays of
l_{z}
.

Yet
V\in\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
, and so there exists
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
so that
d_{T_{V}}(a_{V},b_{V})\succ K
. By the above argument, we must have
a\in l_{x}
and
b\in l_{z}
, as required.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

In the proof above, we used the observation that all but boundedly many shadows for
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
avoided the branch points of
T_{U}
. In fact, this something much stronger is true, namely that all but boundedly-many domains in
U\in\mathcal{U}
have the property that
\delta^{U}_{W}
avoids the branch points of
T_{W}
for each
W\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\sqsubset W
. See Subsection
9.1
below.

9.
The map
\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Y}

The purpose of this section is to define a map
\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Y}
and analyze its features. In particular, the main goal is to prove the following:

Proposition 9.1
.

There exist
\alpha_{\omega}=\alpha_{\omega}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
and a map
\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Y}
so that for any
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
, the tuple
\Omega(\hat{x})\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{\omega}}
. Moreover, if
\Omega(\hat{x})=(x_{U})
, then
q_{U}(x_{U})=\hat{x}_{U}
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

With
\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{\omega}}
in hand, we will be able to use our realization result (Proposition
6.17
) to define the map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
. In Section
11
, we will show that
\widehat{\Psi}
is a coarse inverse of
\widehat{\Omega}
, and therefore
\widehat{\Psi}
is surjective. Combining this with the distance estimates from Section
8
will prove that
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is a quasi-isometric embedding.

The proof of Proposition
9.1
involves analyzing the structure of clusters in depth. This is arguably the most technical part of the construction of the cubical model, and undoubtedly where cubical arguments first appear in spirit though not explicitly.

9.1.
Bipartite domains

The first step is to make the observation that for all but boundedly-many domains in
\mathcal{U}
, we have that
\widehat{T}_{U}
is an interval.

Definition 9.2
.

A domain
U\in\mathcal{U}
is called
\chi
-
bipartite
if there is some
V\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\sqsubset V
so that
T_{V}-\mathcal{N}_{\chi}(\delta^{U}_{V})
has two components which contain all marked points and branch points in
T_{V}
.

•

We denote the set of bipartite domains by
\mathcal{B}(\chi)
.

•

If
U\in\mathcal{B}(\chi)
and
V\in\mathcal{U}
are as above, then we refer to
V
as a
witness
for
U
.

The following lemma explains the bipartite terminology, since it says their collapsed trees are bipartite graphs, i.e. intervals:

Lemma 9.3
.

For any
\chi>E^{\prime}+\alpha_{0}
, if
U\in\mathcal{B}(\chi)
and
V\in\mathcal{U}
is a witness for
U
, then the complement
T_{V}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{U}_{V})=C\sqcup C^{\prime}
has two components. Moreover, the collapsed tree
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a finite-length interval whose endpoints
l_{C},l_{C^{\prime}}
coincide with the projections under
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
of the marked points and rays in
q_{V}(C)
and
q_{V}(C^{\prime})
, respectively. In particular, if
U\in\mathcal{B}(\chi)
, then
U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

By definition of
U\in\mathcal{B}(\chi)
and
V
being a witness for
U
, the complement
T_{V}-\mathcal{N}_{\chi}(\delta^{U}_{V})
has two components
C^{\prime}\sqcup C^{\prime\prime}
. By the BGI property in item (5) of Lemma
6.13
and the assumption that
\chi>E^{\prime}
, the marked points and leaves of
C^{\prime}
which are marked points or rays of
T_{V}
, denoted
l_{C^{\prime}}
, project
E^{\prime}
-close in
T_{U}
, and hence coincide in
\widehat{T}_{U}
, and similarly for
C^{\prime\prime}
. Hence
\widehat{T}_{U}
is an interval with endpoints
q_{U}(\delta^{V}_{U}(l_{C^{\prime}}))
and
q_{U}(\delta^{V}_{U}(l_{C^{\prime\prime}}))
, as required.

To see that the diameter of
\widehat{T}_{U}
is finite, suppose not. Without loss of generality, we may assume that the endpoint
q_{U}(\delta^{V}_{U}(l_{C^{\prime}}))
coincides with
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
for some
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Hence the only marked point in
C^{\prime}
is
\lambda_{V}
. But by assumption
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V},\lambda_{V})>E^{\prime}+\alpha_{0}
, and this violates
\alpha_{0}
-consistency of
(\lambda_{U})
(Lemma
6.16
), which requires that
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V},\lambda_{V})<\alpha_{0}
since
\lambda_{U}\in\partial T_{U}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Figure 10
.
V
is a bipartite domain with witness
U
because
\delta^{V}_{U}
is far away from all branch and marked points of
T_{U}
. The two endpoints of
\widehat{T}_{V}
correspond to the way that
\delta^{V}_{U}
partitions the marked points of
T_{U}
.

We are focusing on bipartite domains is because almost every domain in
\mathcal{U}
is bipartite:

Proposition 9.4
.

For any
\chi>E^{\prime}
, there exists
B_{\chi}=B_{\chi}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,\chi)>0
so that
\#(\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{B}(\chi))<B_{\chi}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}
. We will prove that if
\#\mathcal{V}
is sufficiently large, then
\mathcal{V}
contains a bipartite domain.

Up to dividing
\#\mathcal{V}
by at most
2^{|F\cup\Lambda|}
, we may assume that
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(a,b)\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(a,b)
for some fixed
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
while maintaining
\#\mathcal{V}
to be as large as necessary.

Let
\sigma=\sigma(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
and
\nu=\nu(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
be as in Lemma
9.5
below. Let
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
be the uniform bound on the number of branch points of
T_{U}
for any
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Let
a=a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}=b
be a
\sigma
-subdivision of
\gamma=[a,b]_{W}
.

Now we invoke Proposition
4.3
, which provides
P_{2}(50E,K_{2},\sigma,100\mathbf{b}\nu|F\cup\Lambda|,\chi)>0
(which depends only on
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
, and
\chi
) so that if
\#\mathcal{V}>P_{2}
, then there exists
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(a,b)
and a subcollection
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
so that

\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V}_{W})>K_{2}.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{\prime}=\{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}|p_{\gamma}(\delta^{V}_{U})\cap[a_{i},a_{i+1}]\neq\emptyset\}
, then the proposition also implies that
\#\{1\leq i\leq k-1|\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{i}\neq\emptyset\}\geq 100\mathbf{b}\nu|F\cup\Lambda|\chi
.

Since the
\delta^{V}_{W}
for
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
can only intersect at most two subintervals in the
10E^{\prime}\chi
-subdivision of
[a_{W},b_{W}]_{T_{W}}
, Lemma
9.5
and the lower bound above implies that the number of subintervals containing some
\delta^{V}_{U}
far exceeds
2\mathbf{b}|F\cup\Lambda|
, which bounds the number of subintervals of
[a_{W},b_{W}]_{T_{W}}
containing branch points and marked points. Hence we were able to force
\mathcal{V}
to contain at least one
\chi
-bipartite domain only by requiring it to have at most boundedly-many domains, as required.

∎

The following lemma was used in the proof above:

Lemma 9.5
.

For any
\chi>10E^{\prime}
, there exist
\sigma=\sigma(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,\chi)>0
and
\nu=\nu(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,\chi)>0
so that the following holds:

Suppose
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
and
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(a,b)
. Let
a=a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}=b
be a
\sigma
-subdivision of a
(1,20\delta)
-quasi-geodesic
\gamma
between
a_{U},b_{U}
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
. Set
a_{i}^{\prime}=\phi_{W}^{-1}\circ p_{W}(a_{i})
for each
i
, where
p_{W}:\mathcal{C}(W)\to\phi_{W}(T_{W})
is closest point projection, and
\phi_{W}:T_{W}\to\mathcal{C}(W)
is any uniform quality tree modeling the hull
H_{W}
of
\pi_{W}(F\cup\Lambda)\subset\mathcal{C}(W)
.

Then for any
10E^{\prime}\chi
-subdivision of
[a_{W},b_{W}]_{T_{W}}\subset T_{W}
, each
a_{i}^{\prime},a_{j}^{\prime}
are contained in distinct subintervals when
i\neq j
. Moreover, we have that
d_{T_{W}}(a^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i+1})<\nu
for each
0\leq i\leq k-1
.

Proof.

The proof is a straight-forward application of the techniques from Section
6
. The one subtle point is choosing
\sigma
to be as small enough to guarantee that the subdivision points of
\gamma
land in separate subintervals along the tree geodesic, but large enough to guarantee that their images are at a bounded distance controlled by
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
. The point is that the subdivision width of
[a_{W},b_{W}]_{T_{W}}\subset T_{W}
is fixed (at
10E^{\prime}\chi
), while we get to control both the subdivision width
\sigma
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
and its separation constant
\nu
under the uniform quality quasi-inverse
\phi^{-1}_{W}:\phi_{W}(T_{W})\to T_{W}
. We leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

The reason for the extra variable
\chi
will become clear in what follows. That is, we need to be able to choose
\chi
sufficiently large, depending on the relevant context, but always depending only on
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
.

Hence for the rest of this section, we fix
\chi=\chi(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
, to be determined below. In the end, taking
\chi>5E^{\prime}+\alpha_{0}
works.

9.2.
Honing clusters

We will now explain how to define a map
\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Y}
, so that:

•

\Omega(\mathcal{Q})\subset\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}
for some
\omega=\omega(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
, and

•

when
\hat{x}=(\hat{x}_{U})\in\mathcal{Q}
and
(x_{U})=\Omega(\hat{x})
, then
q_{U}(x_{U})=\hat{x}_{U}
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

In this subsection, we set notation and give a description of the map
\Omega
. We will prove that it has the desired properties in Proposition
9.1
below. Unsurprisingly, the definition is domain-wise in the
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

Let
\hat{x}=(\hat{x}_{U})\in\mathcal{Q}
. When
U\in\mathcal{U}
has
\hat{x}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
, then the definition is simple:
x_{U}=q_{U}^{-1}(\hat{x}_{U})
, which is a unique point. For the other domains, we need some more setup.

Suppose that
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
\hat{x}_{U}\notin\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
. The first step is to associate a collection of marked points
l_{V}\subset F\cup\Lambda
to each
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite domain
V\in\mathcal{B}(\chi)
. We then use these
l_{V}
to hone the clusters of the larger
U\in\mathcal{U}
in which such
V
are involved.

Suppose then that
U\in\mathcal{U}
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal. Since
\widehat{T}_{U}
has no internal cluster points,
\hat{x}_{U}
is either a marked cluster point or a point in
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
. If the former, let
l_{U}
be the set of marked points coming from points in
F\cup\Lambda
identified in the cluster associated to
\hat{x}_{U}
. If the latter, set
l_{U}=\emptyset
.

Now suppose that
U\in\mathcal{U}
is not
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal. We have that either
\hat{x}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
, in which case we set
l_{U}=\emptyset
and
x_{U}=q_{U}^{-1}(\hat{x}_{U})
. Otherwise,
\hat{x}_{U}
is contained in a collapsed cluster
q_{U}(C)
for a cluster
C\subset T_{U}
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
be the domains
V\in\mathcal{B}(\chi)
so that

•

V\sqsubset U
with
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C
, and

•

V
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal.

•

U
is a witness for
V
.

Observe that if
\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)=\emptyset
, then
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(C)
is bounded in terms of
R,r
and the total branching of
T_{U}
, and hence in terms of
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

We now explain the honing process for domains in
\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, which will associate to
\hat{x}_{U}
a bounded diameter subset of the cluster
C
in
T_{U}
(Lemma
9.12
).

Let
V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
. Then we set

C^{V}_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{C}(C\cap(\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{V}_{U})\cup l_{V})),

which is a subtree of
C
, see Figure
11
. Finally, let
B_{U}(\hat{x})=\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)}C^{V}_{U}
be the total intersection of the
C^{V}_{U}
.

Figure 11
.
The cluster
C
(green) in
T_{U}
is identified with
\hat{x}_{U}
in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. Since
V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, its shadow
\delta^{V}_{U}
(orange) avoids all branches of
T_{U}
. Lemma
9.7
implies all marked points and rays in
T_{U}
are identified to the two marked clusters of
\widehat{T}_{V}
. The marked points and leaves
l_{V}
(pink) chosen by
\hat{x}_{V}
determine the half-subtree
C^{V}_{U}
(blue) of
C
chosen by
V
.

In Lemma
9.9
we show that if
V,W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, the
C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\neq\emptyset
. Since each
C^{V}_{U}
is a subtree, we can make an argument in Lemma
9.10
using the Helly property for trees to conclude that
B_{U}(x)
is nonempty, though this takes some care because
C
and the
C^{V}_{U}
can be infinite-diameter when
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
. Finally, in Lemma
9.12
we prove that this intersection has diameter bounded in terms of
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

Thus we can define a tuple
\Omega(\hat{x})=(x_{U})_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
as follows:

•

For
U\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\in E(\hat{x})
, we set
x_{U}=q_{U}^{-1}(\hat{x}_{U})
.

•

For
U\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\notin E(\hat{x})
and
U\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal, we let
x_{U}
be any point in
q_{U}^{-1}(\hat{x}_{U})
.

•

Otherwise, we let
x_{U}\in B_{U}(\hat{x})
.

The following is immediate from the construction:

Lemma 9.6
.

For every
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have
q_{U}(x_{U})=\hat{x}_{U}
.

In Proposition
9.1
below, we prove that
(x_{U})
is a
\omega
-consistent tuple for
\omega=\omega(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
.

9.3.
Bounding intersections

We carry forward the notation from the Subsection
9.2
.

We begin with two simple but important observations. The first is a direct application of Lemma
9.3
and the definition of
C^{V}_{U}
:

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
and
C\subset T_{U}
a cluster with
V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
. Let
T_{U}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{V}_{U})=C^{\prime}\cup C^{\prime\prime}
, and let
l_{C^{\prime}},l_{C^{\prime\prime}}
denote the marked points and rays contained in
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
respectively.

Then
l_{V}\in\{l_{C^{\prime}},l_{C^{\prime\prime}}\}
, and if
l_{V}=l_{C^{\prime}}
then
C^{V}_{U}=\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{V}_{U})\cup(C^{\prime}\cap C)
, and similarly when
l_{V}=l_{C^{\prime\prime}}
.

The second follows directly from the construction of the clusters (Subsection
7.2
), the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, and the uniform bound on branching of the trees
T_{U}
and the size of
|F\cup\Lambda|
:

Lemma 9.8
.

There exists
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that the following holds: Suppose that
C\subset T^{c}_{U}
is a cluster for some
U\in\mathcal{U}
, and
C^{\prime}\subset C
is a (connected) subtree. Then either:

•

\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(C^{\prime})<4r^{\prime}
, or

•

\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
and then there exists
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C^{\prime})
so that
\{\delta^{V}_{U}|V\in\mathcal{V}\}
forms a
(2r^{\prime},r^{\prime})
-net on
C^{\prime}
(Definition
8.4
).

We can now prove that the
C^{V}_{U}
for
V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
have pairwise nonempty intersection:

Lemma 9.9
.

If
V,W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, then
C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\neq\emptyset
. Moreover, if
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},\delta^{W}_{U})>r
, then we have the following:

(1)

At least one of
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C^{W}_{U}
or
\delta^{W}_{U}\subset C^{V}_{U}
holds.

(2)

If
\delta^{V}_{U}
is not a subset of
C^{W}_{U}
, then
\delta^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}=\emptyset
, and similarly for
\delta^{W}_{U}
.

Proof.

Item (2) is an immediate consequence of the main part of the statement, and we shall observe item (1) during the course of the proof of the statement.

By definition of
C^{V}_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{C}((\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{V}_{U})\cup l_{V})\cap C)
, we may assume that
\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{V}_{U})\cap\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{W}_{U})=\emptyset
, and hence that
V\pitchfork W
by item (3) of Lemma
6.13
.

Let
\gamma_{V}
be the marked points and rays of
T_{U}
which are also the marked points and rays in the component of
T_{U}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{W}_{U})
containing
\delta^{V}_{U}
. Define
\gamma_{W}
similarly.

A standard argument using Lemma
9.7
and the BGI property in item (7) of Lemma
7.14
show that
\widehat{T}_{V}
and
\widehat{T}_{W}
are intervals with the labels of the endpoints of
\widehat{T}_{V}
coinciding with
\gamma_{V}-\gamma_{W}
and
\gamma_{W}
, while the labels of the endpoints of
\widehat{T}_{W}
coincide with
\gamma_{W}-\gamma_{V}
and
\gamma_{V}
.

Now if
a\in l_{V}\cap\gamma_{W}\subset F\cup\Lambda
, then
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{a}_{V}
, and thus
l_{V}=\gamma_{W}
. Hence the labels in
l_{V}
choose the half-tree of
T_{U}
containing
\delta^{W}_{U}
precisely when
l_{V}\cap\gamma_{W}\neq\emptyset
, and similarly for
l_{W}
. Note that this proves item (1).

Hence, by the Lemma
9.7
, we may assume, for a contradiction, that
l_{V}\cap\gamma_{W}=l_{W}\cap\gamma_{V}=\emptyset
.

Choose labels
a\in\gamma_{V}-\gamma_{W}
and
b\in\gamma_{W}-\gamma_{V}
. Since
a,b
determine distinct marked clusters or rays in all of
\widehat{T}_{U},\widehat{T}_{V},\widehat{T}_{W}
, their distances in the trees
T_{U},T_{V},T_{W}
are at least
r
, and hence by choosing
r
sufficiently large depending only on
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
, we can arrange that
d_{T_{U}}(a_{U},b_{U})>10E^{\prime}
, and similarly for
V,W
. Item (6) of Lemma
6.13
then says that
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V}(\delta^{W}_{U}),\delta^{W}_{V})<E^{\prime}
, which implies that
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}=\hat{b}_{V}=q_{V}(\gamma_{W})
, and similarly
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}=\hat{a}_{W}=q_{W}(\gamma_{V})
.

Since
V\pitchfork W
, item (8) of Lemma
7.14
says that we cannot have both of
V,W\in E(\hat{x})
. Hence without loss of generality, there are now two cases (1)
W\in E(\hat{x})
and
V\notin E(\hat{x})
, and (2) when
V,W\notin E(\hat{x})
.

In case (1), then
l_{V}\neq\emptyset
, so that
a\in l_{V}=\gamma_{V}-\gamma_{W}
, and hence
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{a}_{V}=q_{V}(l_{V})
. But the above says that
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}
, while
\hat{x}_{W}
is not in a marked cluster, and this contradicts
0
-consistency of
(\hat{x}_{U})
.

In case (2), now
a\in l_{V}
and
b\in l_{W}
, and the above forces that
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{a}_{V}
is in a different marked cluster than
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}=\hat{b}_{V}
, so that
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}
. Similarly
\hat{x}_{W}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}
. Together, these also contradict
0
-consistency.

This completes the proof.
∎

Figure 12
.
The impossible situation in which
V\pitchfork W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
choose non-intersecting sides
C^{V}_{U}
(pink) and
C^{W}_{U}
(blue) of
C
(green). This forces inconsistency of
\hat{x}_{V}
and
\hat{x}_{W}
.

The next step is to conclude that the total intersection is nonempty, which uses but does not follow directly from the Helly property for finite trees:

Lemma 9.10
.

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have

B_{U}=\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)}C^{V}_{U}\neq\emptyset.

Proof.

If
C
has bounded diameter, then
C
and all of the
C^{V}_{U}
are finite diameter trees, and the conclusion is immediate from the Helly property for finite trees.

Suppose instead that
C
has infinite diameter. We will produce a finite diameter subtree
C_{fin}\subset C
satisfying
C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\cap C_{fin}\neq\emptyset
for all
V,W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, while also
B_{U}\subset C_{fin}
. It will then follow from the Helly property for trees that
B_{U}\neq\emptyset
.

Let
\Lambda_{C}=\{\lambda\in\Lambda|U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\text{ and }\lambda_{U}\in C\}
denote all of the rays in
\Lambda
which determine rays in
T_{U}
whose ends are contained in
C
. For each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{C}
, choose
V_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
so that:

•

l_{V_{\lambda}}=(F\cup\Lambda)-\{\lambda\}
;

•

\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{V_{\lambda^{\prime}}}_{U})\subset C^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}
for all
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda(C)

•

If
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
so that some point in
\delta^{W}_{U}
separates
\delta^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}
from
\lambda_{U}
in
T_{U}
, then
l_{W}=l_{V_{\lambda}}=(F\cup\Lambda)-\{\lambda\}
.

Note that such a
V_{\lambda}
exists for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{C}
because of the canonical property in Definition
7.9
for the tuple
(\hat{x}_{U})
, since otherwise there would be an infinite sequence
\{V_{i}\}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
with the sets
\delta^{V_{i}}_{U}
running out the end of
T_{U}
corresponding to
\lambda_{U}
with
\hat{x}_{V_{i}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
, thereby violating the finiteness condition. Hence we may choose such a
V_{\lambda}
by running far out the end of
C
corresponding to
\lambda_{U}
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda(C)
.

Set
C_{fin}=\bigcap_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{C}}C^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}
, see Figure
13
. Observe that
B_{U}\subset C_{fin}
. It suffices to prove the following claim:

Claim 9.11
.

For all
V,W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, we have
C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\cap C_{fin}\neq\emptyset
.

Figure 13
.
The proof of Lemma
9.10
: For each of the finitely-many
\lambda\in\Lambda
determining rays in
T_{U}
, we choose a domain
V_{\lambda}
which is located past every branch point along that ray, then take the intersection of the
C^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}
across all such
\lambda
.

To see the claim, let
E(\Lambda,C)
denote the set of
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
so that
\delta^{W}_{U}
contains a point which separates
\lambda_{U}
from
\delta^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}
for some
\lambda\in\Lambda(C)
.

Observe the following:

(1)

Each
W\in E(\Lambda,C)
uniquely determines such a
\lambda
, and that
l_{W}=l_{V_{\lambda}}=(F\cup\Lambda)-\{\lambda\}
by construction. Hence by convexity of the
\delta
-sets, we have that
C_{fin}\subset C^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}\subset C^{W}_{U}
.

(2)

If
W\notin E(\Lambda,C)
, then for every
\lambda\in\Lambda(C)
, some point of
\delta^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}
separates
\delta^{W}_{U}
from
\lambda
in
T_{U}
. It follows from the definition of
C^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}
that
N_{r}(\delta^{W}_{U})\subset C^{V_{\lambda}}_{U}
for all
\lambda
, and hence that
N_{r}(\delta^{W}_{U})\subset C_{fin}
.

Following these observations, we can now argue by cases. If
V,W\in E(\Lambda,C)
, then observation (1) shows that
C_{fin}\subset C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}
, and we are done. If instead
V\in E(\Lambda,C)
while
W\notin E(\Lambda,C)
, then combining both observations provides that
\delta^{W}_{U}\subset C_{fin}\subset C^{V}_{U}
, and we are done.

Finally, if
V,W\notin E(\Lambda,C)
, then one of two things holds. The first possibility is that
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},\delta^{W}_{U})>r
, in which case Lemma
9.9
says, with loss of generality, that
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C^{W}_{U}
while the second observation says that
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C_{fin}
, and hence
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\cap C_{fin}
, as required. Otherwise,
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},\delta^{W}_{U})<r
, and we may also assume that neither
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C^{W}_{U}
nor
\delta^{W}_{U}\subset C^{V}_{U}
. But then
C^{W}_{U}\cap C^{V}_{U}\subset\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{V}_{U})\cap\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{W}_{U})
, which is contained in
C_{fin}
by the second observation above. This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we prove that
B_{U}=\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)}C^{V}_{U}
has bounded diameter:

Lemma 9.12
.

There exists
B_{1}=B_{1}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
such that for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have

\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(B_{U})<B_{1}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.8
, there exists
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that either
\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(C)<2r^{\prime}
, or for there exists a collection
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
so that
\{\delta^{V}_{U}|V\in\mathcal{V}\}
forms a
(2r^{\prime},r^{\prime})
-net on
C
(Definition
8.4
). Since we are done in the former case, we may assume the latter holds.

We may further suppose that
B_{U}
contains an edge of
T_{U}
with diameter more than
8r^{\prime}
that does not contain any marked or branch points, for we are done if it does not. Then the net provides distinct
V,W\in\mathcal{V}
with
r^{\prime}<d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},\delta^{W}_{U})<2r^{\prime}
with
\delta^{V}_{U},\delta^{W}_{U}\subset B_{U}
.

By Lemma
9.9
, we have that either
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C^{W}_{U}
or
\delta^{W}_{U}\subset C^{V}_{U}
, and possibly both. Supposing only the first containment holds, then in fact
\delta^{W}_{U}\cap C^{V}_{U}=\emptyset
by item (1) of Lemma
9.9
, and hence
\delta^{W}_{U}
is not a subset of
B_{U}
, which contradicts our assumption that
\delta^{W}_{U}\subset B_{U}
.

Hence both containments must hold. Then and moreover
C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\subset\mathrm{hull}_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{V}_{U})\cup\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{W}_{U}))
, which has diameter bounded by
2r^{\prime}+2r
since
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},\delta^{W}_{U})<2r^{\prime}
.

Either way, the diameter of
B_{U}
is bounded and we are done.
∎

9.4.
Lemmas supporting consistency of
\Omega(\hat{x})

We record the following lemmas which will be useful in the proof of consistency in Proposition
9.1
below.

In the following, if
A\subset F\cup\Lambda
, then we let
A_{U}
denote the corresponding collection of marked points and rays of
T_{U}
. If in addition
C
is a cluster, then we let
A_{U}(C)
be the collection of points and rays obtained by closest-point projecting
A_{U}
to
C
in
T_{U}
. The first lemma is used in the arguments for transverse consistency, and the second lemma for nested consistency.

Lemma 9.13
.

Suppose
\hat{x}_{U}
is contained in a cluster
C
in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. If
A=\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)}l_{V}\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathrm{hull}_{C}(A_{U}(C))\subset B_{U}
. In particular,
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(\mathrm{hull}_{C}(A_{U}(C))<B_{1}
.

Proof.

By assumption,
A\subset l_{V}
for all
V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
. Moreover, since
A\neq\emptyset
, we have that
\mathrm{hull}_{C}(A_{U}(C))\subset C^{V}_{U}
for all
V\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, and hence
\mathrm{hull}_{C}(A_{U}(C))\subset B_{U}
, which has diameter bounded by
B_{1}
by Lemma
9.12
.
∎

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
, and
W,U\sqsubset V\in\mathcal{U}
with
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{W}_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})>4E^{\prime}
. If
C\subset T_{V}
is the cluster with
\delta^{U}_{V}\subset C
and
q_{V}(C)=\hat{x}_{V}
, and
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
with
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}
, then
\delta^{U}_{V}\subset C^{W}_{V}
.

Proof.

Since
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, Lemma
9.7
gives that
\widehat{T}_{W}
is an interval with two endpoints labeled by the marked points and rays
l_{W}
of
T_{V}
in the two complementary components of
T_{V}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{V}_{U})
. By assumption,
\delta^{U}_{V}
is contained in one of those complementary components, and item (6) of Lemma
6.13
gives that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}
. Since
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}
by assumption, it follows from item (5) of Lemma
6.13
that
\delta^{U}_{V}
is contained in the same component
C^{\prime}
of
T_{V}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{W}_{V})
chosen by
\hat{x}_{W}
. It follows that
\delta^{U}_{V}\subset C^{W}_{V}=\mathrm{hull}_{C}\left(C\cap\mathcal{N}_{r}(\delta^{W}_{U}))\cup l_{W}\right)
, as required.
∎

9.5.
Proof of Proposition
9.1

Our goal is to prove that there is a consistency constant
\alpha_{\omega}=\alpha_{\omega}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that
\Omega(\mathcal{Q})\subset\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{\omega}}
. The “moreover” part of the statement, namely
q_{U}(x_{U})=\hat{x}_{U}
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, where
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
and
\Omega(\hat{x})=(x_{U})
, is the conclusion of Lemma
9.6
.

We check the consistency inequalities (Definition
6.15
) by a case-wise analysis. Let
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
.

(1)
U\pitchfork V
and
\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
: Then we must have that
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
by
0
-consistency. Let
C
denote the marked point cluster of
T_{V}
containing
\delta^{U}_{V}
, so that
B_{V}\subset C
by construction. We will prove that
B_{V}
and
\delta^{U}_{V}
are at a bounded distance.

If
V
is
\sqsubset
-minimal in
\mathcal{U}
, then
C
is a marked point cluster with diameter bounded in terms of
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
, so we may assume otherwise. Moreover, we may assume that
\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)\neq\emptyset
by Lemma
9.8
, otherwise again
C
has bounded diameter.

Let
a\in F\cup\Lambda
be some marked point or ray with
d_{T_{V}}(a_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<E^{\prime}
, which exists by item (1) of Lemma
6.13
. Note that since
\delta^{V}_{U}\subset C
, we have
a_{V}\in C
by construction of the clusters.

If
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, then
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{W}_{V},a_{V})>\chi>5E^{\prime}+\alpha_{0}
, by choosing the bipartite threshold
\chi=\chi(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>5E^{\prime}+\alpha_{0}
. So
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{W}_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})>3E^{\prime}
, and thus
W\pitchfork U
. Since
W\sqsubset V
, we have
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{W}_{U},\delta^{V}_{U})<E^{\prime}
, so
\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, forcing that
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}
by
0
-consistency of
(\hat{x}_{U})
.

We claim that
a\in l_{W}
. The reason for this is that
\widehat{T}_{W}
is an interval whose endpoints partition
F\cup\Lambda
into two sets by Lemma
9.7
, where
l_{W}
labels the points in
F\cup\Lambda
coinciding with
\hat{x}_{W}
. As such, if
a\notin l_{W}
and hence
\hat{a}_{W}\neq\hat{x}_{W}
, then
d_{T_{W}}(a_{W},\delta^{V}_{W}(a_{V}))>r>\alpha_{0}
, while already
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{W}_{V},a_{V})>5E^{\prime}+\alpha_{0}
, which contradicts the fact that
(a_{U})
is an
\alpha_{0}
-consistent tuple by Lemma
6.16
. Thus we must have
a\in l_{W}
, and so
\hat{a}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}=\hat{x}_{W}
. Hence
a\in l_{W}
for all
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
. We are now done by Lemma
9.13
, which implies that
a_{V}\in B_{V}
, while
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V},a_{V})<E^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{V}}(B_{V})<B_{1}=B_{1}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda)
by Lemma
9.12
.

(2)
U\pitchfork V
with both
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
and
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
: Suppose instead that
\hat{x}_{U}
is contained in the cluster
C
of
T_{U}
containing
\delta^{V}_{U}
. Moreover, assume that
B_{U}
(and hence
x_{U}
) is at least
10r^{\prime}\cdot B_{1}\alpha_{0}
away from any projection of a marked point or ray of
T_{U}
to
C
, where
B_{1}
is as in Lemma
9.12
and
r^{\prime}
is from Lemma
9.8
. We will bound the distance between
B_{V}
(and hence
x_{V}
) and
\delta^{U}_{V}
by producing a domain
Z\sqsubset U
with
Z\pitchfork V
and
\hat{x}_{Z}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{Z}
, thereby allowing us to apply the previous case.

Figure 14
.
The proof of case (2) of Proposition
9.1
: By assuming that
x_{U}
is far from
\delta^{V}_{U}
, we can produce a bipartite domain
Z\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
which chooses the half-tree not containing
\delta^{V}_{U}
, thereby forcing
\hat{x}_{Z}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{Z}
, allowing us to invoke case (1) with
V,Z
playing the roles of
U,V
, respectively.

Let
A\subset F\cup\Lambda
be the set of marked points and rays
a
satisfying
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U},a_{U})<E^{\prime}
, with
A\neq\emptyset
by item (1) of Lemma
6.13
. Let
C^{\prime}=\mathrm{hull}_{C}(B_{U},A_{U})\subset C
. By assumption
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(C^{\prime})>10r^{\prime}B_{1}
, so Lemma
9.8
provides a bipartite domain
Z\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C^{\prime})
so that
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{Z}_{U},B_{U})>r^{\prime}
and
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{Z}_{U},A_{U})>r^{\prime}
, and so that
\delta^{Z}_{U}
separates
B_{U}
from
a_{U}
for all
a\in A
. Since
B_{U}\subset C^{V}_{U}
by definition, it follows that
l_{Z}\cap A=\emptyset
, and hence
\hat{x}_{Z}\neq\hat{a}_{Z}
for all
a\in A
.

Moreover, item (6) of Lemma
6.13
implies that
d_{T_{Z}}(\delta^{V}_{Z},\delta^{U}_{Z}(\delta^{V}_{U}))<E^{\prime}
while item (5) of that lemma implies
d_{T_{Z}}(\delta^{U}_{Z}(\delta^{V}_{U}),\delta^{U}_{Z}(a_{U}))<E^{\prime}
. On the other hand,
\alpha_{0}
-consistency of
(a_{U})
and the assumption that
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{Z}_{U},a_{U})>10r^{\prime}B_{1}\alpha_{0}>\alpha_{0}
implies that
d_{T_{Z}}(a_{Z},\delta^{V}_{Z})<\alpha_{0}
. Hence we have
d_{T_{Z}}(\delta^{V}_{Z},a_{Z})<2E^{\prime}+\alpha_{0}<R
, and it follows that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{Z}=\hat{a}_{Z}
. In particular, we have
\hat{x}_{Z}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{Z}
.

Finally, since
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
by assumption, we let
C^{\prime\prime}
be the marked point cluster of
T_{V}
containing
\delta^{U}_{V}
, and hence also
\delta^{Z}_{U}\subset C^{\prime\prime}
since
Z\sqsubset U
. But now we are in the situation from the first case, where
V,Z
play the roles of
U,V
there, since
Z\pitchfork V
and
\hat{\delta}_{Z}\neq\hat{x}_{Z}
. The conclusion of that case was that
d_{T_{V}}(B_{V},\delta^{Z}_{V})
was bounded distance, while we have
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{Z}_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<E^{\prime}
since
Z\sqsubset U
, and we are done.

(3)
U\sqsubset V
and
\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{x}_{V})
: By
0
-consistency, we have
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
. We will prove that
C^{W}_{V}\cap\delta^{U}_{V}\neq\emptyset
for all
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
. It will follow then that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<4r
.

Let
C
denote the cluster of
T_{V}
containing
\delta^{U}_{V}
and
x_{V}
. First observe that if
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
and
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{W}_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<4E^{\prime}
, then
\delta^{U}_{V}\cap C^{W}_{V}\neq\emptyset
by definition of
C^{W}_{V}
.

On the other hand, if
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
with
4E^{\prime}<d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{W}_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})
, then
W\pitchfork U
. Then item (6) of Lemma
6.13
gives that
d_{T_{W}}(\delta^{U}_{W},\delta^{V}_{W}(\delta^{U}_{V}))<3E^{\prime}
. Hence
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V})=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{x}_{V})\neq\hat{x}_{U}
, and so since
\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
,
0
-consistency implies that
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}
. Lemma
9.14
gives that
\delta^{U}_{V}\subset C^{W}_{V}
. Consequently, we have
C^{W}_{V}\cap\delta^{U}_{V}\neq\emptyset
for all
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, and it follows that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<4r
.

(4)
U\sqsubset V
and
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
: In this final case,
0
-consistency provides
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{x}_{V})=\hat{x}_{U}
. We will bound
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{U}}(x_{U}\cup\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}))
.

We may assume that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})>\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}
, otherwise we are done. Let
a\in F\cup\Lambda
be such that
a_{V}
is contained in the same component of
T_{V}-\mathcal{N}_{\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}}(\delta^{U}_{V})
as
x_{V}
. By the BGI property in item (5) of Lemma
6.13
, we get
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}),\delta^{V}_{U}(a_{V}))<E^{\prime}
. But since
d_{T_{V}}(a_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})>\alpha_{0}
and
(a_{U})
is
\alpha_{0}
-consistent by Lemma
6.16
, we get
d_{T_{U}}(a_{U},\delta^{V}_{U}(a_{V}))<\alpha_{0}
. Hence
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}),a_{U})<\alpha_{0}+E^{\prime}
.

Since
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{x}_{V})
by assumption, we get that
a_{U},x_{U}
are in the same cluster
C
in
T_{U}
. We may assume, for a contradiction, that there is some
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
such that
a_{U},x_{U}
are in separate components of
T_{U}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{W}_{U})
, for otherwise we are done.

Since
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
, Lemma
9.7
says that
\widehat{T}_{W}
is an interval with two endpoints labeled by the marked points and rays in
T_{U}
in the two complementary components of
T_{U}-\mathcal{N}_{E^{\prime}}(\delta^{W}_{U})
. Since
\delta^{W}_{U}
separates
a_{U}
from
x_{U}
in
T_{U}
, it follows that
\hat{a}_{W},\hat{x}_{W}
are not in the same endpoint cluster. Moreover, since
x_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
by definition of
B_{U}
, we have
\hat{x}_{W}\neq\hat{a}_{W}
.

But this contradicts
0
-consistency of
\hat{x}
, since
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}
(because
W\sqsubset U\sqsubset V
) while
\hat{x}_{W}\neq\hat{a}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}(\hat{x}_{V})
, with the latter equality holding by a similar argument for why
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{x}_{V})=\hat{a}_{U}
. This completes the proof of this case, and hence of the proposition.

Figure 15
.
The proof of case (4) of Proposition
9.1
: If
x_{V}
(blue) is far enough away from
\delta^{U}_{V}
(orange), then we can find a marked point or ray
a\in F\cup\Lambda
(pink) so that
a_{U}\sim\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V})
. If also the cluster
C\subset T_{U}
containing
x_{U}
(and
a_{U}
) is wide enough, we can find a domain
W\in\mathcal{B}_{\min}(C)
so that
\delta^{W}_{U}
separates
x_{U}
from
a_{U}
, forcing
a_{W}
and
x_{W}
to be at opposite ends of
T_{W}
, because
C^{W}_{U}
(red) has to contain
x_{U}
by definition of
B_{U}
.

10.
Tree trimming

In this section, we give a general construction that allows us to trim the trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
in various ways while preserving the metric on
\mathcal{Q}
up to quasi-isometry. Applications of these trimming techniques include

•

surjectivity of
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
(Theorem
11.1
),

•

the ability to convert all of the
\widehat{T}_{U}
to simplicial trees while preserving
\mathcal{Q}
up to quasi-isometry (Corollary
10.9
), which is crucial for proving that
\mathcal{Q}
is quasi-median quasi-isometric to a cube complex (Theorem
B
), and

•

modifying the cubical model construction to encode curve graph distance in their hyperplanes (Theorem
F
).

As we will see in the Sections
12
and
13
, the hierarchical geometry of
H
is directly encoded in the cubical geometry of
\mathcal{Q}
, and so tree trimming will also allow to directly alter the cubical structure of
\mathcal{Q}
.

Roughly speaking, the proposition says that if we collapse boundedly-many subtrees of bounded diameter in each component of
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then the resulting collapsing map induces a quasi-isometry from
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}
to the resulting product of trees, and this map restricts to a map between the
0
-consistent sets which preserves (non-)canonicality. First we need some notation.

Let
K
be our largeness threshold for the construction (Subsection
5.2
) and suppose that
B=B(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
is some other constant with respect to which we can make
K
as large as we like (as is the case in applications).

Suppose for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
and each component
E\subset\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
that
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\subset E
is a pairwise disjoint collection of subtrees, with
\mathrm{diam}_{E}(A_{i})<B
and
n<B
for each
i
. Let
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
be the tree obtained by collapsing each of the
A_{i}
to a point across all components
E\subset\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
, and let
\Delta_{U}:\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
be the quotient map. We think of this process as “trimming” the trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
, even though the collapsed sets can be in the interior of edges of the trees.

As with the original quotients
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
, we get induced relative projections
(\hat{\delta}^{\prime})^{V}_{U}
as follows:

•

If
V\sqsubset U
or
V\pitchfork U
, then
(\hat{\delta}^{\prime})^{V}_{U}=\Delta_{U}(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})
.

•

If
U\sqsubset V
, then
(\hat{\delta}^{\prime})^{V}_{U}=\Delta_{U}\circ\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\circ\Delta_{V}^{-1}:\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
.

We get a notion of
0
-consistency relative to these projections in the product
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, and can define
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
to be the set of
0
-consistent tuples in the sense of Definition
7.9
. Moreover, it is clear that if
\Delta:\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\to\overline{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
is the corresponding quotient map, then the restriction
\Delta|_{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}}:\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\to\overline{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
satisfies
\Delta(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
, for trimming a tree will not change the fact that a coordinate coincides with a relative projection. With these definitions in place, we observe that the conclusions of the Collapsed Tree Control Lemma
7.14
hold for this trimmed setup.

For each
a\in F
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we set
\hat{a}^{\prime}_{U}=\Delta_{U}(\hat{a}_{U})
. For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we can similarly define
\hat{\lambda}^{\prime}_{U}=\Delta_{U}(\hat{\lambda}_{U})
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, but the following remark is a word of caution:

Remark 10.1

(Collapsing rays)
.

Notice that when
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
, then the component quotient maps need not extend to bijections
\partial\Delta_{U}:\partial\widehat{T}_{U}\to\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
. This can occur when, for some
\lambda\in\Lambda
with
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, any ray in
\widehat{T}_{U}
representing
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
is contains an infinite sequence of cluster points with consecutive pairs separated by an edge (in
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
) of diameter less than
B^{2}
. As a result, any such ray can be collapsed to a bounded diameter set. In particular, it follows that there is some infinite collection of domains
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}
with
V\sqsubset U
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}
, and so that
(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime}=(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U})^{\prime}
for all
V,W\in\mathcal{V}
. In particular,
\hat{\lambda}^{\prime}_{U}=(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime}
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}
. As in the example in Subsection
1.7
from the introduction, the set
\{\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}|V\in\mathcal{V}\}
represents
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
.

We can now state our tree trimming theorem:

Theorem 10.2

(Tree Trimming)
.

Using the above notation, there exists
B_{tt}=B_{tt}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,K)>0
, so that for any
B<B_{tt}
there exists
L=L(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,B)>0
so that the quotient map
\Delta:\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\to\overline{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
satisfies the following:

(1)

\Delta(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})=\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
,
\Delta(\mathcal{Q})=\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, and
\Delta(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})=(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})^{\prime}
.

(2)

The restriction
\Delta|_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is an
(L,L)
-quasi-isometry.

(3)

If the number of domains
U\in\mathcal{U}
with collapsed subtrees is bounded by
B^{\prime}
, then
\Delta|_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a
(1,BB^{\prime})
-quasi-isometry.

•

Moreover, the map
\Delta|_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is
0
-median.

Proof.

The median “moreover” is proven in Corollary
15.16
below, see that section for the relevant definitions.

We begin by proving item (1). As noted before the statement, the fact that
\Delta
preserves consistency follows from the fact that we are only collapsing subtrees, so coordinates which were relative projections remain relative projections. Hence
\Delta(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
.

(Non-)canonicality is preserved
: To see that
\Delta(\mathcal{Q})\subset\Delta(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
, we confirm canonicality (as in Definition
7.9
) is also preserved. Suppose that
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
. Then
\hat{x}_{U}\notin\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
for all
U
, and it follows that
\Delta_{U}(\hat{x}_{U})\notin\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, since interior points of
\widehat{T}_{U}
cannot become boundary points of
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
by collapsing subtrees. On the other hand, since
\#\{U|\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{a}_{U}\}<\infty
for every
a\in F
and we are only collapsing subtrees, it follows that the same property holds for
\hat{x}^{\prime}
.

To see that
\Delta(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})\subset(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})^{\prime}
, let
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
be non-canonical. Then either (a) there exists
U\in\mathcal{U}
so that
\hat{x}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
, or (b) there exists an infinite collection of domains
\mathcal{V}_{U}
and
a\in F
so that
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{a}_{V}
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
.

In case (a), set
\hat{x}^{\prime}=\Delta(\hat{x})
and suppose that
\hat{x}^{\prime}_{U}\notin\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, for we are done otherwise. Hence we are in the situation discussed before the statement of the proposition. Let
\lambda\in\Lambda
be so that
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
. Since the map
\Delta_{U}:\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
collapses at most
B
-many subtrees of diameter at most
B
in each component of
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
, it follows that there exists an infinite sequence of
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite domains
V_{i}\sqsubset U
so that

•

the sequence
\hat{\delta}^{V_{n}}_{U}
monotonically escapes out the end of
\widehat{T}_{U}
corresponding to
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
,

•

each consecutive pair
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{U},\hat{\delta}^{V_{i+1}}_{U}
satisfies
d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{U},\hat{\delta}^{V_{i+1}}_{U})<B^{2}
,

•

(\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{U})^{\prime}=\hat{x}^{\prime}_{U}
for all
i
, and

•

\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}=\hat{x}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
, with
\lambda
the unique element of
F\cup\Lambda
labeling
\hat{x}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
.

As a consequence of the last item, we see that
\hat{x}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}\neq\hat{a}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
for any
a\in F
, and hence
\hat{x}^{\prime}
is not canonical, as required.

For the rest of item (1), it remains to prove that
\Delta|_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
and
\Delta|_{\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}}:\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}\to(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})^{\prime}
are surjective.

Surjectivity of
\Delta
: Let
\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
. We will build
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\Delta(\hat{x})=\hat{y}
by a domain-wise argument, by a construction closely related to the construction of
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Y}
in Section
9
. As in that construction, the idea is to pick out a point of
C_{U}\subset\widehat{T}_{U}
by using
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal domains, and then show that the corresponding tuple is
0
-consistent. Set
C_{U}=\Delta^{-1}_{U}(\hat{y}_{U})
.

We begin by dealing with two easy cases:

•

If
U\in\mathcal{U}
be such that
\hat{y}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}^{\prime}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then we set
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
.

•

If
\hat{y}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
is not a marked or cluster point, then
C_{U}
contains no marked or cluster points, and we let
\hat{x}_{U}
be any point therein.

The general case is when
\hat{y}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
is a marked or cluster point, and we construct
\hat{x}_{U}
by induction on
\sqsubset
-level in
\mathcal{U}
.

The base case is when
U
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal, and hence
\hat{y}_{U}
is a marked point of
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
(since
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
has no cluster points). Note that
\mathrm{diam}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(C_{U})<B^{2}
, but it is possible that
\Delta_{U}:\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
identifies distinct marked points of
\widehat{T}_{U}
. We need to choose one of them.

Claim 10.3
.

If
W,Z\in\{V\in\mathcal{U}|V\pitchfork U,\mathrm{and}\hskip 7.22743pt\hat{y}_{V}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}\}
, then
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{Z}_{U}
.

Proof of Claim
10.3
.

Suppose instead that
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{Z}_{U}
. Then
W\pitchfork Z
, and Lemma
10.8
(which appears after the proof of the theorem) implies that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{Z}_{W}
and
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{Z}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{Z}
. But since
\hat{y}_{W}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{Z}_{W}
and
\hat{y}_{Z}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{Z}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{Z}
, we get a contradiction of
0
-consistency of
\hat{y}
.
∎

Observe that we did not require that
U
was
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal for the above claim, which will be useful later.

What Claim
10.3
allows us to do is define
\hat{x}_{U}
for
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal
U
as follows:

•

If
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}=\{V\in\mathcal{U}|V\pitchfork U\hskip 7.22743pt\mathrm{and}\hskip 7.22743pt\hat{y}_{W}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime}\}\neq\emptyset
, then we set
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
for any (and hence all, by the claim)
W\in\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}
.

•

If
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}=\emptyset
, then we let
\hat{x}_{U}
be any marked point in
C_{U}
.

At this point, we also want to define
l_{U}\in F\cup\Lambda
to be the collection of elements
a\in F\cup\Lambda
so that
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{a}_{U}
. We set
l_{U}=\emptyset
when
\hat{y}_{U}
(and hence
\hat{x}_{U}
) is not a marked point. These labels will play a similar role as they did in the construction of
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
, see especially Subsection
9.2
.

For the inductive step, suppose that
U
is not
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal and that we have defined
\hat{x}_{V}
for all
V\sqsubset U
and, in particular, for all
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal
V\in\mathcal{U}
.

Now if
V\sqsubset U
and
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\lambda}_{V}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{V}
for some
\lambda\in\Lambda
, then we set
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. Similarly, if there exists
V\sqsubset U
so that
\hat{x}_{V}\in\widehat{T}_{V}
is not a marked, ray, or cluster point, then we set
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. Note that in these cases, we either have that
\hat{y}_{V}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{V}^{\prime}
or
\hat{y}_{V}
is not a marked point, ray, or cluster point, and so
\hat{y}_{U}=(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime}
by
0
-consistency of
\hat{y}
, meaning that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C_{U}
, so that we have
\hat{x}_{U}\in C_{U}
, as required.

In the general case,
\hat{x}_{V}\in\widehat{T}_{V}
is either a marked or cluster point (and not a ray) for every
V\sqsubset U
. Set
C=C_{U}
and let
\mathcal{V}_{U}
denote the set of
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal domains
V\sqsubset U
so that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C
(or equivalently,
(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime}=\hat{y}_{U}
). For each
V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
, set
C^{V}_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{C}(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\cup\pi_{C}(l_{V}))
, namely the convex hull in
C
of
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
and the projections of the marked points or rays labeled by the elements of
l_{V}
down to the convex subset
C
. The following claim is an “exact” analogous version of Lemma
9.9
:

Claim 10.4
.

For every
V,W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
, we have
C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\neq\emptyset
. Moreover:

(1)

At least one of
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
or
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\in C^{V}_{U}
holds.

(2)

If
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
but
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\notin C^{V}_{U}
, then
l_{V}\subset l_{W}
and
C^{V}_{U}\subset C^{W}_{U}
(and hence
C^{V}_{U}=C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}
).

Proof of Claim
10.4
.

We may assume that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
, and hence that
V\pitchfork W
. If
C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}=\emptyset
, then
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\notin C^{W}_{U}
and
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\notin C^{V}_{U}
, and it follows that
l_{V}\cap l_{W}=\emptyset
. The BGI property of Lemma
7.14
and Item (6) of Lemma
6.13
imply that
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}\notin\Delta^{-1}_{V}(\hat{y}_{V})
and
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}\notin\Delta^{-1}_{W}(\hat{y}_{W})
, and hence
\hat{y}_{V}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V})^{\prime}
and
\hat{y}_{W}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W})^{\prime}
, which is a contradiction. This proves the main part of the claim and also item (1).

For item (2), suppose that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
but
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\notin C^{V}_{U}
. It follows that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
is contained in one of the components of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\}
which is chosen by
l_{W}
, while the components of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\}
chosen by
l_{V}
do not contain
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
. Suppose that
a\in l_{V}-l_{W}
. Then
\hat{a}_{U}
is a marked point in a component of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\}
not containing
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
, and so
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
separates
\hat{a}_{U}
from
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
. Since every leaf of
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a marked point, it follows that there exists some
b\in l_{W}
so that
\hat{b}_{U}
is contained in the component of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\}
containing
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, with
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
separating
\hat{b}_{U}
from
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
. It follows then that the BGI property of Lemma
7.14
implies that
\hat{a}_{W}=\hat{b}_{W}=\hat{x}_{W}
, which is a contradiction. Hence
l_{V}\subset l_{W}
. The fact that
C^{V}_{U}\subset C^{W}_{U}
now follows from the facts that
l_{V}\subset l_{W}
and
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
, completing the proof of (2) and the lemma.

∎

With this claim in hand, we have two main subcases, whose specifics we state as the following claim, part of which is analogous to Lemma
9.10
:

Claim 10.5
.

The following hold:

(1)

If
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}\neq\emptyset
, then either

(a)

The diameter of
C_{U}
is infinite and there exists some
\lambda\in\Lambda
so
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial C_{U}
and
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}=\{\lambda\}
, or

(b)

Otherwise, every
a\in\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}
labels a marked point of
C_{U}
(i.e., does not label an infinite end of
C
).

(2)

If
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{diam}(C_{U})=\infty
, then there exist
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\Lambda
labeling the infinite ends of
C_{U}
, and
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
, so that

(a)

l_{V_{i}}=F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda_{i}\}
for each
i
,

(b)

If
C_{fin}=\bigcap_{i}C^{V_{i}}_{U}
, then
C_{fin}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathrm{diam}(C_{fin})<\infty
, and

(c)

C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\cap C_{fin}\neq\emptyset
for all
V,W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
.

•

Consequently, combining the above with the Helly property for trees provides that
\bigcap_{V_{U}}C^{V}_{U}\neq\emptyset
and has finite diameter.

Proof of Claim
10.5
.

Suppose first that
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}\neq\emptyset
. Suppose that the diameter of
C_{U}
is infinite and that
\lambda\in\Lambda
with
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial C_{U}
(by which we mean that
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
represents a ray in the Gromov boundary of the tree
C_{U}
), with
\lambda\in\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}
. We will prove that
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}=\{\lambda\}
.

Since we are assuming that
\hat{y}_{U}\notin\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, there exists a sequence
V_{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
with
\hat{\delta}^{V_{n}}_{U}
monotonically escaping out the end of
C_{U}
corresponding to
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
, as earlier in the proof of this theorem. Taking an element
V_{n}
of this sequence sufficiently far out the end corresponding to
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
gives that
\hat{\delta}^{V_{n}}_{U}
partitions
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
away from every other marked point and branched point of
\widehat{T}_{U}
. As a consequence
C^{V_{n}}_{U}
contains only
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
, and hence
l_{V_{n}}=\{\lambda\}
, which forces
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}=\{\lambda\}
, as required.

As a consequence, if (1)(a) does not hold, then the only other possibility is that every
a\in\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}
labels a marked point of
C_{U}
(i.e.,
a\in F\cup\Lambda
but
\hat{a}_{U}\notin\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
), proving the dichotomy of items (1)(a) and (1)(b).

To deduce (2), suppose that
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{diam}(C_{U})=\infty
. Let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\Lambda
label the infinite ends of
C_{U}
. For each
i
, there exists a sequence of elements of
\mathcal{V}_{U}
whose
\hat{\delta}
-sets monotonically escape out the end corresponding to
(\hat{\lambda}_{i})_{U}
. Since
\lambda_{i}\notin\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}=\emptyset
, it follows that infinitely-many of the elements
V
in this sequence must satisfy
l_{V_{i}}=F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda_{i}\}
. Choose such a
V_{i}\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
for each
i
. Observe that
C_{fin}=\bigcap_{i}C^{V_{i}}_{U}\neq\emptyset
, as
\lambda_{j}\in l_{V_{i}}
for all
i\neq j
, and also
\mathrm{diam}(C_{fin})<\infty
, as every infinite end of
C_{U}
is cut off in this intersection.

As in the proof of Claim
9.11
, set
E(\Lambda,C_{U})
to be the set of
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
so that
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
separates
(\hat{\lambda}_{i})_{U}
from
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{U}
for some
i
. As in that proof, we have the following observations:

(1)

Each
W\in E(\Lambda,C_{U})
uniquely determines an
i
, and we necessarily have
l_{W}=F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda_{i}\}
and so
C_{fin}\subset C^{V_{i}}_{U}\subset C^{W}_{U}
.

(2)

On the other hand, if
W\notin E(\Lambda,C_{U})
, then the definition of each
V_{i}
implies that
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\in C^{V_{i}}_{U}
for each
i
, and hence
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\in C_{fin}
.

With these observations in hand, let
V,W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
and we prove that
C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\cap C_{fin}\neq\emptyset
for all
V,W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
, which will complete the proof of the claim. If
V,W\in E(\Lambda,C_{U})
, then observation (1) implies that
C_{fin}\subset C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}
. If
V\in E(\Lambda,C_{U})
while
W
is not, then combining both observations gives
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\in C_{fin}\subset C^{V}_{U}
, which is what we wanted. Finally, if
V,W\notin E(\Lambda,C_{U})
, then Claim
10.4
implies that, without loss of generality,
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
, while observation (2) says that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C_{fin}
, implying
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C^{V}_{U}\cap C^{W}_{U}\cap C_{fin}
. This completes the proof of part (2) and hence of the claim.
∎

Observe that
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}C^{V}_{U}
may contain many marked points and cluster points, and we want to be able to choose a unique coordinate
\hat{x}_{U}
inside of this intersection. To do so, we need to use the other information encoded in these marked and cluster points. Claim
10.3
allows us to decide between the marked points, and the following claim allows us to choose between the cluster points:

First, set
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}=\{V\sqsubset U|(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}(\hat{y}_{U})\neq\hat{y}_{V}\}
. We need a claim which is the nesting version of Claim
10.3
:

Claim 10.6
.

Suppose
V,W\in\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}
, then
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
.

Proof of Claim
10.6
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, so that
V\pitchfork W
. Then item (10) of Lemma
7.14
implies that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})
and
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U})
. But then
\hat{y}_{W}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W})^{\prime}=(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W})^{\prime}((\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime})
and
\hat{y}_{V}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V})^{\prime}=(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}((\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U})^{\prime})
, which contradicts
0
-consistency of
\hat{y}
, completing the proof.
∎

What Claim
10.6
allows us to do is choose between cluster points in
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}C^{V}_{U}
, in the same way Claim
10.3
will allow us to choose between marked points.

We need one final claim, which will allow us to deal with the situation when
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}C^{V}_{U}
contains both marked points and cluster points, since it says that we are forced into a consistent choice:

Claim 10.7
.

The following holds:

(1)

If there exists
V\pitchfork U
so that
\hat{y}_{V}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}
, then for all
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
with
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, we have
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}
.

(2)

If there exists
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
with
\hat{y}_{W}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W})^{\prime}(\hat{y}_{U})
, then for all
V\pitchfork U
with
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, we have
\hat{y}_{V}=(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}
.

•

In particular, if
V\in\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}
and
W\in\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}
, then
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
.

Proof.

Suppose
V\pitchfork U
as in item (1), and let
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
with
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. Then
W\pitchfork V
, and since
W\sqsubset U
, we have
(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V})^{\prime}=(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}\neq\hat{y}_{V}
, and hence
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}
, by definition of
\hat{x}_{W}
.

Now suppose
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
, as in item (2), and that
V\pitchfork U
with
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. Then
W\pitchfork V
. By
0
-consistency of
\hat{y}
, we have that
\hat{y}_{U}=(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U})^{\prime}
. But since
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
, we have
(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U})^{\prime}
, and so
\hat{y}_{V}=(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}=(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V})^{\prime}
since
W\sqsubset U
. This completes the proof.
∎

We are finally ready to define our tuple
\hat{x}
, which we do domain-wise for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
:

(1)

If
\hat{y}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}^{\prime}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then we set
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
.

(2)

If
\hat{y}_{U}
is not a marked or cluster point of
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then we let
\hat{x}_{U}\in C_{U}
be any point in
\Delta^{-1}_{U}(\hat{y}_{U})
.

(3)

For any
U
of types (1) or (2): If
V\pitchfork U
or
U\sqsubset V
, we set
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, and if
U\sqsubset V
, we set
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{x}_{U})
.

(4)

If
U
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal and
\hat{y}_{U}
is a marked point of
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then either

(a)

The set
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}=\{W\in\mathcal{U}|W\pitchfork U\hskip 7.22743pt\textrm{and }\hskip 7.22743pt(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W})^{\prime}\neq\hat{y}_{W}\}
is nonempty, and we set
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
for any (and hence all by Claim
10.3
)
W\in\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}
;

(b)

\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}=\{V\sqsubset U|(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}(\hat{y}_{U})\neq\hat{y}_{V}\hskip 7.22743pt\textrm{and}\hskip 7.22743pt(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W})^{\prime}(\hat{y}_{U})\neq\hat{y}_{W}\}
is nonempty, and we set
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
for any (and hence all by Claim
10.6
)
V\in\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}
; or

(c)

\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}
and
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}
are both empty, and we set
\hat{x}_{U}
to be any point of
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
contained in
C_{U}
, in particular a point which is not a cluster or marked point.

(5)

Proceeding inductively, if
U
is not in cases (1)–(4) above, then Claim
10.5
gives the following possibilities:

(a)

The diameter of
C_{U}
is infinite and there is some
\lambda\in\Lambda
so that
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial C_{U}
and
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}l_{V}=\{\lambda\}
. In this case, we set
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
.

(b)

We have
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}C^{V}_{U}\neq\emptyset
and has finite diameter. Then there are three cases:

(i)

\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
for any
W\in\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}
if
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}\neq\emptyset
,

(ii)

\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
for any
V\in\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}
when
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}\neq\emptyset
, or

(iii)

Otherwise, both
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\pitchfork}
and
\mathcal{W}_{U}^{\sqsubset}
are empty, and we set
\hat{x}_{U}
to be any point in
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}\cap C_{U}
.

Note that the choice in item (
5
) above is well-defined by Claim
10.7
.

Finally, we prove that
\hat{x}=(\hat{x}_{U})
is
0
-consistent by a case-wise analysis, similar to the proof of Proposition
9.1
. Showing this will complete the proof of surjectivity of
\Delta:\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\to\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
.

U\pitchfork V
:
We may assume that
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, for we are done otherwise. Then
\hat{y}_{V}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}
, and so
\hat{y}_{U}=(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime}
by
0
-consistency of
\hat{y}
. It follows that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C_{U}=\Delta^{-1}_{U}(\hat{y}_{U})
, and note that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\hat{a}_{U}
for some
a\in F\cup\Lambda
by Lemma
7.14
, with
\hat{a}_{U}
not representing a ray in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. If
U
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal, then our choice of
\hat{x}_{U}
in (
4
) says that
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, as required.

Now suppose that
U
is not
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal. Set
\mathcal{V}_{U}
to be the set of
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal
W\sqsubset U
with
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\in C_{U}
. Let
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
be so that
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. Then
W\pitchfork V
and
(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V})^{\prime}=(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}\neq\hat{y}_{V}
, and so
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}=\hat{a}_{W}
, with the first equality following from our choice in (
4
) and the last equality by
0
-consistency of
a
. But this says that
a\in l_{W}
, and hence
\hat{a}_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
for all
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
(including those for which
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\hat{a}_{U}
).

It follows then that
\hat{a}_{U}\in\bigcap_{W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}C^{W}_{U}
and so
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{a}_{U}
by definition of
\hat{x}
in item (
5(b)i
) above.

V\sqsubset U
:
We may assume that
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{x}_{U})
, for we are done otherwise. Then
\hat{y}_{V}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V})^{\prime}(\hat{y}_{U})
, and
0
-consistency of
\hat{y}
implies that
\hat{y}_{U}=(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})^{\prime}
, and thus
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C_{U}=\Delta^{-1}_{U}(\hat{y}_{U})
. We claim that for all
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
, we have
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
.

To see this, is suffices to consider
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
with
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. Then
W\pitchfork V
. But
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{x}_{U})
, so if also
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\notin C^{W}_{U}
, then
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}
, while also
\hat{x}_{W}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}
, which both use item (10) of Lemma
7.14
. But then
\hat{y}_{V}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V})^{\prime}
and
\hat{y}_{W}\neq(\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W})^{\prime}
, which contradicts
0
-consistency of
\hat{y}
.

Hence
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in C^{W}_{U}
for all
W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}
. It follows that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in\bigcap_{W\in\mathcal{V}_{U}}C^{W}_{U}
, and then (
5(b)ii
) says that
\hat{x}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
.

Hence
(\hat{x})
is
0
-consistent. Since
\Delta(\hat{x})=\hat{y}
because
\hat{x}_{U}\in\Delta^{-1}_{U}(\hat{y}_{U})
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we are done with the proof of surjectivity of
\Delta:\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\to\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
.

\Delta|_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a quasi-isometry
: Since
\Delta|_{\mathcal{Q}}
is surjective by the above, it suffices to relate distances. For that, note that
d_{\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}}\geq d_{\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}}
, so we only need to prove that
d_{\mathcal{Q}}\prec d_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
, and for this it suffices to bound the distances in each
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

Let
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\mathcal{Q}
with
\hat{x}^{\prime}=\Delta(\hat{x})
and
\hat{y}^{\prime}=\Delta(\hat{y})
. We may assume that
\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{y}_{U}
for any
U\in\mathcal{U}
that we will consider. We begin by making a useful reduction.

Observe that item (8) of Lemma
7.14
implies that there are at most boundedly-many domains
U\in\mathcal{U}
for which
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
are in the same component of
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
, where the bound is controlled by
\mathfrak{S}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
. For such
U
, we have
d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})\leq d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\hat{x}^{\prime}_{U},\hat{y}^{\prime}_{U})+B^{2}
because at most
B
-many subtrees of diameter
B
have been deleted from that edge. Hence we may deal with these boundedly-many domains with an additive constant in the distance estimate comparing
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
and
d_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}(\hat{x}^{\prime},\hat{y}^{\prime})
. Hereafter, we assume that at least one of
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
coincides with a cluster, or they are separated by at least one cluster.

Figure 16
.
The proof of Theorem
10.2
: In case (1), we consider a
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal domain like
V
, which is guaranteed to have a large diameter while also only containing a bounded number of trimming subtrees, and hence trimming only affects the distance between
\hat{x}_{V},\hat{y}_{V}
by a bounded amount. In case (2), the geodesic in
\widehat{T}_{U}
between
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
(pink) must cross a number of marked cluster points and hence potentially arbitrarily many trimmed subtrees (blue). Some of these may be sporadic (green), but there are only boundedly-many of these across all
\widehat{T}_{U}
. Hence the cluster points labeled by
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite domains decompose
[\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}]_{\widehat{T}_{U}}
into components which contain only boundedly-many trimmed subtrees, allowing for these domains to account for the lost distance by a bounded multiplicative constant.

There are two cases, (1)
U
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
