-minimal and (2) otherwise.

Case (1)
:
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
has boundedly-many components, where the bound depends on
|F\cup\Lambda|
, as each collapsed point is a marked point. So there are at most
B|F\cup\Lambda|
-many subtrees of diameter at most
B
in
\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
being collapsed. By item (8) of Lemma
7.14
and Proposition
9.4
, there are at most boundedly-many (in
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
) such
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal
U
for which either one of
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
is not a cluster point or
U
is not bipartite. We may deal with such
U
by increasing the additive constant in the distance estimate.

The general case is where
U
is bipartite and
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
are the distinct endpoint clusters of the interval
\widehat{T}_{U}
(Lemma
9.3
). In this case,

d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\hat{x}^{\prime}_{U},\hat{y}^{\prime}_{U})\geq d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})-B^{2}\geq K-B^{2}-N,

where
N=N(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
by item (6) of Lemma
7.14
. Hence by choosing
K=K(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
sufficiently large, we can guarantee that

d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})\leq d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\hat{x}^{\prime}_{U},\hat{y}^{\prime}_{U})+B^{2}\leq(1+B^{2})d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\hat{x}^{\prime}_{U},\hat{y}^{\prime}_{U})

and thus account for these domains via a bounded multiplicative error. Moreover, by choosing
K
sufficiently large, we can guarantee that
\mathrm{diam}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})
is as large as necessary, which will allow us to use a multiplicative error and these
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite domains to account for domains up the
\sqsubset
-lattice in the other case.

Case (2)
: Suppose that
U\in\mathcal{U}
is not
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal. Since
\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{y}_{U}
, there are some
k\leq 2
, intervals
E_{1},\dots,E_{k}\subset\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
, and cluster points
C_{1},\dots,C_{k-1}
with
E_{i},E_{i+1}
connected at
C_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
, and each
C_{i}
separating
\hat{x}_{U}
from
\hat{y}_{U}
.

Note that it is possible that each
C_{i}
is
sporadic
, namely
C_{i}
either is

•

a marked point cluster, or

•

an internal cluster containing no
10E^{\prime}
-bipartite domains for which
U
is a witness (Definition
9.2
).

However, the set of such sporadic clusters across
\widehat{T}_{U}
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
is bounded in terms of
|F\cup\Lambda|
and
10E^{\prime}
, which is bounded in terms of
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
. In particular,
k<N_{s}=N_{s}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)
. In particular, the number of edge components adjacent to such clusters is bounded, and so the total contributed distance loss between
\hat{x},\hat{y}
and
\hat{x}^{\prime},\hat{y}^{\prime}
from collapsing at most
B
-many subtrees in any of these components is bounded in terms of
\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|
, and
B
. Thus we can deal with this lost distance with an additive constant.

Hence, for every
U\in\mathcal{U}
which is not
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal, the geodesic
[\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}]\subset\widehat{T}_{U}
can be decomposed along non-sporadic cluster points
C^{\prime}_{1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{n}
so that each edge in the complement of the
C^{\prime}_{i}
has only boundedly-many bounded diameter subintervals collapsed. In other words, there exists
B^{\prime\prime}=B^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|,B)>0
so that

d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(C^{\prime}_{i},C^{\prime}_{i+1})\leq d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\Delta_{U}(C^{\prime}_{i}),\Delta_{U}(C^{\prime}_{i+1}))+B^{\prime\prime}.

As the
C^{\prime}_{i}
are non-sporadic clusters, each coincides with
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{U}
for some
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal
10E^{\prime}
-bipartite domain
V_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
for which
U
is a witness. This means (Definition
9.2
) that the
10E^{\prime}
-neighborhood of
\delta^{V_{i}}_{U}
avoids all marked points and branched points of
T_{U}
. Since
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{U}
for each
i
, we have that
\hat{x}_{V_{i}},\hat{y}_{V_{i}}
must coincide with the marked point clusters contained in the two components of
T_{U}-\delta^{V_{i}}_{U}
. In particular,
\hat{x}_{V_{i}}\neq\hat{y}_{V_{i}}
are at distinct marked point clusters, and thus
d_{\widehat{T}_{V_{i}}}(\hat{x}_{V_{i}},\hat{y}_{V_{i}})\succ K
because
V_{i}
is bipartite and
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal (this uses item (6) of Lemma
7.14
). Moreover, by choosing
K
sufficiently large, we can guarantee that
d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}}(\hat{x}^{\prime}_{V_{i}},\hat{y}^{\prime}_{V_{i}})>1
. As a consequence, we have

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(C^{\prime}_{i},C^{\prime}_{i+1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\Delta_{U}(C^{\prime}_{i}),\Delta_{U}(C^{\prime}_{i+1}))+B^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\Delta_{U}(C^{\prime}_{i}),\Delta_{U}(C^{\prime}_{i+1}))+B^{\prime\prime}d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}}(\hat{x}^{\prime}_{V_{i}},\hat{y}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}).

Hence we can account for the lost distance along
[\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}]
between each pair of successive non-sporadic clusters
C^{\prime}_{i},C^{\prime}_{i+1}
by distances in the
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite
V_{i}
. Similarly, we can use
V_{1},V_{k}
to account for any lost distance between
\hat{x}_{U},C^{\prime}_{1}
and
C^{\prime}_{n},\hat{y}_{U}
, respectively. Since each domain in
\mathcal{U}
nests into at most boundedly-many domains in
\mathcal{U}
by the Covering Lemma
4.8
, the proof of case (2) is complete.

Finally, the “moreover” part is clear: if only boundedly-many trees are trimmed, then the global distance is only decreased by the sum of all of the diameters of the collapsed subtrees. Hence only an additive error is necessary. This completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma was used in the proof:

Lemma 10.8
.

Suppose that
U,V,W\in\mathcal{U}
are pairwise transverse and
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
. Then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}
and
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}
.

Proof.

By choosing the cluster separation constant
r
sufficiently large, we can guarantee that
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},\rho^{W}_{U})>100E
since
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U}
. Thus if also
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}
, we also have
d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V},\rho^{W}_{V})>100E
. Now choose
x\in\mathbf{P}_{W}
the product region in
\mathcal{X}
for
W
(Subsection
2.6
), making
d_{U}(x,\rho^{W}_{U})<E
and
d_{V}(x,\rho^{W}_{V})<E
. It follows that
d_{U}(x,\rho^{V}_{U})>E
and
d_{V}(x,\rho^{U}_{V})>E
, which contradicts the
E
-consistency of
x
. This completes the proof.
∎

10.1.
Replacing
\widehat{T}_{U}
with simplicial trees

In this subsection, we quickly observe how to use Tree Trimming
10.2
to replace all
\widehat{T}_{U}
with simplicial trees which encode the relative projection data into the vertices while only changing
\mathcal{Q}
up to quasi-isometry with controlled constants. This refinement will be necessary produce the cubical structure of
\mathcal{Q}
via a direct argument later in Section
13
.

The goal is to replace each
\widehat{T}_{U}
with a simplicial tree (i.e., a 1-dimensional cube complex), where all cluster points and marked points are vertices. The issue is that the intervals between cluster and marked points can have non-integer lengths, and the idea is to trim any such interval by collapsing a subinterval of length less than
1
. Tree Trimming
10.2
will tell us that the resulting quotient map is a uniform quasi-isometry.

To setup notation, fix
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Observe that each component
E\subset\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
is a tree with valence bounded by
|F\cup\Lambda|
and
\mathcal{X}
, with leaves all cluster points or marked points of
\widehat{T}_{U}
, and with no internal marked or cluster points. By choosing the cluster separation constant
r
sufficiently large, we we may choose intervals
J_{1},\dots,J_{n}
, with

•

n
controlled by
\mathcal{X}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
;

•

each
J_{i}
adjacent to a distinct leaf of
E
;

•

|J_{i}|<1
for each
i
;

•

the distances between each pair of leaves in
E
is larger than
2
;

•

and if we collapse each
J_{i}
to a point, the resulting tree
E\to E^{\prime}
has the property that all distances between leaves in
E^{\prime}
are integers.

Let
\Delta_{U}:\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
denote the map that quotients each
J_{i}
to a point for all such intervals across all edge components
E\subset\widehat{T}^{e}_{U}
for every
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Observe that each collapsed interval
J
is identified with the cluster point to which it was adjacent. We again refer to the images of marked and cluster points in the
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
using the same terminology. Note that by construction, every pair of cluster points or marked points in each
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
are at integer distance, and thus we can subdivide the edges of each
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
to make it a simplicial tree with marked and cluster points at vertices.

Finally, set
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, let
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
be the set of
0
-consistent tuples, and let
\Delta:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
all be as defined in the previous subsection. It follows from Tree Trimming
10.2
that the map
\Delta:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a uniform quasi-isometry, since only boundedly-many (in
\mathcal{X}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
) subtrees of diameter at most
1
are collapsed in each edge component.

The following summarizes this discussion:

Corollary 10.9
.

For any finite collection
F\cup\Lambda
of interior points, boundary points, and hierarchy rays in an HHS
\mathcal{X}
, let
\{\widehat{T}_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be the family of collapsed trees and
0
-consistent set as produced in Section
7
. Then up to a uniform quasi-isometry of
\mathcal{Q}
(in
\mathcal{X}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|)
, we may assume that every
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a simplicial tree with all collapsed points and marked points being vertices.

11.
Coarse surjectivity of
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}

In this section, we prove that
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is coarsely surjective, which we do by proving that the composition
\widehat{\Psi}\circ\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}
is coarsely constant. Namely:

Theorem 11.1
.

There exists
B_{0}=B_{0}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,K)>0
and a map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
so that for any
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\widehat{\Psi}\circ\widehat{\Omega}(\hat{x}),\hat{x})<B_{0}
.

As a corollary of this and Theorem
8.1
, we get the following:

Corollary 11.2
.

There exists
L^{\prime}=L^{\prime}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,K)>0
so that
\Phi:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is an
(L^{\prime},L^{\prime})
-quasi-isometry. Moreover,
\widehat{\Psi}
and
\widehat{\Omega}
are quasi-inverses.

Proof.

The fact that
\Phi
is a quasi-isometric embedding follows from Theorem
8.1
plus the Distance Formula Theorem
2.9
, while Theorem
11.1
says that
\Phi
is coarsely-dense, making it a quasi-isometry.
∎

Remark 11.3
.

Note that our proof of the Distance Formula
2.9
(in Corollary
14.3
below) does not rely on either of the above results.

We will break the proof of Theorem
11.1
into two steps, first analyzing the composition at the level of the trees
T_{U}
, and then the collapsed trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
.

11.1.
Coarse surjectivity of
\Psi

We first prove that
\Psi:H\to\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{0}}
is coarsely surjective, where
\Psi(H)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{0}}
for
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
by Lemma
6.16
. To do this, we need to define a map from
\Phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\to H
for any
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, which we do as follows:

Recall that Proposition
6.17
provides a
\beta=\beta(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|,K,\alpha)>0
so that
\Phi(\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha})\subset\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\mathcal{C}(U)
consists of
\beta
-consistent tuples. Thus we can define a map from
\Phi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\to H
by

\Phi_{\alpha}=\mathrm{ret}_{H}\circ\mathrm{Real}_{\beta}\circ\Phi:\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\to H,

where
\mathrm{Real}_{\beta}
assigns a point of
\mathcal{X}
to any
\beta
-consistent tuple, and
\mathrm{ret}_{H}:\mathcal{X}\to H
is the coarse Lipschitz retraction provided by Lemma
6.6
, whose quality is controlled by
\mathcal{X}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

We now state and prove our first main step of the coarse inverse theorem. One interpretation is that, with a distance formula-like “metric” on
\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
, the composition
\Psi\circ\Phi_{\alpha}
is coarsely the identity. But in fact, the proposition gives much more information, since we get explicit bounds on component-wise distances, including a global bound on all component-wise distances (depending on
K
), and a bound on the number of domains satisfying a medium-size distance bound (with this size independent of
K
).

We note that, in the statement below, we need the extra flexibility of dealing with
\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, because Proposition
9.1
provides an
\alpha_{\omega}=\alpha_{\omega}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that
\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{\omega}}
, and likely
\alpha_{\omega}>\alpha_{0}
.

Proposition 11.4
.

For any
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, there exist
D_{1}=D_{1}(K,\alpha,\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that for all
x=(x_{V})\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
, we have

(1)

For all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\psi_{U}\circ\Phi_{\alpha}(x))<D_{1}

(2)

\#\{U\in\mathcal{U}|d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\psi_{U}\circ\Phi_{\alpha}(x))>2\alpha+3E^{\prime}\}<D_{1}
.

•

In particular, by choosing
L>2\alpha+E^{\prime}
, we get

\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}[d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\psi_{U}\circ\Phi_{\alpha}(x)]_{L}<D_{1}^{2}.

Proof.

The proof of (1) is essentially a definition chase: Let
x=(x_{U})\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
, let
(x^{\prime}_{V})=\Phi_{\alpha}(x)
be the
\beta=\beta(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|,K,\alpha)>0
consistent tuple provided by Proposition
6.17
, and let
y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Real}_{\beta}(\Phi_{\alpha}(x))
be a realization.

Let
\theta_{\beta}=\theta_{\beta}(\alpha_{0},\mathfrak{S})>0
be the constant produced by the Realization Theorem
2.4
for the
\beta
-consistent tuple
(x^{\prime}_{V})
, so that
d_{V}(y^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{V})<\theta_{\beta}
for each
V\in\mathfrak{S}
, where we recall that
\mathfrak{S}
is the full domain index set for the ambient HHS
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
.

Observe that since the image of
\phi_{V}:T_{V}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
is within Hausdorff distance
C=C(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
of
H_{V}=\mathrm{hull}_{V}(F\cup\Lambda)
, we get that
x^{\prime}_{V}\in\mathcal{N}_{C}(H_{V})
for each
V\in\mathfrak{S}
. It follows that
y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{hull}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\theta_{\beta}+C}(F\cup\Lambda)
(see Definition
6.4
). In particular, if
y\in\mathrm{ret}_{H}(y^{\prime})
, then
d_{V}(y,y^{\prime})<\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}(\beta,\mathfrak{S})
for all
V\in\mathfrak{S}
. It follows that
d_{V}(y,x^{\prime}_{V})<\theta_{0}+\theta_{\beta}+C
for all
V\in\mathfrak{S}
.

On the other hand,
y=\mathrm{ret}_{H}(y^{\prime})\in H
, and so
d_{U}(y,H_{U})<\theta
, where
\theta=\theta(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)
is the hull constant defining
H
. Then since
d_{Haus}(\phi_{U}(T_{U}),H_{U})<C
and
\phi^{-1}_{U}
is a
(C,C)
-quasi-isometry, we see that
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\psi_{U}\circ\Phi_{\alpha}(x))<d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\phi^{-1}_{U}\circ p_{U}(y)))<C(\theta_{0}+\theta_{\beta}+\theta+2C)+C
, giving us the desired bound for (1).

For the proof of (2), let
y=(y_{U})\in\Psi\circ\Phi_{\alpha}(x)
. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{U\in\mathcal{U}|d_{T}(x_{U},y_{U})>2\alpha+3E^{\prime}\}
. Set
K_{1}=\alpha+E^{\prime}
and choose
K_{2}=K_{2}(\mathfrak{S},|F|,D_{1},\alpha)>0
to be determined shortly. By assuming, for a contradiction, that
\#\mathcal{V}
is sufficiently large, we will be able to employ Strong Passing-up
4.7
to produce a domain
W\in\mathcal{U}
for which
d_{T_{W}}(x_{U},y_{U})>D_{1}
, violating the first conclusion of the current proposition.

By passing to a subset if necessary, we may assume that
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(a,b)
for some fixed
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
and that
\#V>P_{1}(K_{1},K_{2})
, where
P_{1}
is the function provided by Proposition
4.3
, since otherwise
\#\mathcal{V}
is bounded as desired.

Hence by Proposition
4.7
, there exists a subset
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
and
W\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{2}}(a,b)
so that
V\sqsubset W
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
and

\mathrm{diam}_{W}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\rho^{V}_{W}\right)>K_{2}.

Figure 17
.
The proof of case (2) of Proposition
11.4
: Not both of
x_{U}
and
y_{U}
(both blue) can be close to
\delta^{V}_{U}
in
T_{U}
, and similarly for
V
. The BGI property then forces
x_{W}
and
y_{W}
to be relatively close to
\delta^{U}_{W}
and
\delta^{V}_{W}
, respectively, which then forces
d_{T_{W}}(x_{W},y_{W})
to be larger then
D_{1}
, violating the bound from item (1).

Observe that we may choose
K_{2}=K_{2}(\mathfrak{S},|F|,D_{1},\alpha)>0
sufficiently large so that
\mathrm{diam}_{T_{W}}\left(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\delta^{V}_{W}\right)>D_{1}+2E^{\prime}
. Let
U,V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
realize this diameter, hence forcing
U\pitchfork V
.

Now if
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\delta^{V}_{U})>\alpha
and
d_{T_{U}}(y_{U},\delta^{V}_{U})>\alpha
, then
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},y_{V})<2\alpha
since both are
\alpha
-consistent, contradicting the assumption that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},y_{V})>2\alpha+3E^{\prime}
. Consequently, exactly one of
x_{U}
and
y_{U}
is
\alpha
-close to
\delta^{V}_{U}
in
T_{U}
, and similarly for
V
. Without loss of generality, assume that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<\alpha
and
d_{T_{V}}(y_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<\alpha
.

Also observe that the geodesic
[a_{W},b_{W}]
in
T_{W}
(which is possibly a bi-infinite geodesic) between
a_{W},b_{W}
must pass within
E^{\prime}
of both
\delta^{U}_{W},\delta^{V}_{W}
by the BGI property (item (5) of Lemma
6.13
). Since
d_{T_{W}}(\delta^{U}_{W},\delta^{V}_{W})>K_{2}
, we may assume, without loss of generality, that
[a_{W},b_{W}]
first passes close to
\delta^{U}_{W}
then
\delta^{V}_{W}
. It follows then from item (6) of Lemma
6.13
that
d_{T_{U}}(b_{U},\delta^{V}_{U})<2E^{\prime}
and
d_{T_{V}}(a_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})<2E^{\prime}
.

It follows then that
d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},b_{U})>2\alpha+3E^{\prime}-2E^{\prime}>E^{\prime}
and
d_{T_{V}}(y_{V},a_{V})>2\alpha+3E^{\prime}-2E^{\prime}>E^{\prime}
. Hence the BGI property (item (5) of Lemma
6.13
) says that the geodesics in
T_{W}
between
x_{W},b_{W}
and
y_{W},a_{W}
must pass within
E^{\prime}
of
\delta^{U}_{W}
and
\delta^{V}_{W}
, respectively. It follows then that
d_{T_{W}}(x_{W},y_{W})>d_{T_{W}}(\delta^{U}_{W},\delta^{V}_{W})-2E^{\prime}\geq D_{1}+2E^{\prime}-2E^{\prime}=D_{1}
, which is a contradiction.

∎

11.2.
The map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
and coarse surjectivity

We are now ready to define the map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
using our work from Section
9
as follows. Given a tuple
(\hat{x}_{U})\in\mathcal{Q}
, let
(x_{U})_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{\omega}}
be a
\alpha_{\omega}
-consistent partial tuple, where
x_{U}\in B_{U}
as defined in Subsection
9.2
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
\alpha_{\omega}=\alpha_{\omega}(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
is provided by Proposition
9.1
. Since
(x_{U})\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{\omega}}
, we can apply the map
\Phi_{\alpha_{\omega}}:\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha_{\omega}}\to H
as defined above in Subsection
11.1
. Hence by composing these we obtain a map:

\widehat{\Omega}=\Phi_{\alpha_{\omega}}\circ\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to H.

Since
\widehat{\Psi}(H)\subset\mathcal{Q}
by Lemma
7.15
, we get that
\widehat{\Psi}\circ\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}
. We are now in a position to prove that this composition is coarsely the identity:

Proof of Theorem
11.1
.

We need to prove that there exists
B_{0}=B_{0}(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|,K)>0
so that for any
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
, if
\hat{y}\in\widehat{\Psi}\circ\widehat{\Omega}(\hat{x})
, then we have

d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})<B_{0}.

For this, we will use Proposition
11.4
, which provides
D_{1}=D_{1}(K,\alpha_{\omega},\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda)>0
so if
(x_{U})=\Omega(\hat{x})
and
(y_{U})\in\Psi\circ\widehat{\Omega}(\hat{x})
then

(1)

d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})<D_{1}
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, and

(2)

\#\{U\in\mathcal{U}|d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})>2\alpha_{\omega}+3E^{\prime}\}<D_{1}
.

Let
\mathcal{V}=\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{x}_{U}\neq\hat{y}_{U}\}
. Item (9) of Lemma
7.14
says that the number of domains
U\in\mathcal{U}
so that
\hat{x}_{U}
and
\hat{y}_{U}
are both not contained in marked point clusters is bounded solely in terms of
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
. Hence by the global bound
D_{1}
in property (1) above, we may assume that for every
V\in\mathcal{V}
, both
\hat{x}_{V},\hat{y}_{V}
are at marked point clusters.

Moreover, by Proposition
9.4
, all but boundedly-many domains in
\mathcal{V}
are bipartite, so we may again use property (1) to further reduce to the case where every
V\in\mathcal{V}
is bipartite.

Hence if we consider
\mathcal{V}_{min}
the set of
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite domains
V\in\mathcal{V}
, then each
\widehat{T}_{V}
is a non-degenerate interval by Lemma
9.7
. Thus if
V\in\mathcal{V}_{min}
, then the assumption that
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{y}_{V}
are at distinct marked cluster points implies that
d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{x}_{V},\hat{y}_{V})\succ K
by item (6) of Lemma
7.14
. Since
2\alpha_{\omega}+E^{\prime}
is independent of our choice of
K
, we can choose
K
large enough to guarantee that
\#\mathcal{V}_{min}<D_{1}
.

The next step is to bound the number of domains
V
higher in the
\sqsubset
-lattice where
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{y}_{V}
, though we will not do so directly. Instead, we observe that in all but boundedly-many domains in
\mathcal{V}
, we have that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},y_{V})<2\alpha_{\omega}+3E^{\prime}
, which is a constant independent of
K
. The idea is to now use the Tree Trimming Theorem
10.2
as follows.

Set
B=2\alpha_{\omega}+3E^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}-\mathcal{V}_{min}
be the set of domains
V\in\mathcal{U}
where
\hat{x}_{V}\neq\hat{y}_{V}
are at distinct marked cluster points and
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},y_{V})<B
, and so that at least one of the cluster points contained in
[\hat{x}_{V},\hat{y}_{V}]
is labeled by a
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite domain not in
\mathcal{V}_{min}
. Observe that all but boundedly-many cluster points satisfy this last property, by the bound on
\#\mathcal{V}_{min}
and the Covering Lemma
4.8
, which bounds the number of domains in
\mathcal{U}
into which the domains in
\mathcal{V}_{min}
can nest. In particular,
\#(\mathcal{V}-\mathcal{V}^{\prime})
is bounded in terms of
D_{1},\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
by the above reductions.

For each
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, let
A_{V}
denote the set of these segments and let
\Delta_{V}:\widehat{T}_{V}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
denote the map which collapses each segment to a point. Theorem
10.2
says that the combined map
\Delta:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a quasi-isometry, with constants depending only on
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
and
B
. Moreover, by its construction we have
d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\Delta_{U}(\hat{x}_{U}),\Delta_{U}(\hat{y}_{U}))\leq d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

Observe that
\Delta_{V}(\hat{x}_{V})=\Delta_{V}(\hat{y}_{V})
by construction. Hence we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\Delta_{U}(\hat{x}_{U}),\Delta_{U}(\hat{y}_{U}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\Delta_{U}(\hat{x}_{U}),\Delta_{U}(\hat{y}_{U}))+\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}}(\Delta_{V}(\hat{x}_{V}),\Delta_{V}(\hat{y}_{V}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}d_{\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}}(\Delta_{U}(\hat{x}_{U}),\Delta_{U}(\hat{y}_{U}))

and this final sum is bounded since each term is at most
D_{1}
by property (1) above, and
\#(\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}^{\prime})
is bounded by
D_{1},\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
, as observed above.

Hence we get that
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})
is bounded in terms of
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
, and
K
, as required, completing the proof.

∎

12.
The induced wall-structure on
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}

In this section, we analyze the combinatorial structure of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, the
0
-consistent set in the product of simplicial trees
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
, as produced in Corollary
10.9
.

Since each of the
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a tree and thus a cube complex,
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
naturally admits the structure of a cube complex, and we can use its hyperplanes to induce walls on
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. The main goal of this section is to study the resulting wall-space, in particular to prove that it forms a pocset (Proposition
12.18
). Much of this discussion is straight-forward in the interior case (i.e., when
\Lambda=\emptyset
), so the bulk of the work happens in the infinite case.

12.1.
Hierarchical families of trees

It is useful to axiomatize our setup in the finite case, and indeed this proves useful later for the applications in Section
17
. The following definition axiomatizes the properties we need for Proposition
12.18
and its supporting lemmas when
\Lambda=\emptyset
.

Definition 12.1

(Hierarchical family of trees)
.

A
hierarchical family of trees
is the following collection of objects and properties:

(1)

A finite index set
\mathcal{U}
of
domains
with
relations

\sqsubset,\pitchfork,\perp
so that

•

\sqsubset
is anti-reflexive and anti-symmetric, and gives a partial order with a bound on the length of chains;

•

\pitchfork
is symmetric and anti-reflexive;

•

There is a unique
\sqsubset
-maximal element
S
;

•

Any pair of domains
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
satisfies exactly one of the relations.

(2)

There is a bound on the size of any pairwise
\perp
-comparable subset of
\mathcal{U}
.

(3)

To each
U\in\mathcal{U}
there is an associated finite simplicial tree
\widehat{T}_{U}
.

(4)

A finite collection of labels
F
and, on each tree, a finite set of
marked points
, which are labeled by
F
, with each element of
F
labeling exactly one marked point of
\widehat{T}_{U}
. Moreover, for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, each leaf of
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a marked point.

(5)

A family of
relative projections
determining:

•

For each
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
with
V\sqsubset U
or
V\pitchfork U
, there is a vertex
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{(0)}_{U}
. Moreover, each component of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
contains a marked point.

•

If
U,V,W\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\perp V
and
V\sqsubset W
and either
U\sqsubset W
or
U\pitchfork W
, then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}
.

(6)

(BGI) There exist projection maps
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}:\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{V}
when
V\sqsubset U
, satisfying the following bounded geodesic image property:

•

If
C\subset\widehat{T}_{U}-\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
is a component, then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(C)
coincides with
\hat{f}_{V}
for any
f\in F
with
\hat{f}_{U}\in C
.

From the above set of data, one can define consistency equations and the
0
-consistent subset

\mathcal{Q}
of the
total space

\mathcal{Y}\subset\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}^{(0)}_{U}
as in Definition
7.9
.

We observe that our hierarchical setup satisfies this set of properties:

Lemma 12.2
.

The family of trees
\{\widehat{T}_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
with associated collapsed relative projections
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, as produced in Section
7
starting from a reduced tree system (Definition
7.1
associated to the hierarchical hull of a finite set of internal points in an HHS
\mathcal{X}
satisfies Definition
12.1
.

Proof.

Items (1) and (2) follow from the Definition
2.1
of an HHS. Items (3), (4), and (5) are contained in Definition
7.5
, while item (6) is in Definition
7.7
.

∎

Remark 12.3

(Infinite case,
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
)
.

While it is possible to axiomatize the infinite setting (when
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
), we were unable to agreeably and succinctly package the required extra axioms, which all essentially encode various structural applications of Strong Passing-Up
4.7
. As with much of this article, the infinite setting asserts itself several times throughout the discussion, with the finite setting being relatively straight-forward.

Remark 12.4

(Other generalizations)
.

It seems possible that the construction in this section works in greater generality. For instance, one could define a
hierarchical family of cube complexes
, where the
\widehat{T}_{U}
are higher dimensional cube complexes, and perhaps the relative projections are convex subcomplexes, not necessarily points. As we do not have a direct application in mind, we have not pursued this more general version of this machinery.

Remark 12.5

(Proofs in this section)
.

We will proceed with the proofs in this section by using the various parts of Definition
12.1
to prove the (usually much simpler) finite case when
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
, and then supplement this setup with hierarchical facts (such as Strong Passing-Up
4.7
) when dealing with the infinite case. The more general framework in the interior case is necessary for the proof of Theorem
F
in Section
17
.

12.2.
A quick primer on CAT(0) cube complexes, wallspaces, and Sageev’s construction

As we have arrived at the point in the paper where we begin constructing cube complexes, we review some basics. For a detailed introduction to cube complexes, see Sageev’s notes
[
Sag14
]
as well as Hruska-Wise
[
HW14
]
. Wallspaces were introduced by Haglund-Paulin
[
HP98
]
, though we most closely follow Roller’s approach
[
Rol16
]
to Sageev’s cubulation machine
[
Sag95
]
.

A
cube complex
is a CW complex obtained by gluing a collection of unit Euclidean cubes of various dimensions along their faces by a collection of (Euclidean) isometries. A cube complex
\Sigma
is
non-positively curved
when it satisfies Gromov’s link condition, and every non-positively curved simply connected cube complex admits a unique CAT(0) metric.

A
hyperplane
in a CAT(0) cube complex
\Sigma
is a connected subspace whose intersection with each cube
\sigma=[0,1]^{n}
is either
\emptyset
or the subspace obtained by restricting exactly one coordinate to
\frac{1}{2}
. In particular, every hyperplane partitions
\Sigma
. This motivates the definition of a half-spaces and pocsets, following Roller
[
Rol16
]
.

Definition 12.6

(Pocset)
.

Let
(\Sigma,<)
be a partially-ordered set. A
pocset
structure on
\Sigma
has the additional structure of an order-reversing involution
A\mapsto A^{*}
satisfying

(1)

A\neq A^{*}
and
A,A^{*}
are
<
-incomparable;

(2)

A<B\implies B^{*}<A^{*}
.

•

Given
A<B
, the
interval
between
A
and
B
is
[A,B]=\{C|A<C<B\}
.

•

The pocset
(\Sigma,<)
is
locally finite
if every interval is finite.

•

Given
A,B\in\Sigma
, we say
A
is
transverse
to
B
if none of
A<B,A<B^{*},A^{*}<B,A^{*}<B^{*}
holds. The pocset
(\Sigma,<)
has
finite width
if the maximal size of a pairwise transverse subset is finite.

In a CAT(0) cube complex, every vertex is contained in exactly one half-space associated to each hyperplane. The following notion abstracts the combinatorial structure of such a family of half-spaces:

Definition 12.7

(Ultrafilters and the DCC)
.

Given a locally finite pocset
(\Sigma,<)
, an
ultrafilter

\alpha
on
\Sigma
is a subset of
\Sigma
satisfying

(1)

(Choice) Exactly one element of each pair
\{A,A^{*}\}
is an element of
\alpha
;

(2)

(Consistency)
A\in\alpha
and
A<B\implies B\in\alpha
.

•

An ultrafilter
\alpha
satisfies the
descending chain condition
(DCC) if every descending chain of elements terminates.

We can now define the dual cube complex to a locally finite, discrete, finite-width pocset:

Definition 12.8

(Dual cube complex)
.

Given a locally finite, discrete, finite-width pocset
(\Sigma,<)
, we define a CW complex
X(\Sigma)
as follows:

(1)

The
0
-cells
X^{(0)}
of
X
are the DCC ultrafilters on
(\Sigma,<)
.

(2)

Two
0
-cells
\alpha,\beta
are connected by an edge in
X^{(1)}
if and only if
|\alpha\triangle\beta|=2
, i.e.
\alpha=(\beta-\{A\})\cup\{A^{*}\}
for some
A\in\Sigma
.

(3)

Define
X^{(n)}
inductively by adding an
n
-cube whenever the boundary of one appears in the
X^{(n-1)}
-skeleton.

See Sageev’s notes
[
Sag14
]
for a thorough discussion of the above.

The following is a theorem of Roller
[
Rol16
]
, building on Sageev
[
Sag95
]
:

Theorem 12.9

(Sageev’s machine)
.

Given a locally finite, finite-width pocset
(\Sigma,<)
, the CW complex
X(\Sigma)
is a finite dimensional CAT(0) cube complex, where the dimension is equal to the width of
(\Sigma,<)
.

•

We call
X(\Sigma)
the
dual cube complex
to
(\Sigma,<)
.

12.3.
Inducing walls on
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}

For the rest of this section, fix a finite set
F\cup\Lambda
of internal points and hierarchy rays in an HHS
\mathcal{X}
, along with their associated structures, such as the
K
-relevant set
\mathcal{U}
and its associated simplicial trees
\widehat{T}_{U}
, as we have been working with in most of this paper. Our goal is to induce a wallspace structure on the
0
-consistent set
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, and then prove that it gives a pocset. In Section
13
, we prove that the dual cube complex to this wallspace on
\mathcal{Q}
is isometric to
\mathcal{Q}
, as described in Theorem
13.6
.

To begin, observe that
\mathcal{Y}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}
is a product of cube complexes. It is a general fact that a product of cube complexes admits a natural cubical structure, where the global hyperplanes come from the hyperplanes in each component in a natural way. Using this observation, we can induce a wallspace structure on
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
as follows.

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, a
component hyperplane

h_{U}
in
\widehat{T}_{U}
is simply the midpoint of an edge in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. Each such hyperplane
h_{U}
then determines a
global hyperplane

\bar{h}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
of the following form:

\bar{h}_{U}=h_{U}\times\prod_{V\in\mathcal{U}-\{U\}}\widehat{T}_{V}\cup\partial\widehat{T}_{V}.

Set
\mathbf{h}_{U}=\bar{h}_{U}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, which we refer to as a
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-
hyperplane
. We make some basic observations about these
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes:

Lemma 12.10
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
, and
h_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
be a hyperplane. The following hold:

(1)

If
\hat{z}\in\mathbf{h}_{U}
, then the coordinates
\hat{z}_{V}
of
\hat{z}
satisfy:

(a)

If
U\sqsubset V
or
U\pitchfork V
, then
\hat{z}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
.

(b)

If
V\sqsubset U
, then
\hat{z}_{V}=\hat{f}_{V}
for some
f\in F\cup\Lambda
.

(2)

If
h_{V}\subset\widehat{T}_{V}
is another component hyperplane, then
\mathbf{h}_{U}\cap\mathbf{h}_{V}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
U\perp V
.

Proof.

For (1), first assume that
U\pitchfork V
. Then
h_{U}=\hat{z}_{U}
is not a marked or cluster point, and hence
0
-consistency implies that
\hat{z}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. Similarly, if
U\sqsubset V
but
\hat{z}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, then the BGI property (
6
) of Definition
12.1
implies that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{z}_{V})=\hat{f}_{V}
for some
f\in F\cup\Lambda
. But
\hat{z}_{U}=h_{U}
is not a marked point, which is a contradiction, forcing
\hat{z}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
.

Now if
V\sqsubset U
, then
\hat{z}_{U}=h_{U}
is not a marked or cluster point, and so
\hat{z}_{U}\neq\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, and so
\hat{z}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\hat{z}_{U})
by
0
-consistency, while the latter equals
\hat{f}_{U}
for some
f\in F\cup\Lambda
by the BGI property (
6
) of Definition
12.1
.

Finally, Now (2) follows from (1), for (1) says that if
U\notperp V
and
\hat{z}\in\mathbf{h}_{U}\cap\mathbf{h}_{V}
, then one of
\hat{z}_{U},\hat{z}_{V}
must be at a marked or cluster point, which is a contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

We observe the following immediate corollary of item (2) of Lemma
12.10
and (
2
) of Definition
12.1
, namely finite dimensionality of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
:

Corollary 12.11
.

The number of pairwise intersecting
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes is bounded by the number of pairwise orthogonal domains in
\mathcal{U}
, and is hence finite.

12.4.
Half-spaces in
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}

We next observe that the
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes form a wallspace structure on
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
.

If
h_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
is a component hyperplane and
\widehat{\pi}_{U}:\mathcal{Y}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
is the coordinate projection, then
\mathbf{h}_{U}=\widehat{\pi}_{U}^{-1}(h_{U})
. Since
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{h_{U}\}
has two components
\widehat{T}_{U}^{+},\widehat{T}_{U}^{-}
, we get a partition

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}_{\mathbf{h}_{U}}\sqcup\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}_{\mathbf{h}_{U}}\sqcup\mathbf{h}_{U}=\overline{\mathcal{Q}}

where
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\pm}_{\mathbf{h}_{U}}=\widehat{\pi}_{U}^{-1}(\widehat{T}_{U}^{\pm})\cap\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, which we call the
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-
half-spaces
associated to
\mathbf{h}_{U}
.

Notation 12.12
.

In what follows, we will usually not need to refer to both a half-space and its dual. Thus we will usually refer to the
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space associated to some tree hyperplane
h_{U}
as
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
, and the corresponding half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{h_{U}\}
as
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
.

The following lemma gives a description of these half-spaces, and in particular shows both are nonempty:

Lemma 12.13
.

For each
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{U}
, the following description holds:

(1)

If
U\perp V
, then
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{U})=\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})=\widehat{T}_{V}
.

(2)

If
U\pitchfork V
, then

•

\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
.

•

If
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})=\widehat{T}_{V}
. Otherwise,
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
.

(3)

If
V\sqsubset U
, then

•

\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(h_{U})
.

•

If
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})=\widehat{T}_{V}
. Otherwise,
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})
.

(4)

If
U\sqsubset V
, then

•

\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
.

•

If
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})=\mathrm{hull}_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\cup l_{U})
, where
l_{U}
are the labels of the marked points in
\widehat{T}_{U}
contained in
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
.

Proof.

The proof of the description of the component projections of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
is a straightforward exercise in the definitions utilizing Lemma
12.10
and we leave most of it to the reader. The one exception is item (4), which deserves a comment.

When
U\sqsubset V
, then
\widehat{T}_{V}-\{\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\}
consists of some number of components, each of which contains some marked point or ray. By the BGI property in item (5) of Definition
12.1
, each of these components projects to a marked cluster point in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. By construction,
h_{U}
partitions all marked points and rays in
\widehat{T}_{U}
, and so each half-space
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
contains some (nonempty) set of marked points or rays, whose labels we denote by
l_{U}
. Hence each component of the subtree
\mathrm{hull}_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\cup l_{U})
, namely the convex hull in
\widehat{T}_{V}
of
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
and
l_{U}
, projects to a marked point contained in
\widehat{T}_{U}
by the BGI property (
6
) of Definition
12.1
. This completes the proof.
∎

12.5.
Intersection and nesting of half-spaces

In this subsection, we prove some useful descriptions of half-space intersections and nesting. We note that the first lemma is an exact version of
[
DMS20
, Lemma 4.6]
.

Lemma 12.14
.

Let
h_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
and
h_{V}\in\widehat{T}_{V}
be component hyperplanes with corresponding
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes
\mathbf{h}_{U},\mathbf{h}_{V}
, and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
be choices of half-spaces corresponding to half-trees
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\subset\widehat{T}_{U}-\{h_{U}\}
and
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}\subset\widehat{T}_{V}-\{h_{V}\}
, respectively. Then
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}\neq\emptyset
if and only if one of the following holds:

(1)

U\perp V
;

(2)

U=V
and
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\cap\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}\neq\emptyset
;

(3)

U\pitchfork V
and either
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
or
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
;

(4)

U\sqsubset V
and either
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
or
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V})\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
.

Proof.

Item (1) follows from item (1) of Lemma
12.13
. Item (2) follows from the definition of the half-spaces as inverse images under component-wise projection. Item (3) is a consequence of item (2) of Lemma
12.13
, for if
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, so if
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
, we cannot have
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}\neq\emptyset
. Finally, item (4) is a consequence of items (3) and (4) of Lemma
12.13
, for if
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
, then
\widehat{\pi}_{U}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V})=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V})
by item (3) of that lemma. Hence if
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}\neq\emptyset
, then we must have that
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V})\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, as required. This completes the proof.
∎

A similar analysis gives a description of when half-spaces are nested:

Lemma 12.15
.

Using the same notation as above, we have
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
if and only if one of the following holds:

(1)

U=V
and
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\subset\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
;

(2)

U\pitchfork V
and
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
and
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
;

(3)

U\sqsubset V
and
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
and
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\subset\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V})
;

(4)

V\sqsubset U
and both
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
and
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
.

•

In particular, if
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\neq\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
and
U,V
are not orthogonal or equal, then one of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
,
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{V}
,
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
, or
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{V}
holds. On the other hand, if
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}=\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
or
U,V
are orthogonal, then none of the above relations holds.

Proof.

Item (1) follows from the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
as preimages under component-wise projection to
\widehat{T}_{U}=\widehat{T}_{V}
. Items (2)–(4) are direct consequences of items (2)–(4) of Lemma
12.13
, respectively.

We now prove the last statement. The statement follows immediately from (1) when the domain labels for
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
are the same. Suppose then that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
correspond to half-trees
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U},\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
for tree-hyperplanes
h_{U},h_{V}
in
\widehat{T}_{U},\widehat{T}_{V}
, respectively. When
U\pitchfork V
, if
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\nsubset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
, then
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
is contained in the complement of
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, and hence item (2) above implies that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{V}
. For
U\sqsubset V
, if
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\nsubset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
, then
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
. It follows that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}_{V}-\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
, and hence item (3) implies that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{V}
. See Figure
18
. Finally, the claim when
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}=\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
follows immediately from the definitions, while when
U\perp V
, the claim also requires item (1) of Lemma
12.13
. This completes the proof.

Figure 18
.
The four nesting possibilities in Lemma
12.15
: In (a), the domain labels for
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
are the same and we have
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
because
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\subset\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
. In (b),
U\pitchfork V
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
because
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
while
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
. In (c), we have
U\sqsubset V
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
because
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\subset\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V})
. In (d), we have
U\sqsubset V
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
because
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V})\subset\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
.

∎

12.6.
Half-spaces are nonempty

In this subsection, we prove that every
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space contains a point in
\mathcal{Q}
. This fact is almost immediate when
\Lambda=\emptyset
, but requires some work in the infinite case. It is crucial for proving local finite-ness in the proof that the induced wall-space on
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
forms a pocset (Proposition
12.18
), as well as proving that only points in
\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
are contained in infinite descending chains of half-spaces (Proposition
12.17
).

Lemma 12.16
.

For any
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
, we have that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\mathcal{Q}
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{U}\cap\mathcal{Q}
are nonempty. Moreover, if
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
is the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U}
corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
, then the following hold:

(1)

If
a\in F
with
\hat{a}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then
\hat{a}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\mathcal{Q}
.

(2)

If
\lambda\in\Lambda
with
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then
\hat{\lambda}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
be the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U}
corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
. Note that there is some
z\in F\cup\Lambda
so that
\hat{z}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
. First suppose that we can take
z=a\in F
so that
\hat{a}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then
\hat{a}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\mathcal{Q}
. This is because
\left(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\sqcup\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{U}\sqcup\mathbf{h}_{U}\right)\cap\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}
is indeed a partition of
\mathcal{Q}
, and by Lemma
12.13
and our assumption that
\hat{a}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, we cannot have
\hat{a}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{U}\cup\mathbf{h}_{U}
. This proves part (1) of the statement, and the whole statement when
\Lambda=\emptyset
. Also, observe that part (2) is immediate from this observation as well.

The bulk of the work is in when we must take
z=\lambda\in\Lambda
with
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}\cup\partial\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
. Observe that when this happens, the opposite half-tree of
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
contains some marked point for an element of
F\neq\emptyset
, and hence
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{U}\cap\mathcal{Q}\neq\emptyset
. For showing
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\mathcal{Q}\neq\emptyset
, there are a three main cases:

(1)

U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
and
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
represents a ray;

(2)

U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
and
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
is a cluster point;

(3)

U\notin\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
.

In each case, the idea is to construct a tuple
\hat{w}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
and prove that it is canonical (Definition
7.9
). The arguments are related, and each invokes Strong Passing-Up
4.7
at a key moment.

Case (1)
: In this case, there are infinitely-many vertices in
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
which are not marked, branched, or cluster points. Choose a vertex
w\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
sufficiently out the ray for
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
so that no marked point or branch points separates
w
from
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
.

Define a tuple
\hat{w}
domain-wise as follows:

•

\hat{w}_{U}=w
;

•

If
V\pitchfork U
or
U\sqsubset V
, then
\hat{w}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}=\hat{\lambda}_{V}
;

•

If
V\sqsubset U
, then we set
\hat{w}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}(h_{U})=\hat{\lambda}_{V}
;

•

Otherwise, we fix
a\in F
and for any
V\perp U
, we set
\hat{w}_{V}=\hat{a}_{V}
.

Observe that
\hat{w}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
by construction, in particular it is
0
-consistent. To see canonicality, let
b\in F
and set
\mathcal{V}_{b}=\{V\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{b}_{V}\neq\hat{w}_{V}\}
. If
\#\mathcal{V}_{b}<\infty
for all
b\in F
, then
\hat{w}
is canonical and we are done.

Let
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)=\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
, which we recall are pairwise orthogonal (Lemma
3.9
). Then Lemma
7.18
implies that there is a cofinite subset
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
which partitions into subsets
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{n}
so that
V\sqsubset U_{i}
for each
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}
.

Assuming that
U=U_{1}
, we observe that when
i>2
, then by Definition
3.10
we have
\hat{w}_{U_{i}}=\hat{\lambda}_{U_{i}}=\hat{a}_{U_{i}}
for our fixed
a\in F
. Since
\#\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(a,b)<\infty
(Corollary
2.8
), it follows that
\#\mathcal{V}_{i}<\infty
for each
i>2
.

On the other hand, observe that if
V\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{1}
, then
\delta^{V}_{U}
separates
b_{U}
from
q^{-1}_{U}(w)
in
T_{U}
by the BGI property of Lemma
6.13
. On the other hand, if
\#\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{1}=\infty
, then Strong Passing-Up
4.7
implies that the spread of the
\rho^{V}_{U}
in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, and hence
\delta^{V}_{U}
in
T_{U}
, must be infinite, which is impossible because
d_{T_{U}}(b_{U},q^{-1}_{U}(w))<\infty
. Hence
\#\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{1}<\infty
and
\hat{w}
is canonical. This proves case (1).

Case (2)
: Now we assume that
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
but
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
is a cluster point. By definition of
\widehat{T}_{U}
, since
d_{T_{U}}(b_{U},\lambda_{U})=\infty
for all
b\in F
, it follows that
\#\{V\in\mathrm{Rel}_{50E}(b,\lambda)|V\sqsubset U,\hskip 7.22743pt\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}_{U}\}=\infty
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{U}
be any
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal domain with
V\sqsubset U
and
\delta^{V}_{U}
at least sufficiently far past any branched or marked point of
T_{U}
along the ray to
\lambda_{U}
, so that
[b,c]\cap\mathcal{N}_{E}(\rho^{V}_{U})=\emptyset
for any geodesic
[b,c]
between
b,c
in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, for all
b\in F
and
c\in F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda\}
. The BGIA
7
then implies that
d_{V}(b,c)<E
for all such
b,c
and hence
d_{V}(b,\lambda)>K-E
, since
V\in\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

It follows from item (6) of Lemma
7.14
that
d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{b}_{V},\hat{\lambda}_{V})\succ K
, and in particular that
\hat{b}_{V}\neq\hat{\lambda}_{V}
for all
b\in F
. Thus there exists a vertex
w\in\widehat{T}_{V}
which is not a marked or cluster point (there are none of the latter) which separates
\hat{b}_{V}
from
\hat{\lambda}_{V}
for all
b\in F
.

Now define a tuple
\hat{w}
analogously to the above, where we now let
V
control the related projections:

•

\hat{w}_{V}=W
;

•

If
V\pitchfork Z
or
V\sqsubset Z
, then
\hat{w}_{Z}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{Z}
;

•

Otherwise, we fix
a\in F
and for any
V\perp U
, we set
\hat{w}_{V}=\hat{a}_{V}
.

Note that
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimality says that no domain
Z\sqsubset V
. Hence the above gives a complete tuple and checking
0
-consistency is straight-forward, and
\hat{w}\in\mathcal{Q}_{U}
since
\hat{w}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
by construction.

For canonicality, we argue in a similar fashion to case (1): If
b\in F
and
\#\{Z\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{b}_{Z}\neq\hat{w}_{Z}\}=\infty
, then Lemma
7.18
provides a cofinite subset which admits a partition into subsets of domains which nest into the finitely-many support domains
\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}=\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
. Since
V\sqsubset U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, the coordinate of
\hat{w}_{X}
for
X\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)-\{U\}
is
\hat{a}_{X}
, and hence the only possibly infinite subset corresponds to
U
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be this infinite subset. By Strong Passing-Up
4.7
, we can choose
Z\in\mathcal{V}
so that
\delta^{Z}_{U}
is much further out the ray toward
\lambda_{U}
than
\delta^{V}_{U}
in
T_{U}
. In this case we have
V\pitchfork Z\sqsubset U
and it follows from item (6) of Lemma
6.13
that
d_{T_{Z}}(\delta^{V}_{Z},\delta^{U}_{Z}(\delta^{V}_{Z}))<E^{\prime}
, while the BGI property of Lemma
6.13
also implies that
d_{T_{Z}}(b_{Z},\delta^{U}_{Z}(\delta^{V}_{Z})<E^{\prime}
, and so
\hat{b}_{Z}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{Z}
, whereas
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{Z}=\hat{w}_{Z}
, by construction. This contradicts the assumption that
Z\in\mathcal{V}
, and hence
\#\mathcal{V}<\infty
, as required.

Case (3)
: The last and final case uses similar ideas. Since we are assuming that there does not exist
b\in F
so that
\hat{b}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, it follows that
U
is not orthogonal to every domain in
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, as the projection of
\lambda
to those domains is determined by some point in
F
(Definition
3.10
).

Let
V\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
so that
U
and
V
are not orthogonal. We argue case-wise depending on how
U
relates to
V
.

When
V\pitchfork U
or
V\sqsubset U
, then it follows that
\hat{\lambda}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. In both cases, it follows from Lemma
12.13
that
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})=\widehat{T}_{V}
. Now we are in a similar position to cases (1) and (2), where either
\hat{\lambda}_{V}
represents a point in
\partial\widehat{T}_{V}
or is a cluster point. In the former case, we argue as in (1) by choosing a vertex
w\in\widehat{T}_{V}
which is not at a cluster point or branched point, and then completing it to a tuple
\hat{w}
. Note that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
is a marked point by item (2) of Lemma
7.14
, and hence any vertex will do for the argument. In the latter case, we again pass to a
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal domain
Z\sqsubset V
with
\hat{\delta}^{Z}_{V}=\hat{\lambda}_{V}
in which we can enforce that
\hat{b}_{Z}\neq\hat{\lambda}_{Z}
for any
b\in F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda\}
. Analogous arguments suffice to prove canonicality in both cases.

The final case is where
U\sqsubset V
. In this case,
\hat{\lambda}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\hat{\lambda}_{V})=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{T}^{\lambda}_{V})
where
\widehat{T}^{\lambda}_{V}\subset\widehat{T}_{V}-\{\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\}
is the component containing
\hat{\lambda}_{V}
. The same two subcases as above, namely where
\hat{\lambda}_{V}
is a ray or cluster point, happen again, and similar argument works.

This completes the proof of the lemma.

∎

12.7.
Descending chains of half-spaces

In this subsection, we prove a proposition which gives us control over infinite nested sequences of half-spaces, which will be useful to verify the descending chain condition (Definition
12.7
) in the definition of the map from
\mathcal{Q}
to its dual cube complex in Theorem
13.6
below, as well as later understanding the simplicial boundary of (the cubical dual of)
\mathcal{Q}
in Section
16
. We note that this proposition only has content when
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
.

Proposition 12.17
.

Let
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. Then
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}=\overline{\mathcal{Q}}-\mathcal{Q}
if and only if there exists a sequence
\{\mathcal{Q}_{U_{i}}\}
of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces with
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U_{i}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U_{i+1}}\subsetneq\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U_{i}}
for all
i
.

Proof.

Both directions of the proof proceed by a similar argument, either relying on the existence of an infinite domain
W\in\mathcal{U}
in which
\hat{x}
picks out a boundary point of
\widehat{T}_{W}
, and otherwise by using Lemma
7.17
, which is a consequence of Strong Passing-Up
4.7
, to violate the canonicality condition.

No DCC implies non-canonicality
: First suppose that we have such a sequence for
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. We claim that
\hat{x}
is not canonical in the sense of Definition
7.9
.

There are two cases: (1) up to passing to an infinite subsequence, there is a domain
W\in\mathcal{U}
so that the defining domains of the
U_{i}
all equal
W
, or (2) there is no such infinite subsequence.

In case (1), part (1) of Lemma
12.15
implies that
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U_{i+1}}\subsetneq\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U_{i}}
for all
i
, where
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U_{i}}
is the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U_{i}}
chosen by its defining tree-hyperplane
h_{i}
. The fact that the sequence is infinite and the trees are properly nested then forces the tree-hyperplanes
h_{i}
to run out an end of
\widehat{T}_{W}
corresponding to some
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Hence each
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U_{i}}
contains the end labeled by
\hat{\lambda}_{W}
, forcing
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{\lambda}_{W}
, which violates the first part of the definition of canonicality.

In case (2), we use Lemma
7.17
, which allows us to pass to a (relabeled) subsequence
\{V_{1},V_{2},\dots\}
and provides elements
a\in F
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
so that

(1)

V_{j}\pitchfork V_{i}
for
i\neq j
;

(2)

For
j>i
, we have
\hat{\delta}^{V_{j}}_{V_{i}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}
and
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{j}}=\hat{a}_{V_{j}}
;

(3)

For
k<i
, we have
\hat{\delta}^{V_{k}}_{V_{i}}=\hat{a}_{V_{i}}
and
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{k}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{k}}
.

For each
i
, let
h_{V_{i}}
be some tree-hyperplane in
\widehat{T}_{V_{i}}
(which exists by property (1) above), and let
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
be the half-tree containing
\hat{a}_{V_{i}}
, thus determining a
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}
. Observe that, by construction, we have for each
i
that
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i+1}}_{V_{i}}=\hat{a}_{V_{i}}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
while
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{i+1}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i+1}}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i+1}}
and similarly
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{i+1}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i+1}}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i+1}}
. Hence Lemma
12.15
implies that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i+1}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
. Note that part of this discussion is that
\hat{a}_{V_{i}}\neq\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
.

Now item (2) of Lemma
12.13
forces
\hat{x}_{V_{i+1}}=\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{i+1}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i+1}}\neq\hat{a}_{V_{i}}
for each
i
. It follows that
\hat{x}
is not canonical as in Definition
7.9
, as required.

Non-canonicality implies no DCC
: Now suppose that
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}-\mathcal{Q}
is not canonical (Definition
7.9
). Then either (a) there exists
U\in\mathcal{U}
so that
\hat{x}_{U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
or (b) no such
U
exists but there does exist a sequence of domains
U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
with
\hat{x}_{U_{i}}=\hat{\lambda}_{U_{i}}\in\widehat{T}_{U_{i}}
for some fixed
\lambda\in\Lambda
, with
\hat{x}_{U_{i}}\neq\hat{a}_{U_{i}}
for any
a\in F
and each
i
.

Case (a)
: The distance between
\hat{x}_{U}
and
\hat{a}_{U}
for any
a\in F
is infinite, and so we can find a sequence of tree-hyperplanes
h_{U,i}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
each of which separate
\hat{a}_{U}
from
\hat{x}_{U}
, and which monotically converge to
\hat{x}_{U}
in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. If we define
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U,i}
to be the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{h_{U,i}\}
containing
\hat{x}_{U}
, then Lemma
12.15
implies that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U,i+1}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U,i}
for all
i
, providing the desired sequence of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces.

Case (b)
: This case follows from an almost identical application of Lemma
7.17
. The only difference is now that conclusion is different, namely that the chain of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-halfspaces
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i+1}}\sqsubsetneq\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}
produced by the argument is infinite. This completes the proof of the lemma.

∎

12.8.
\mathcal{Q}
half-spaces form a pocset

With these preliminaries in hand, we can now prove that the family of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
half-spaces with the inclusion relation forms a pocset (Definition
12.6
):

Proposition 12.18
.

The set of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces together with the containment relation
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
forms a locally finite, finite-width pocset where transverse half-spaces are labeled by orthogonal domains.

Proof.

We verify the various items in Definition
12.6
. Items (1) and (2) follow directly from the definitions and the fact that the relation is just inclusion. The fact that transversality for the pocset corresponds to orthogonality of labels is Lemma
12.15
. Finite width is Corollary
12.11
.

The bulk of the work is local finiteness. Note that local finiteness is immediate when
\Lambda=\emptyset
, because then the set of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes is finite. Hence we are already done when
\Lambda=\emptyset
, and more generally when our setup is that of a hierarchical family of trees (Definition
12.1
).

To see local finiteness when
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
, let
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}
be nested
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
half-spaces, and
\mathcal{V}
be the set of domains labeling the half-spaces in
[\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}]
. Suppose for a contradiction that
[\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}]
is infinite. Then either
\#\mathcal{V}
is finite or infinite.

\#\mathcal{V}
is finite
: Then there exists some domain
W\in\mathcal{U}
labeling infinitely-many of the half-spaces in
[\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}]
. It follows that there is an infinite ordered subcollection of half-spaces
\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}\}\subset[\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}]
corresponding to a sequence of tree-hyperplanes
h_{i}\in\widehat{T}_{W}
, so that the
h_{i}
monotonically run out a ray end of
\widehat{T}_{W}
corresponding to a distinct
\lambda\in\Lambda
, and we can even choose the
h_{i}
to be at least
10
-away from any branched, marked, or cluster point of
\widehat{T}_{W}
.

By construction, we must have
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}
for each
i
. Lemma
12.15
says that
U,Z
are not orthogonal to
W
, and moreover:

•

If
U\pitchfork W
or
U\sqsubset W
, then items (2) or (3), respectively, of Lemma
12.15
imply that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
.

•

If
W\sqsubset U
, then item (4) of Lemma
12.15
implies that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i
, where
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
is the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U}
corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
. Moreover,
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})
is a marked point by the BGI property (
6
) of Definition
12.1
.

•

If
W=U
, then
h_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i
by item (1) of Lemma
12.15
, where
h_{U}
is the tree-hyperplane in
\widehat{T}_{U}=\widehat{T}_{W}
corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
, and
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
is the associated half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{W}-\{h_{i}\}
.

In each of these cases, the half-trees
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
are forced to nest
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{1}\subsetneq\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{2}\subset\cdots
and by our choice of the
h_{i}
, we have that every marked or cluster point in
\widehat{T}_{W}
is contained in each
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
. Note that this implies that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i+1}
for each
i
, which is an ascending chain and hence Proposition
12.17
does not apply. However, we can now derive a contradiction based on where
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}
lies in
\widehat{T}_{W}
.

Observe that if
Z\pitchfork W,Z\sqsubset W
, then
\hat{\delta}^{Z}_{W}
is at a marked or cluster point, and hence
\hat{\delta}^{Z}_{W}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
, which violates the assumption that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}
for all
i
. Similarly, if
W\sqsubset Z
, then
\hat{\delta}^{Z}_{W}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{Z})\in\widehat{T}_{W}
is a marked point, where
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{Z}
is the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{Z}
corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}
, and hence is contained in each
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
. And finally, when
Z=W
, then the tree-hyperplane
h_{Z}
corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}
must be contained in
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
for sufficiently large
i
. Applying the appropriate part of Lemma
12.15
to each of these situations results in the conclusion that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
for some
i
, which is a contradiction.

\#\mathcal{V}
is infinite
: In this case, we apply Lemma
7.17
to obtain an infinite subcollection
\{V_{1},V_{2},\dots\}\subset[\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}]
of domains and elements
a\in F
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
so that:

(1)

V_{j}\pitchfork V_{i}
for
i\neq j
;

(2)

For
j>i
, we have
\hat{\delta}^{V_{j}}_{V_{i}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}
and
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{j}}=\hat{a}_{V_{j}}
;

(3)

For
k<i
, we have
\hat{\delta}^{V_{k}}_{V_{i}}=\hat{a}_{V_{i}}
and
\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{k}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{k}}
.

Our goal is to use this family of domains to build a chain of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces, which is either descending and contains
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
, in which case any
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\mathcal{Q}
(which exists by Lemma
12.16
) must actually be in
\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
by Proposition
12.17
, or is ascending and is contained in
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}
, in which case we can force
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{Z}\cap\mathcal{Q}
to contain a point in
\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
, with both outcomes being a contradiction.

We begin with restricting how
U
relates to the
V_{i}
. First, note that
U
is not orthogonal to any
V_{i}
by Lemma
12.15
because
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}
. Second, the Covering Lemma
4.8
says that
U
only nests into finitely-many domains in
\mathcal{U}
, so we may presume that
U
does not nest into any of the
V_{i}
. This allows us to reduce to the case where either (a)
V_{i}\sqsubset U
for all
i
or (b)
U\pitchfork V_{i}
for all
i
. We treat case (a); the proof of case (b) requires replacing occurrences of
\widehat{T}^{U}_{*}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})
with
\widehat{T}^{U}_{*}
, where
*
is the contextually appropriate
V_{i}
.

For each
i
, let
h_{i}
be the tree-hyperplane in
\widehat{T}_{V_{i}}
corresponding to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}
, and
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
the corresponding half-tree of
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}-\{h_{V_{i}}\}
.

We proceed by proving two claims:

Claim 12.19
.

If
i<j
, then we cannot have that both
\hat{a}_{V_{i}},\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}
are contained in the same half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{V_{i}}-\{h_{V_{i}}\}
and that
\hat{a}_{V_{j}},\hat{\lambda}_{V_{j}}
are contained in the same half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{V_{j}}-\{h_{V_{j}}\}
. In particular, we cannot have
\hat{a}_{V_{i}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}
and
\hat{a}_{V_{j}}=\hat{\lambda}_{V_{j}}
for
i\neq j
.

Proof of
12.19
.

Since
i\neq j
, we have
V_{i}\pitchfork V_{j}
, and hence Lemma
12.15
implies that some pair of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{j}},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{V_{i}},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{V_{j}}
nests, which is impossible given the assumptions and the characterization of nesting for transversely labeled domains in Lemma
12.15
.
∎

Hence we may assume that, for all
i
, we have
\hat{a}_{V_{i}}\neq\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}
and exactly one of
\hat{a}_{V_{i}}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
or
\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
holds.

Claim 12.20
.

If
\hat{a}_{V_{i}}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V_{i}}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
for some
i
, then for any
j>i
, we have
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V_{j}}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})=\hat{a}_{V_{j}}
. In particular, we have
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i+1}}\subset\cdots
.

•

Otherwise, we have
\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V_{i}}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
, and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}\supset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i+1}}\supset\cdots
.

Proof of Claim
12.20
.

Given
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
(which exists by Lemma
12.16
), the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}
and Lemma
12.15
imply that
\hat{x}_{V_{i}}=\hat{a}_{V_{i}}
, while the above reduction says that
\hat{a}_{V_{i}}\neq\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}=\hat{\delta}^{V_{j}}_{V_{i}}
for any
j>i
. Hence
0
-consistency of
\hat{x}
gives
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V_{j}}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U})=\hat{x}_{V_{j}}=\hat{\delta}^{V_{i}}_{V_{j}}=\hat{a}_{V_{j}}
, as required. The conclusion that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i+1}}\subset\cdots
is immediate from Lemma
12.15
.

If, on the other hand, we have
\hat{\lambda}_{V_{i}}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
, then Claim
12.19
implies that
\hat{a}_{V_{i}}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
. The conclusion that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}\supset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i+1}}\supset\cdots
again follows from Lemma
12.15
.
∎

Hence we may assume that either
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{1}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{2}}\subset\cdots
or
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{1}}\supset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{2}}\supset\cdots
. In the former case, since
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{Z}
for all
i
, any
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{Z}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
(Lemma
12.16
) must be contained in the descending chain
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{V_{1}}\supset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{V_{2}}\supset\cdots
forcing
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
by Proposition
12.17
, contradicting that
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}=\overline{\mathcal{Q}}-\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
. In the latter case, since
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V_{i}}
for all
i
, it follows that any
\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
is contained in the above descending chain, and hence Proposition
12.17
and Lemma
12.16
give another contradiction. This completes the proof of the proposition.

∎

13.
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
is a cube complex

In this section, we analyze the geometry of
\mathcal{Q}
using the induced wall structure studied in the last section, Section
12
. Our main goal is to prove that
\mathcal{Q}
is isometric to its cubical dual, via a map which identifies the
0
-cubes at infinity of the dual with
\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
.

We remark that it is possible to show that
\mathcal{Q}
is a complete connected median subalgebra of
\mathcal{Y}
, hence endowing it with a CAT(0) metric by work of Bowditch
[
Bow16
, Theorem 1.1]
, or when
\Lambda=\emptyset
then
\mathcal{Y}
is finite dimensional thus making
\mathcal{Q}
quasi-median quasi-isometric to a cube complex
[
HP23b
, Proposition 2.12]
. In fact, one need not assume that the collapsed trees are simplicial for this argument, though the fact that it is quasi-isometric to a cube complex (via the work in this section) is useful for proving connectivity. This version of
\mathcal{Q}
has a natural cuboid structure, in the sense of Beyrer-Fioravanti
[
BF21
, Subsection 2.4]
. It is possible that this CAT(0) cuboid approach may be useful in settings where one wants to directly encode distances from the hyperbolic spaces
\mathcal{C}(U)
into the distances in the collapsed trees, as these distances are altered when passing to the simplicial versions of the trees.

On the other hand, our approach is more concrete and hands on, as we directly analyze the structure of
\mathcal{Q}
and then prove that it is
isometric
to its cubical dual, thereby encoding hierarchical information into the cubical structure of
\mathcal{Q}
.

13.1.
Separation and distance in
\mathcal{Q}

We begin by describing the
\ell^{1}
-metric on
\mathcal{Q}
in terms of wall separation. The following definition is standard:

Definition 13.1

(Hyperplane separation)
.

We say a
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{U}

separates

\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
if there is some choice of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{U}
so that
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
while
\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{U}
.

•

For any
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, we let
\mathcal{W}(\hat{x}|\hat{y})
denote the set of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes separating
\hat{x}
from
\hat{y}

The following is immediate from the fact that the
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes are defined as preimages of component-wise projection of the tree-hyperplanes:

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
h_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
be a component hyperplane with its corresponding
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{U}
. Then
\mathbf{h}_{U}
separates
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
if and only if
h_{U}
separates
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
.

For
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\mathcal{Q}
(which, recall, are
0
-consistent tuples of vertices in the
\widehat{T}_{U}
), we let
\mathcal{W}(\hat{x}|\hat{y})
denote the sets of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes
\mathbf{h}_{U}
separating
\hat{x},
from
\hat{y}
. As an easy application of this setup, we have:

Proposition 13.3
.

For any
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\#\mathcal{W}(\hat{x}|\hat{y}).

Proof.

By definition of the
\ell^{1}
-metric on
\mathcal{Q}
, we have
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
. On the other hand, the
\ell^{1}
-metric on
\widehat{T}_{U}
says that, for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, the distance
d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
is the number of component hyperplanes
h_{U}
separating
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
. Hence we are done by Lemma
13.2
.
∎

13.2.
The cubical dual to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
(is
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
)

Let
\mathcal{W}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
denote the collection of all
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces as described at the beginning of Subsection
12.4
. By Proposition
12.18
,
(\mathcal{W}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}),\subset)
is a locally finite, finite-width pocset structure (Definition
12.6
).

Let
\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
denote the Sageev dual cube complex provided by Theorem
12.9
, where the internal
0
-cubes
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}(\mathcal{Q})
correspond to DCC ultrafilters on
(\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{Q}),\subset)
, and the
0
-cubes at infinity
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})
correspond to ultrafilters which are not DCC (see Definition
12.7
). Equivalently, we think of ultrafilters as consistent choices of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces (Definition
12.7
). We endow
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
with the path metric on its
1
-skeleton, i.e. the
\ell^{1}
- or combinatorial metric.

Our goal in this subsection is to prove that
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
are isometric under a map which extends to a bijection at infinity, namely between
\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
and
\mathcal{D}^{(0)}(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})
. In particular, this will show that hierarchical consistency and cubical consistency coincide exactly in
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
.

In order to define
\mathcal{D}^{-1}
in the case where
\Lambda\neq\emptyset
, we need the following lemma:

Lemma 13.4
.

Let
\Sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
be a consistent collection of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces. For each
U
, let
\Sigma_{U}=\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}\}
denote the half-spaces labeled by
U
, with
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
the corresponding half-trees of
\widehat{T}_{U}
. Then either

(1)

There exists a vertex
\hat{x}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
so that
\hat{x}_{U}\in\bigcap_{i}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
, or

(2)

\bigcap_{i}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset
and there exists a unique
\lambda\in\Lambda
so that
\hat{\lambda}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
.

Proof.

First, observe that if
\bigcap_{i}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then it must contain a vertex. Next, suppose that
\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
are vertices as in the statement. Then there exists a tree-hyperplane
h_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
separating them, while
h_{U}
only determines one half-space in
\Sigma
, which is impossible. Hence, if
\bigcap_{i}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then it contains a unique vertex.

On the other hand, suppose that
\bigcap_{i}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset
. It follows that
\partial\widehat{T}_{U}\neq\emptyset
, since otherwise
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a finite tree, while consistency says that all half-spaces intersect, and then the Helly property for trees would imply that the intersection of all tree half-spaces is nonempty, a contradiction.

Let
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\Lambda
be the labels which (bijectively) determine the points in
\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
. For each
i
, let
h_{i}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
be any tree hyperplane in
\widehat{T}_{U}
which is at least
10
-away from any branch point along any geodesic from
\hat{f}_{U}
to
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
in
\widehat{T}_{U}
for any
f\in F
. Any particular, there is a unique geodesic ray from any
h_{i}
to
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
for each
i
.

Now suppose that
J_{i},J^{\prime}_{i}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
are some tree-hyperplanes which are further toward
\lambda_{i}
than
h_{i}
, with
J^{\prime}_{i}
further along than
J_{i}
. Observe that consistency of the ultrafilter
\Sigma
implies that if the half-tree
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}
chosen by the
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space in
\Sigma
determined by
J^{\prime}_{i}
contains
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
, then so does the half-tree corresponding to
J_{i}
, as the latter contains the former. Hence either every such hyperplane escaping toward
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
chooses the infinite end corresponding to
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
, or only finitely-many do.

We now claim that our assumption that
\bigcap_{i}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset
implies that exactly one
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
exhibits this behavior, namely that every tree-hyperplane further out than
h_{i}
toward
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
chooses the half-tree containing
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
.

To see existence, suppose otherwise; we note the proof of this part is similar to the proof of Lemma
9.10
. Then, for each
i
, we can replace each
h_{i}
by some further out hyperplane
h^{\prime}_{i}
along the ray toward
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
, namely the first tree-hyperplane which chooses the half-tree not containing
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
. We can then consider the subtree
\widehat{T}^{fin}_{U}
which is the convex hull of the
h^{\prime}_{i}
and
\hat{f}_{U}
for
f\in F
. By construction,
\bigcap_{i}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}=\bigcap_{i}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}\cap\widehat{T}^{fin}_{U})
, while also
