\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}\cap\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{j}\cap\widehat{T}^{fin}_{U}\neq\emptyset
for all
i,j
. The Helly property for trees thus says that
\bigcap_{i}\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, which is a contradiction.

Next, suppose that both
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
and
\hat{\lambda}^{k}_{U}
exhibit this behavior, for
i\neq k
. We will derive a contradiction of (ultrafilter) consistency using Lemma
12.15
. By construction and consistency, we must have that the half-trees
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{J_{i}},\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{J_{k}}
chosen by
\Sigma
for
h_{i}
and
h_{k}
contain
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
and
\hat{\lambda}^{k}_{U}
, respectively. However, by construction, the opposite half-tree
\widehat{T}^{*}_{J_{k}}
to
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{J_{k}}
contains
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{J_{i}}
. If
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{J_{k}},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{J_{k}},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{J_{i}}
are their respective
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces, then consistency implies that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{J_{i}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}_{J_{k}}
, which contradicts the assumption that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{J_{k}}\in\Sigma
. Hence there can be only one end of
\widehat{T}_{U}
which exhibits this behavior.

Finally, it remains to prove that
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j
, that is that every half-tree chooses the end containing
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
. In particular, we have that the tree-hyperplane
h_{i}
chooses the half-tree
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{h_{i}}
containing
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
. Now let
\widehat{T}^{*}_{h_{i}}
be the opposite half-tree, and suppose that
h\in\widehat{T}^{*}_{h_{i}}
is any other tree-hyperplane. If
\widehat{T}_{h}
is the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{h\}
containing
\hat{\lambda}^{i}_{U}
, then
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{h_{i}}\subset\widehat{T}_{h}
, and hence their respective
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{h_{i}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{h}
satisfy
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{h_{i}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{h}
by Lemma
12.15
. Hence consistency of
\Sigma
implies that
\widehat{T}_{h}
must be the the half-tree corresponding to the
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space in
\Sigma
. This completes the proof.
∎

With this lemma in place, we can define the maps:

Definition 13.5

(Dual cubical maps)
.

We define the maps
\mathcal{D}:\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\to 2^{\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{Q})}
and
\mathcal{D}^{-1}:\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})\to\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
as follows:

(
\mathcal{D}
)

Given a tuple
\hat{x}=(\hat{x}_{U})\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, we set
\mathcal{D}(\hat{x})
to the set of orientations where, for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
and component hyperplanes
h_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
, the corresponding
\mathcal{Q}
-hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{U}
chooses the
\mathcal{Q}
-half-space
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
to correspond to the half-space of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{h_{U}\}
containing
\hat{x}_{U}
.

(
\mathcal{D}^{-1}
)

Given a consistent collection of half-spaces
\Sigma=(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U})\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
, define
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\Sigma)=(\hat{y}_{U})\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
as follows: For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, let
\Sigma_{U}
denote the set of half-spaces labeled by
U
. Lemma
13.4
provides two cases:

(a)

The intersection of the collection of half-trees of
\widehat{T}_{U}
corresponding to the
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces in
\Sigma_{U}
contains a single vertex of
\widehat{T}_{U}
, which we define to be
\hat{y}_{U}
.

(b)

Otherwise, the intersection is empty and Lemma
13.4
produces some
\lambda\in\Lambda
so that all corresponding half-trees in
\widehat{T}_{U}
contain
\hat{\lambda}_{U}
, and we set
\hat{y}_{U}=\hat{\lambda}_{U}
.

Theorem 13.6
.

Both
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{-1}
are well-defined with images contained in
\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, respectively. Moreover, the following hold:

(1)

\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
if and only if
\mathcal{D}(\hat{x})\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})
.

(2)

\Sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})
if and only if
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\Sigma)\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
.

(3)

\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{D}^{-1}=\text{id}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})}:\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})\to\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
.

(4)

The restriction
\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{D}^{(0)}(\mathcal{Q})
is an isometry.

(5)

The restriction
\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})
is a bijection.

Proof.

We prove the claims in order:

\mathcal{D}
is well-defined
: Let
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. Consistency (item (2) of Definition
12.7
) of the choice of half-spaces
\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\}
defined by
\mathcal{D}(\hat{x})
is immediate: if
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
, then
\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{V}
. When
\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\}
satisfies the DCC, then the fact that
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
is exactly the statement of Proposition
12.17
. Similarly, when
\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\}
does not satisfy the DCC, then
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
.

\mathcal{D}^{-1}
is well-defined
: Let
\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\}
be a consistent choice of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces and let
(\hat{y}_{U})=\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\})
be as in Definition
13.5
. This tuple is well-defined by Lemma
13.4
.

We claim that the tuple
\hat{y}=(\hat{y}_{U})
is contained in
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, and we check
0
-consistency by a domain-wise analysis.

•

Let
U\pitchfork V
and suppose that
\hat{y}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
. Then there exists tree a tree hyperplane
h_{V}
in
\widehat{T}_{V}
with chosen half-space
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
with
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
. Let
h_{U}
be any tree-hyperplane in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. We claim that the corresponding chosen half-tree
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
contains
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
. It will follow then that the intersection of all half-trees in
\widehat{T}_{U}
contains
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
, and hence
\hat{y}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
.

To see this, let
\widehat{T}^{*}_{U},\widehat{T}^{*}_{V}
denote the complementary half-trees to
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U},\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
, respectively. The fact that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
implies that
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}\in\widehat{T}^{*}_{V}
. So if also
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\notin\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, then item (2) of Lemma
12.15
implies that
\mathcal{Q}_{U}\nsubset\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{V}
. But our consistent set of choices of half-spaces chose
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
, so
\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{V}
is not in our set of half-spaces, which violates the assumption that this set was (ultrafilter) consistent, giving a contradiction.

•

Let
U\sqsubset V
and suppose that
\hat{y}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
. Then there exists some
\mathcal{Q}
-half-space
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
coming from a tree-hyperplane
h_{V}
with corresponding half-space
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V}
which does not contain
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
. We claim that for all tree-hyperplanes
h_{U}
, the chosen corresponding half-tree
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
must contain the point
\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{V})
. If not, then part (4) of Lemma
12.15
implies that
\mathcal{Q}_{V}\subset\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{U}
, and hence
\mathcal{Q}_{U}\subset\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{V}
, which contradicts the assumption that our chosen
\mathcal{Q}
-half-space for
h_{V}
is
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
.

Thus we have established the first part of the statement. It suffices to check the numbered items.

Item (1) is an immediate consequence of Proposition
12.17
, for if
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
, then it is contained in some infinite descending chain of half-spaces, and vice versa.

For (2), let
\Sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})
. In view of Proposition
12.17
, it suffices to show that
\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
for all
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\in\Sigma
. But this is clear, since
\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
, and
\hat{y}_{U}\in\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
by construction, where
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
is the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U}
chosen by
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
, where
U
is the label of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}
.

For (3), let
\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\}\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
be a consistent family of
\mathcal{Q}
-half-spaces, and let
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\})=\hat{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
be as defined above. Then
\mathcal{D}(\hat{x})
is the family of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-spaces, where for each tree-hyperplane
h_{U}
, we choose the
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space whose corresponding tree half-space
\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
contains
\hat{x}_{U}
. By the coordinate-wise definition of
\mathcal{D}^{-1}
—which, for each
U
, was either defined as an intersection over all half-trees, or as the unique boundary point in
\partial\widehat{T}_{U}
which is contained in every half-tree—this
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space must coincide with the original
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-half-space in the family
\{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{U}\}
. This completes the proof that
\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{D}^{-1}=id_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})}
and hence of the theorem.

For (4) and (5), observe that item (3) implies that
\mathcal{D}:\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
is a bijection, while (1) and (2) imply that this restricts to a bijection
\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}^{\infty})
, as required for (5). On the other hand, Proposition
13.3
implies that
\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
is an isometric embedding, thus making it an isometry. This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

14.
Cubical hierarchy paths and the distance formula

In this section, we use the cubical structure of the cubical models to produce special hierarchy paths in any HHS
\mathcal{X}
:

Definition 14.1
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
y\in\mathcal{X}^{\infty}
with
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}(x,y)\to\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)
any a cubical model. If
\gamma
is any combinatorial geodesic (ray) in
\mathcal{Q}(x,y)
between
\widehat{\Psi}(x)
and
\widehat{\Psi}(y)
, we call
\widehat{\Omega}(\gamma)
a
cubical path
in
\mathcal{X}
between
x,y
.

The following is the main result of this section:

Proposition 14.2
.

There exists
L=L(\mathcal{X})>0
so that any cubical path between
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
y\in\mathcal{X}^{\infty}
is an
L
-hierarchy path.

The existence of hierarchy paths and the proof of the lower (and more difficult) bound in the Distance Formula
2.9
are usually intertwined. Notably, this is not the case for us, where we only need to know the existence of an
\ell^{1}
-geodesic in any cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
to obtain the latter as a consequence of Proposition
8.3
, Corollary
11.2
, and Theorem
13.6
:

Corollary 14.3
.

There exists
K_{0}\geq 0
such that for all
K>K_{0}
and any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\left[d_{U}(x,y)\right]_{K}\prec d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y).

Proof.

This statement is
[
BHS19
, Lemma 4.19]
, whose relatively short proof hinges on the existence of a path in
\mathcal{X}
between
x,y
whose length is coarsely the sum on the left (which they prove exists in
[
BHS19
, Corollary 4.16]
). Our proof proceeds as follows:

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be any cubical model for
x,y
. Theorem
13.6
, any combinatorial geodesic
\gamma
between
\hat{x},\hat{y}
in the cubical dual
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
to
\mathcal{Q}
is a geodesic in
\mathcal{Q}
, while Corollary
11.2
implies that
\widehat{\Omega}(\gamma)
is sent to a uniform quasi-geodesic between
x,y
. Finally, Proposition
8.3
says that the length of
\widehat{\Omega}(\gamma)
is coarsely bounded below by the left-hand side of the sum in the statement, completing the proof.
∎

We will begin by describing a procedure due to Niblo-Reeves
[
NR98
]
for constructing combinatorial geodesics (or rays) between
0
-cells of a cube complex, which we include for completeness and summarize in Proposition
14.5
. With this in hand, we prove that any combinatorial geodesic in a cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
for a hierarchical hull
H
gets sent to a hierarchy path, proving Proposition
14.8
.

14.1.
Niblo-Reeves normal paths

In
[
NR98
]
, Niblo-Reeves provide a way of producing very nice combinatorial geodesics (and rays) in any CAT(0) cube complex which traverse the hyperplanes between any pair of
0
-cells in a maximally efficient fashion. In this subsection, we describe their construction.

Let
X
be a CAT(0) cube complex, and
x,y
be
0
-cells in
X
, possibly at infinity. Let
\mathcal{W}(x|y)
denote the hyperplanes in
X
which separate
x
from
y
. For any hyperplanes
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x|y)
, we write
H<H^{\prime}
if one half-space of
H
contains
x
and the other half-space of
H
contains both
y
and
H^{\prime}
. The following is straight-forward exercise:

Lemma 14.4
.

The relation
<
is a partial order on
\mathcal{W}(x|y)
. Moreover,
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x|y)
are
<
-incomparable if and only if
H
and
H^{\prime}
cross.

Assuming that
x\in X^{(0)}
is a finite
0
-cell, and
y
is any
0
-cell (possibly at infinity), we can now recursively define a sequence of vertices
(z_{i})\subset X^{(0)}
using this partial order as follows:

•

We set
z_{0}=x_{0}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{1}\subset\mathcal{W}(z_{0}|y)=\mathcal{W}(x|y)
be the set of hyperplanes which are
<
-minimal. These are pairwise incomparable and hence pairwise cross by Lemma
14.4
. We set
z_{1}
to be the unique
0
-cell determined by flipping the orientations on the hyperplanes in
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
from the orientations in
z_{0}
, and leaving all other orientations the same. Observe that
z_{0}
and
z_{1}
are opposite corners of a unique cube in
X
, and hence
d^{\infty}_{X}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq 1
, with equality when
\mathcal{Z}_{1}\neq\emptyset
.

•

We now define
\mathcal{Z}_{2}\subset\mathcal{W}(z_{1}|y)
to be the
<
-minimal hyperplanes in
\mathcal{W}(z_{1}|y)
, and define
z_{2}
similarly.

•

Preceding recursively, this process either terminates at
y
when
y
is a finite
0
-cell, or limits to
y
when
y
is at infinity.

•

For each
i
, let
\gamma_{i}
be a combinatorial geodesic segment between
z_{i}
and
z_{i+1}
.

The following summarizes Niblo-Reeves construction:

Proposition 14.5
.

For each
i
for which
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, we have
d^{\infty}_{X}(z_{i},z_{i+1})=1
. Moreover, if
X
is a finite dimensional cube complex, then concatenation of the
\gamma_{i}
segments forms a combinatorial geodesic (ray) from
x
to
y
. In particular,
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)=\mathcal{W}(x|y)
.

We call the paths constructed as above
Niblo-Reeves paths
, or sometimes
normal cube paths
.

Remark 14.6
.

Note that any combinatorial geodesic (ray) between
\hat{x}
and
\hat{y}
satisfies
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)=\mathcal{W}(\hat{x}|\hat{y})
, though having this statement in hand will be useful in the next section.

14.2.
All combinatorial geodesics in
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
are hierarchical

In this subsection, we prove that any combinatorial geodesic in
\mathcal{Q}
is sent to a hierarchy path in
H
. We begin with the following lemma, which says that combinatorial geodesics are hierarchical in
\mathcal{Q}
:

Lemma 14.7
.

Let
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
and
\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. Let
\gamma
be any combinatorial geodesic (ray) in
\mathcal{Q}
from
\hat{x}
to
\hat{y}
. Then for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, the projection
\widehat{\pi}_{U}(\gamma)\subset\widehat{T}_{U}
is an unparameterized geodesic between
\hat{x}_{U}
and
\hat{y}_{U}
.

Proof.

Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
and let
\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(x|y)
be labeled by
U
, corresponding to tree-hyperplanes
h,h^{\prime}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
h<h^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{W}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})
, i.e. the unique (ray) in
\widehat{T}_{U}
between
\hat{x}_{U}
and
\hat{y}_{U}
must cross
h
before
h^{\prime}
. Then Lemma
12.15
implies that
\mathbf{h}<\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{W}(\hat{x}|\hat{y})
. Hence
\gamma
crosses
\mathbf{h}
before
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
, and thus
\widehat{\pi}_{U}(\gamma)
crosses
\mathbf{h}
before
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
. In particular,
\widehat{\pi}_{U}(\gamma)
must project onto the geodesic in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. Moreover, since combinatorial geodesics do not cross a hyperplane twice, it follows that
\widehat{\pi}_{U}(\gamma)
does not backtrack in
\widehat{T}_{U}
either. This completes the proof.
∎

With this in hand, we prove our hierarchy path statement:

Proposition 14.8
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. Set
F=\{x,y\}
,
\Lambda=\emptyset
, and let
\mathcal{Q}
be the cubical model for
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
and
\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. Let
\gamma
be any combinatorial geodesic (ray) between
\hat{x}
and
\hat{y}
. Then
\widehat{\Omega}(\gamma)\subset H
is a hierarchy path between
\widehat{\Omega}(\hat{x}),\widehat{\Omega}(\hat{y})
with uniform constants depending only on
|F\cup\Lambda|
and the constants involved in the construction of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
.

•

Moreover, when
F=\{x,y\}
,
\Lambda=\emptyset
,
\hat{x}=\widehat{\Psi}(x)
, and
\hat{y}=\widehat{\Psi}(y)
, then the image in
\mathcal{X}
under
\widehat{\Omega}
of a combinatorial geodesic between
\hat{x},\hat{y}
in
\mathcal{Q}
is a hierarchy path between
x,y
with constants depending only on
\mathcal{X}
.

Remark 14.9
.

One could use the fact that
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
is quasi-median (Theorem
15.20
below) plus the useful fact that median quasi-geodesics are hierarchy paths
[
RST18
]
to conclude that the image of a combinatorial geodesic (ray) in
H
is a hierarchy path (or ray). But the work in
[
RST18
]
requires the distance formula (Theorem
2.9
), and one consequence of our construction is a new proof of the distance formula, so it is important that we have a direct proof.

Proof.

Note that the “moreover” statement is immediate. Also, the image under
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
of a geodesic is automatically a quasi-geodesic by Corollary
11.2
. So it suffices to prove that no backtracking occurs in any domain, and note that up to an error involving the choice of largeness constant
K
(Subsection
5.2
) and
|F\cup\Lambda|
, we need only consider domains in
\mathcal{U}
.

Let
\gamma
be a combinatorial geodesic (ray) between
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}
and
\hat{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
. We want to show that for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have that
\pi_{U}(\widehat{\Omega}(\gamma))\subset\mathcal{C}(U)
is an unparameterized quasi-geodesic. Recall that
\widehat{\Omega}
is defined coordinate-wise, first by defining a tuple of coordinates in the product of trees
\mathcal{Y}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}
, then by pushing this tuple in
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{C}(U)
and applying Realization
2.4
; see Subsection
9.2
for a full description. The first step above involves a map
\Omega:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}\subset\mathcal{Y}
, where
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}
is the
\omega
-consistent subset of
\mathcal{Y}
, and
\Omega
is defined by coordinate-wise maps
B_{U}:\mathcal{Q}\to T_{U}
. Since the latter two steps only introduce a bounded error in each coordinate, it suffices to show that the coordinate-wise maps
B_{U}(\gamma)\subset T_{U}
is an unparameterized quasi-geodesic in each
T_{U}
.

Let
(x_{U})=(B_{U}(\hat{x}))=\Omega(\hat{x})
and
(y_{U})=(B_{U}(\hat{y}))=\Omega(\hat{y})
be the tuples in
\mathcal{Y}
determined by
\hat{x},\hat{y}
, respectively. Let
r=r(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
be the cluster separation constant (Subsection
7.2
). The following claim is sufficient to complete the proof:

Claim 14.10
.

There exists a constant
\xi=\xi(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|,K)>0
such that for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have that
B_{U}(\gamma)
satisfies the following two properties:

(1)

If
[x_{U},y_{U}]_{U}\subset T_{U}
is the geodesic in
T_{U}
between
x_{U},y_{U}
, then
d^{Haus}_{T_{U}}(B_{U}(\gamma),[x_{U},y_{U}]_{U})<\xi
, and

(2)

If
s<t\in[0,d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})]
, then
d_{T_{U}}(B_{U}(\gamma(s)),y_{U})-d_{T_{U}}(B_{U}(\gamma(t)),y_{U})<\xi
.

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we let
\gamma_{U}=\widehat{\pi}_{U}(\gamma)
, and recall that Lemma
14.7
implies that
\gamma_{U}
is an unparameterized geodesic in
\widehat{T}_{U}
.

The proof is by induction on the
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-level of domains in
\mathcal{U}
. First, suppose that
U\in\mathcal{U}
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal. Then
\widehat{T}_{U}
contains no cluster points, and hence
B_{U}(\gamma)=q^{-1}_{U}(\gamma_{U})
. As such, (1) and (2) are automatic from Lemma
14.7
and the fact that
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
is a uniform quasi-isometric embedding whose image is within uniform Hausdorff distance of
\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
with constants depending only on
\mathcal{X}
and
|F\cup\Lambda|
.

Now suppose that
U\in\mathcal{U}
is not
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal. For (1), observe that if
\hat{z}\in\gamma
so that
\hat{z}_{U}\in\gamma_{U}
is not at a cluster point in
\widehat{T}_{U}
, then
z_{U}:=B_{U}(\hat{z})=q^{-1}_{U}(\hat{z}_{U})
must lie on
[x_{U},y_{U}]_{U}
. This is because
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
isometrically preserves the edge components of the decomposition
T_{U}=T^{e}_{U}\cup T^{c}_{U}
, and this decomposition determines a decomposition of
[x_{U},y_{U}]_{U}
, whose edge components are in isometric bijective correspondence (via
q_{U}
) with the components of
\gamma_{U}
in the complement of the cluster points it contains.

Hence if
\hat{z}\in\gamma
with
d_{T_{U}}(z_{U},[x_{U},y_{U}]_{U})>0
, then
\hat{z}_{U}
must be contained in a cluster point of
\widehat{T}_{U}
. Suppose, for a contradiction, that
d_{T_{U}}(z_{U},[x_{U},y_{U}]_{U})>100r
. Let
C\subset T^{c}_{U}
be the cluster component containing
z_{U}
, and let
W\in\mathcal{U}
be any
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite domain with
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{W}_{U},z_{U})>10r
and
d_{T_{U}}(\delta^{W}_{U},[x_{U},y_{U}]_{U})>10r
. Since
W
is
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal and bipartite, it is involved in the definition of
z_{U},x_{U}
, and
y_{U}
. And since
\delta^{W}_{U}
separates
z_{U}
from
x_{U},y_{U}
, it follows that
\hat{z}_{W}
is one endpoint of interval
\widehat{T}_{W}
, and
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{y}_{W}
is the other, where
\widehat{T}_{W}
is nondegenerate (with length coarsely
K
) by item (6) of Lemma
7.14
. However this contradicts Lemma
14.7
, so (1) is proven.

For (2), suppose for a contradiction that there exist
s<t\in[0,d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{x},\hat{y})]
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
so that
d_{T_{U}}(B_{U}(\gamma(s)),y_{U})-d_{T_{U}}(B_{U}(\gamma(t)),y_{U})>100r
. Observe that we must have that
\gamma_{U}(t),\gamma_{U}(s)\in\widehat{T}_{U}
are at the same cluster point, otherwise we get a contradiction of Lemma
14.7
. Set
s_{U}=B_{U}(\gamma(s))
and
t_{U}=B_{U}(\gamma(t))
, and let
C\subset T^{c}_{U}
be the cluster component containing
s_{U},t_{U}
. Our contradiction assumption implies that there exists a
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite
W\in\mathcal{U}
with
\delta^{W}_{U}\subset C
so that one component of
T_{U}-\delta^{W}_{U}
contains
x_{U}
and
t_{U}
, while the other component contains
y_{U}
and
s_{U}
, with
x_{U},t_{U},y_{U},s_{U}
all at least
10r
-away from
\delta^{W}_{U}
.

Since
W
is involved in the definition of all of
x_{U},s_{U},y_{U},t_{U}
, we get that
\hat{x}_{W}=\hat{s}_{W}\neq\hat{y}_{W}=\hat{t}_{W}
are at opposite ends of the (nondegenerate) interval
\widehat{T}_{W}
, which again contradicts Lemma
14.7
. This completes the proof of the claim and hence the proposition.
∎

14.3.
Hyperplanes and product regions

We finish this section with an observation about how cubically-defined paths in
\mathcal{X}
navigate its product regions (see Subsection
2.6
).

Lemma 14.11
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a cubical model for any finite
F\subset\mathcal{X}
and finite set of hierarchy rays
\Lambda
. Then there exists a constant
\nu^{\prime}>0
depending only on
\mathcal{X}
,
|F\cup\Lambda|
, and the constants in the construction of
\mathcal{Q}
, so that for any
\mathcal{Q}
-hyperplane
\mathbf{h}
, if
U\in\mathcal{U}
is the domain label for
\mathbf{h}
, then
\widehat{\Omega}(\mathbf{h})\subset\mathcal{N}_{\nu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{P}_{U})
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{x}\in\mathbf{h}
and
(x_{U})=\Omega(\hat{x})\in\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}T_{U}
. Then Lemma
12.13
implies that for any
U\sqsubset V
or
U\pitchfork V
, we have
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\hat{x})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
. Suppose, for a contradiction, that
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})>100r
, where
r=r(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
is the cluster separation constant (Subsection
7.2
).

Since
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\hat{x})=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, there exists a cluster
C\subset T^{c}_{V}
containing both
x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V}
. Moreover, there exists a
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite
W\in\mathcal{U}
with
W\sqsubset V
so that
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{W}_{V},x_{V})>10r
and
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{W}_{V},\delta^{V}_{V})>10r
. Note that
W\pitchfork U
. When
U\sqsubset V
, an application of Lemma
2.17
implies that
d_{T_{W}}(\delta^{U}_{W},\delta^{V}_{W}(\delta^{U}_{V}))<E^{\prime}
and hence
\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}
is at one endpoint of the (nondegenerate) interval
\widehat{T}_{W}
, whereas
\hat{x}_{W}
is at the other endpoint, which contradicts Lemma
12.13
. Hence no such
W
exists, and
d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})
is bounded in terms of the construction, showing that
\widehat{\Omega}(\mathbf{h})\subset\mathcal{N}_{\nu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{P}_{U})
for
\nu^{\prime}>0
controlled by the setup.

On the other hand, when
U\pitchfork V
, then there exists
a\in F\cup\Lambda
, so that
d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{V}_{U},a_{V})<E^{\prime}
. Since
\delta^{W}_{V}
separates
x_{V}
from
a_{V}
, it follows that
\hat{x}_{W}
and
\hat{a}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}
are opposite endpoints of the (nondegenerate) interval
\widehat{T}_{W}
, which contradicts Lemma
12.13
. This completes the proof.
∎

15.
Median structures, hierarchical and cubical

In this section, we complete the proof of our cubical model theorem, Theorem
B
, by explaining why the map
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is quasi-median.

The purpose of isolating this discussion in its own section is to make transparent the ways medians behave in the various parts of the construction, instead of having them sprinkled throughout the paper. Incidentally, this shows how disconnected medians are from the underlying construction, as compared to Bowditch’s construction
[
Bow19
]
, where the median property is a crucial part of the axiomatic setup. To be sure, the median property of Behrstock-Hagen-Sisto’s original construction
[
BHS21
]
has proven useful in essentially every application
[
BHS21
,
HHP20
,
DMS20
,
DZ22
]
.

We begin the section with a very brief overview on (coarse) medians. We then define hierarchical coarse medians and cubical medians, and then explain how the former winds itself to within bounded distance of the latter in the cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
.

Remark 15.1
.

In a cube complex, one can take medians between triples of
0
-cubes which are possibly at infinity. This requires the formalism of a median algebra, which we do not need here for our purposes. In the same vein, one can define a median algebra structure on the set of extended
0
-consistent tuples
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
in any of our cubical models. We leave this for future work.

15.1.
(Coarse) median overview

In
[
Bow13
]
, Bowditch introduced the notion of a coarse median space, to generalize some of the properties that median spaces possess, see
[
CDH10
]
and also Bowditch’s book
[
Bow22
]
for a thorough treatment of the latter. We begin with defining a median metric space (see e.g.
[
Ver93
,
CDH10
]
):

Definition 15.2

(Intervals and medians)
.

Let
x,y\in X
be points in a metric space
(X,d_{X})
. The
interval
between
x,y
in
X
is the set

I(x,y)=\{z\in X|d_{X}(x,y)=d_{X}(x,z)+d_{X}(z,y)\}.

•

We say
X
is a
median space
if for all
x,y,z
we have that

I(x,y)\cap I(x,z)\cap I(y,z)

contains exactly one point, which is called the
median
of
x,y,z
and denote by
\mathfrak{m}_{X}(x,y,z)
.

We get a particularly nice description of this property for graphs:

Definition 15.3

(Median graph)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graph with unit edge lengths endowed with its path metric
d
. We say
\Gamma
is a
median graph
if there exists a map
\mathfrak{m}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma^{3}\to\Gamma
such that the following holds:

•

If
x,yz\in\Gamma
, then
d(x,y)=d(x,\mathfrak{m}_{\Gamma}(x,y,z))+d(\mathfrak{m}_{\Gamma}(x,y,z),y)
, and similarly for the pairs
x,z
and
y,z
.

Note that when
\Gamma
is a tree, then
\mathfrak{m}_{\Gamma}(a,b,c)
is precisely the center of unique tripod subgraph of
\Gamma
with
a,b,c
as leaves.

While the above definition may seem specialized, work of Chepoi
[
Che00
]
shows that median graphs are in bijective correspondence with the
1
-skeleta of CAT(0) cube complexes. This fact motivates the following definition due to Bowditch:

Definition 15.4

(Coarse median)
.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space and
\mathfrak{m}:X^{3}\to X
be an operation satisfying the following properties:

(1)

(triples) There exists constants
\kappa,h(0)
such that for all
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime},c,c^{\prime}\in X
, we have

d(\mathfrak{m}(a,b,c),\mathfrak{m}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}))\leq\kappa(d(a,a^{\prime})+d(b,b^{\prime})+d(c,c^{\prime}))+h(0).

(2)

(tuples) There exists a function
h:\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
so that for any finite
F\subset X
with
|F|=n\leq\infty
, there exists a CAT(0) cube complex
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and maps
\alpha:F\to\mathcal{F}^{(0)}_{n}
and
\beta:\mathcal{F}^{(0)}_{n}\to X
so that
d(f,\beta(\alpha(f))<h(p)
for all
f\in F
, and so that

d(\beta(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}(x,y,z)),\mathfrak{m}_{X}(\beta(x),\beta(y),\beta(z)))\leq h(n)

for all
x,y,z\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{X}
are the medians in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and
X
, respectively.

Remark 15.5
.

We note that the existence of a cube complex in the tuple condition for coarse median spaces is not unrelated to the cubical models constructed in this paper and in
[
BHS21
,
Bow19
]
. The key point is that the maps
\alpha,\beta
above need only preserve the medians and not necessarily distances.

Notably, every hyperbolic space is coarse-median, where we can take the approximating cube complexes to be Gromov modeling trees, and medians to be coarse centers of triangles. To motivate our next observation, we need the following definition:

Definition 15.6

(Quasi-median)
.

A map
\phi:X\to Y
between coarse median spaces is
C
-quasi-median
if for any triple
a,b,c\in X
, we have
d_{Y}(\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{X}(a,b,c)),\mathfrak{m}_{Y}(\phi(a),\phi(b),\phi(c)))<C
.

The following is an exercise in
\delta
-hyperbolic geometry along the lines of Lemma
6.2
:

Lemma 15.7
.

Let
X
is
\delta
-hyperbolic,
F\subset X
and
\Lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
are finite, and
\phi:T\to\mathrm{hull}_{X}(F\cup\Lambda)
be any tree modeling the hull of
F\cup\Lambda
in
X
. Then
\phi
is
C
-quasi-median for some
C=C(\delta)>0
.

15.2.
Coarse medians in HHSes

The following construction was originally contained in
[
BM11
]
for mapping class groups, and was later generalized to every hierarchically hyperbolic space as in Definition
15.4
by Bowditch by
[
Bow17
]
; see also
[
BHS19
]
.

Given an HHS
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
and
a,b,c
, consider the tuple
(\mathfrak{m}_{U}(a,b,c))_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}=(\mathfrak{m}_{U}(\pi_{U}(a),\pi_{U}(b),\pi_{U}(c)))_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}
. The following is contained in
[
BHS19
, Lemma 2.6]
:

Lemma 15.8
.

The tuple
(\mathfrak{m}_{U}(a,b,c))_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}
is
\theta_{\mathfrak{m}}
-consistent for some
\theta_{\mathfrak{m}}=\theta_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathcal{X})>0
.

Hence we can define a map
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}^{3}\to\mathcal{X}
by taking
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c)
to be any realization point for the tuple
(\mathfrak{m}_{U}(a,b,c))_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}
as provided by Realization
2.4
, and this map
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}
gives a coarse median structure on
\mathcal{X}

[
BHS19
, Theorem 7.3]
.

15.3.
Media in hierarchical hulls

For the rest of this section, we fix our usual setup of a finite set
F\cup\Lambda
of internal points and hierarchy rays in an HHS
\mathcal{X}
. Fix the hierarchical hull threshold
\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
provided by Proposition
3.14
, and let
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\theta_{0}}(F\cup\Lambda)
be the hierarchical hull and
\mathrm{ret}_{H}:\mathcal{X}\to H
be the coarse retraction provided by Lemma
6.6
.

Recall that
H
has an induced HHS structure (Lemma
6.8
), in which the index set is
\mathcal{U}
and the hyperbolic spaces are
\{\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)|U\in\mathcal{U}\}
, with projections being compositions
r_{U}\circ\pi_{U}:H\to\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
, where
r_{U}:\mathcal{C}(U)\to\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
is the hyperbolic retraction onto the quasi-convex set
\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda)
. For any
a,b,c\in H
, let
\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c)
be the coarse median of
a,b,c
in
H
, using this induced HHS structure. Finally, let
i_{H}:H\to\mathcal{X}
be the inclusion, which is defined by completing the partial tuples defined by points in
H
by setting, for any
V\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathcal{U}

f_{V}
for fixed
f\in F
.

The following lemma is straight-forward, and says that all of the various reasonable ways of building a median coincide:

Lemma 15.9
.

There exists
A=A(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|,\theta_{0})>0
so that the following hold:

(1)

The map
i_{H}:H\to\mathcal{X}
is an
A
-median
(A,A)
-quasi-isometric embedding,

(2)

For any
a,b,c\in H
, each of
d_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c),\mathrm{ret}_{H}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c))),
and
i_{H}(\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c))
are at pairwise distance at most
A
.

Proof.

The fact that
i_{H}:H\to\mathcal{X}
is an
(A,A)
-quasi-isometric embedding for
A=A(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|,\theta_{0})>0
is an application of the Distance Formula
2.9
, since each of the hyperbolic hulls
H_{V}
of
\pi_{V}(F\cup\Lambda)\subset\mathcal{C}(V)
for
V\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathcal{U}
has uniformly bounded diameter (controlled by our largeness constant
K
), and hence we can use a threshold so that all of those terms vanish.

For the rest of the statement, note that by definition
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c)=(\mathfrak{m}_{U})_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}
is a realization point for the tuple of hyperbolic medians of the projections of
a,b,c
, which are uniformly close to
H_{U}
for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
, by definition of
H
. Since each
H_{U}
is uniformly quasi-convex in the hyperbolic space
\mathcal{C}(U)
and hence median quasi-convex (with constants depending only on
\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|,\theta_{0}
), each coordinate
\mathfrak{m}_{U}
is uniformly close to the hyperbolic hull
H_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{U}(a,b,c)
. On the other hand, the retraction
\mathrm{ret}_{H}
is defined by realizing the tuple obtained by coordinate-wise closest-point projecting
\pi_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c))
to
H_{U}
. Hence the coordinates of
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c)
and
\mathrm{ret}_{H}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c))
are uniformly close, and hence the points themselves are uniformly close by the Distance Formula
2.9
.

It remains to see that
d_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c),i_{H}(\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c))<A
, but this again is essentially by construction, since each of the coordinates for
U\in\mathcal{U}
used to define
\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c)
and
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{X}}(a,b,c)
are uniformly close, while the coordinates for
V\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathcal{U}
are again bounded in terms of
K
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 15.10
.

Median quasi-convexity of hulls is contained in
[
BHS19
,
RST18
]
, but we have included a simple proof for completeness. Note that the proof uses the Distance Formula
2.9
, which is not a problem for us, as our median considerations are essentially independent of the rest of the construction, and in particular of our proof of the upper bound of the distance formula (Corollary
C
).

In particular, Lemma
15.9
allows us to work with medians as defined in the hull
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

15.4.
Median structure of
\mathcal{Q}

In this subsection, we will prove that
\mathcal{Q}
admits a median structure directly from the definitions. In this subsection, we need not assume that the
\widehat{T}_{U}
are simplicial trees, as produced by Corollary
10.9
.

We begin by describing intervals in
\mathcal{Q}
:

Lemma 15.11
.

For any
\hat{a},\hat{b}\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have
I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})=\{\hat{z}\in\mathcal{Q}|\hat{z}_{U}\in[\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U}]\hskip 3.61371pt\text{for all}\hskip 3.61371ptU\in\mathcal{U}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{a},\hat{b}\in\mathcal{Q}
and let
\hat{z}\in I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})
, which we observe contains any combinatorial geodesic between
\hat{a},\hat{b}
. Note that the
\ell^{1}
-distance sums corresponding to
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b}),d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{z}),
and
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{z},\hat{b})
all have finitely-many positive terms. Hence we have the following by definition of
\hat{z}\in I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{V\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{z})+d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{z},\hat{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{V\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{a}_{V},\hat{z}_{V})+\sum_{V\in\mathcal{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{V}}(\hat{z}_{V},\hat{b}_{V}).

But if
\hat{z}_{U}\notin[\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U}]
for some
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then
d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{z}_{U})+d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{z}_{U},\hat{b}_{U})\gneq d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U})
. Since the triangle inequality forces
d_{\widehat{T}_{W}}(\hat{a}_{W},\hat{z}_{W})+d_{\widehat{T}_{W}}(\hat{z}_{W},\hat{b}_{W})\geq d_{\widehat{T}_{W}}(\hat{a}_{W},\hat{b}_{W})
for all
W\in\mathcal{U}
, this says that the last line of the above equation is bigger than the first, giving us a contradiction and completing the proof.

∎

Observe that each
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a tree, and hence has a median metric structure, where
\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})
is the center point of the unique tripod with
\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U}\in\widehat{T}_{U}
as its leaves. Equivalently,

\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})=[\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U}]\cap[\hat{a}_{U},\hat{c}_{U}]\cap[\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U}]=I_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U})\cap I_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})\cap I_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U}),

where
[\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U}]
is the unique geodesic in
\widehat{T}_{U}
between
\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U}
, etc.

The natural candidate for a median for
\mathcal{Q}
is to take the tuple of medians in each coordinate, namely an exact version of the HHS setup:

Lemma 15.12
.

For any
\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have
\left(\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})\right)\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Proof.

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, set
\mathfrak{m}_{U}=\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})
. We claim that the tuple
(\mathfrak{m}_{U})
is
0
-consistent and canonical.

For consistency, let
U\pitchfork V
. By
0
-consistency of
\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}
, up to switching the roles of
U,V
and substituting
\hat{c}
for one of
\hat{a},\hat{b}
, we have
\hat{a}_{V}=\hat{b}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, with Lemma
15.11
forcing
\mathfrak{m}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, since it lies on the unique geodesic between
\hat{a}_{V}
and
\hat{b}_{V}
which is a point.

Now let
U\sqsubset V
, and we may assume that
\mathfrak{m}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
, otherwise we are done. Up to exchanging the roles of
\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}
, we have that
[\hat{a}_{V},\hat{b}_{V}]\cap\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}=\emptyset
. Then
0
-consistency and the BGI property of Lemma
7.14
forces that
\hat{a}_{U}=\hat{b}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}([\hat{a}_{V},\hat{b}_{V}])=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{V})
, which shows that
\mathfrak{m}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{V})
, as required.

Finally, for canonicality, observe that since
\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}
are canonical, we have that
\hat{a}_{U}=\hat{b}_{U}=\hat{c}_{U}
for all but finitely-many
U\in\mathcal{U}
, and hence
(\mathfrak{m}_{U})
is canonical by Definition
7.9
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 15.13

(Median structure on
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
)
.

It is possible to show that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
admits the structure of a median algebra, where the median is defined as above. Notably, medians of non-canonical tuples need not be non-canonical, just as medians of triples of
0
-cubes in the Roller boundary of a cube complex can be contained in the interior of the space. The median structure on
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
seems useful and we leave any investigation of it for future work.

With these lemmas in hand, we can prove our structural statement:

Proposition 15.14
.

For any
\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}\in\mathcal{Q}
, set
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c})=\left(\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})\right)
. Then

I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})\cap I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{c})\cap I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{b},\hat{c})=\{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c})\}.

•

In particular, the map
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}^{3}\to\mathcal{Q}
defined a metric median structure on
\mathcal{Q}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
15.11
, any point in
I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})\cap I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{c})\cap I_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{b},\hat{c})
must have
U
-coordinate
\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, so we are done by Lemma
15.12
, which says that the tuple
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c})=\left(\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})\right)
is contained in
\mathcal{Q}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 15.15

(
\mathcal{Q}
is a median sublagebra of
\mathcal{Y}
)
.

In fact, one can show that taking tuples of medians (as we have done above) constitutes a median algebra structure on
\mathcal{Y}=\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}
, and hence
\mathcal{Q}
is a median subalgebra because it is closed under taking medians. This fact is useful, as discussed at the beginning of this section, to prove that
\mathcal{Q}
admits a CAT(0) metric, even when the
\widehat{T}_{U}
are not simplicial trees. See
[
Bow22
, Subsection 3.1]
for the appropriate definitions.

We can also use this median structure to prove that any tree-trimming map produced by Theorem
10.2
is
0
-median:

Corollary 15.16
.

Any tree-trimming map
\Delta:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
10.2
is
0
-median.

Proof.

The map
\Delta:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a coordinate-wise combination of collapsing maps
\Delta_{U}:\widehat{T}_{U}\to\widehat{T}^{\prime}_{U}
, which only collapses subtrees to points. Hence each
\Delta_{U}
preserves the coordinate medians, meaning
\Delta
preserves global medians.
∎

15.5.
Cubical media

Our next goal is to understand the median structure on a CAT(0) cube complex, so that we can show that the above median structure on
\mathcal{Q}
and its cubical dual
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
coincide.

Intervals in cube complexes have particular nice descriptions, which we state at the following standard lemma
[
Che00
]
:

Lemma 15.17
.

Let
a,b\in X
be
0
-cells of a CAT(0) cube complex. Then

(1)

Any
\ell^{1}
-geodesic between
a,b
is contained in
I_{X}(a,b)
.

(2)

Any
0
-cell
z\in I_{X}(a,b)
is contained in an
\ell^{1}
-geodesic between
a,b
.

(3)

For any
0
-cell
z\in I_{X}(a,b)
corresponds to a consistent collection of half-spaces with the following orientation constraints:

•

If
\mathbf{h}
is a hyperplane which separates
a
from
b
, then
z
is in the half-space of
\mathbf{h}
containing
a,b
.

•

Otherwise,
\mathbf{h}
can choose either orientation for
z
.

This allows us to give the following equivalent characterization of the median of a triple of points in a cube complex:

Lemma 15.18
.

For any three
0
-cells in a CAT(0) cube complex
a,b,c\in X
, the median
\mathfrak{m}_{X}(a,b,c)
is the
0
-cell corresponding to tuple of half-space orientations where each hyperplane
\mathbf{h}
chooses the half-space containing the majority of
a,b,c
.

15.6.
Cubical medians in
\mathcal{Q}

In this subsection, we now assume that
\mathcal{Q}
has the simplicial structure as produced by Corollary
10.9
, namely where each
\widehat{T}_{U}
is a simplicial tree.

Proposition 15.19
.

The dualization map
\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
is
0
-median.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
is an isometry with inverse
\mathcal{D}^{-1}
, it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{D}^{-1}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})\to\mathcal{Q}
is
0
-median.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
be consistent, canonical collections of orientations and
\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}
their images under
\mathcal{D}^{-1}
. Lemma
15.18
says that
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})}(a,b,c)
is the unique set of orientations, where each hyperplane chooses the half-space containing the majority of
a,b,c
. Recall that Lemma
13.2
says that a hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{U}
separates
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\mathcal{Q}
if and only if its component hyperplane
h_{U}
separates
\hat{x}_{U}
from
\hat{y}_{U}
.

Hence for each
\mathcal{Q}
-hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{U}
, we have that
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})}(a,b,c)
chooses the half-space of
\mathcal{Q}
corresponding to the half-tree of
\widehat{T}_{U}-\{h_{U}\}
containing the majority of
\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U}
. But this means that
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})}(a,b,c)
forces all tree-hyperplanes in
\widehat{T}_{U}
to choose the half-tree containing
\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})
, meaning that
\left(\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}})(a,b,c))\right)_{U}=\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{a}_{U},\hat{b}_{U},\hat{c}_{U})
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, completing the proof.
∎

15.7.
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is quasi-median

Finally, we prove that our map
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
(Subsection
7.7
) is quasi-median, which completes the proof of Theorem
B
:

Theorem 15.20
.

There exists
QM=QM(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that the map
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is
QM
-quasi-median.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
d_{\mathcal{Q}}(\widehat{\Psi}(\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c)),\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}))
is bounded in terms of
\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|
, where
a,b,c\in H
and
\widehat{\Psi}(a)=\hat{a}
,
\widehat{\Psi}(b)=\hat{b}
, and
\widehat{\Psi}(c)=\hat{c}
. Set
\widehat{\Psi}(\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c))=\hat{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}))=\hat{\mathfrak{m}}
. Hence it suffices to bound
d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}_{U},\hat{\mathfrak{m}}_{U})
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

Recall that the map
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
is defined coordinate-wise via
\hat{\psi}_{U}=q_{U}\circ\psi_{U}=q_{U}\circ\phi_{U}^{-1}\circ r_{U}\circ\pi_{U}
, where
r_{U}:\mathcal{C}(U)\to\phi(T_{U})
is the retraction onto
\phi_{U}(T_{U})
,
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
is the tree-model map (which is a uniform quasi-median quasi-isometry by Lemmas
6.2
and
15.7
), and the quotient map
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
which collapses cluster subtrees to points.

Set
a_{U}=\psi_{U}(a)=\phi_{U}^{-1}\circ r_{U}\circ\pi_{U}(a)
, and similarly for
b,c
.

Claim 15.21
.

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we have
d_{T_{U}}(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{U}}(a_{U},b_{U},c_{U}),\psi_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c)))
is bounded in terms of
\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|
.

Proof.

By combining Lemmas
15.7
and
15.9
, we have that
r_{U}\circ\pi_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c))
is uniformly close (in
\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|
) to
\phi_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{U}}(a_{U},b_{U},c_{U}))
in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, and hence the claim follows from Lemma
15.7
.
∎

Claim 15.22
.

For any
x,y,z\in T_{U}
, we have
q_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{U}}(x,y,z))=\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z})
. That is,
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
is
0
-median.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the fact that
q_{U}
just collapses subtrees to points.
∎

With these in hand, we can now prove the theorem. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Claim
15.21
implies that
d_{T_{U}}(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{U}}(a_{U},b_{U},c_{U}),\psi_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{H}(a,b,c)))
is bounded, while Claim
15.22
says that
q_{U}(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{U}}(x,y,z))=\mathfrak{m}_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z}
coincide. On the other hand,
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
is
1
-Lipschitz, so we are done.
∎

15.8.
Restating main theorem in full generality

We finish this section by restating our cubical model theorem in full generality.

Theorem 15.23

(Cubical model theorem, general version)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a (proper) hierarchically hyperbolic space,
F
a nonempty finite set of interior points, and
\Lambda
a finite set of hierarchy rays, with
H
their hierarchical hull. Let
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
be the set of relevant domains where
K
is as in Subsection
5.2
. Let
\{T_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
be the trees in a reduced tree system for
F\cup\Lambda
as in Definition
7.1
, and let
\{\widehat{T}_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
be the family of collapsed trees with
\mathcal{Q}
the
0
-consistent canonical subset of
\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}
. Then there exists
C=C(\mathcal{X},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that:

(1)

The map
\widehat{\Psi}:H\to\mathcal{Q}
(Subsection
7.7
) is a
C
-quasi-median
(C,C)
-quasi-isometry.

(2)

We can replace the trees
\{\widehat{T}_{U}\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
with their simplicial versions as in Corollary
10.9
, and this induces a
0
-median
(C,C)
-quasi-isometry between canonical
0
-consistent sets.

(3)

The simplicial version of
\mathcal{Q}
is 0-median isometric to its cubical dual (Subsection
13.2
).

Proof.

That any family of Gromov modeling trees for
H_{U}=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{C}(U)}(F\cup\Lambda)
(which exist by Lemma
6.2
) for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
can be converted into a reduced tree system (Definition
7.1
) is explained in Subsection
7.1
. The rest of the construction happens in that generality, with the first conclusion being Theorem
13.6
, the second conclusion being Corollary
10.9
, and the third conclusion being a combination of Corollary
11.2
and Theorem
15.20
. This completes the proof.
∎

16.
Simplicial and hierarchical boundaries

In this section, we analyze the simplicial boundary
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
of the cubical models
\mathcal{Q}
for the hierarchical hull
H
of some finite set of points and hierarchy rays
F\cup\Lambda
. As above, let
K
be sufficiently large (Subsection
5.2
) and
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

We begin with a brief discussion of the simplicial boundary of a cube complex (introduced by Hagen
[
Hag13a
]
) and the (untopologized) simplicial structure of the hierarchical boundary
\partial H\subset\partial\mathcal{X}
for
H
(introduced by Hagen, Sisto, and the author
[
DHS17
]
). With these preliminaries in hand, we then begin our investigation in their connection in earnest. Our ultimate goal is the following theorem:

Theorem 16.1
.

There exists a simplicial isomorphism
\partial\widehat{\Omega}:\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial H
. Moreover,
\partial\widehat{\Omega}
extends the map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
in the following sense:

•

If
\mathcal{W}
is a UBS representing a simplex
\nu\subset\partial_{\Delta}(\mathcal{Q})
with
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})
and
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{W})
converging to
\pi_{U}(\lambda)\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
for some
\lambda\in\Lambda
, then
\partial\widehat{\Omega}(\nu)
contains a boundary
0
-simplex
\sigma
so that
\mathrm{supp}(\sigma)=U
and
\mathfrak{p}(\sigma)=\pi_{U}(\lambda)
. In fact, all
0
-simplices of
\partial\widehat{\Omega}(\nu)
arise in this way.

In the above, the
dust
of a UBS refers to (relative) projection data encoded into its hyperplanes, see Definition
16.15
.

16.1.
UBSes

In this subsection, we introduce the notion of a UBS in and the simplicial boundary of a CAT(0) cube complex. Our treatment follows Hagen
[
Hag13a
]
, but see also
[
FFH23
]
.

A UBS in a CAT(0) cube complex is a family of hyperplanes that “point” in a given “direction”.

Definition 16.2

(Unidirectional, inseparable, facing triples, and UBSes)
.

Let
X
be a CAT(0) cube complex and
\mathcal{W}
a set of hyperplanes in
X
.

•

We say that
\mathcal{W}
is
unidirectional
if for every hyperplane
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{W}
, one half-space of
\mathbf{h}
contains all but finitely-many hyperplanes in
\mathcal{W}
.

•

We say that
\mathcal{W}
is
inseparable
if whenever
\mathbf{h}
is a hyperplane separating
\mathbf{h}_{1},\mathbf{h}_{2}\in\mathcal{W}
, then we have
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{W}

•

Three hyperplanes
\mathbf{h}_{1},\mathbf{h}_{2},\mathbf{h}_{3}
in
X
form a
facing triple
if for each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, some half-space of
\mathbf{h}_{i}
contains both of
\mathbf{h}_{j},\mathbf{h}_{k}
for
j,k\in\{1,2,3\}-\{i\}
.

•

We say that
\mathcal{W}
is a
unidirectional boundary system
(UBS) if it is infinite, unidirectional, inseparable, and contains no facing triples.

The defining properties of a UBS generalize the properties of the family of hyperplanes crossed by a combinatorial geodesic ray
[
Hag13a
, Lemma 3.3]
:

Lemma 16.3
.

Let
\gamma
be a combinatorial geodesic ray in a CAT(0) cube complex
X
and
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)
the set of hyperplanes crossed by
\gamma
. Then
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)
is a UBS.

Note that the definition of a UBS does not prevent one from containing infinite subfamilies of hyperplanes, so that every hyperplane in one family crosses (almost) every hyperplane in the other. In fact, every UBS essentially admits a decomposition into such a collection of families.

Definition 16.4

(Almost equivalence, minimality)
.

We say that two UBSes
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}
in a CAT(0) cube complex are
almost equivalent
, written
\mathcal{V}\sim\mathcal{W}
, if
\#(\mathcal{V}\triangle\mathcal{W})<\infty
.

•

A UBS
\mathcal{W}
is
minimal
if any UBS
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{W}
satisfies
\mathcal{V}\sim\mathcal{W}
.

By Proposition
12.18
, there is bound on the cardinality of a set of pairwise intersecting hyperplanes in
\mathcal{Q}
. Hence the following lemma implies that any UBS in
\mathcal{Q}
contains a minimal UBS
[
Hag13a
, Lemma 3.7]
:

Lemma 16.5
.

If a CAT(0) cube complex
X
contains no infinite family of pairwise-crossing hyperplanes, and
\mathcal{W}
contains a UBS, then
\mathcal{W}
contains a minimal UBS.

The following useful theorem due to Hagen
[
Hag13a
, Theorem 3.10]
(see also
[
Hag18
, Theorem A]
) implies that every UBS in
\mathcal{Q}
admits a
minimal decomposition
in the following sense:

Theorem 16.6
.

Suppose that
X
is a finite dimensional CAT(0) cube complex. Let
\nu
be an almost-equivalence class of UBSes. Then
\nu
has a representative of the form
\mathcal{W}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}
, where
n<\infty
, each
\mathcal{V}_{i}
is minimal, and for all
1\leq i<j\leq k
, if
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{j}
, then
\mathbf{h}
crosses all but finitely-many hyperplanes in
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

•

Moreover, this decomposition is unique in the sense that if
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{U}_{i}
is almost-equivalent to
\mathcal{W}
, and each
\mathcal{U}_{i}
is minimal, then
n=k
and, up to re-ordering, we have
\mathcal{V}_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i}
for all
i
.

The above theorem will allow us to focus on understanding the structure of minimal UBSes in
\mathcal{Q}
. Recall that
\mathcal{Q}
is finite dimensional by Corollary
12.11
, so we can apply this theorem directly. But before we begin our analysis, we need one more tool.

Definition 16.7
.

Given a set of hyperplanes
\mathcal{V}
in a CAT(0) cube complex
X
, the
inseparable closure

\overline{\mathcal{V}}
of
\mathcal{V}
is the intersection of all inseparable sets of hyperplanes containing
\mathcal{V}
.

•

Equivalent,
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
consists of
\mathcal{V}
along with every hyperplane
\mathbf{h}
for which there exists
\mathbf{h}_{1},\mathbf{h}_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
so that
\mathbf{h}
separates
\mathbf{h}_{1}
from
\mathbf{h}_{2}
.

We make a basic but useful observation about inseparable closures of subsets of UBSes in any CAT(0) cube complex:

Lemma 16.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a UBS in a CAT(0) cube complex and
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{A}
is an infinite subset. Then
\overline{\mathcal{V}}\subset\mathcal{A}
and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
is a UBS. Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}
is minimal, then
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
is minimal and almost equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

The fact that
\overline{\mathcal{V}}\subset\mathcal{A}
is by definition of inseparable closure, since
\mathcal{A}
is inseparable. Hence
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
is unidirectional, infinite, inseparable, and has no facing triples because
\mathcal{A}
does not, making
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
a UBS. The fact that
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
is minimal and almost equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
is immediate from the definition of minimality.
∎

16.2.
Simplicial boundary

Hagen’s Theorem
16.6
allows for the definition of the simplicial boundary.

Definition 16.9

(Simplices at infinity)
.

Let
X
be a finite dimensional CAT(0) cube complex.

•

A UBS
\mathcal{W}
is
n
-dimensional
if the minimal decomposition of
\mathcal{W}
has
n
factors.

•

A
0
-simplex at infinity
is an almost-equivalence class of minimal UBSes.

•

A
n
-simplex at infinity
is an almost-equivalence class of
(n+1)
-dimensional UBSes.

•

If
\nu,\omega
are simplices at infinity, we say
\nu\leq\mu
if and only if there exist representatives
\mathcal{V}
of
\nu
and
\mathcal{W}
of
\omega
so that
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{W}
.

We note that the relation
\leq
defined above is a partial-order by Theorem
16.6
.

Definition 16.10

(Simplicial boundary)
.

Let
X
be a CAT(0) cube complex in which every collection of pairwise crossing hyperplanes is finite. The
simplicial boundary

\partial_{\Delta}X
of
X
is the geometric realization of the abstract simplicial complex whose set of simplices is the set of simplices of
X
at infinity, in which
\nu
is a face of
\omega
if and only if
\nu\leq\omega
.

16.3.
Simplices in the HHS boundary

We now turn to discussing the (untopologized) HHS boundary of our hierarchical hull
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(F\cup\Lambda)
. We begin with a general definition of the simplicial structure of the HHS boundary
\partial\mathcal{X}
of any HHS
\mathcal{X}
. This construction was introduced in
[
DHS17
]
.

The difference between the following definition and Definition
3.1
is that we are only describing the simplices in
\partial\mathcal{X}
, and Definition
3.1
describes a point in a simplex. The underlying definitions are otherwise the same.

Definition 16.11

(Boundary simplex, support set)
.

Given an HHS
\mathcal{X}
, we can define
boundary simplicies
and their support sets as follows:

•

A
boundary
0
-simplex

\sigma
consists of

–

A
support domain

\mathrm{supp}(\sigma)\in\mathfrak{S}
,

–

A single point
\mathfrak{p}(\sigma)\in\partial\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{supp}(\sigma))
.

•

A
boundary
n
-simplex

\Delta
is a collection of
0
-simplices
\{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\}
whose supports are pairwise orthogonal.

–

The
support set

\mathrm{supp}(\Delta)
of an
n
-simplex
\Delta
is
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta)=\{\mathrm{supp}(\sigma_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{supp}(\sigma_{n})\}
.

•

If
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
are two boundary simplices, let
\Delta\leq\Delta^{\prime}
mean that
\Delta\subset\Delta^{\prime}
.

Clearly, the relation
\leq
is partial order. This will allow us to define the simplicial structure on
\partial H
:

Definition 16.12
.

For any HHS
\mathcal{X}
, the
HHS boundary

\partial\mathcal{X}
of
\mathcal{X}
is the geometric realization of the abstract simplicial complex whose set of simplices is the set of boundary simplices as in Definition
16.11
, in which a simplex
\Delta
is a face of
\Delta^{\prime}
if and only if
\Delta\leq\Delta^{\prime}
.

Remark 16.13
.

We note that every boundary point
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{X}
as in Definition
3.1
determines a unique boundary simplex in the sense of Definition
16.11
: Indeed, if
\lambda
is as in Definition
3.1
, then
\lambda
lies in the unique simplex
\Delta
whose
0
-simplices correspond to the
\lambda_{U}\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
for each
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
, where
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
is pairwise orthogonal by definition. As noted in Remark
3.2
, the associated constants
\{a_{U}|U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\}
with
\sum_{U\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)}a_{U}=1
just determine a point in the interior of the simplex
\Delta
.

We note the similarity between Definition
16.10
and Definition
16.11
. In Theorem
16.29
below, we will see that this similarity is actually the result of an isomorphism.

16.4.
Simplicial structure the HHS boundary of the hierarchical hull
H

Our next goal is to describe the simplicial structure on the HHS boundary of the hierarchical hull
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(F\cup\Lambda)
.

For this part, we use the induced HHS structure on
H
, as discussed in Lemma
2.13
and Subsection
6.2
. Using this structure, we get the following description of the boundary
0
-simplices in the HHS boundary
\partial H
of
H
, which of course also describes all higher simplices:

Lemma 16.14
.

For every boundary
0
-simplex
\sigma\in\partial H
, there exists
\lambda\in\Lambda
so that
\mathrm{supp}(\sigma)\in\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)
and
\mathfrak{p}(\sigma)=\pi_{\mathrm{supp}(\sigma)}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

This is immediate from the fact that the boundaries of the hyperbolic spaces in the induced HHS structure on
H
are completely determined by the projections of the elements in
\Lambda
.
∎

16.5.
Ray representatives of minimal UBSes

The main work in the rest of this section is an analysis of the UBSes in the cubical structure of
\mathcal{Q}
, which is provided by Theorem
13.6
.

In this subsection, we analyze minimal UBSes in
\mathcal{Q}
. The idea will be to associate to each minimal UBS
\mathcal{W}
a domain in
\mathcal{U}
on which it is supported, a corresponding point
\lambda\in\Lambda
, and a particular representative of
\mathcal{W}
in its almost equivalence class whose “dust” in
\mathcal{C}(U)
is effectively a ray representing
\pi_{U}(\lambda)
.

Recall that every hyperplane
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{W}
in a UBS
\mathcal{W}
is labeled by its
support domain

\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\in\mathcal{U}
. We can consider
\mathcal{W}_{U}=\{\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{W}|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\sqsubseteq U\}
, the set of hyperplanes whose domain labels
V
satisfy
V\sqsubset U
or
V=U
. To any such hyperplane
\mathbf{h}
we can associate a point
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathbf{h})\in\mathcal{C}(U)
as follows:

Definition 16.15

(Dust)
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be a UBS and
U\in\mathcal{U}
. If
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{W}_{U}
with
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})=V
, the
dust
of
\mathbf{h}
in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, denoted by
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathbf{h})
, is

•

\rho^{V}_{U}
when
V\sqsubset U
, or

•

\phi_{U}(q_{U}^{-1}(h_{V}))
when
V=U
.

where
q_{U}:T_{U}\to\widehat{T}_{U}
is the quotient map from Definition
7.5
and
\phi_{U}:T_{U}\to\mathcal{C}(U)
is the map associated to
T_{U}
.

Of course, we can apply the dust map
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}
to a large collections of hyperplanes, e.g. all of
\mathcal{W}_{U}
, and we will frequently do so.

The following is immediate from the construction:

Lemma 16.16
.

If
\mathcal{W}
is a UBS in
\mathcal{Q}
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then

\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{W}_{U})\subset\mathcal{N}_{2E}(\phi_{U}(T_{U})).

Our next goal is to show that the dust of a UBS spreads out in at least one domain.

Definition 16.17
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be a UBS in
\mathcal{Q}
. The
support set
of
\mathcal{W}
is

\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})=\{U\in\mathcal{U}|\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{W}_{U}))=\infty\}.

The first observation is that every UBS has non-empty support:

Lemma 16.18
.

For any UBS
\mathcal{W}
in
\mathcal{Q}
, we have
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

We claim that since
\mathcal{W}
is infinite, there exists
U\in\mathcal{U}
so that
\mathcal{W}_{U}
is infinite. Now, if
\#\{\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{W}|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})=U\}=\infty
for some
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})
and we are done as the associated tree-hyperplanes must have infinite diameter in
\widehat{T}_{U}
and hence
\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{W}))=\infty
. On the other hand, if
\#\{\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{W}|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})=U\}<\infty
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then there exists an infinite subset
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{W}
so that if
\mathbf{h}\neq\mathbf{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
, then
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\neq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}^{\prime})
. Passing to an infinite subset if necessary, we may assume that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V})\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(a,b)
for some fixed
a,b\in F\cup\Lambda
. In this case, Strong Passing-up
4.7
implies that there exists
W\in\mathcal{U}
and an infinite subset
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
so that
Z\sqsubset W
for all
Z\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})
, and
\mathrm{diam}_{W}(\bigcup_{Z\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})}\rho^{Z}_{W})=\infty
. This completes the proof.
∎

The next goal is to prove that the support set of a minimal UBS contains a unique domain, and moreover that any such minimal UBS admits a “ray” representative in its equivalence class.

Lemma 16.19
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be a minimal UBS in
\mathcal{Q}
. Then there exists
U\in\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})=\{U\}
. Moreover, for any
A>0
, there exists
\lambda\in\Lambda
and a minimal UBS
\mathcal{A}_{A}
with
\mathcal{A}_{A}\sim\mathcal{W}
so that

(1)

For all
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{A}_{A}
, we have
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\sqsubset U
or
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})=U
.

(2)

d_{U}(\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{A}_{A}),\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda\}))>A.

•

In particular, we have that
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{A}_{A}))\subset\mathcal{C}(U)
converges to
\pi_{U}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{C}(U)
.

Proof.

Let
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})
. By Lemma
16.16
and the definition of
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})
, there exists
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
f\in F
so that
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{W}_{U})
spreads out along a ray from
f
to
\pi_{U}(\lambda)
in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, i.e.,

\mathrm{diam}_{U}(\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{W}_{U})\cap\mathcal{N}_{2E}(\mathrm{hull}_{U}(f,\pi_{U}(\lambda))))=\infty.

In particular, we can choose an infinite sequence
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{W}_{U}
so that
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{V})
represents
[\pi_{U}(\lambda)]\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
.

As such, for any
A>0
, we can choose an infinite subsequence
\mathcal{V}_{A}\subset\mathcal{V}
so that
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{V}_{A})
still represents
[\pi_{U}(\lambda)]\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
, while also

d_{U}(\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{V}_{A}),\mathrm{hull}_{U}(F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda\}))>A.

Observe that since
\mathcal{W}
is a UBS, we have that
\mathcal{V}_{A}
is unidirectional, infinite, and without any facing triples. Hence by Lemma
16.8
, we have that its inseparable closure
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{A}
is a minimal UBS which is almost equivalent to
\mathcal{W}
. It remains to prove that
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{A}
satisfies the rest of the conclusion in the lemma.

Claim 16.20
.

For all
\mathbf{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{A}
, we have
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\sqsubseteq U
.

By definition of
\mathbf{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{A}
, there exist
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
-hyperplanes
\mathbf{h}_{B},\mathbf{h}_{C}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
which are separated by
\mathbf{h}
. Let
V=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}),B=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}_{B}),
and
C=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}_{C})
.

Observe that since
\mathbf{h}
separates
\mathbf{h}_{B}
from
\mathbf{h}_{C}
, Proposition
12.18
implies that
B,C
are not orthogonal to
V
. Since both
B,C\sqsubseteq U
, it follows that
U
and
V
are not orthogonal. Since we are also done if either
V\sqsubseteq B
or
V\sqsubseteq C
holds, we have that one of
B\sqsubset V
or
B\pitchfork V
holds, and similarly either
C\sqsubset V
or
C\pitchfork V
holds. In particular,
B,C
have well-defined relative projections to
V
.

Finally, note that if
U\sqsubset V
or
U\pitchfork V
, then
\hat{\delta}^{B}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{C}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{V}
because
B,C\sqsubset U
, whereas Lemma
13.2
requires that
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h})
separate
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{B})=\hat{\delta}^{B}_{V}
from
\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{C})=\hat{\delta}^{C}_{V}
, which is impossible. Hence
V\sqsubseteq U
, proving the claim. ∎

The following claim now completes the proof of the lemma:

Claim 16.21
.

There exists
\zeta=\zeta(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
so that if
\mathbf{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{A}
separates
\mathbf{h}_{B},\mathbf{h}_{C}\in\mathcal{V}_{B}
, then
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathbf{h})\subset\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}(\mathrm{hull}_{U}(\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathbf{h}_{B})\cup\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathbf{h}_{C})))
.

As above, let
V=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}),B=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}_{B}),
and
C=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}_{C})
. Let
\hat{x}\in\mathbf{h}_{B}
and
\hat{y}\in\mathbf{h}_{C}
, so that
\hat{x}_{V}\in\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{B})
and
\hat{y}_{V}\in\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h}_{C})
. Set
h_{V}=\widehat{\pi}_{V}(\mathbf{h})
and
h_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}
if
V\sqsubset U
and the tree-hyperplane
h\in\widehat{T}_{U}
if
V=U
.

First suppose that
h_{U},\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U}
are all distinct in
\widehat{T}_{U}
. Then
h_{U}
separates
\hat{x}_{U}
from
\hat{y}_{U}
. This is because
h_{V}
separates
\hat{x}_{V},\hat{y}_{V}
in
\widehat{T}_{V}
, and is immediate when
V=U
, and otherwise when
V\sqsubset U
, this follows from the BGI property (7) of Lemma
7.14
and
0
-consistency of
\hat{x},\hat{y}
. But this implies that
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathbf{h})
coarsely separates
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathbf{h}_{A})
from
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathbf{h}_{B})
in
\mathcal{C}(U)
, with the coarseness depending only on
\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|
, so we are done in this case.

Now suppose that
h_{A},\hat{x}_{U},
and
\hat{y}_{U}
are not distinct. This forces the non-distinct
\widehat{T}_{U}
-coordinates to be at a cluster point, which forces
V\sqsubsetneq U
and at least one of
A\sqsubsetneq U
or
B\sqsubsetneq U
.

There are several subcases, involving similar arguments. The first main subcase is when
A\pitchfork V
and
B\pitchfork V
. In this case, Lemma
12.13
implies that
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{A}_{V}
and
\hat{y}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{B}_{V}
. But since
h_{V}
separates
\hat{x}_{V}
from
\hat{y}_{V}
, this says that
\hat{\delta}^{A}_{V}\neq\hat{\delta}^{B}_{V}
, which is impossible if
\rho^{V}_{U}\notin\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}_{2E}(\mathrm{hull}_{U}(\rho^{A}_{U}\cup\rho^{B}_{U}))
.

For the remaining subcases, we may assume without loss of generality that
A\sqsubset U
and
\hat{\delta}^{A}_{U}=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{U}\neq\hat{y}_{U}
. The two cases are when
B=U
and
B\sqsubset U
, and we explain the former case, leaving the latter for the reader.

We may assume that
A\pitchfork V
, otherwise
d_{U}(\rho^{A}_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<E
and we are done. Let
T_{U}
denote the Gromov tree for
F\cup\Lambda
, and let
y_{U}=q^{-1}_{U}(\hat{y}_{U})
. Let
C
be the cluster in
T_{U}
containing both
\delta^{V}_{U}
and
\delta^{A}_{U}
. Assuming that
\rho^{V}_{U}\notin\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}_{\zeta}(\mathrm{hull}_{U}(\rho^{A}_{U}\cup\phi^{-1}_{U}(y_{U})))
for some
\zeta=\zeta(\mathfrak{S},|F\cup\Lambda|)>0
, there exits a
\sqsubset_{\mathcal{U}}
-minimal bipartite domain
W\in\mathcal{U}
so that
W\sqsubset U
,
W\pitchfork U
,
W\pitchfork A
, and
\delta^{W}_{U}
separates
\delta^{V}_{U}
from
\delta^{A}_{U}
and
y_{U}
in
T_{U}
.

This, along with the BGI property (7) of Lemma
7.14
and Lemma
12.13
, implies that
\widehat{\pi}_{W}(\mathbf{h})=\hat{\delta}^{V}_{W}
and
\hat{\delta}^{A}_{W}=\hat{y}_{W}=\hat{x}_{W}
are at the two distinct endpoints of
\widehat{T}_{W}
. But then
0
-consistency implies that
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{V}=\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{y}_{V}
, which is a contradiction of the assumption that
h_{V}
separates
\hat{x}_{V}
from
\hat{y}_{V}
. This completes the proof.

∎

16.6.
General UBSes

Now let
\mathcal{W}
be any UBS in
\mathcal{Q}
. The goal is to use Lemma
16.19
above to study the components of a minimal decomposition of
\mathcal{W}
provided by Hagen’s Theorem
16.6
:

We begin with a simple hierarchical fact:

Lemma 16.22
.

Suppose that
V,W,U,Z\in\mathfrak{S}
so that
V,W\sqsubset U
with
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},\rho^{W}_{U})>3E
, while also
Z\perp V
and
Z\perp W
. Then
Z\perp U
.

Proof.

We cannot have
U\sqsubset Z
or
U=Z
. And if
Z\sqsubset U
or
Z\pitchfork U
, then
d_{U}(\rho^{Z}_{U},\rho^{V}_{U})<E
and
d_{U}(\rho^{Z}_{U},\rho^{W}_{U})<E
, showing
d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},\rho^{W}_{U})<2E
, which is a contradiction. Hence
Z\perp U
and we are done.
∎

Lemma 16.23
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be a UBS in
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{W}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}
its minimal decomposition. Then
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\perp\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V}_{j})
for all
i\neq j
.

Proof.

By Lemma
16.19
, we have that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V}_{i})=\{U_{i}\}
for some
U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
and each
i
. In particular,
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})=\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
and any UBS almost equivalent to
\mathcal{W}
has the same support set by Hagen’s Theorem
16.6
. Lemma
16.19
also provides a representative of each
\mathcal{V}_{i}
so that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\sqsubseteq U_{i}
for each
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
and each
i
.

Let
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Then by Theorem
16.6
, for any
j\neq i
, there exists a cofinite subset
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{j}\subset\mathcal{V}_{j}
so that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\perp\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}^{\prime})
for any
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{j}
. Since
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{j})=\{U_{j}\}
and
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}^{\prime})\sqsubseteq U_{j}
for all
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{j}
, either some
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{j}
has
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h}^{\prime})=U_{j}
, or there exists
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{j}
whose
\rho
-sets are far apart in
\mathcal{C}(U_{j})
, and hence Lemma
16.22
implies that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})\perp U_{j}
.

Now either there exists infinitely-many
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
with
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{h})=U_{i}
, or otherwise there exist
\mathbf{h}_{1},\mathbf{h}_{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
whose
\rho
-sets are far apart in
\mathcal{C}(U_{i})
and hence Lemma
16.22
implies that
U_{i}\perp U_{j}
. Either way, we are done.
∎

The following proposition simply combines Lemmas
16.19
and
16.23
plus the uniqueness of the minimal decomposition of
\mathcal{W}
up to almost-equivalence from Theorem
16.6
:

Proposition 16.24
.

For any UBS
\mathcal{W}
in
\mathcal{Q}
, any minimal decomposition
\mathcal{W}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}
has the following properties:

(1)

For each
i
, there exists
U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
so that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V}_{i})=\{U_{i}\}
.

(2)

For
i\neq j
, we have
U_{i}\perp U_{j}
.

(3)

For each
i
, the trail of dust
\mathrm{Dust}_{U_{i}}(\mathcal{V}_{i})
converges to
\pi_{U_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U_{i})
for some
\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda
.

•

In particular, the
U_{i}
and
\lambda_{i}
are independent of the choice of representative of
\mathcal{W}
from its almost equivalence class.

Remark 16.25
.

In (3) above, the dust set
\mathrm{Dust}_{U_{i}}(\mathcal{V}_{i})
is countable. The claim that its
trail converges
to
\lambda_{i}
is that, given any enumeration, the corresponding sequence of points in
\mathcal{C}(U_{i})
converges to
\lambda_{i}
.

16.7.
Visibility of
0
-simplices and surjectivity

As we will see below in Theorem
16.29
, the above structural results are enough to provide a simplicial embedding of
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
into
\partial H
. To see surjectivity, we need to combinatorial rays, such as those constructed in Proposition
14.5
.

Recall from Lemma
16.3
above that if
\gamma
is a combinatorial geodesic in
\mathcal{Q}
, then the set
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)
of hyperplanes crossed by
\gamma
determines a UBS
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)
.

Definition 16.26

(Visibility)
.

A simplex
\nu
of
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
is
visible
if there exists a combinatorial geodesic ray
\gamma
such that
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)
represents
\nu
. We say
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
is
fully visible
if every simplex is visible.

The following lemma proves that every simplex in
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
is visible, and is moreover required for proving that our boundary map is surjective in Theorem
16.29
below.

Lemma 16.27
.

Let
\Delta
be a simplex in
\partial H
, with the
0
-simplices
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}
of
\Delta
satisfying
\mathrm{supp}(\sigma_{i})=U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
and
\mathfrak{p}(\sigma_{i})=\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda
for each
i
. Then there exists a combinatorial geodesic ray
\gamma
in
\mathcal{Q}
so that the corresponding UBS
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)
has
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma))=\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
, and for each
i
, the trail of
\mathrm{Dust}_{U_{i}}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma))
in
\mathcal{C}(U_{i})
converges to
\pi_{U}(\lambda_{i})
in
\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
.

Proof.

Define a
0
-consistent tuple
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\infty}
as follows:

•

For each
i
, set
\hat{x}_{U_{i}}=\hat{\lambda}_{i,U_{i}}
, i.e., the coordinate of
\hat{\lambda}_{i}
in
\widehat{T}_{U_{i}}
.

•

For any
V\in\mathcal{U}
with
V\sqsubset U_{i}
or
V\pitchfork U_{i}
for some
i
, set
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U_{i}}_{V}=\hat{\lambda}_{i,V}
.

•

For any
V\in\mathcal{U}
with
U_{i}\sqsubset V
for some
i
, set
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U_{i}}_{V}=\hat{\lambda}_{V}
.

•

Fix
f\in F
, and for any
V\in\mathcal{U}
with
V\perp U_{i}
for all
i
, set
\hat{x}_{V}=\hat{f}_{V}
.

It is a straight-forward exercise to confirm that
\hat{x}
is
0
-consistent, after observing that the fact that the
U_{i}
are pairwise orthogonal means that, for instance,
\hat{\delta}^{U_{i}}_{V}=\hat{\delta}^{U_{j}}_{V}
if
U_{i},U_{j}\pitchfork V
. Observe also that
\hat{x}
is not canonical, because for each
i
either

(1)

\hat{\lambda}_{i,U}\in\partial\widehat{T}_{U_{i}}
, or

(2)

There exists an infinite-diameter cluster
C
in
T_{U_{i}}
which contains a ray limiting to
\lambda_{i,U}\in\partial T_{U_{i}}
.

In item (1),
\hat{x}
fails property (1) of Definition
7.9
of canonicality. In item (2), there exists an infinite-diameter cluster
C
in
T_{U}
which contains a ray limiting to
\lambda_{U}\in\partial T_{U}
. In this latter case, there is an infinite sequence of bipartite
\sqsubset
-minimal domains
(W_{k})
so that, for all
k
, we have
W_{k}\sqsubset U_{i}
with one endpoint of
\widehat{T}_{W_{k}}
labeled by
F\cup\Lambda-\{\lambda_{i}\}
and the other endpoint labeled by
\lambda_{i}
, and hence coinciding with
\hat{\lambda}_{W_{k}}
, which violates property (2) of the definition of canonicality.

Moreover, we can use the above cases to argue that
\mathcal{W}(\hat{f}|\hat{x})
is infinite as follows: In case (1), there exists an infinite sequence of hyperplanes labeled by
U_{i}
which separate
\hat{f}_{U_{i}}
from
\hat{\lambda}_{U_{i}}
, and in case (2), each domain in the sequence
(W_{k})
supports a hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{k}
whose tree-hyperplane
h_{k}
separates
\hat{f}_{W_{k}}
from
\hat{\lambda}_{W_{k}}
, while also
\mathbf{h}_{k}
separates
\mathbf{h}_{k-1}
from
\mathbf{h}_{k+1}
in
\mathcal{Q}
for each
k\geq 2
(this again uses the fact that each
W_{k}
is bipartite and
\sqsubset
-minimal).

Now apply Proposition
14.5
to produce a combinatorial geodesic ray
\gamma
in
\mathcal{Q}
, so that if
