\mathcal{W}(\gamma)=\mathcal{W}(\hat{f}|\hat{x})
.

We claim that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma))=\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
. To see this, observe that the above argument shows that
\mathrm{diam}_{U_{i}}(\mathrm{Dust}_{U_{i}}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma)))=\infty
for each
i
, and hence
U_{i}\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma))
for each
i
. On the other hand,
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma))
is pairwise orthogonal by Lemma
16.23
, so it suffices to eliminate candidate domains
V
which satisfy
V\perp U_{i}
for all
i
. However, by construction
\gamma
projects to
\hat{f}_{V}
for all
V\perp U_{i}
for all
i
, and since
\hat{f}
is canonical (Definition
7.9
) by Proposition
7.12
, we have that
\#\{V\in\mathcal{U}|\hat{f}_{V}\neq\hat{a}_{V}\}<\infty
for all
a\in F
. Hence
\mathrm{Dust}_{V}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma))
is contained in a bounded neighborhood of
\pi_{V}(F)
for all
V\perp U_{i}
for all
i
, and thus no domain orthogonal to all of
\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
can be in the support of
\mathcal{W}(\gamma)
.

Finally, we observe that for each
i
, Proposition
16.24
implies that
\mathrm{Dust}_{U_{i}}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma))
converges to some boundary point, and hence it must converge to
\pi_{U_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U_{i})
by construction and by the existence of sequences of hyperplanes of type (1) or (2) above. This completes the proof.

∎

As a corollary, we get:

Corollary 16.28
.

Every simplex in
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
is visible. In particular,
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
is fully visible.

16.8.
The map
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial H

With the above in hand, we can prove our isomorphism theorem:

Theorem 16.29
.

There exists a simplicial isomorphism
\partial\widehat{\Omega}:\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial H
. Moreover,
\partial\widehat{\Omega}
extends the map
\widehat{\Omega}:\mathcal{Q}\to H
in the following sense:

•

If
\mathcal{W}
is a UBS representing a simplex
\nu\subset\partial_{\Delta}(\mathcal{Q})
with
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{W})
and
\mathrm{Dust}_{U}(\mathcal{W})
converging to
\pi_{U}(\lambda)\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U)
for some
\lambda\in\Lambda
, then
\partial\widehat{\Omega}(\nu)
contains a boundary
0
-simplex
\sigma
so that
\mathrm{supp}(\sigma)=U
and
\mathfrak{p}(\sigma)=\pi_{U}(\lambda)
. In fact, all
0
-simplices of
\partial\widehat{\Omega}(\nu)
arise in this way.

Proof.

Let
\nu
be a simplex in
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
. We define a simplex
\partial\widehat{\Omega}(\nu)
in
\partial H
as follows:

Let
\mathcal{W}
be a UBS representing
\nu
with minimal decomposition
\mathcal{W}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}
. We now use Proposition
16.24
.

For each
i
, let
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{V}_{i})=\{U_{i}\}
and let
\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda
so that the trail of
\mathrm{Dust}_{U_{i}}(\mathcal{V}_{i})
converges to
\pi_{U_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U_{i})
.

Define a boundary
n
-simplex
\Sigma
in
\partial H
as follows:

•

For each
i
, let
\sigma_{i}
be the boundary
0
-simplex with
\mathrm{supp}(\sigma_{i})=U_{i}
and
\mathfrak{p}(\sigma_{i})=\pi_{U_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\in\partial\mathcal{C}(U_{i})
.

•

Set
\Sigma
to be the unique
n
-simplex with
0
-simplices
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}
.

Observe that
\Sigma
is indeed a boundary
n
-simplex in
\partial H
, and moreover all of its
0
-simplices arise in the way described in the “moreover” part of the statement of the theorem. Also observe that
\partial\widehat{\Omega}
clearly respects the simplicial structures of
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}
and
\partial H
, and hence defines a simplicial embedding.

Finally, Lemma
16.27
immediately gives that
\partial\widehat{\Omega}
is surjective and hence an isomorphism. This completes the proof.

∎

16.9.
Equivariant boundary isomorphisms

In this subsection, we briefly observe that HHS automorphisms induce isomorphisms between simplicial boundaries of a cubical model and its isometric image. This basically boils down to an application of Corollary
7.19
.

Recall that a map
h:X\to Y
between cube complexes is a
cubical isomorphism
if it induces a bijection between half-spaces that preserves inclusion and complementation. The reader can confirm that isomorphic cube complexes have isomorphic simplicial boundaries.

Corollary 16.30
.

If
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
is an HHS automorphism, the isometry
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}_{g}
provided by Corollary
7.19
is a cubical isomorphism and hence it extends to a simplicial isomorphism
\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}\to\partial_{\Delta}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

Proof.

Since the half-spaces in the cubical structure of
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
are determined by the half-trees of the
\widehat{T}_{U}
and
\widehat{T}_{gU}
, respectively, which are isometrically identified via
g
, its clear that the set of half-spaces are in bijective correspondence under
g
. Moreover, this correspondence clearly preserves complementation and that is preserves nesting is an application of Lemma
12.15
. This completes the proof.
∎

With this corollary, we complete the proofs of the equivariance parts of Theorems
B
and
D
from the introduction.

17.
Capturing curve graph distance from cubical models

In this section, we give an application of our refined cubical machinery. The goal is to show that one can encode distance in the top-level hyperbolic space associated to many HHSes
\mathcal{X}
by the combinatorics of hyperplanes in the cubical models. For this purpose, we will work in a slightly restricted class of HHSes, which notably includes all of the main examples, including all HHGs. We explain this context next.

17.1.
Unbounded products and the ABD structure

The following definitions come from joint work of the author with Abbott and Behrstock
[
ABD21
]
.

Definition 17.1
.

An HHS
\mathcal{X}
has

•

the
bounded domain dichotomy
if there exists
B>0
so that if
U\in\mathfrak{S}
has
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(U))>B
, then
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(U))=\infty
;

•

unbounded products
if for any
U\in\mathfrak{S}
not
\sqsubset
-maximal with
\mathrm{diam}(\mathbb{E}_{U})=\infty
, we have
\mathrm{diam}(\mathbb{F}_{U})=\infty
;

•

clean containers
if for each
U\in\mathfrak{S}
and each
V\sqsubset U
with
\{Z\in\mathfrak{S}_{V}|Z\perp V\}\neq\emptyset
, then the associated container provided by the orthogonality axiom (
3
) is orthogonal to
V
.

Note that all HHGs have the bounded domain dichotomy because of the requirement of a cofinite action of the set of domain labels, as do all of the main examples. On the other hand, some natural HHS structures, like those on right-angled Coxeter groups, do not have unbounded products. However, all of the main examples have the clean containers property, making the following meaningful
[
ABD21
, Theorem 3.11]
:

Theorem 17.2
.

Any hierarchically hyperbolic space
\mathcal{X}
with clean containers satisfying the bounded domain dichotomy admits an HHS structure with unbounded products, clean containers, and the bounded domain dichotomy.

For the rest of this section, we will refer to the HHS structure in Theorem
17.2
on an HHS with clean containers and the bounded domain dichotomy as the
ABD structure
.

The following is the only consequence of this fact that we will need:

Lemma 17.3
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
is an HHS with the bounded domain dichotomy and unbounded products. If
U\in\mathfrak{S}
has
\mathrm{diam}\mathcal{C}(U)=\infty
, then there exists
V\in\mathfrak{S}
with
V\perp U
so that
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(V))=\infty
.

Proof.

If
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(U))=\infty
, then the fact that
\mathcal{X}
is normalized (which we have made a blanket assumption, see Definition
2.10
) means that there exist
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
so that
d_{U}(x,y)
is as large as we need. In particular, the Distance Formula
2.9
on
\mathbb{F}_{U}
implies that
\mathrm{diam}(\mathbb{F}_{U})=\infty
. Since
\mathcal{X}
has unbounded products, we have
\mathrm{diam}(\mathbb{F}_{U})=\infty
, and hence for any
n
there exist
a_{n},b_{n}\in\mathbb{E}_{U}
with
d_{\mathbb{E}_{U}}(a_{n},b_{n})>n
. Since
\mathbb{E}_{U}
also has the bounded domain dichotomy with constant
B
, the distance formula on
\mathbb{E}_{U}
then implies that for some sufficiently large
N_{B}
, we have

\sum_{V\perp U}[d_{V}(a_{N_{B}},b_{N_{B}})]_{B}>B,

which implies that for at least one
V\perp U
, we have
d_{V}(a_{N_{B}},b_{N_{B}})>B
, and hence
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(V))=\infty
. Since
U\perp V
, we are done.
∎

17.2.
Separated hyperplanes and the statement of the theorem

Our goal is to encode curve graph distance via hyperplane combinatorics in cubical models. In order to complete the setup, we need the following definition due to Genevois
[
Gen20
]
:

Definition 17.4

(Separated hyperplanes)
.

Two disjoint hyperplanes
H_{1},H_{2}
in a CAT(0) cube complex
X
are
L
-separated (
L\geq 0
) if the number of hyperplanes meeting them both and containing no facing triple has cardinality at most
L
. A set
\{H_{i}\}
hyperplanes is an
L
-separated chain
if each
H_{i}
separates
H_{j}
from
H_{k}
for all
j<i<k
, and the set is pairwise
L
-separated.

•

For any
x,y\in X^{(0)}
and
L\geq 0
, we let
d^{X}_{L}(x,y)
denote the length of any maximal
L
-separated chain of hyperplanes separating
x,y
.

Notably, Genevois proves that this function
d^{X}_{L}
defines a distance on the vertices of any CAT(0) cube complex
X
. While we will not be able to define a new distance on an HHS using our current cubulation techniques, we can capture distance in the top-level hyperbolic space of a broad through restricted class of HHSes as follows:

Theorem 17.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
be an HHS with the bounded domain dichotomy equipped with its ABD structure, and
\sqsubset
-maximal domain
S\in\mathfrak{S}
. For any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, there exists a cubical model
\mathcal{Q}
for
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)
, so that

d_{\mathcal{C}(S)}(x,y)\asymp d^{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}(x,y),

where the coarse constants associated to
\asymp
depend only on the structure
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
.

The lower bound is
[
DZ22
, Theorem C]
, though there we need to take some separation constant
L=L(\mathcal{X})\geq 0
, as the limiting models from that paper do not encode domain relations in hyperplane labels, as is the case with our cubical models
\mathcal{Q}
. We give a short proof of the above better lower bound next in Lemma
17.6
which does not require a special cubical model. The upper bound requires adapting an observation from the world of surfaces.

17.3.
Lower bound

The following is an improved version of
[
DZ22
, Theorem C]
:

Lemma 17.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
be an HHS with the bounded domain dichotomy equipped with its ABD structure, and
\sqsubset
-maximal domain
S\in\mathfrak{S}
. For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
y\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\infty}
and any model
\mathcal{Q}
for
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)
, we have

d_{\mathcal{C}(S)}(x,y)\prec d^{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}(x,y),

with constants depending only on
\mathcal{X}
and the constants in the construction of
\mathcal{Q}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma^{\prime}
be a combinatorial geodesic (ray) in
\mathcal{Q}
between
\hat{x}=\widehat{\Psi}(x)
and
\hat{y}=\widehat{\Psi}(y)
, where
\widehat{\Psi}:\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)\to\mathcal{Q}
is a quasi-isometry from Corollary
11.2
with constants depending only on
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\gamma=\widehat{\Omega}(\gamma^{\prime})
be the corresponding cubical path (ray) between
x,y
in
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathcal{W}(\gamma^{\prime})
denote the set of hyperplanes
\gamma^{\prime}
crosses in
\mathcal{Q}
.

It follows from Claim
14.10
of Proposition
14.8
and Lemma
16.16
that
d^{Haus}_{S}(\mathrm{Dust}_{S}(\mathcal{W}(\gamma^{\prime})),[x,y]_{S})<\alpha=\alpha(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Q})
is uniformly bounded in terms of
\mathcal{X}
and the construction of
\mathcal{Q}
, where
[x,y]_{S}
is any geodesic in
\mathcal{C}(S)
between
x,y
.

Choose a
(10\alpha,\alpha)
-net
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
along
[x,y]_{S}
(Definition
8.4
), and for each
i
let
w_{i}\in\pi_{S}(\gamma)
so that
d_{S}(z_{i},w_{i})<\alpha.
For each
i
, let
\mathbf{h}_{i}\in\mathcal{W}(\gamma^{\prime})
be some hyperplane crossing a cube adjacent to
\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})
, where
w_{i}\in\pi_{S}(\widehat{\Omega}(\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})))
. Finally, for each
i
, let
U_{i}\in\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
denote the domain label for
\mathbf{h}_{i}
.

We claim that the
(\mathbf{h}_{i})
form a
0
-separated chain between
\hat{x},\hat{y}
. To see this, note that as long as
\alpha>5E
and
i\neq j
, we have that
d_{S}(\mathrm{Dust}_{S}(\mathbf{h}_{i},,\mathbf{h}_{j})>5E
, and hence there does not exist a domain
V\in\mathfrak{S}
so that
V\perp U_{i}
and
V\perp U_{j}
. In particular, not only do
\mathbf{h}_{i}
and
\mathbf{h}_{j}
not cross, no hyperplane in
\mathcal{Q}
crosses both of them, for any
i\neq j
by item (2) of Lemma
12.10
.

It follows then that
d^{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\geq n\asymp d_{S}(x,y)
, where the constants in
\asymp
depend only on
\mathcal{X}
and the choices made in the construction of
\mathcal{Q}
, and in particular not on
x,y
. This completes the proof.
∎

17.4.
Standing assumption

For the rest of this section, we fix
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
to be an HHS with the bounded domain dichotomy and unbounded products endowed with its ABD structure, for which
S\in\mathfrak{S}
is the
\sqsubset
-maximal domain. The goal is to prove the reverse bound of Lemma
17.6
for some variation of our cubical model construction, whose definition involves an idea from the world of surfaces.

17.5.
Motivation: Filling curve pairs

Let
\Sigma
be a finite type surface and
\alpha,\beta
simple closed curves. Then
\alpha,\beta
are
filling
if, up to isotopy, the complement
\Sigma-(\alpha\cup\beta)
is a union of disks and once-punctured disks. Let
\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
denote the curve graph of
\Sigma
, whose vertices are isotopy classes of simple closed curves on
\Sigma
, with two classes connected by an edge if and only if they have disjoint representatives. Then
\alpha,\beta
are filling if and only if
d_{\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)}(\alpha,\beta)\geq 3
, because if
\gamma
is a simple closed curve disjoint (up to isotopy) from
\alpha\cup\beta
, then
d_{\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)}(\alpha,\beta)\leq 2
by the triangle inequality.

Suppose now that
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
are a pair of (complete) markings on
\Sigma
(in the sense of
[
MM00
]
) which have sufficiently large projections to the curve graphs associated to a family of subsurfaces
\mathcal{V}
of
\Sigma
. If
\alpha
is any geodesic between the projections of
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
, then the Bounded Geodesic Image Theorem
[
MM00
, Theorem 3.1]
says that
d_{\mathcal{C}{\Sigma}}(\alpha,\partial V)\leq 1
for any
V\in\mathcal{V}
. It is not hard to show that if in addition the subsurfaces in
\mathcal{V}
have pairwise filling boundaries, then
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\partial V)\asymp\#\mathcal{V}
, since each has to be pairwise at least distance
3
away, while also being distance
1
from a geodesic.

In the rest of this section, we will first generalize this filling philosophy to the setting of HHSes with unbounded products, and then tweak our cubical models to encode this filling property into the structure of their hyperplanes.

17.6.
Filling pairs of domains

We begin with a definition that is motivated by the notion of a pair of filling simple closed curves on a finite-type surface.

Definition 17.7

(Filling domains)
.

We say a pair of domains
U,V\in\mathfrak{S}
is
filling
if there does not exist a domain
W\in\mathfrak{S}
with
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(W))=\infty
so that
W\perp U
and
W\perp V
.

Observe that if
U,V
are not filling, then
d_{S}(\rho^{U}_{S},\rho^{V}_{S})\leq 2E
. On the other hand:

Lemma 17.8
.

Suppose
U,V\in\mathfrak{S}
are filling and at least one of
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(U))=\infty
or
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(V))=\infty
. Then if
W\in\mathfrak{S}
with
U\sqsubset W
or
V\sqsubset W
, we have
W=S
.

Proof.

Suppose instead that
W\sqsubsetneq S
. Since at least one of
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(U))=\infty
or
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(V))=\infty
, the Distance Formula
2.9
provides that
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{F}_{W})=\infty
, and hence
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{E}_{W})=\infty
by our unbounded products assumption. But then Lemma
17.3
implies that some
Z\perp W
has
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(Z))=\infty
, which contradicts the assumption that
U,V
fill, giving us a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 17.9
.

For any
K_{0}>\max\{B,E\}
, if
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathrm{Rel}_{K_{0}}(x,y)
is a pairwise filling collection of domains, then
\#\mathcal{V}\prec\mathrm{diam}_{S}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\rho^{V}_{S})
.

Proof.

Note that by choosing
K_{0}>B
(the bounded domain dichotomy constant, Definition
17.1
), we have that
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{C}(V))=\infty
for all
V\in\mathcal{V}
. Then the Large Links Axiom
6
provides domains
T_{1},\dots,T_{N}\in\mathfrak{S}
with
N\asymp d_{S}(x,y)
so that each of the domains in
\mathcal{V}
nest into one of the
T_{i}
. On the other hand, Lemma
17.8
says the only domain in which any pair of the domains in
\mathcal{V}
nests is
S
. Hence each
V\in\mathcal{V}
nests into at most one
T_{i}
, and thus
\#\mathcal{V}\leq N\asymp d_{S}(x,y)
, as required.
∎

17.7.
Well-separated cubical models

In this subsection, we explain how to modify the standard cubical model for the hull of a pair of points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, so that any collection of
0
-separated hyperplanes is labeled by a pairwise filling collection of domains. The construction uses the setting of hierarchical family of trees as in Definition
12.1
, not in the HHS itself, as well as Tree Trimming
10.2
.

Fix points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, their hull
H=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)
, and set of relevant domains
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Rel}_{K}(x,y)
, where
K
is the largeness constant as in Section
5
, nd also larger than the bounded domain dichotomy constant
B
so taht we can eventually apply Lemma
17.8
. Let
(\widehat{T}_{U})_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
be the associated collection of simplicial trees with their relative projections, as produced by Tree Trimming
10.2
as in Corollary
10.9
.

We begin with the definition of extra domains which act as a firewall against fake
0
-separation in the cubical model for
x,y
:

Definition 17.10

(Firewall domains)
.

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\neq S
, the
\sqsubset
-maximal domain, the
firewall
for
U
is a domain label
V_{U}
and a copy of the unit interval
\widehat{T}_{V_{U}}=[0,1]
, along with the following relation and (collapsed) relative projection data:

•

V_{U}\sqsubset S
and
\hat{\delta}^{S}_{V_{U}}\equiv 0
is a constant map.

•

For each
W\in\mathcal{U}
with
W,U
not filling, we set
V_{U}\perp W
.

•

If
W\in\mathcal{U}-\{S\}
forms a filling pair with
U
, then we set
W\pitchfork V_{U}
,
\hat{\delta}^{V_{U}}_{W}=\hat{\delta}^{U}_{W}
and
\hat{\delta}^{W}_{U_{V}}=0
.

•

We set
\hat{x}_{V_{U}}=\hat{y}_{V_{U}}=0
.

Lemma 17.11
.

The set of domain labels
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}\cup\{V_{U}|U\in\mathcal{U}-\{S\}\}
along with its associated set of trees, projections, and relative projections, is a hierarchical family of trees. Hence we can define its
0
-consistent set
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We discuss the non-immediate items of Definition
12.1
. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}-\{S\}
. The BGI property follows from defining
\hat{\delta}^{S}_{V_{U}}\equiv 0
. Finally, the tuples
(\hat{x}_{U}),(\hat{y}_{U})
are in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
because in each of the addition
\widehat{T}_{V_{U}}
domains, the coordinate coincides with all possible relative projections (which all come from transverse relations). This completes the proof.

∎

As proven in Theorem
13.6
, the
0
-consistent
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is isometric to a cube complex, and we refer to
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
as the
firewall cubical model
for
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)
. The following explains the relationship between
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
:

Lemma 17.12
.

The map
\Delta:\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Q}
which collapses each of the new unit intervals
\widehat{T}_{V_{U}}
for
U\in\mathcal{U}-\{S\}
is a uniform quasi-isometry, with constants depending only on
\mathcal{X}
. Moreover, the map
\Xi:\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\widehat{T}_{U}\to\prod_{U\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}\widehat{T}_{U}
which fixes coordinates for
U\in\mathcal{U}
and assigns
0
for the domains in
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}-\mathcal{U}
is an isometric embedding.

Proof.

The first statement is an immediate consequence of Tree Trimming
10.2
. The second statement is an immediate consequence of the observation that the only new hyperplanes in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
that are not in
\mathcal{Q}
are labeled by domains in
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}-\mathcal{U}
, and these new hyperplanes cannot separate vertices whose new coordinates are all the same by Lemma
13.2
.
∎

With this in hand, we can prove the following, which is the main point of the construction:

Proposition 17.13
.

A pair of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
-hyperplanes
\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
are
0
-separated if and only if their domain labels are filling.

Proof.

We first observe that

(1)

Any hyperplane in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
labeled by
S
crosses no other hyperplane, and

(2)

For every
U\in\mathcal{U}-\{S\}
, there is a hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{V_{U}}
in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
labeled by
V_{U}
which crosses every hyperplane
\mathbf{h}
which is labeled by a domain
W\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
for which
U,W
is not a filling pair.

(1) is an immediate consequence of Proposition
12.18
. (2) is an immediate consequence of the construction, plus Lemma
12.13
, which says that the midpoint
\frac{1}{2}=h_{V_{U}}\in\widehat{T}_{V_{U}}=[0,1]
determines a nonempty hyperplane
\mathbf{h}_{V_{U}}
, and Proposition
12.18
, which says that
\mathbf{h}_{V_{U}}
crosses any hyperplane labeled by a domain orthogonal to
V_{U}
, which is, by definition, any domain not making a filling pair with
U
.

With these observations in hand, first suppose that
\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
are
0
-separated, with domain labels
U,W\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
respectively. Then (1) and (2) above says that
U,W
are a filling a pair (with possibly one or both being
S
). On the other hand, if
U,W
are a filling pair, then we are done by Lemma
17.8
plus our assumption that
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{S})
satisfies the bounded domain dichotomy. In particular, by choosing the largeness constant
K
to be larger than the constant
B
in Definition
17.1
, we guarantee that the set of domains in
\mathcal{U}
orthogonal to both
U,W
is empty. Hence no hyperplanes can cross both
\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
by Lemma
12.10
. This completes the proof.
∎

We also need the following observation which is true of any cubical model in any HHS
\mathcal{X}
:

Lemma 17.14
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
be two points in any HHS and
\mathcal{Q}
any cubical model for the hull of
x,y
. For any
U\in\mathcal{U}
, if
m
is the number of
\mathcal{Q}
-hyperplanes labeled by
U
separating
x
from
y
, then
m\prec d_{U}(x,y)
, where the constant of
\prec
depends only on
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

By item (4) of Lemma
7.14
, we have
m\leq d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\hat{x}_{U},\hat{y}_{U})\leq d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},y_{U})\prec d_{U}(x,y)
, so we are done.
∎

We are now ready to prove the upper bound:

Proposition 17.15
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
and the firewall cubical model
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
for
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,y)
satisfies

d_{\mathcal{C}(S)}(x,y)\succ d^{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}_{0}(x,y),

with constants depending only on
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{h}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{h}_{n}
be a
0
-separated chain separating
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
. By Proposition
17.13
and Lemma
17.9
, the number
m
of domains
U\sqsubsetneq S
labeling hyperplanes in the chain satisfies

m\prec\mathrm{diam}_{S}(\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\rho^{V}_{S})\prec d_{S}(x,y),

with the latter coarse inequality provided by the BGIA
7
. On the other hand, the number of domains in the chain labeled by
S
is
n-m
. Hence we are done by Lemma
17.9
and Lemma
17.14
.
∎
